    November 20th, 2016 | 02:04 A.M.

    The radio began to crackle more and more the further up the mountain she drove, but she didn’t want to make the drive without it. The car didn’t have a cassette deck or a cd player installed in the center console. She should have picked a different model on her way out of danger, but the cars at the side of the road had been slim pickings. Plus, she’d had to move fast—had she dawdled, her wranglers might have decided that she’d been gone too long and needed to be put back to sleep. She was tired of the relentless fucking silence of the Red Room. Hundreds of girls conditioned to be quiet, where the only sound was the footsteps of the doctors and military personnel that guarded and studied them.

    She was out of it now, for the moment, having cut out her tracker about three-hundred miles back where she first started her journey. Unless they finally found a way to hack a black widow’s blood, she was free to pursue her escape without the Red Room tracking her movements. She’d even left behind her tactical suit and widow’s bites just in case back-up trackers were sewn into the fabric or programmed into the electronics. She wasn’t taking any risks—not this time. The last time she tried to leave, they forced her to endure severe reconditioning and punishment upon recapturing her, but they let her live. She’d gotten away with that first attempt on the count of being the most lethal and prolific child assassin they ever created, but her skills and reputation would only get her so far. Already, new girls were being bought, new girls were being kidnapped, new girls were being conditioned. And Yelena, no matter how talented, was still property in their eyes. Damaged property at that— now that she’d shown that even their most cruel programs could not keep her loyal.

    The radio continued to crackle as Yelena stared at the road ahead. While making the drive, the hours had whittled away, and night befell the world around her, leaving nothing to illuminate the mountain road she found herself on aside from the truck’s headlights. They were strong, but Yelena still spent most of her concentration on taking the curves in the road properly and not driving against any large rock formations or trees close-by. On the passenger seat next to her, several empty wallets were gathered into a small pile. Meanwhile, the cash Yelena scavenged from them was tucked away neatly into the back pocket of the jeans she’d stolen from someone’s backyard. In the foot space, several grocery bags took up residence, making crinkly noises every time they shifted in tandem with the truck.

    The music suddenly cut off completely, replaced by horrifically loud static, and Yelena let out an exasperated breath as she reached towards the console with her right hand, blindly fiddling with the knob of the radio that allowed her to switch stations. Outside, a snowstorm was brewing. There’d been stretches here and there of gentle snowflakes falling from the sky, which wasn’t much of a problem while it was still daytime and fairly bright outside. But now, with only her headlights and the full moon above to show her the way, she didn’t need the added distraction of snow to obscure her vision.

    Quietly cursing in Russian as the radio continued to crackle in and out of range, Yelena took her eyes off the road only long enough to make sure she was moving the dial in the right direction, before returning them to the road. As soon as she did, she knew she fucked up, because when she saw something large and furry cross the road up ahead, it was already too late to slam the brakes and avoid hitting whatever it was. Her foot felt like it broke through the floor of the truck with how hard she depressed the pedal, and she instinctively closed her eyes as the truck crashed into the body of the animal, throwing Yelena forward upon impact. Her seatbelt grew tight and unforgiving, most likely causing bruises along the way, but the airbag didn’t deploy, and the car came to a halt soon after, leaving Yelena clutching the steering wheel and breathing heavily.

    “Сука,” Yelena muttered to herself, consciously unclenching her hands from around the wheel and lifting her head to see what fresh horrors she had wrought upon the world. Even though it might have been irrational, Yelena’s mind could not stomach the thought of something as innocent as a stag or a deer being mutilated at her hands. Killing humans was almost second nature to her at this point, but no wildlife had ever tried to hurt her.

    Clenching her jaw in determination, Yelena unbuckled her seatbelt and staggered out of her truck and into the snow. Her headlights were still functioning as intended, although Yelena wouldn’t be surprised if the glass in them cracked from the impact. Still, they were enough to illuminate the hulking shape that lay in front of her, and Yelena’s heart sank into her stomach as she finally realized what it was.

    The black fur was matted in places with blood—far more blood than the collision with the car could have caused, which might explain why it had been crossing the road at such a slow speed in the first place and why it didn’t move out of the way. Even in its slumped over form, its shoulders nearly came up to Yelena’s stomach, and its limbs were sprawled out underneath it. Its ears were still and unmoving, indicating that the giant wolf at her feet had been knocked unconscious. At least… she thought it was a wolf. It was hard to tell with the muzzle turned away. It could have been a dog, although Yelena had never seen a dog that size before.

    “Okay,” Yelena whispered to herself, stretching out her hand but stopping herself at the last second. The wolf’s chest moved up and down, but she wasn’t sure how long that would last, given the amount of blood loss she could see even without proper lighting. “Alright. Stay right there.” Retreating back to the cabin of the truck, Yelena climbed inside and turned on the light above the center console, before rooting around in one of her many grocery bags. The radio crackled onwards, occasionally finding its way to a station before leaving it again, but Yelena paid it no mind as she finally found what she was looking for.

    With her flashlight in hand, Yelena pushed herself back out of the cabin and went to the back, where she pulled off the tarp that covered the truck bed. There was a pile of blankets in one corner that had come with the truck, as well as a canister of gas Yelena placed there herself during her last pitstop at a gas station. Dumping the tarp on the ground, she reached inside for the pile of blankets, while the snow continued to fall around her. It was cold as hell in the wilderness of Maine, but Yelena had survived Russian winters on more than one occasion.

    Running back to the wolf, Yelena laid out the blanket next to it, before pushing the wolf away from the car and onto the fabric. It was difficult, because the body of the animal seemed to be made of nothing but heavy muscle and bone, but she hadn’t earned the title of most prolific child assassin for nothing. Being stronger than any opponent aided her many times, and right now, it helped her roll a wolf onto a blanket and drag that blanket all the way to the back of the truck. There were spare metal parts on the truck that allowed for a rudimentary ramp to be built, which Yelena used to drag the wolf up and up and up until it was safely squared away on the bed.

    Breathing heavily at the exertion, Yelena tucked a second blanket around the hulking body, then jumped back down, returned the metal sheets to one side of the wolf, before finally pulling the tarp back on and redoing the knots that would keep it in place. Her hands shook by the time she was done, but she still took a second to gather herself, leaning her forehead against the side of her car and allowing the whispers of the wind to calm her down.

    As soon as she was back in her car, she switched off the crackling static of the radio, lest she miss any howls or whines or other signs of pain from her wounded new friend. Then, just before she reached up to turn off the overhead lights, she hesitated. There was fresh blood on her hands, glistening in the warm yellow glow of the old light bulb. Taking a deep breath, she squeezed her eyes shut for a moment and counted to three before opening them again and reaching inside one of the grocery bags with her less bloody fingers. The sealed box of tissues grew red and brown in places as she pulled off the top, but by the time she could scrub at her fingers, she considered the minor damage more than worth it.

    Once she was satisfied with the state of her hands, Yelena finally turned off the light, buckled herself back in, and continued to drive. The snow fell heavier now, but she didn’t feel like she could slow down any more than she already had. While the heater in her truck worked just fine, keeping her warm and toasty in her stolen brown overcoat, her furry friend in the truck bed didn’t have such luxury. The cold might do it some good in the short term, but the longer they stayed on the road, the more Yelena was concerned about the wolf freezing to death.

    And while she was an expert at burying fellow widows and targets that needed to be hidden from view, that wasn’t the life she wanted. Not now, not ever.

    Completing the journey took another two hours and change, and Yelena only stopped once to squint at an old-fashioned paper map to make sure she drove in the right direction. The single piece of electronics she had on her was a burner phone, and it wasn’t hooked up to the internet or GPS. She knew the coordinates of the place she wanted to get to, but she’d never been there before and not even a widow’s intuition was enough to take the right exit on a highway that no one had ever heard of, except for the people who happened to live on that stretch of the road.

    The full moon did its best to light up the small one-story stone cabin she drove up to, the truck coming to a rolling halt right in front of the porch. Leaving the engine running just in case there was something or someone unwelcome waiting for her, Yelena got out of the car and rounded to the back, checking if her passenger was still alive. As soon as she removed the tarp, she noticed how warm it was, and relief and worry started to war within her chest with equal fervor. Her furry friend was a fighter, it seemed, but running a fever.

    “Okay, buddy,” Yelena whispered to the unmoving form, reaching out and squeezing its tail. “I’ll set things up inside and then I’ll come back and get you. Hang tight.” She readjusted the tarp, then walked to the passenger side of the truck and grabbed the first grocery bag. The key to the cabin was hidden inside a hollow stone several feet away, imbedded in the thick roots of a tree too far from the front door to be noticed if you didn’t know what you were looking for. The door unlocked with a satisfyingly loud click, but before Yelena hit the light switch to illuminate the place, she set the grocery bag down by the front door, removed her gun from her shoulder holster, and checked each room for potential intruders.

    The back door was locked; the bathroom and bedroom were empty. And the open living area and kitchen didn’t leave much room to hide, so Yelena felt pretty confident that no one had beat her to this location. It would have surprised her if they did, considering that no one knew about this place except for her and Natasha. But in a world that housed the Red Room, anything was possible.

    Annoyingly, when Yelena hit the light switch, nothing happened, meaning that the electronics would need to be checked. It was most likely an easy fix for an issue born of months or years of neglect. Or maybe it had just been turned off temporarily while the cabin wasn’t in use, but Yelena didn’t have the patience or the mental fortitude to go outside, get her flashlight, and go in search of the problem at hand. Instead, she returned to her truck, got the rest of her grocery bags, then went back outside to check the firewood storage of the cabin. When she saw that there was enough to get a fire going, she trembled all over with relief, then grabbed as much as she would need and returned to the main living area. Any widow worth her salt knew how to get a fire started. Having to find shelter wasn’t uncommon on missions, so it didn’t take her much time. Satisfied that the fire would keep crackling without her, Yelena pushed the furniture out of the way and rolled up the rug in front of the fireplace, leaning it up against the wall. Then, she removed the bulky first-aid-kit from one of the bigger grocery bags and opened it up. She’d bought it for herself in case things turned violent, but it would do her just as well to aid the wolf-dog she’d run over.

    Returning to her truck, she finally shut it off and closed and locked it, before going back to the tarp and undoing all the knots and knobs to loosen it. She carried it inside and covered the floor with it in the hopes of sparing the wooden floors from the blood of the animal. Then, she used the same metal plates to create a make-shift ramp and dragged the wolf off the truck bed and through the snow on the same blanket as before. She stopped in front of the fireplace, wondering briefly if it was a good idea to warm the animal even more if it really was running a fever.

    By the time she fixed up the truck and returned inside, the fire was big and bright enough to illuminate the hulking beast in front of it. Now it was obvious that it was a wolf and not a dog, and Yelena exhaled slowly and deeply in vague indecision. In her entire life, she’d never seen a wolf that size. It was huge enough to threaten her life if thoroughly provoked, but she couldn’t bear the thought of leaving it to die out in the woods, even if it might attack her upon waking. If it did, it wouldn’t do so out of cruelty, but merely out of a sense of self- preservation, and Yelena couldn’t fault it for that. Not when she’d done the very same thing so many times.

    With the door to the cabin closed and the room finally mostly visible, Yelena went to work.

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 04:23 A.M.

    A battery-powered radio in the stone cabin was tuned to what Yelena assumed was the one radio station that could be picked up this far up the mountain. It didn’t matter what old or wretched song it played; Yelena was glad of its presence all the same. Especially because the sound of cleaning matted fur from gouges and gunshot wounds was neither pleasant nor calming. What made the whole situation worse was realizing the poor animal under her hands had been hurt in much the same manner as Yelena herself had been hurt before. So, she drowned out the quiet with bad music and talked quietly in Russian, hoping that whatever dormant lizard brain the wolf possessed would hear her even in its unconscious state and realize that she was not a threat.

    She’d picked up a razor on her supply run to the grocery store where she’d purchased snacks and meals that would be easy to prepare without a functioning microwave or oven. Not because she was particularly fussed about keeping herself hairless, but because on occasion, being able to dismantle the tool and come away with several small but very sharp blades could come in handy. What she’d quickly realized, however, was that the razor was utterly incapable of handling shaving patches of long, thick wolf hair, so Yelena had returned to the age-old method of cutting hair with a very sharp knife. It worked well enough.

    By the time she was done patching up the animal to the best of her ability, it was early enough for the sun to have risen, but even if she’d drawn the curtains to keep it out, she was too full of adrenaline to lie down and go to sleep. Leaving the wolf to rest, Yelena got to her feet and walked into the bathroom, where, at the very least, she knew the indoor plumbing still worked. She’d filled a bowl with water there earlier, where she’d subsequently wrung out the cloth she used to wipe away the worst of the blood from the wolf’s several wounds before getting to work.

    Her reflection looked ashen, and there were streaks of blood on her face from where she’d wiped away her sweat. Placing her hands on the porcelain rim of the sink, she closed her eyes and went through everything that took place in the last few days. Escaping from her handlers, ridding herself of everything that tied her to the Red Room, sneaking into someone’s backyard and stealing their clothing, hijacking the truck and driving it away, going on a grocery store run while avoiding all the cameras, driving for hours on end, until finally properly setting the broken hindleg bone of a giant black wolf, splinting it and wrapping it in gauze.

    She was tired; she was wide-awake; she was worried; she was relieved; she was in danger; she was safe; she was powerful; she was weak; she was hurt; she was free.

    Opening her eyes, she stared at herself.

    “What are you looking at?” she asked, feeling and seeing the corners of her mouth move into a scowl. “What are you looking at?” She clenched her hands harder around the porcelain before finally looking away. No one was controlling her now. No one was looking at her now. No one was expecting anything of her now.

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 05:44 A.M.

    In lieu of going to sleep, Yelena went outside and looked for the electrical box affixed to the side of the cabin. She wasn’t entirely sure where it was, and the snow made trudging around outside very annoying, but as soon as she found it and opened it up to check its contents, she was glad she made the trip. Fortunately, her hunch the night before had been correct; someone had turned off the electronics to protect them from short-circuiting while no one was around to fix anything that might occur.

    Once the electricity was restored, she surveilled the area around the cabin and shoveled a pathway from the front door to the truck, which was covered in a heavy duvet of thick, white snow. It wasn’t powdery in the slightest, and she briefly considered whether it would be worth it to clean it off the hood of the truck, just in case she needed to make a quick getaway or embark on a grocery run. In the end, she decided against it, because she had enough food and water to last her at least a couple of days, and if the Red Room came to get her, they wouldn’t leave her truck untouched. Plus, the weather report of the local newspaper she purchased said the weather was supposed to get better around the same time as she might start to run out of supplies. Of course, when she made those calculations, she didn’t have a wolf staying with her who might need something fresh and bloody to eat.

    Maybe she was finally going mad after holding it together for so long, but Yelena considered it might not be the worst idea to set up a few snares around the place. Besides—all of that instant-food she purchased might not be enough if the weather decided to be difficult, even if the wolf didn’t survive and wouldn’t need to be fed. It was always a good idea to take precautions and come up with contingency plans.

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 10:56 A.M.

    After spending the whole morning outside and setting up traps, Yelena returned with a mighty need for food and a shower, even if the water wouldn’t warm up entirely. Or at all. Covered in sweat and blood and grime and slate, she was desperate to scrub at herself until her body was clean. And common sense told her to care for the wound on her thigh where she’d removed the Red Room’s new and improved tracking device.

    Stomping her boots on the mat inside the front door, Yelena shook her shoulders to get more of the snow off her, and only realized belatedly that the wolf had moved. It was no longer laying on its side in front of the fire. Rather, it had turned so it was facing the open doorway, its broken leg tucked awkwardly under its body and its front paws level with the ground.

    Crystal blue eyes stared at Yelena, while a dangerously low growl started to reverberate inside the room. Now that it was half-standing, half-leaning, it seemed even taller and deadlier, and made Yelena feel equal parts foolish and in awe.

    “Hey,” Yelena whispered, keeping her body perfectly still now that she’d noticed the predator staring at her. “Hey, hey, shh.” She wasn’t sure if she should keep looking it directly in the eyes, but somehow, averting her gaze felt just as risky. “You know, you really shouldn’t be standing up. Your leg is broken. And your shoulders have been shredded to shit.” She slowly lifted her hands, as though the animal was capable of understand human social cues. Her heart nearly broke when she realized that the wolf was shaking like a leaf from head to toe, clearly terrified. “It’s okay. I’m not going to hurt you. I promise.” Slowly lowering herself down to her right knee, she reached towards her left, where she’d placed some of the grocery bags the night before. When the growling grew in volume, she made gentle shushing noises again. “It’s okay. I have some beef jerky we can share.”

    If the wolf decided to lunge at her, she knew it would only have the strength to do it once. With a leg as badly broken as that, it might be able to get on top of her, but it would be easy to kick away. Not to mention the two gunshot wounds to its back and shoulder severely limiting its strength. The fact that the wolf was upright at all was a miracle that Yelena wasn’t sure how to feel about.

    But it didn’t lunge, not even when she started rooting around in the bag until she found what she was looking for. Pulling out the jerky, Yelena slowly showed the package to the wolf, whose eyes never once wavered from her every move. She’d never seen eyes that blue on any animal—the Red Room didn’t give her much occasion to spend time on social media websites, looking at adorable pictures of cats or other critters. Neither had she seen such intimidatingly long teeth before, not even on the huge brown bears that roamed the Russian wilderness.

    “See?” Yelena asked softly, ripping open the jerky and pulling out a long strip. “You don’t have to be scared.” She didn’t want to get too close, lest the animal decided on a surprise attack, but she slowly moved over on her knees so she could throw the jerky in front of the wolf without accidentally smacking it in the face. “There, we can share. I’m hungry, also. I can imagine how much pain you must be in. I’ve been shot before, too.” Slowly, Yelena sat down on her heels and pulled out a strip of beef for herself. Sticking it in her mouth, she watched as the wolf slowly lowered itself down, never taking its eyes off Yelena. Gradually, the growl tapered off in favor of a nose sniffing the jerky that had just been thrown to it in a gesture of goodwill. “It’s good. I promise.” Yelena took another bite and placed her free hand on her knee.

    Finally, after another minute of staring at each other, the wolf opened its mouth and snagged the meat, gobbling it up quickly and barely chewing it, by the looks of it. A whine escaped its massive maw, and Yelena couldn’t help but feel charmed as she threw another strip of meat, which was eaten a lot faster than the first. Soon enough, the bag was empty, and Yelena carefully set it aside. Now that there was no more food readily available, the wolf went back to staring at her.

    “I can’t believe you’re awake,” Yelena said, sliding her legs out from under her and moving backwards until she was sitting up against the stone wall of the cabin. There was enough distance between them that she was confident the wolf didn’t feel threatened, so when she stretched out her legs, the wolf didn’t flinch away or start growling again. Instead, it finally lay down on its belly, its broken leg sticking out awkwardly to the side. Its tail was perfectly still, but its ears twitched upwards in a show of vague interest rather than open hostility. “The last time I got shot in the shoulder, it took me a lot longer to get back into shape. And I had actual doctors to look after me. I don’t think I’m the best vet in the universe. Your wounds will probably scar.” She brought her fingers to her right shoulder and rubbed at it, feeling the phantom pain of the bullet ripping through flesh and sinew. “Mine doesn’t look too bad.”

    She was tired, and she was smelly, and she was hungry, but there was a wounded animal in the middle of the living room that didn’t look like it’d be all too happy with letting her pass to the bathroom or bedroom, let alone the hallway. Plus, there was cold air blowing into the main living area from the still open front door. This was not an ideal situation.

    “If I promise not to make any sudden moves to freak you out, can I get up and close this door?” Yelena gestured with her thumb, while the wolf continued to stare at her. “If you want to leave, I can let you out later, but I just don’t think you’re in any condition to walk anywhere right now.” When there wasn’t more growling or whining or any other sort of reaction, Yelena slowly slid her butt over the ground until she was next to the door. The wolf’s eyes followed her all the way, and it didn’t so much as blink until the door was shut. “Okay, I’m going to turn my back on you now to lock this. If you decide to do something mean while I’m not looking, I will be very, very upset.”

    Locking the door took all of five seconds, and by the time Yelena faced her furry companion once more, the wolf had closed its eyes. Taking a deep and quiet breath, Yelena moved back over to her place against the wall, dragging one of the grocery bags with her. At the sound of rustling plastic, the wolf opened one of its eyes, and Yelena shrugged.

    “You had most of that jerky. I’m still hungry,” she said unapologetically, pulling out a bag of dried banana slices and opening it up with little fanfare. “You have no idea how long I’ve been on my feet.” She stuffed three of the chips into her mouth and started to chew as she thought back to when the Red Room had first sent her on the mission she’d fled from. “It’s been at least forty-eight hours since I’ve had any sort of sleep. I think I deserve a treat.” When she ate another handful, the wolf lifted its head from its front paws and sniffed the air, clearly interested in this new snack, too. She was pretty sure it was fine to feed bananas to dogs, but she wasn’t sure if the same applied to their undomesticated ancestors. She didn’t want to fix the wolf’s shoulder and leg only to have it die of an intestinal blockage or because of food poisoning because it lacked a key enzyme in breaking down potassium or some stupid shit like that.

    “No,” Yelena replied after swallowing the dried fruits in her mouth. “You can’t have any.” She set aside the bag and pulled out a bottle of water instead, taking a sip. The wolf eyed that, too, and Yelena let out a sigh and set both the closed bottle and the bag of banana chips back into the plastic grocery bag. “Okay, if you promise to stay calm and exactly where you are, I will bring you a bowl of water. You hear me?” She wasn’t gone far enough to expect a response and she didn’t get one, not even when she pushed herself to her feet and slowly retreated towards the kitchen area at the other end of the room. She kept her face towards the wolf for as long as she could, using her hands to find her way around all the furniture she’d pushed aside to make room several hours ago, but when she finally reached the sink, she didn’t have a choice but to turn and reach up towards one of the cupboards where she’d gotten the other bowl right before doing surgery on the animal she was now talking to.

    Muttering something unkind about herself in Russian, Yelena filled the bowl, and took refuge in the knowledge that the wolf would not easily be able to jump her right now, even with her back turned. It, too, would have to navigate around the furniture, and that was a whole lot trickier with a broken leg. Even the wound on Yelena’s right thigh, which she hadn’t disinfected and cleaned in several hours, was making every movement uncomfortable for her. Temporarily distracted by her own aches and weariness, Yelena took longer than necessary to return, even after the bowl was as full as she thought was appropriate. She only turned when she heard rustling from behind her, and quickly realized that a wolf might not be a dog, but that a dog had come from a wolf, and that while Yelena had never owned a dog, she was pretty sure if left unattended, they could get some pretty stupid ideas. Like sticking their snoots into plastic bags and rooting around for the snack they’re not supposed to have.

    “Hey,” Yelena called, doing her best not to sound as exasperated as she felt. “Come on, I don’t want you to get sick.” Carrying the bowl in both hands, Yelena rounded the dinner table and couch and slowly approached her furry friend, which had dragged itself across the tarp and blanket, until it could stick its face into the bag. Now, it looked up at her, cheeks bulging with banana chips and looking weirdly guilty for a dangerous, wild predator. “That is so not helpful.” By the time she came to a stop, she realized she was closer to the wolf than she probably should have been, but when it tried to scrabble back while dragging its broken hind leg and whimpered in pain, Yelena felt all annoyance slide off her and be replaced with concern. “Alright, okay,” she said, setting down the bowl and slowly sliding it towards the wolf, who looked torn between whining and growling and made a weird twirping sound instead. “I’ve learned my lesson. I’ll look up all the junk food I brought.” She pulled her hand out of reach, while the wolf slowly lowered its head and sniffed at the water. “I keep telling you, though, you should not be moving around.” She grabbed the plastic grocery bag and returned to the kitchen area, where she removed the slobbery banana chips and set them on the counter to be inspected later.

    The water bottle was also slobbery but could obviously be salvaged by running a wet rag over it and setting it aside, and the rest of the food and tools inside were still sealed. Thankfully, that meant she didn’t have any qualms about patting them clean, before placing them in a cupboard that she hoped the wolf wouldn’t be able to get to.

    “The wolf,” she whispered to herself, turning back towards the area in front of the fireplace and listening to the avid slorp, slorp, slorp of a big tongue licking up water. “I think I need a better name for you.” Closing the cupboard, she retrieved another two plastic bags from beside the door. “What should I call you?” Tilting her head to the side, she contemplated her options. “We could go with something classic. How about Spot?” When the wolf didn’t even look up, Yelena hummed in thought and leaned against the wall next to the front door, holding the grocery bags in front of her legs. “Hm, yes, I agree. Too basic.” She walked back to the kitchen counter and set the bags down on top. “How do we feel about Fanny? I always wanted a dog called Fanny.” Again, there was nothing, and Yelena pulled out several cans of soup. “Okay, yes, I know. You are not a dog.”

    After opening the cupboard once more, she neatly stacked the cans on the top shelf.

    “I will keep thinking about it,” she promised to the wolf, quietly continuing to unpack her spoils.

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 11:29 A.M.

    Once Yelena was done with packing away all of the food and water and equipment she brought, she was desperate for that shower and for at least three to five hours of sleep. Her mental batteries were utterly depleted, and not even a giant predator was going to keep her from her current objective: Taking care of herself.

    The wolf had stopped slobbering several minutes ago, and a quiet once again descended upon the cabin, since Yelena hadn’t bothered to turn on the radio since walking inside. The background noise of her furry friend had been enough to keep her mind off of the relentless silence she wanted no part in.

    “I don’t know if you’re still awake,” Yelena said aloud as she started moving in the direction of the hallway. “But like I said—I have had a hell of a time for the last two days, so if you need something, now’s the time to say it.” Walking sideways, Yelena kept her hands visible at all times while the wolf looked up and watched her go. “Because otherwise, I am about to treat myself to a lukewarm shower and a one-way ticket to voluntary unconsciousness.”

    The wolf blinked but didn’t otherwise show any indication that it had any clue what she was talking about. Which, in fairness, Yelena shouldn’t have been surprised by. Right now, with her body running on fumes and her mind a million miles away, she wasn’t quite sure what she was even saying in the first place. Instead of telling her that it was fine and to go do what she needed to do, the wolf laid its head back on its front paws and closed its eyes. Its ears were still turned towards Yelena’s direction, but it seemed fine with the thought of Yelena leaving its immediate line of sight.

    “Okay,” Yelena confirmed, mostly to herself, before finally leaving the main living area and entering the bathroom, where she promptly turned on the shower without so much as having closed the door behind herself. She fixed that issue only after she had another look at the broad back of the wolf. As soon as she was alone with her thoughts, she took a slow breath, leaned her forehead against the wood of the door, and closed her eyes. She could have fallen asleep right there, but the call of the water was too strong to ignore.

    Oddly, while she was washing away the remnants of her past life – of the past two days – she found herself wishing she was back in the living room with the wolf. In the Red Room, they had been under constant supervision. Every interaction was recorded. Every word spoken aloud was analyzed for insurrection or for new ways of manipulating them into doing Dreykov’s bidding. The thoughts of doubt had always plagued her; the thoughts of doubt had plagued them all. What if the widow by her side wasn’t saying this because she wanted to? What if she was repeating a piece of script that Dreykov and his lackeys had written for her? And what of Yelena herself? Were these thoughts her own or were they his? Would she even know the difference? But now, here, in a cabin in the woods in the mountains of Maine, the words she spoke to the wolf were different. The animal had not been programmed. The animal would not deceive her. The animal would listen without understanding, without demanding answers from her, without judgement for what she had done. When she looked into its eyes, all she saw was an iron will to survive, to live, to be well enough to run and chase and be free. When she looked into its eyes, she saw—

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 08:01 P.M.

    Hours later, after cleaning first her tired body and then the angry stab wound on her thigh, Yelena woke to the sound of quiet scratching. It was dark and warm and comfortable inside the blanket cocoon she’d built for herself. Curled up into the fetal position with a knife held loosely in her right hand, Yelena forced herself into consciousness when the scratching didn’t stop. She was trained from the age of six to jumpstart her body and mind, no matter which phase of the sleep cycle she was woken up from. Gripping the knife tightly, Yelena body rolled off the bed – blanket in tow – and got into a fighting position, ready to defend herself against whatever enemy dared to enter her temporary home.

    It didn’t take her long to realize that the scratching was coming from the direction of the hallway, and that it sounded suspiciously like animal claws dragging across nice wooden floors. Almost immediately, Yelena relaxed against her will, although she didn’t relinquish the knife as she tip-toed her way towards the bedroom door. Quickly, she slipped on a pair of socks and her boots just in case she needed to run again. Night had fallen, telling Yelena that she had slept for longer than she had intended, which was as much of a shock to her as being woken up in the first place.

    A quick survey of her bedroom revealed no intruders, and neither did her sweep of the bathroom. The back door was still locked and did not look remotely broken into, so she slowly approached the dark living area. There was a shape in front of the door, and the scratching was coming from there, too. But without turning on the light, Yelena couldn’t be sure if it was the wolf or something merely pretending to be the wolf. Alas, she had no intention of startling a dangerous predator because she wanted to case the area. Besides, how likely was it that the Red Room had followed her all the way here?

    “Probably very,” Yelena whispered to herself, which immediately made the scratching at the front door stop. Instead, it was replaced by a low whine, which cut off only seconds later, like her furry companion did not want to admit that it needed assistance. Sighing inwardly, Yelena bit the bullet and hit the light switch to her left, which bathed the whole room and the hallway behind her in the warm, yellow light of an incandescent lightbulb mounted to the ceiling. Squinting into the sudden brightness, Yelena could finally see the whole room, and that the wolf was half-sitting, half-standing on three shaky legs. “You want to go outside. I see that. I respect that. You probably have to pee – I can relate. But I just don’t know how you expect to get very far with your leg like that. There are porch steps, you know.”

    Flattening its ears, the wolf whined again, and this time it didn’t cut itself off. Briefly, Yelena considered whether she should question her sanity for thinking that this animal was showing more signs of comprehension than some humans she’d interacted with, but then she pushed the thought aside. What did she know about dogs or wolves or any animals, really? Maybe experiences like this were normal.

    “Okay,” Yelena acquiesced, lifting the knife into view, and deliberately showing herself setting it on top of the cupboard to her right. “I do hope you like peeing in the snow.” Slowly, carefully, she approached the wolf, taking care not to make any sudden movements as she walked. “If you bite me when I go to open the door, I won’t give you any more water or snacks. And all the rabbits and birds I catch with my snares, I will keep to myself.” Yelena stopped about three feet away from the wolf, but it didn’t show any signs of aggression. Not like before, when it had been scared of her after first waking up. She would probably have to switch out its bandages soon and check the splint on its broken leg. With all the wriggling and moving about, she was concerned the splint had slipped or that the bones had shifted. She wanted to keep the leg set right, but there was also the question as to whether the wolf would even let her get that close for long enough to check, especially when it was in pain.

    Slowly, she reached towards the deadbolt and undid it, before pulling the key from her back pocket and showing it to the wolf. Lifting its maw, it sniffed the key and her hand, and Yelena felt distinctly charmed at the warm chuffs of hot wolf breath on her palm. In spite of the giant teeth she’d seen and the clear uncertainty that remained in the animal’s body after having been shot and otherwise hurt by humans, she wanted nothing more than to dig her fingers into the remaining fur and scritch. She wanted the wolf to lay on its back and let her pet its belly. She wanted to grab its big, chunky face and talk to it like it was a puppy.

    “You are very cute,” Yelena said instead, keeping her voice carefully neutral as she finally unlocked the front door. “You know, I thought some more about what kind of names we could give you.” She hit the switch by the front door that turned the porch light on before half-opening the door and waiting for the wolf to scrabble back so she wouldn’t hit it in the face. “I see your rejection of Spot and Fanny. Which is fair. But I was thinking—” She watched as the wolf hobbled forward and towards the stairs, where it slowed down and clearly gaged the best way to go about getting down. “We could go for more lupine names, maybe? Which—tell me.” She followed slowly behind, leaning against the wooden post that connected the porch to the overhanging roof section. “We could go very traditional, very classic, and call you Loup. Or maybe Romulus.” The wolf, again, did not acknowledge either suggestion, and Yelena sighed warmly as it set a slow, cautious paw onto the uppermost step. Its legs wobbled horribly. “I mean, I did see during surgery that you are female, but I think it could work. I saw a video online once during a reconnaissance mission where someone called their female snake Frank and it really worked for them.”

    When the wolf slipped forward a little and wobbled even more, Yelena descended the stairs. She couldn’t keep watching.

    “Okay, so not Romulus,” she said, presenting her hands. “Do you want me to help you down?” Her furry friend growled, before trying to move forward and nearly losing its balance entirely. With its ears flattening, its growl turned into that strange twirping sound again as it evidently warred with itself. “You know,” Yelena started, kneeling down in front of the wolf and establishing eye contact between them. “There’s nothing wrong with needing help.” She offered her hands for more sniffing, and the wolf obliged. Maybe the Red Room had damaged her brain beyond repair, but she couldn’t imagine a predator so much like her to bite without cause. Not after it had eaten her food, drunk her water, and allowed her close enough to do damage. “I could not have escaped from the Red Room without my sister. I would not have found this shelter without my sister. I would not be alive today… if it were not for her.” The wolf wobbled on its own for another thirty seconds. Then, finally, it moved back onto the porch and looked at her with its big, blue eyes that glinted even in the light of the waning moon and the porch lamp behind them.

    “Alright,” Yelena confirmed, getting back to her feet. “I still have no idea how you are even upright. But you have made the right decision, my friend.” Scraping together all of her energy, Yelena rounded the wolf. “Look, I know you don’t know what I’m saying, but I will say it anyway, yes? I am going to pick you up under your belly and carry you down the steps so you can go about your business. I’ll shovel it further away in the morning.”

    She didn’t grab the wolf right away; she wasn’t that far gone. No, she placed her fingers on its back first, to get it used to her touch. For a wolf, its fur was surprisingly soft, which Yelena hadn’t expected. Now that she took the time to appreciate it against her palms, she realized she had assumed it would be coarse and rough from having to withstand the tough climates and rough surroundings of the mountain. Last night, when she was sorting through clumps of blood and cutting off matted fur with her knife, it felt very different to her. This was much nicer.

    “Please don’t freak out,” Yelena said as she curled her arms and hands around the belly of the beast. “Let’s try to stay calm and not bite the nice black widow who is trying to help.” Her face smushed against the fur, Yelena started lifting. “Сука. You are heavy.” Now she was the one wobbling, as she tried to lift a wolf that seemed several times her size and only appeared to be growing inside her grip. “Okay, as soon as you are healed, we are putting you on a diet. No more jerky.”

    As she slowly descended the stairs, she was surprised at how incredibly still the wolf managed to stay in her arms. It didn’t squirm; it didn’t try to get away; it didn’t bite or snap or growl.

    “Good girl,” Yelena grunted as she finally reached the snowy ground in front of the porch and lowered the wolf back onto its one semi-good hindleg. “Good girl, Mother Russia.” A beat passed between them as the wolf found new purchase on its front paws. “No, okay. No. I didn’t like it as soon as I said it. Besides, Russia never did anything for me.” Breathing heavily, Yelena fell backwards into the snow and stared up at the clear night sky while the wolf slowly slunk off to do its business. While the coldness of the ground and the snow soaked into her clothes, Yelena allowed herself a moment of pure relaxation. There was nothing in these woods that wanted to harm her for malicious reasons. Nothing that wanted to manipulate her. Nothing that would jump out and attack because she’d said something that a man didn’t like. “It is very nice out here, don’t you think?”

    Exhaling deeply, Yelena expelled the last of her thoughts through her mouth, watching the water particles escape into the air. For the first time in her life, she felt like a dragon, emitting sulphureous vapors in preparation for spewing fire. The thought made her smile.

    “I should probably check the snares,” she said to no one in particular, not expecting any kind of answer. Still, when she didn’t get one, she sat up and looked out into the darkness, seeing nothing but the tree line and the truck. The wolf was nowhere to be found, and a ball of worry settled in the pit of her stomach. “If you did not want to stay, you could have at least said goodbye…” Getting to her feet, she turned her head in all directions, but still couldn’t make out any movement among the gently swaying leaves. “Hm,” she grunted, kicking the tip of her toe against a particularly hard patch of ice. “Very sad.”

    With one more sigh, she returned to the cabin and grabbed her flashlight.

    Those snares wouldn’t check themselves.

    ##

    November 20th, 2016 | 08:54 P.M.

    During her morning activities, she had been very enthusiastic and had put a lot of effort into setting up snares in ten different locations around her cabin. Now, with night befalling the mountain and the wolf scampering at the first chance it got, leaving her alone once more, she felt less impressed at having to trudge from place to place. The only silver lining was that her traps had actually born fruit, so to speak.

    “Thank you,” she said as she knelt next to the third rabbit she caught. She gently freed it from the trap and stroked its little head with the tips of her fingers. “Thank you for lending me your strength.” Quietly, she spoke a prayer that Melina had taught her when she was a little girl. Sometimes, she’d recited it to herself in her head during reconditioning or while recovering from a particularly grievous wound. These animals deserved her respect. They would nourish her and give her strength. They were nothing like the deaths the Red Room wrought upon the world. They were not senseless, nor were they cruel. “Thank you.”

    Getting back to her feet, she continued onwards to check the final two traps, but found them empty and undisturbed. Turning back in the direction of the cabin, she slowly started making her way through the snow with the rabbits carefully tucked into her arms. Now that the wolf was gone, she could probably take down the snares, but the snow had yet to melt away, so she decided to leave them up for now. If she caught anything else tomorrow, she would reduce the amount by half, because she didn’t want any of the meat to go to waste. Who knew how long she would even keep staying here. If she had to leave in a hurry, she didn’t want to do so with a fully stocked fridge of animals who had given their lives for her survival. She would not waste them.

    Keeping her eyes on cone of light at her feet, she didn’t make herself hurry as she plodded along. Once inside, she would get the fire going again, she would put on one of the big sweaters she found in a bottom drawer in the bedroom, and then she would skin and cut the rabbits and prepare them for storage in the small freezer next to the sink. She might even make some of her own jerky. There was plenty of salt in the cabin, and Yelena had bought even more on her trip here. She was so thoroughly caught up in her plans for the small animals in her arms, she didn’t even notice the hulking figure sitting in front of the porch, not until she was close enough to shine her flashlight on it.

    “Oh,” Yelena said, utterly surprised at the sight of the wolf waiting for her. Its big wolf butt sat in the snow with the broken leg once again sticking awkwardly out to the side in its splint. “I thought you’d gone.” Shining her flashlight across the body of the wolf, she didn’t notice any new blemishes or any fresh dirt on its fur. Whatever it had been up to aside from relieving itself in private, Yelena would probably never know. She didn’t particularly care if she was honest with herself. All she really cared about was that she wasn’t alone, after all. “Let me get these inside and then I will help you up the stairs.” Turning off the flashlight, Yelena tucked it into the right front pocket of her jeans, before slowly approaching the wolf and passing it very deliberately. She was almost past it when she felt something tug at the sleeve of her jacket. “Hm?”

    When she turned her head, she saw the big blue eyes of her furry friend looking up at her. Huge and round and full of innocent hopefulness. When she didn’t move, the wolf tugged a little bit more insistently, and Yelena looked down at the rabbits in her arms.

    “Are you hungry?” she asked as she moved her arm far enough away that the wolf had to let her go. “A whole bag of jerky and banana chips not enough to keep you fed?” She was so relieved it had come back, she felt foolish enough to stick her hand back out and settle it on top of the wolf’s head, stroking it between its eyes and along the back of its nose. To her utter delight, it neither snapped nor growled at her. Instead, it closed its eyes and allowed her to keep going. She kept her pets slow and deliberate, and when she removed her fingers to hand over one of the rabbits, she only did so after rubbing and scritching one of the big black ears. As she left the wolf outside for a moment while it devoured the rabbit, Yelena shook her head to herself. There was no way that this wolf hadn’t grown up amongst humans. It was too calm —too gentle—too accustomed to human touch. Which, in truth, only made Yelena feel more awful about the wounds the animal had been made to endure. She wondered angrily whether perhaps it had lived somewhere with a person that loved it, before escaping or running off on a chase, only to find itself in a situation with people who did not understand it. Or who were afraid of it.

    Her fingers shook with uncontrollable rage on behalf of her new friend outside as she lowered the two remaining rabbits into the sink in the kitchen. She knew it wasn’t possible, but she longed to put her hands on the people who harmed the wolf. She wanted to rip them apart with her bare hands—make them feel an ounce of the pain she’d been made to—she opened her eyes, fingers shaking even worse as she turned on the radio to drown out the sound of the silence in the cabin.

    ##

    November 24th, 2016 | 05:25 P.M.

    Over the four days that followed, Yelena found herself growing more and more comfortable with the prolonged presence of another predator. In the Red Room, no widow was ever left unattended or on her own for very long while stationed at home base. At all times, she had shared a room with another widow, but never for more than a day or two. It did little to stop Yelena from caring about the others, but she always recognized the attempt at separation for what it was.

    Now, with this new company, she found herself settling into the rhythms of her new life with terrifying ease. While the wolf continued to sleep in front of the fireplace on the reinstated rug, Yelena rolled herself into her comfortable blanket cocoon every night, feeling safe in the knowledge that the wolf would hear anyone that might try to sneak inside from the front, while she would hear anyone who might try to sneak in from the back. The tarp from the truck had also been returned outside, and Yelena spent the afternoon of the third day in the cabin kneeling in the snow and scrubbing wolf blood from the material with lukewarm water from the house while the wolf watched her silently from the porch. Every morning, Yelena woke to the sound of gentle scratching of claws on wood, and every morning, she helped carry the wolf down the porch steps so it could go and do its business in the forest while Yelena took the opportunity to watch the morning sunrise while she gathered whatever had been caught in the remaining snares she left outside. Once she returned, the wolf would be waiting for her dutifully, back ramrod straight and ears perking higher and higher up the closer she got. It didn’t take her long to decipher the emotions hidden behind the many gestures of the wolf’s ears.

    After feeding the wolf and refilling its water bowl, Yelena then went about the business of redressing and cleaning her own and her companion’s wounds. The wolf usually took a lot longer to clean and dress, because it liked to squirm around whenever she took out the materials needed to disinfect the wounded areas, but Yelena was nothing if not efficient in her care. And besides, they had already come this far. She wasn’t about to let an infection catch hold of her friend, not now that she’d spent so much time nursing it back to health.

    Later in the day, while the wolf rested, Yelena usually went out on walks around the mountain. Partly because she still couldn’t quite believe that the Red Room hadn’t found her and she wanted to ensure the area was still secure, and partly because the thought of getting to walk somewhere under her own power filled her with so much joy and purpose that she couldn’t bear to sit still and do nothing inside the cabin for hours on end. In spite of the stab wound on her thigh, she had more excess energy and excitement than she knew what to do with. Usually, she only returned to the cabin when nighttime rolled around because she knew it was time to help the wolf down the porch steps again, before feeding it for dinner.

    In some ways, this newfound freedom was settling into just as many rhythms as her life in the Red Room. But one thing that struck her every time she left the cabin or petted the wolf or cooked a selection of dried foods and fresh rabbit for breakfast, lunch, or dinner, was how different it felt. How different it felt to make decisions. No matter what happened, she couldn’t imagine going back to anything else ever again.

    ##

    November 25th, 2016 | 09:11 P.M.

    On the fifth evening of her stay at the cabin, Yelena took down a book from one of the shelves in the main living area and cracked the spine. At the sound, the wolf looked up from its paws and stared at her, ears turned in her direction. It hadn’t looked scared or dangerous at all since that first day, and Yelena decided that maybe she didn’t need to slink off to her bedroom for the next few hours until she fell asleep while listening to terrible music from the radio.

    “Have you read this one?” Yelena asked, walking over to the couch, which she had yet to push back into its original position, and slumping down on top of it. The wolf lay on its side with its back to the fire, blinking up at her sleepily. In fact, it had been sleeping a lot during the day, which definitely gave her pause and made her worry. There was the persistent issue of those two slowly closing gunshot wounds and the broken leg, of course, but Yelena was still concerned. She had never helped a wounded animal before, and she didn’t have access to any resources other than what she knew of first aid applied to humans. “I have not.” She turned over the book and showed the cover to the wolf. “It’s called Safe Harbor.”

    The wolf dropped its head back to the rug and closed its eyes, clearly uninterested.

    “Reese Conlon, Lt.Col USMCR, is the new sheriff who has heads turning amidst speculation as to who will be the first woman to capture her attentions. Doctor Victoria King has been betrayed by love once and refuses to risk heartbreak again,” Yelena read from the back cover, raising both eyebrows and clicking once with her tongue. “Well, well, well. This sounds appropriately like it might be a good time, don’t you think?” She turned the book back over and flipped it open, skipping the acknowledgements and going straight to the first chapter. “How do you feel about Reese as a name? You could be the new sheriff in town.”

    The wolf let out a huffy breath and Yelena chuckled.

    “Not a fan of the police?” she asked, quickly skimming the first paragraph. “I understand.” Sliding further down, she put her head on the armrest of the couch and lifted the book up above her head. “How much do you want to bet that Victoria King finds love by the end of the book?” She looked at the wolf, who opened one eye to peer up at her. “What, you don’t think so? Okay, I will bet you…” She stretched out her legs and set the book upon her chest. “I will bet you two rabbits! No, three!” From the back, the radio started playing a new song. When the wolf huffed again from deep within its chest, Yelena picked the book back up and started reading aloud. “Chapter One! Provincetown’s newest deputy sheriff pulled her cruiser to a stop in the parking lot overlooking Herring Cove.” The wolf whined, then slowly and deliberately rolled around until its back was turned to Yelena. “You are so rude. You’re lucky I think you are very, very adorable…”

    Several seconds passed in which Yelena stared at the ceiling and took one relaxed breath after another, basking in the sensation of her chest moving up and down and up and down. The activity was as simple as it was pleasurable, and when she closed her eyes, she melted even further into the couch, consciously loosening every muscle from the top of her head to the bottom of her feet.

    “We should go visit a harbor,” she whispered, stretching her toes, and wriggling them in time with the bad music. “We could walk along the shore; you could try to catch seagulls. Oh! We could play fetch with a frisbee!” She couldn’t stop the smile that spread over her lips at the mental images her brain was cooking up. “And then we could get corndogs from one of the vendors. Or cotton candy. Or just a regular hot dog. I will not be super picky. Or we could get all of it. It wouldn’t just have to be one day. We could stay there for a week, or for a month. No one can tell us when to leave. I think that’d be nice. Don’t you?”

    Gentle doggy snores filled the room, pushing Yelena further into that boneless sense of utter relaxation.

    ##

    November 26th, 2016 | 06:02 A.M.

    When she woke up on the sixth morning, the book was no longer on top of her chest. Yelena never tended to move much while she was asleep, so this struck her as odd. Opening her eyes into the early morning sunshine, she realized belatedly that there was something heavy on her stomach, and a closer look revealed that it was the wolf’s big head.

    “Good morning,” Yelena whispered, looking into her friend’s crystal blue eyes. Behind it sat the closed book, its spine turned towards her on the coffee table she’d pushed up against the back wall of the cabin on her first night. Neatly placed on the wood, like Yelena put it there herself before falling asleep. Only she knew, without the shadow of a doubt, that she didn’t. For a moment, the idea of another human being inside the cabin filled her chest with adrenaline, but the weight of the wolf’s head on her stomach gave her pause. “Did you pick up the book?” Her voice was thick with sleep and gentle disorientation, but the wolf only looked at her. Then, when Yelena didn’t make a move to get up, it lifted its head and limped forward until its face was nearly level with Yelena’s. “Your breath smells very unpleasant.” The wolf licked at her cheek.

    “Okay,” Yelena said, blinking slowly. “You have convinced me.” Bringing both of her hands into view so she wouldn’t startle the animal, Yelena slowly wrapped her fingers and arms around the wolf’s neck and tugged it down, hugging the enormous head to her chest.

    “Cuddles first, then I will let you out to do your business.” Closing her eyes, Yelena laid her cheek against the wolf as best she could from her position, while the wolf’s tail slapped against the wooden floor. Once, twice, three times. “So lucky.” It slapped a few more times. “Maybe that’s what I should call you.” The wolf whined and sniffed at her, making her laugh. “Okay, okay! I get it! Less canoodling, more moving.”

    As soon as she let go, the wolf limped towards the front door, although it was already moving a lot faster than it had the day before. This, like so many other things she’d noticed while interacting with the wol—with Lucky—was something that continued to give her pause. As a black widow, Yelena healed much faster than any regular human. The knock-off super soldier serum they’d given to her and the other widows knit nearly any hurt tissue back together, but this was a wolf. Sure, it was a big wolf, but who would benefit from giving any kind of serum to an animal to make it heal faster? And why would it be out in the woods, having been shot to hell? Unless… Unless Yelena wasn’t the only one who was on the run six days ago.

    Slowly getting to her feet, she first stuck her feet into the shoes she kicked off the night before, then she followed the wolf to the door and started stroking its head as she unlocked the door with the key she always kept on her. By the time she opened it completely, the wolf had already squeezed past her legs and out into the open, leaving Yelena on her own to shake her head at its excited antics. “So much energy, Lucky.” Closing the door behind her, she readied herself mentally to attempt her morning ritual of gracefully hefting a solid hunk of wriggling meat down several icy steps without falling, but before she could so much as try to stoop down to assist, Lucky had already descended the stairs by herself, tail wagging in time with her big wolf-booty. The execution was a bit clumsy, but it got the job done, leaving Yelena feeling utterly impressed. “Look at you go! Good girl!”

    A wolfish bark shot from Lucky, before she started ambling off in the direction of her favorite outdoor toilet, leaving Yelena feeling half-relieved that her shoulders and back and arms got a bit of a rest, and half-deflated that Lucky didn’t need her help anymore.

    “You are losing your mind,” she whispered to herself as she walked in the direction of her first snare, shaking her head at herself this time. “Full of contradictions.” Some of the snow had finally melted away, which meant that Yelena could drive into the small village that wasn’t too far away from here to pick up new supplies. She could even mail that letter she prepared three days ago. And once that was done, all that would be left would be to wait for it to arrive. And then… what would she do with a big wolf who followed her around? Maybe it was for the best that Lucky was healing up and growing more and more independent.

    While the Red Room had made Yelena long for something other than silence, it had also made her want to be alone and away from other people. Away from prying, judging eyes, making this new arrangement between her and her new friend all the more complicated for her. Because no, obviously Lucky was not a human. She could not speak to Yelena; she could not reply whenever she was asked a question; she couldn’t even really understand what Yelena was trying to say. But to Yelena, who had not known the loving touch of another person since she was six years old, even this connection felt like a terrifying thing. A fragile offering of the universe. Lucky, who seemed just as much of an aberration to the natural order of things as Yelena herself, felt as real and tangible to her as few other things ever had. Coming to a halt in the middle of the forest, she leaned her back against the tree to her left and stared out at the white landscape all around her. Bits of life could be found all over the place. Small patches of hardy moss grew on the side of trees or on especially big root structures. Birds flew about the thin canopy, and other critters suited to the surroundings could be heard and perceived in other ways at nearly every time of day. If she listened hard enough, she could even make out the soft splashing of a nearby creek which – despite the cold – had not completely frozen over.

    “I am you. As much as you are me,” Yelena whispered to herself, looking up at the sky.

    “Look, Natasha. Look.” She closed her eyes. “I am upside down again.” Expelling her breath in a slow and steady exhale, Yelena opened her senses to the woods. “How about you?”

    ##

    November 26th, 2016 | 09:30 A.M.

    It took Yelena a lot longer to return to the cabin after checking the snares. Partly because she felt sluggish from her many desperate musings, and partly because she was afraid that Lucky would not be there by the time she got back. But she was, sitting dutifully on the porch and waiting to be let back inside. As soon as Yelena came into view, Lucky howled at her in greeting and started ambling over to her, tail wagging back and forth, nearly slipping on the wet stairs, because she couldn’t put down her broken leg properly.

    “There you are,” Yelena said with a smile, immediately feeding the big black wolf one of the small prey animals in her arms. “Good girl, Lucky.” She couldn’t help herself from cooing as the wolf started cracking bones and flesh and skin with its giant teeth. “Eat up. I’m driving into town after cleaning these, so you won’t get to go outside for a few hours.” She patted the wolf’s broad flank, then passed her by and entered the cabin, going about her daily business of preparing the animals she caught for storage.

    It was routine at this point, to turn on the radio and get to skinning. So routine, it took her several seconds to notice Lucky coming back inside and pulling the doorknob closed with her scary teeth. By the time the latch clicked with a slow and deliberate snick, Yelena stared at the wolf with what she was sure must have been an expression of utter bafflement. It almost seemed like Lucky was moving slowly so as not to startle her out of her concentration, but that thought was too ridiculous to entertain. Setting aside her knife, Yelena watched Lucky hobble towards the fireplace, before plonking herself down in front of it and letting out a heavy breath of contentment.

    “Maybe people were onto something when they invented the word cabin fever,” Yelena concluded, going back to her task. “Or maybe all of this is just a very vivid hallucination…” Raising her voice, she gripped her knife a little bit tighter and with renewed purpose. “What do you think, Lucky? Do you think I’m going mad? Should we be worried?”

    Lucky’s tail slapped heavily against the floor, and when she yawned and showed her one million razor sharp teeth, Yelena nodded.

    “Okay. Thank you for your input. I will put your recommendations under advisement.” She made another well-placed cut along the back of the rabbit and hummed along to the music from the radio. Slowly but surely, she grew accustomed to all the weird and off-putting tracks it liked to play day in and day out. So much so, she thought she might start to miss it once she moved away from this cabin. “But I will have you know; I am very skeptical as to whether your plan of yawning until my mental health improves will actually work.” Lucky whined at her. “Oh, now you want to talk?” Another whine. “Yes, you are very funny.”

    With a smile, Yelena accepted more tail slapping on the carpet as a victory.

    ##

    November 26th, 2016 | 04:45 P.M.

    The close-by village didn’t have much in terms of swanky boutiques or expensive jewelry shops, so Yelena made do without them. In spite of its humble size, the local clothing store offered her a wider selection than she had ever seen in her entire life. Having mostly been forced to wear combat or tactical gear, the thought of purchasing a simple pair of jeans for no one but herself was about as intoxicating as a shot of vodka straight into her nervous system. Most of the time she was away from the cabin, she spent shopping at the grocery store, but it wasn’t her very first stop. No, the first thing she did was to push her letter into a mailbox in town. Fingers sweaty and shaking, she stood in front of the box for an inappropriate length of time, willing it to take flight and reach its destination within the very same moment.

    Hours later, when she started driving back up the mountain, she did so with a sense of accomplishment. She’d written that letter. She’d sent that letter. She’d made the decision to put her feelings into words and to set the rest of her life in motion. She’d done that. All by herself. The feelings stayed with her the entire drive back. Even when the snow started falling again and obscured her vision, reminding her of her escape, she didn’t allow the memories to take over. She was free now. Free to make her own decisions.

    Chest burning with desperate happiness, Yelena parked her truck outside the cabin and jumped from the car, twirling in the snow, breathing in the late afternoon cold. Her fingers, newly adorned with glittering rings, grabbed hold of the many shopping bags she stashed in front of the passenger seat, and once the space was empty, Yelena hip-checked the door closed with a theatrical flourish.

    Humming softly to herself, she set down the bags on the porch and swiftly unlocked the front door to the cabin, shouldering her way inside after recapturing the spoils of her much-needed shopping spree. She was in such a good mood, she didn’t even panic too much when she realized that the spot in front of the fireplace was empty and that there was no Lucky immediately in sight. Still, she quickly unloaded the bags onto the floor, closed the door behind her, and then ran through the main living space.

    “Lucky?” she asked, completely forgetting to be cautious as she checked the bathroom (which was empty), then grabbed the handle of the half-ajar bedroom door. Relief crashed upon her like a torrent when she burst inside. “You—” She stopped, mouth opening and heart thumping wildly. “I was worried.” Her words were utterly unnecessary because Lucky was not hurt or bleeding or in danger. She was sleeping—on Yelena’s pillow. “Foolish wolf.”

    Exhaling shakily, Yelena leaned her back against the doorjamb, before slowly sliding down as the adrenaline left her body. “Foolish—”

    It took less than ten seconds for Lucky’s head to pop up from the bed, and when she saw Yelena sitting on the floor and fighting back tears from the sudden loss of stress, her claws scrabbled uselessly on the duvet for a moment before finding purchase. Then, before Yelena could so much as process the movement, Lucky was off the bed and right in front of her, licking and whining at her face.

    “I’m okay,” Yelena tried to reassure the wolf, lifting her hands and allowing Lucky to sniff them. “I’m okay.” She ran her hands through the fur on Lucky’s chest, before leaning her face forward and resting it there. More whining greeted her, until finally Yelena wrapped her arms around the big, bulky body and squeezed the wolf for all it was worth. “I’m okay. You’re okay. We’re both okay.”

    Lucky yowled in agreement.

    ##

    November 27th, 2016 | 01:15 A.M.

    What happened next should have been utterly predictable to Yelena, who prized herself on being highly intelligent and extremely talented. And yet, when Yelena woke up that night to the feeling of something big and heavy depressing her mattress, her first instinct was not to suspect that it was the wolf she’d made friends with, but that it was a dangerous intruder who wanted to hurt her. Which was how she ended up on all fours on the ground, knife in hand and ready to take a life to protect her own. Somehow, despite the fact that wolves could not make facial expressions in the same way as humans could, Yelena thought Lucky looked about ready to burn her a mix CD in apology for scaring her out of bed.

    From that point on, sleeping curled up to a big predator was neatly folded into Yelena’s everyday routine. Which had the added side effect of not being woken up by claws on wood, but by a big wolf nose poking at her face until she decided to unfurl herself from her blanket nest. It was good. Above all else, it was warm. Yelena quickly figured out that Lucky did not develop a fever from her injuries on that first night, but that she naturally ran inexplicably hot. Another benefit to falling asleep next to Lucky was that they ended up hanging out a lot more in her bed, which aided in Yelena’s quest to finish reading Safe Harbor. Out loud. No matter how much Lucky whined and nipped at her fingers or the edges of the book.

    ##

    December 2nd, 2016 | 07:22 A.M.

    On the thirteenth day, Yelena woke up and found Lucky still curled up in bed, which was unusual for her. Instead of licking at her face, Lucky merely stretched out her back and continued to snooze while Yelena got out of bed.

    “No morning business today?” Yelena asked, rounding the bed, and reaching out to scratch Lucky’s big, fluffy belly. A low whine was her only response, and Yelena decided to let her be for the moment to use the opportunity to take an early shower. “Okay. But if you need to go while I’m busy, that is on you.” Lucky barely twitched her tail, and Yelena didn’t bother trying to get her to engage any further than that.

    She spent all of a minute rummaging around in the bottom drawer of the closet to fish out a fresh pair of underwear, before picking up the sweater and jeans combo she’d worn the day before. It was still good to go for one or two more days before she needed to add it to the wash pile. During her second trip into town, she’d done some laundry at the one and only laundromat, so she was doing pretty well on that front.

    After one more smile at the sleepy wolf, Yelena crossed the hallway into the bathroom and locked the door behind herself. It wasn’t really necessary, considering that she was the only person in the cabin, but it didn’t feel right to leave herself open and vulnerable to attack while she was naked. Taking out an insurgent while dripping wet from head to toe wasn’t fun—she had plenty of first-hand experience.

    The water still didn’t heat up past lukewarm, but Yelena was used to it and didn’t mind the brief flash of cold before her body got used to the change in temperature.

    Once she was done, she dressed herself quickly and efficiently, and was still drying her hair with the towel in her hands, when she heard the distinct sound of a loud and angry growl. The volume was startling. Yelena had never heard her wolf produce anything as threatening or as deadly, not even thirteen days ago when she first got to this place.

    Adrenaline shooting through her veins, she grabbed the gun she’d stashed in one of the cupboards, and quickly and quietly unlocked the bathroom door. The growling and barking grew louder and more aggressive, and Yelena didn’t waste any more time. Opening the bathroom door, she looked around the corner to gage the number of intruders, before nearly dropping the gun at the view she caught from inside the main living area.

    Lucky covered the entrance to the hallway, her fur standing up in all directions, making her look even bigger and scarier than she already did when she was calm. Her whole body trembled with the growl, but she didn’t look scared like she had when faced with Yelena for the first time. Instead, she looked seconds away from an outright attack. Opposite her, in the open doorway to the cabin—

    “Natasha,” Yelena gasped, keeping her gun held loosely at her side as she righted herself. Her sister caught the movement right away, but she didn’t take her eyes from the raging wolf in between them. She had a gun in her hands too, but it wasn’t trained at Lucky – not really. It was aimed in her general direction, but Yelena could tell that she was just as reluctant to needlessly kill an animal as Yelena was. Still, Yelena kept to the doorway of the bathroom. With nano machines capable of creating the perfect likeness of another person, she could not be entirely sure that it really was Natasha, not until—

    With a twist of her mouth, Natasha whistled. Quietly at first, then more loudly, and Yelena’s shoulders unclenched entirely.

    “Okay,” Yelena said loudly, doing her best not to startle Lucky, who still looked ready to snap and jump at Natasha at any moment. “Let’s all just stay calm.” She approached with steady footsteps, moving herself into Lucky’s line of sight. But before she could get any closer to Natasha, the wolf moved in front of Yelena again, shielding her with its massive body. “Oh— oh no. No. Shh, it’s okay, Lucky.” Hands shaking, she lowered them until only the very tip of the fur brushed against her. “It’s just my sister. It’s just my sister. Shh. Just Natasha.” Kneeling down, she wrapped her arms around Lucky’s trembling body and hugged her, holding her back. “Just Natasha. Natasha. My sister Natasha. Shh. She won’t hurt us. She won’t hurt us. You’re okay. We’re okay.”

    Gradually, the wolf’s unbroken hindleg started bending, and the deep and dangerous growl in its chest started to fade and taper off. With every calming word and squeeze from Yelena, more and more of the tension melted away, until Lucky’s fur was back to normal, and she turned her head to bump it against Yelena’s shoulder.

    “Good girl, Lucky,” Yelena whispered, closing her eyes, and hugging the wolf a little harder. “Good girl.”

    With a whine, Lucky nipped at her sweater until Yelena let her go.

    “When did you have time to get a dog?” Natasha asked, closing the front door behind herself without making any sudden moves, before returning her gun to the holster secured inside her jeans. Her hair was cut to shoulder-length and just as vibrant as Yelena remembered.

    “She’s a wolf,” Yelena countered, seconds before she and Natasha met each other halfway across the living area and pulled each other into a desperate, crushing hug. “She’s clearly a wolf, you stupid poser.”

    “Shut up, you brat. If it growls like a dog and protects you like a dog, it’s a fucking dog,” Natasha shot back, holding Yelena even more fiercely to her, like she was afraid that if she let go for even a second, Yelena would turn into dust and float away. Her hand reached up to the back of Yelena’s neck, cupping it securely. “I can’t believe you got out. And that you even found this place.”

    “You gave me the coordinates,” Yelena replied, breathing in the familiar scent of her sister. They hadn’t seen each other in two years. Not since Yelena had been recaptured by the Red Room. “I repeated them to myself every day I was there.”

    “Little rebel,” Natasha said, pulling away and cupping Yelena’s cheeks. “You look exactly the same.”

    “And you look worse,” Yelena said, pulling at Natasha’s hair. “Why did you cut it off?”
