    “It was getting too long. I’ve been thinking about going even shorter with it. How do you feel about a bob cut?” She turned her head and twirled her hair back and forth, reaching up and covering the bottom half to simulate the possibly new look. “Also: Bangs. Yes or no?”

    “Absolutely not,” Yelena shot her down, barely holding back her disgust. “No.”

    “Fine,” Natasha said with a smirk. “Buzzkill.” She let go of Yelena and looked around the cabin. “How long have you been hanging out in here by yourself?” Her eyes lingered on the haphazard furniture arrangement and the many bags on the kitchen counter. At her words, Lucky barked, as if to say: I was here the whole time, too. With two raised eyebrows, Natasha looked at the wolf. “Are you planning on keeping that thing?”

    “Don’t call her that,” Yelena said, walking over to Lucky and scritching her right ear. “She’s been keeping me company.” Kneeling down, Yelena pressed her cheek to the wolf’s head.

    “She’s very sweet, once you get to know her. I found her half shot to death in the middle of the road. After I ran her over with the truck.”

    With a grunt of acknowledgement, Natasha opened the refrigerator and peered inside.

    ##

    December 2nd, 2016 | 09:57 P.M.

    As it turned out, Natasha was not here in a big Avengers jet like Yelena expected (and secretly hoped for). Instead, she was on the run, too. Only, she wasn’t on the run from the Red Room, but from the US American government, which didn’t really help Yelena’s nerves very much. In the back of her mind, she’d assumed meeting up with Natasha would mean support from the other, more powerful Avengers. Or at the very least from the whole S.H.I.E.L.D. operation that supported them.

    Alas, Yelena was used to rolling with the punches, so she accepted the fact that Natasha rolled up to the cabin in a truck of her own, and that she didn’t have any grandiose plans about their lives together. There would be no peaceful walks along the shore with the current political climate and the Sokovia Accords haunting Natasha’s every step. In a way, Yelena supposed it made life easier and more difficult at the same time. On the one hand, she didn’t have to make friends with a bunch of men in tights, but on the other hand, her life with Natasha in New York City would also remain a fantasy – for the moment.

    Of course, Natasha had a couple of other safe houses that she planned to move them to, but for the moment, she didn’t seem to be in any sort of hurry. Her truck was just as beaten up as Yelena’s and with the snow continuing to fall in thick, heavy blankets, it made sense for them to stay inside the cabin for at least a few more days.

    It was odd, having Natasha back, but in the best of ways. They went to check the snares together that same morning after letting Lucky out to do her business in the woods, and Natasha teased her on how perfectly she’d set them all up, calling her a filthy overachiever while Yelena threw a snowball in her face. They chased each other all the way back to the cabin, each carrying a small prey animal. Yelena couldn’t remember the last time she cried so much from laughing as she pushed Natasha into the snow and continued on, smiling when she saw that Lucky sat in front of the porch steps, waiting for her.

    Barely slowing down, Yelena fed the rabbit in her arms to the wolf, just as Natasha finally caught up to her and followed her back inside.

    The rest of the day went by in much the same routine, Natasha slotting seamlessly into it like she’d been there since the very first day. She didn’t even complain when Yelena turned on the radio and started singing along to some of the worst songs, while Natasha skinned and prepped the meat of the bird she carried home.

    The first hiccup came with nighttime arrangements. Yelena and Natasha had shared a bed before, and the bed inside the cabin was certainly big enough to house both of them comfortably. But when Yelena got under the covers and picked up her book, Natasha and Lucky entered the bedroom at the same time, and Lucky looked on despondently as Natasha sat down on the empty side of the mattress, leaving no room.

    With a whine, Lucky put her front paws onto the duvet and wobbled on her one good hindleg. “Aw,” Natasha cooed when Lucky yowled and chewed at the book in Yelena’s hands. “Did I take her side of the bed?” She lifted her legs up and slid under the covers, turning onto her side, and propping her cheek up on her elbow. “Now I feel bad.”

    “As you should,” Yelena countered, setting aside Safe Harbor, and sliding further down.

    Reaching out, she stroked Lucky’s chin and scritched at her throat. When the big black wolf yowled again, Yelena sighed and patted her lap. “Come on. We can make this work.”

    Laughing, Natasha watched as Yelena groaned when Lucky scrambled onto the bed and laid her massive weight on top of her. With a glare, Yelena slapped her upside the head. “Shut up.”

    With a smirk, Natasha slipped a little further towards the other side of the bed, before patting the space in between them.

    “Might be better if your dog doesn’t crush you all night,” she offered, reaching out towards Lucky, and presenting her fingers for sniffing. Instead, Lucky bared her teeth and flattened her ears, covering somehow even more of Yelena’s body in a protective stance. Natasha’s smirk turned into a genuine smile. “Now I feel a little less bad about taking so long to get here. I was worried you’d be lonely, but—” She pulled back her hand. “I’m glad you found a friend along the way.”

    “Me, too,” Yelena revealed, hugging Lucky to her.

    ##

    December 7th, 2016 | 01:42 P.M.

    Together, they spent four days inside the cabin. Hanging out, catching up, cooking rabbit stew, playing with Lucky in the snow, and genuinely having a good time just existing without the constant threat of supervision. It was good. In fact, it was better than good; it was everything and more than Yelena had ever dared to hope for.

    Which was, of course, why it couldn’t last forever.

    They had a very good lunch on the fifth day after Natasha had first shown up, eighteen days after Yelena and Lucky first reached the cabin. Belly full and smile content, Natasha drove into town to buy new supplies and get in touch with one of her contacts so they could leave Maine and fly somewhere else instead. Yelena was particularly fond of visiting Australia, but Natasha said to her in no uncertain terms that her favorite destination was off the table. In all fairness, a double-digit flight in terms of hours spent airborne probably wouldn’t have been very fun for Lucky, either.

    While Natasha was away, Yelena started gathering up her things and considered what to take from the cabin with her. She had yet to finish Safe Harbor, since neither Natasha nor Lucky were very good listeners, so she’d already stashed it inside her backpack. She’d also taken down the snares around the forest, planning on cooking all the preserved animals in the freezer and taking their nourishing bodies with them in a little cooler. Avid planner that she was, Natasha picked up on her last visit into town. It was shockingly pink.

    Once they left, Yelena knew she would miss this place. She wouldn’t miss the lukewarm water, but she would miss the feeling of being truly herself. She wouldn’t miss the constant need to keep the fireplace fed, but she would miss letting Lucky out every morning and watching her little wolf paws make indents in the snow. She wouldn’t miss having to make the drive up and down the mountain, but she would miss that feeling of leaving the busy world and coming home to a place that didn’t demand anything of her but her presence.

    She just started rearranging the furniture to make it all look like it had the night she arrived before she went through the place like a hurricane, when she heard the sound of something heavy falling to the floor.

    “Lucky?” Yelena asked, righting herself and taking her hands off the back of the couch.

    When the wolf didn’t respond, she furrowed her brows and called out again, worry sneaking into her heart. “Are you okay? Did your booty slip off the bed again?” The thump was followed by the sound of a half-whine, half-growl, setting off an alarm in Yelena’s mind. Leaving the couch to its own devices, Yelena hurried through the main living area of the cabin and towards the bedroom. Lucky slunk off to nap after goring herself on a special treat of beef jerky Natasha purchased for her. Maybe the jerky had gone bad. Maybe all the salt and the excitement of the snack had caused an upset stomach and—

    Opening the door to the bedroom, Yelena stormed inside.

    “Lucky?” she asked, chest filling with panic when she spotted the black wolf on the floor. It was spasming, its limbs trembling from head to toe as it convulsed, its mouth wide open and its tail fully extended. “No! No, no, no!” Falling to her knees in front of the animal, Yelena tried to steady its head and to pry its jaws apart to make sure it wasn’t choking on its own tongue, but the bones were clamped down shut.

    For a second, Lucky’s blue eyes opened and focused on her before they rolled back, and Lucky’s body shook even more violently than before. Putting down the head, Yelena got to her feet and ran from the room. She’d left her burner phone in the kitchen since she hadn’t ever used it in the first place. But now, it was time to call Natasha and have her return with a vet or with a doctor or with someone who knew what the fuck she was supposed to do.

    Without losing a breath, she grabbed the phone from the kitchen counter, and opened up the contacts while running back.

    “Pick up, pick up, pick up,” Yelena chanted to herself as she hit the call button.

    Just as she heard the third ring, she nearly dropped the phone right where she stood in the doorway.

    Lucky’s body continued to convulse dangerously, and her bones snapped as the muscles and features of the wolf changed shape. The fur receded, the teeth shrank, and the limbs elongated and shrunk in all manner of places, until nothing remained of Yelena’s friend. In its place, a woman lay next to the bed, naked as the day she was born, shivering on the ground, and gasping for breath. The splint Yelena fashioned out of materials she had on hand slid off the human leg it couldn’t cling to, and several scars littered the places Yelena knew Lucky had been shot.

    The woman’s hair was as black as Lucky’s fur, and when she looked at Yelena through pained, half-lidded eyes, the blue irises that stared back at her were unmistakably—the same.

    ##

    December 7th, 2016 | 02:00 P.M.

    Yelena’s thumb found the end call button without looking.

    “Yelena,” the woman wheezed, voice sounding deep and rough and wolf-like. “I—can explain.”

    “You—” Yelena started, trying frantically to make sense of what the hell she was looking at. She had heard of the Hulk, who could turn into a giant green monster man. She had heard of Ant-Man, who could make himself small using Pym particles and science. She had heard of Ultron, a machine who gained sentience and nearly destroyed a whole city. But she had never heard of a woman who could turn into an animal and fool her so effortlessly.

    “I’m sorry,” the woman said, digging her fingernails into the carpet underneath her. She still looked like she was in pain, and Yelena had to work very hard to keep her feet rooted to the floor so as not to reach out. “I’m so sorry.”

    “A human,” Yelena said, squeezing her phone with near enough power to break it. “You’re a human.”

    The woman slowly forced herself to sit up on her knees while the muscles of her biceps bunched and tensed at the clear exertion. Lucky’s body had been huge, and while this woman wasn’t exactly short or soft around the edges, Yelena wondered where all of that mass had disappeared to. And how likely it was to return. And what of all those teeth? What of Yelena’s thoughts of likeness. Of kin.

    “Werewolf,” the woman muttered, leaning her back against the side of the bed and letting out a painful gasp. “I’m—a werewolf.” There were more scars on her. Scars from accidents or wounds that had nothing to do with those that Yelena patched up weeks ago. Scars that were older. Scars that were fading. “I’m sorry I couldn’t tell you—I couldn’t, fuck.” She brought her hand to her head and squeezed shut her eyes, like her brain was caught up in a piercing headache. “I couldn’t shift back.”

    How much had this woman seen of Yelena? How much had she heard? How much had Yelena told her? How many stories of the Red Room had Yelena recounted, feeling safe in the knowledge that no one else would ever get to hear about them? She felt violated; she had placed her trust in this animal, and now—now it—and what of—what of Lucky and—

    “My name is Kate,” the woman said, her expression so reminiscent of the wolf Yelena nearly broke right then. “Kate Bishop.”

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena repeated, dropping her phone, reaching into the holster in her jeans and pulling out her gun.

    “Yelena—” Kate said, scooting away from her until her back was against the small wooden nightstand.

    “Who sent you?” Yelena asked, keeping the gun loose in her hand and trained on the floor, ready to be used, while Kate’s face turned ashen on top of pained. Even in her nakedness, she held herself like the predator she was. Like the wolf. Like Yelena’s friend— “Who sent you here to spy on me? Who sent you here to kill me? Who? Was it Dreykov? Tell me!”

    “No one sent me!” Kate yelled, voice cracking as she slowly pushed herself to her feet.

    “Don’t lie to me!” Yelena snarled, taking a step forward but keeping the gun trained on the floor. She couldn’t stop looking into those eyes and flashing back to the last three weeks. Curling up with Lucky, reading with Lucky, snuggling with Lucky, feeding Lucky, petting Lucky, talking to Lucky. Her friend. Her wolf. An aberration just like Yelena. Not in body, but in spirit.

    “I’m not lying!” Staggering forward like she wasn’t quite sure how to walk on two legs, Kate grabbed Yelena’s shoulders and stared her down. “I’m not lying.” Tears overflowed from her eyes as she nudged their foreheads together and whined. “I’m not lying. I’m not lying. I’m not lying.” Her arms were strong and gentle as she pulled Yelena into a hug. “I’m sorry.

    Please. I’m sorry. I couldn’t—I couldn’t shift back. I got shot and I—They kept chasing me, and I—my leg. I couldn’t—if I could have shifted, I would have—please.” Her hands held Yelena tightly, even while the gun stayed steady and rigid in Yelena’s hand.

    “You’re naked,” Yelena observed, utterly unsure how to deal with the myriad of emotions flowing through her system.

    “Sorry,” Kate said, but she didn’t pull away. “I didn’t think it would happen today. I thought it would take at least another day or two.” Her biceps tightened around Yelena, who realized belatedly that this was the first time she was being held by a woman who wasn’t family. A woman who had listened to her talk for eighteen days about her life as an assassin and who hadn’t shied away from her once. “Maybe I should have left the first time you let me out.” She pulled back her face and her cheeks were wet, giving Yelena the strangest desire to reach up and wipe them clean. “I thought about it, but—when I saw you standing there in the snow, looking into the darkness, and wondering where I’d gone—I couldn’t—I didn’t want to leave you behind. I didn’t—” Her ears were turning red. “I didn’t want you to be alone.”

    Throat growing tight, Yelena closed her eyes.

    “Stupid wolf,” she muttered, finally pushing her gun back into its holster after switching the safety on.

    “Hey, that’s not fair,” Kate protested for a second, before Yelena returned the embrace and held her in a crushing hug.
