    cw: blood See the end of the chapter for more notes November 19th, 2016 | 09:00 A.M.

    Stepping from the train car, Kate pulled on the strap of her backpack with her left hand, rucking it up a little bit higher on her back. With her right hand, she continued to hold onto her duffle bag, doing her best to ignore the myriad of smells that permeated Grand Central Station at all hours of the day. Her nose had always been better than most, and the whole being a werewolf thing didn’t exactly help matters.

    Whistling a jaunty little tune to herself, Kate jumped from step to step as she made her way off the platform. The people around her seemed very busy and didn’t take much notice of her, which was just as well. Whenever the full moon approached, she tended to be more energetic than normal—and she was pretty energetic already.

    Exiting through the big doors at the front entrance, Kate took a deep breath of the semi-clean open air of New York City. Out on the street, there were different aromas than inside the station, but they were almost as overpowering. Out here, she could taste the delicious smell of sizzling, fresh hot dogs with ketchup and mustard, and she took a few minutes out of her day to purchase one from a nearby vendor. Scarfing it down with little decorum, Kate closed her eyes and let out a moan of pure satisfaction. Fifteen hours from now—roughly around midnight—her transformation would take root, and she’d be able to hunt for some fresh meat. For now, this hot dog would do nicely.

    Mostly sated, Kate skipped along the sidewalk with a grin. Despite the cold season, the sun graced them all with its rays, only strengthening her good mood. She looked forward to running through the woods and chasing a few squirrels. Most of the time, she didn’t eat anything smaller than a hare, but it was fun to chase them all the same.

    She could have hailed a cab to take her where she wanted to go, but she was having too much fun exercising her legs. It took her thirty minutes to walk from Grand Central to Bishop Security Headquarters, yet she wasn’t out of breath when she reached the front desk and greeted the young, white woman behind it.

    “Hey Darleen, do you know if my mom’s in a meeting right now?” Kate asked, leaning on the desk with her left hand and putting on her most charming smile. On a logical level, she knew that none of the people here could tell that she was close to vibrating out of her skin, and yet she didn’t relax until she saw Darleen’s easy smile. Darleen’s hair was black and perfectly coiffed, and her lips were darkened with the appropriate amount of understated lipstick. She was roughly Kate’s age—maybe a year or two older than her—and Kate knew there was a very good reason she had this job. She knew there was a very good reason that most people who worked at Bishop Security had their jobs.

    “Good morning, Ms. Bishop. Mrs. Bishop is in her office. She is entertaining some significant potential investors at the moment, but the meeting’s scheduled to end in about fifteen minutes if you want to wait?” Darleen said, pulling something up on her desktop PC, possibly to ensure she had the right meeting in mind.

    “Sounds like a plan,” Kate said with a thumbs up, giving Darleen one last smile before straightening her back. “Have a nice day, Darleen. Thanks for your help.”

    “Of course, Ms. Bishop,” Darleen said with a sweet smile and a slight blush to her cheeks. Kate had never been shy about her interests, and before she knew just why Darleen was hired, she even entertained the thought of asking the woman out. “Have a wonderful day, Ms.

    Bishop.”

    Giving her a second thumbs up, Kate turned and walked towards the row of elevators.

    Everybody greeted her as she walked past, and Kate sent them all waves and smiles. Bishop Security had existed since well before Kate was born, so she’d pretty much grown up around most of the people who worked there. The company settled in this ginormous building when Kate was ten years old, so she knew each floor and meeting room like the back of her hand. She remembered playing hide and seek with the various interns who were often tasked with looking after her, and she knew what lay beneath the building, too. She knew what lay beneath the parking lots, away from curious eyes.

    As a child, she’d always felt safe and protected around these people, but all that dramatically changed after she turned seventeen. As she stood in the elevator with three employees who chatted to her about her college classes, Kate tried not to imagine what they’d do if they found out what she really was—if they found out what she’d been turned into that fateful night and what her mother had since been hiding from them.

    The three others got off on the sixth floor, while Kate stayed in the elevator until the eighteenth. Quickly checking her phone, Kate was pleased to see that her travels through the building had already eaten up seven minutes of the fifteen Darleen had warned her about.

    Sitting still in her current condition, while not impossible, was not fun.

    Stepping off the elevator, Kate passed through a corridor lined with glass walls. In the rooms beyond, she could see high-ranking staff members in suits and skirts tapping on their keyboards or speaking into their headsets. Some of them raised their hands when they noticed her passing by, but most were too focused on what was on their screens. The glass was soundproof—some special material that her mother purchased from Stark Industries—which was definitely necessary to keep prying ears from overhearing something they shouldn’t.

    At the end of the corridor, Kate could see her mother’s glass office. Right in front of it, two comfortable couches with an assortment of very fancy chairs and a coffee table in between them adorned either side of the waiting area. Another large desk was set up to the left of her mother’s door. The Black woman with short-cropped dark hair who sat behind it smiled when she saw Kate.

    “What’s up, Dee?” Kate asked, offering the woman a fist bump. “Looks like my mom’s in a super important meeting.” Moving her eyes beyond her mother’s assistant, Kate allowed them to travel the broad backs and shoulders of the men and women who occupied the even more comfortable chairs in her mother’s office. One of the men was Black, while the other two were white. The two women flanking them were also white, and both wore expensive-

    looking charcoal pantsuits. “Big investors?”

    No one else was around, which probably meant her mother didn’t have another meeting after this one for quite some time. Usually, the waiting area in front of her mother’s office was packed with people who wanted to speak to her. Seeing it so empty at nine forty-five on a Thursday morning was weird. Then again, it was possible that her mom’s next meeting would be holographic.

    “You know I can’t talk about your mother’s clients,” Dee said, offering Kate a box of homemade cookies. Reaching inside, Kate picked out one of the more chocolatey-looking ones. Holding it loosely, she tried not to make it too obvious that she was studying the people who listened to her mother speak and watched her gesticulate. “They’ve been in there for close to two hours. Should be done in about—”

    “Six more minutes, yeah,” Kate said, finally stuffing the cookie into her mouth and chowing down on it. “S’good,” she said, gesturing at the box before giving Dee a thumbs up. Dee’s smile was warm as she offered Kate the cookies again, who snagged up two more.

    “You’ve been talking to Darleen,” Dee said with a finger-wag seconds before Kate saw her mother’s five visitors get to their feet simultaneously. “That girl has been waiting for you to —” As soon as the door behind Dee opened, a scent permeated the hallway that Kate was pretty sure only she could pick up. A mixture of gun oil, ash, and something oddly chemical stung Kate’s nose. Dee’s mouth abruptly closed, and she got to her feet. Holding her hands in front of her lap, Dee fixed a pleasant smile on her lips and nodded to the three men while the two women briefly lingered in front of Kate’s mom.

    “Thank you so much for stopping by today,” her mother said, reaching out and taking the hand of the woman on the left. “Ms. Kelly, it was such a pleasure to meet you.” Then, she turned to the woman on the right and shook her hand. “Ms. Trask, the same goes for you.

    And for your partners, of course.” Kate’s mom nodded to the three men, who nodded back to her. Only now did her mother realize that Kate was standing opposite Dee with her backpack and duffle bag. “Ah, Kate.” Her smile turned a little wooden. “Ms. Kelly, Ms. Trask, meet my daughter, Kate Bishop.”

    “Hi,” Kate said, lifting her free hand and waving at them with the two cookies.

    The five visitors all said their hellos, then they all said their goodbyes again. Dee rounded her desk to escort them to the elevator and a waiting intern who would take them the rest of the way out of the building. Kate watched their very straight and very muscled backs as they briskly walked out of sight.

    “It’s not polite to stare,” Kate’s mom said, wrapping her fingers around Kate’s arm and tugging on her until they were both inside the office. Once the door was closed, Eleanor let go of Kate and sat on the couch in the corner where the three men previously sat. Kate dropped her duffle on the floor by one of the chairs, then pulled off her backpack and slumped into the cushions. “How was the train ride?”

    “Awesome, awesome,” Kate replied, finally stuffing the two cookies into her mouth. Outside of the office, Dee returned to her desk. Kate saw the woman pick up one of her cookies and bite into it before returning to her work. “Those suits looked like some serious business.” “I rather wish you hadn’t been here,” Eleanor replied, placing her right arm on the back of the couch, and studying Kate’s face. “How’s the fever?”

    “Good,” Kate replied quickly, drumming her fingers on her belly, and pulling a face at her mom’s old-ass terminology. The fever. How spooky. “Manageable. You don’t have to worry about me, Mom. This isn’t my first rodeo. I didn’t even bite anyone on the way here.”

    Eleanor shook her head.

    “That is not what I meant, and you know it,” she said, scooting closer to Kate’s chair and reaching out to take her hand. When she touched Kate’s overheated skin, she briefly pulled back before renewing their connection with a firmer grip. “You know why I worry.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, looking at her old sneakers. “I know, mom.”

    With a sigh, Eleanor got to her feet and looked out over the many glass offices beyond her own.

    “Do you remember Senator Robert Kelly?” she asked, running her fingers across Kate’s hair. Today, her mother wore an expensive black and white dress that Kate had never seen on her before. She assumed it was from a new designer collection that Kate didn’t have the patience or energy to keep up with. Her question sparked some semblance of memory in Kate’s mind, but not enough to form a picture, so she shook her head. “About three decades ago, before you were born, he ran on an anti-mutant campaign. But that wasn’t the only thing he was fighting against.”

    “He was a hunter?” Kate asked, barely remembering hearing about the guy her mom was talking about. Maybe she’d read about it online, or maybe she’d heard about it in high school. Her business degree didn’t feature a whole lot of classes about politics.

    “He was the hunter,” Eleanor clarified, moving away from Kate’s hair and towards the gigantic floor-to-ceiling window that made up the entire wall behind the couch. “At the height of his political career, he’d slain more supernatural creatures than many entire families combined. Your father always admired him greatly, but we never got the chance to work with him.”

    “How devastating,” Kate grumbled, hating the veneration in her mother’s voice.

    “Things were different then,” Eleanor said, leaving no room for argument. “Regardless of what allowances I make for you, Bishop Security is lucky to be making a deal with his daughter. Ms. Kelly and Ms. Trask have resources that we simply do not.”

    Allowances, Kate thought bitterly. Meeting with her mother always left a bad taste in her mouth these days. There was no doubt in her mind that her mother loved her—the fact that she was still alive even after being turned was proof enough. If the hunters who worked for and with her mother found out the truth, Kate would be killed without hesitation, and Eleanor might befall the same fate. And still, Kate could barely live with the fact that her mother continued to run Bishop Security. The company was a mere front. It did the bare minimum of security work in order to hide the hunters who committed indiscriminate murder of supernatural beings.

    “I’m so happy for you,” Kate said sarcastically, getting to her feet and joining her mom by the window. It wouldn’t do either of them any good if she kept harping on the fact that her mom was being hypocritical. They’d had the argument before. “Can I borrow one of the cars tonight? I want to head out of town for my fever party.”

    A furrow appeared between Eleanor’s brows, possibly at Kate’s deliberately flippant choice of words.

    “Kate,” she said, cupping Kate’s cheek and brushing her thumb along it. This time, she didn’t flinch away from the heat of Kate’s skin, as her blue eyes shone only with her motherly concern. For a second, Kate pretended there was no ideological divide between them. “Do you really think that is the best idea? Why don’t you stay in one of the cages at home? You’ll be safe there.”

    Taking a deep breath through her nose, Kate stamped down on the violent reaction those three simple sentences provoked. Just the thought of a cage sent her mind back—back to when she’d first been turned. She’d been seventeen, still living under her mother’s roof. Almost all of her transformations following that fateful night six years ago had been forced into the largest cage her mother kept at the penthouse. She’d only been allowed to run free since she moved away for college.

    “It’s okay, Mom,” Kate said, ignoring the itching memories of throwing her gigantic body against metal bars that didn’t so much as bend under her immense power. She only half-

    remembered some of those nights; she hated being cooped up, even as a human. But as a wolf? Inside that cage, the call of the moon made her lose control on more than one occasion. “I’ll drive for a couple of hours. I already checked out the route I want to take on a map. I found a forest that’s really far away from any people who might see me.”

    “I don’t like it when you’re out there in that condition,” Eleanor continued, ignoring Kate’s instant scowl. “I know you think you can control yourself, but you can’t be sure. You don’t know what might happen or how the moon will affect your judgment—”

    “I have been out there for four years,” Kate growled, taking her mother’s wrist to pull her hand off her own face. She loved her mom. She loved her mom, and she loved her dad—

    before and after he died. Kate knew why her mother behaved the way she did—and Kate knew why she would never change her mind. But that truth didn’t make any of this easier. “I have never hurt another person. I’m not the monster you think I am.”

    “Honey,” Eleanor said, her expression a mixture of annoyance and concern. “I know you’re not a monster.” She pulled her hand from Kate’s grasp to cup the side of her neck instead. “You’re my baby, and I worry about you. There are monsters out there, and they won’t hesitate to—”

    “There are monsters under this roof, Mom,” Kate said, thinking back to the night she was turned. She remembered the bite of the werewolf in her side as it tossed her, scared for its own life. She remembered aiming her silver arrows at it just before it attacked her. She remembered her mother tucking her in shortly after her father died, and telling her about how, under a hunter president, those aliens never would have attacked and taken her father from them. She remembered the hatred she felt for all that was different. She remembered seeing Hawkeye jumping off a building—just a human with human weapons—and feeling so inspired. “And the people who work here won’t hesitate, either.”

    With a sigh, Eleanor leaned forward to kiss Kate’s forehead.

    “I love you,” she said, her blue eyes full of a sorrow Kate could hardly understand.

    “Sweetheart, I’m sorry. Of course, you can take a car. Just make sure it’s one of the less extravagant ones, hm?” She tried to smile, and Kate felt her righteous anger deflate like a popped balloon.

    “Yeah,” Kate said with a nod. “Yeah, okay. Sure.”

    They spent another twenty minutes catching each other up on Kate’s classes and official Bishop Security business before Kate left her mom to her work and decided to head home.

    On the way out of the building, as she greeted and passed more and more undercover hunters who would shoot her without a second thought, she wondered if coming home around the full moon had been a mistake. As far as she knew, her mom was her only living relative, and although their paths had split in entirely different directions, Kate didn’t want to let go. There was also the small detail that her mom was paying her way through college, but Kate didn’t like to linger on how that hard truth made her feel sometimes.

    Still, Kate made the trip, in spite of how very stupid it was to stray so close to hunter territory. Tomorrow, The Battle of New York celebrated its thirteenth anniversary, giving Kate the opportunity to spend time with her mother and out of class. She might not have come if her mom didn’t sound so lonely on the phone the week before. Even though her mother asked her to stay put for this very short vacation, Kate would have come down for Thanksgiving in four days anyway.

    When she arrived at her mom’s penthouse after walking several blocks, Kate greeted the doorman before riding the elevator and letting herself inside. She already knew which car she would be taking tonight, so she didn’t need to check for any more preparations. Everything she needed was in her backpack and her duffle. Putting both down on the floor at the foot of her bed, Kate slumped face-first into her nicely made pillows and duvet. The energy continued to pulse back and forth under her skin, promising her many great things to come. Soon, she’d be able to run faster than the wind, climb higher than any bear, and jump further than any human athlete.

    Until then, she’d make do.

    ##

    November 19th, 2016 | 10:59 P.M.

    Her mom came home for a very early dinner at five thirty, after which Kate grabbed her bag and the keys to her mom’s silver SUV. The drive from New York City to a small town at the border between New Hampshire and Maine took her roughly four hours, but she didn’t linger for very long. After a quick stop at a diner for a burger and a generous helping of fries, Kate drove further from the town center and parked the car in a random parking lot at the edge of town. She’d be back for it in the morning, so she didn’t worry about towing.

    Jumping from the car, Kate looked up at the sky, her pupils dilating as she watched the moon disappear and reappear between the clouds. It was almost time. She had less than an hour until the transformation would begin.

    With a smile, she grabbed her duffle from the passenger seat before locking the SUV and stuffing the keys into her bag. At this time of night on a random Saturday, the sleepy town around her was deserted. She could smell a few people here or there, but none of them were very close to her. Still, she was pretty sure no one would have batted an eyelash at her either way because she was dressed in a pair of grey sweatpants and a jumper, while her feet were wedged into running shoes. To any bystander, she probably looked like someone passing through and stopping to get some exercise. The only thing that might spark curiosity was the time of night, but even that could be explained away with a quick: Those weird city types. She reached the stretch of forest where she intended to start her midnight journey within the next few minutes, and with a last look left and right, she crossed into the tree line. Once inside, she listened for any human noises around her and kept her attention on not being discovered. All too soon, her more rational faculties would be slightly hampered by the moon’s influence. It wasn’t like her mom always insinuated; Kate didn’t lose control or all sense of good taste and decency. Being a wolf just… simplified things.

    When she couldn’t hear anything but a few animal critters rushing through the underbrush, she allowed herself to relax. Now, she just needed to find the right tree to hide her duffle bag and clothes. A few more minutes of running and jumping brought her to a gigantic fir tree that was dense with deep, blue-green needles. Looking around herself one more time, Kate slipped her jumper and sweatpants off her body before stuffing both into the duffle. In the nude, she willed her nails to shift into claws, then slung the duffle over her shoulder and scaled the fur tree.

    The fir was one of the tallest trees in the area, so from up there, Kate felt even closer to the moon and her beautiful glow. Gently placing the duffle on one of the highest branches, Kate tied it down against the trunk with a rope she brought, before standing up and closing her eyes. The breeze caressed her skin and swept through her hair. The thick aroma of the fir was heavy in her nostrils, droning out the more unpleasant smells of cars and oil that permeated many human spaces. Up there, she felt as free as she ever had.

    “I’m here,” she whispered to the moon, looking up at its majestic glow. Once upon a time, she hated and feared its power. Before she was turned, the moon had been a dangerous adversary to her family. Supernatural creatures were drawn to it, strengthened by it. Now that she was a wolf, she knew why her parents feared it. They should. The moon’s power was great, and no mere mortal could ever bring her down. She was the queen of the oceans and the mother of all that lurked in the shadows.

    In her throat, she felt the howl before she let it go. Inside, she was already half-wolf, half- human. Only a few minutes now. She could feel it. She was so close.

    A gunshot rang through the night, flinching Kate into a crouch on her branch, hiding behind the needles of the fir that dulled her senses to the outside world with its sweet scent. Now that she was no longer transfixed by the moon’s beauty, she could clearly hear the sound of footsteps and smell what she should have been smelling for several minutes: A mixture of gun oil, ash, and something oddly chemical that burned her nostrils.

    “Crap,” she whispered to herself, jumping down a few more branches to better hide behind the foliage and blend in with the rest of the trees. Her body burned with the desire to shift, yet she held it back as much as she could. The hunters were advancing on her position quickly. If she changed now, she would not be able to hide from them.

    A twig snapped under someone’s foot.

    “Careful,” another person hissed, just as the muzzle of a rifle glinted in the moonlight. They were underneath her. Her only saving grace was their refusal to use flashlights while on the prowl. She wasn’t surprised. Although they’d just fired a shot at something, they were most likely wearing night vision goggles to avoid anything supernatural being scared off by the unnatural light. Even if they looked up, they would not see her. The needles of the fir she was crouching on would protect her from their view.

    Just a few more minutes, she told herself, her throat swelling with the desire to howl.

    Clenching her teeth, Kate dug her claw-nails into the trunk of the fir and squeezed her eyes shut. Please. Please, just a few more minutes. We need to keep quiet. Her arm muscles tightened as fur sprouted from the skin of her shoulders. No. No, no, no, no!

    Below her, the hunters continued to walk over the densely covered forest ground. The roaring in her ears couldn’t mask the thumping of their hearts or the wet clicking of their throats as they swallowed. When the clouds shifted, and the rays of moonlight fell on her skin, shining through the needles of the fir she clung to, she knew it was over.

    A roaring snarl slipped from her mouth as her body contorted. More fur sprouted on her as her claws were forced to release their grip on the trunk. Tugging at the skin of her chest, Kate lost her balance on the branch and toppled over the side. Her shift continued mid-air, but she wasn’t fully transformed as she landed on the hard ground below. As she looked up, she recognized some of the stony faces of the hunters.

    Ms. Kelly, Ms. Trask, meet my daughter, Kate Bishop.

    Her face broke apart at the seams as the maw of the wolf beneath forced its way out. Her teeth, long and bloody from her former human self, snarled as the hunters raised their weapons. But the damage was already done before they started shooting. Even as Kate jumped in the opposite direction and started running, she knew she was completely fucked. The silver bullet slamming into her left shoulder and wrenching a pained whine from her throat didn’t hurt nearly as much as the realization that they’d seen her. They’d seen her mid- shift.

    She barely knew where she was going as she slammed her giant paws on the ground over and over and over again. More bullets whizzed by her head as she suddenly realized the hunters must have found mountain bikes or some other form of quick transportation to give chase.

    Her back exploded with fresh pain as another silver bullet slammed into her flesh. She felt nearly frantic with panic as she jumped over a log, her mind driving itself insane with thoughts of capture.

    Up ahead, there was only the darkness of the forest. Behind her, there was death.

    The next time she jumped, she nearly crashed into a boulder she hadn’t noticed. But when she swerved to avoid it, she collided with an old truck that sped along a dirt road leading through the forest. Something in her left leg shattered on impact, but she didn’t slow down to check if she had run into friend or foe. Adrenaline kept her going as she ran further. Further. Further.

    The yelling stopped, but Kate didn’t stop running. The frantic sound of heartbeats stopped, but Kate didn’t stop running. The smell of gun oil and chemicals stopped, but Kate didn’t stop running. The sensation of being chased stopped, but Kate didn’t stop running.

    Around her, snow began to fall. The higher up she went, the colder it became. Further and further. She had to get away. If they caught her, they would kill her. If they caught her, they would bring her body to her mother. If they caught her, they would stuff and mount her broken corpse as a twisted trophy. Kate knew what hunters did. Kate knew because she had been a hunter once, and she had almost done it to others. If she hadn’t been turned before the full initiation ritual, she would have been on the other side of the rifle.

    When she finally ran out of energy, she had no idea where she was. Limping slowly, she clawed her way up a shallow ramp of dirt and shrubbery. There was a road in front of her. She was so slow. She was so tired. Light suddenly illuminated the road. Light. Something big slammed into her side, knocking her down—light, and then darkness.

    ##

    December 7th, 2016 | 02:17 P.M.

    Yelena’s body was warm; Yelena’s body was soft; Yelena’s body was strong. Kate never wanted to let her go. She had seen the pain in the other woman’s eyes and listened to a hundred small stories of the torture she endured. Finally, after eighteen days of nudging Yelena’s hand or licking her cheeks, Kate could hold her in her arms.

    Keeping her eyes closed, Kate breathed in the familiar smell of Yelena’s skin and the sweater she was wearing. She wasn’t quite sure what to say now that she had a mouth to speak and vocal cords that could do more than bark and growl.

    “When do you think Natasha will be back?” Kate asked, her words slightly muffled.

    “She did not give me an estimate,” Yelena replied, sounding equally unsure about their situation. “I do not know how long it will take her to reach her contact.” Her hands never moved on Kate’s back, but her fingers felt gentle and reassuring. One palm covered the scar of the gunshot wound Kate had sustained on her left shoulder. “You should get dressed.”

    “Uhm, yeah. I guess,” Kate confirmed, reluctant to give up the embrace. “Sometimes I forget I don’t have fur anymore after I transform back.” She didn’t move to untangle herself, and neither did Yelena. “Even though it feels really different. The air is a whole lot less cold when you’re covered from head to toe in a nice blanket that’s also attached to you—”

    Finally, Yelena unfurled her arms from around Kate and walked to the closet.

    Sheepishly, Kate pulled on a pair of jeans whose pant legs stopped two inches above her ankles and a thick cable knit sweater Yelena wore out in the snow from time to time. By the time she was no longer naked, Yelena seemed a little less uncomfortable, but she still wouldn’t look at her. Something that felt an awful lot like guilt swam through Kate’s system. “I really am sorry,” Kate said, sticking her hands into the pockets of her borrowed jeans. She missed the easy camaraderie of the last eighteen days; she missed Yelena looking at her with all the affection in the universe; she missed being the center of her friend’s attention. “Yes, so you have said.” Gazing around the room, Yelena appeared to look for something to say. When nothing came, Kate contemplated hugging her again but decided against it. Yelena had been very affectionate with her—well, with Lucky—but that didn’t mean she would be as comfortable with Kate in her slightly less furry form. Maybe their embrace just now had been a fluke.

    “Do you… want to go sit and talk?” Kate asked, hunching her shoulders forward in the hopes of making herself seem smaller. When Yelena finally looked at her, those green eyes full of unnamed emotions, Kate had the strangest urge to lay on top of her like a big, weighted blanket.

    “The kitchen will be fine,” Yelena said, turning and striding out of the bedroom.

    Picking up the makeshift splint her left leg slipped out of after transforming back, Kate followed Yelena through the short hallway and into the main living area. Now that she was upright, the room looked a lot different to her. Still, as she studied the couch, she longed for the many times she and Yelena had snuggled on it together. Right now, she wouldn’t even mind listening to that awful book Yelena liked to read.

    By the time Kate walked over to the kitchen island, Yelena had already taken up position behind it on one of the chairs. When Kate stopped to look at her, Yelena gestured at the chair on the opposite side, causing a few puzzle pieces to slip into place in Kate’s brain. Of course. Yelena would want to put space between them after this.

    Stay strong, Kate told herself, putting the splint on the island before sitting on the chair Yelena had indicated. She’s probably never seen a werewolf before. The fact that she didn’t shoot you is already a great sign of Only Good Things to Come.

    “You claim that you have not been sent to spy on me,” Yelena said, pulling her gun from her holster to place it on the island, loosely keeping her hand on the grip. Furrowing her brows, Kate stared at the weapon, doing her best not to scowl. At least the chances of the gun containing any silver bullets were slim to none.

    “Yeah,” Kate said, folding her hands on top of the island to keep them visible.

    “Prove it.” Yelena gestured at Kate with her free left hand.

    Furrowing her brows deeper, Kate blinked at her friend.

    “Uhm… how?” she asked, moving her right hand to her cheek to scratch at it. “I’ve never really been in a situation like this. The only person who knows I’m a werewolf is my mom, and she doesn’t exactly love talking about it. Or, uhm, I guess maybe she didn’t at this point. She might be dead. I don’t know if her new hunter friends were super happy after finding out I’m a wolf.” As soon as the words were out of her mouth, she regretted them. The thought of her mom lying in a pool of her own blood as Trask, Kelly, and their three-dude-entourage stood over her made her blood boil. Clenching her hands into fists, she tried to hide her anger as she lowered her gaze.

    “Hunter friends?” Yelena asked, her hands perfectly still. “I assume they hunt people like you?”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, studying the grooves and scars on Yelena’s fingers. It wasn’t lost on her that Yelena had used the word people instead of things. But then… she was like that.

    “Why would your mother befriend someone who would hurt her daughter?” At her question, Kate finally looked up. While Yelena’s expression might have looked inscrutable upon first inspection, Kate had spent a great deal of time staring at her face and studying her unguarded emotions. Something about this one made Kate wonder if Yelena was with her, in this kitchen, or if her thoughts had veered off to the place she once called home.

    “My parents come from two very long lines of hunters. My dad’s was more prolific and had more money, but that’s about the only difference between them,” Kate said, unclenching her fingers and letting out a deep breath. “That’s what makes all of this so ironic, I guess. I got bitten six years ago on a hunt. It was just me and my mom out there, chasing another wolf.” Sitting upright, Kate pulled up the bottom of her sweater to reveal the long, jagged bite marks across her side. “Hunters are supposed to kill themselves as soon as they get turned, but my mom—she wouldn’t let me.”

    Yelena’s eyes moved across the scars.

    “And your mother’s new friends,” Yelena said, returning her gaze from Kate’s stomach to her eyes. “You say they were the ones who shot you, broke your leg, and made you pass out on that stretch of the road where I found you?”

    After dropping the sweater back down, Kate recounted the events of the chase and her collision with the truck in the woods. “My stuff’s probably still up on that beautiful, giant fir tree,” Kate muttered, sparing a thought to the SUV she left behind and the blue-green needles that shielded her from view and protected her. Pulling her mind back to the moment, Kate blinked slowly at Yelena. “Do you believe me now that I’m not spying on you?”

    “No,” Yelena said, but her tone of voice belied the truth. “You should be suspicious by nature, Kate Bishop. It will help you stay alive.” The way Yelena said her name sent shivers up and down Kate’s back and made the hairs on her arms stand to attention—and it wasn’t from fear. “You said you could not shift back into a person while you were injured. That seems… impractical.”

    “It was the silver,” Kate said, flexing the fingers of her left hand as she thought of the silver arrows locked away inside the old chest in her childhood bedroom. After her turning, the arrows burned her palm when she tried to touch them to her skin. Since then, there had been no close calls with hunters; there had been no close calls, except for the one that mattered now. “It’s poisonous to werewolves.”

    “Is it?” Yelena asked, softly shaking her head. “You should not tell me. I might use this information against you.”

    “No, you won’t,” Kate said with a shrug, sliding her right thumb along her left palm.

    “Besides, it’s not like it’s a secret.”

    “No?”

    Kate blinked at her again, wondering if Yelena was being purposefully obtuse. Then, something clicked in her brain, and her mouth dropped open. “Did they not have any movies or TV shows at the Red Room? ‘Cause the fact that silver kills monsters is, like, super common knowledge in mainstream media, even if the existence of monsters isn’t.” When Yelena didn’t say anything, Kate nodded. “Okay, yeah, fine. You probably wouldn’t tell me that if I were a spy and could use this knowledge against you. I get the concept.”

    “You are learning,” Yelena drawled, reminding Kate of the many times Yelena poked fun at her when she was in her wolf-form. Warmth gathered in her chest at the sudden burst of familiarity and affection. “So, it took your body eighteen days to remove the poison from your flesh? That seems inefficient as well as inconvenient.”

    “The bullets were in there for hours,” Kate said, purposefully thinking of anything but the searing pain of the gunshot wounds. “I don’t remember how I ended up in front of your truck at all.” She rubbed her hand over her face and tried to focus on the trees and rocks she passed on the way. She couldn’t. “I just remember running.”

    Silence elapsed between them. As Kate tried to regain her equilibrium, Yelena kept her hand on the grip of her gun and studied her quietly but with great intent. They slept in the same bed for several days; Kate had never felt more comfortable around anyone, not even her parents. It was hard to forget the sound of Yelena’s breathing or the easy way she smiled at her.

    “Silver,” Yelena said, pursing her lips and turning her head to the side as she continued to stare. “You say it hurts monsters. I have several questions about this.” With a nod, Kate sat back in her chair and waited for Yelena to speak. “When you say monsters, this implies that there are more than just wolves?”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, lifting her left shoulder in a half-shrug. “You know,” Kate stopped herself, remembering Yelena’s lack of knowledge in certain areas. “Uh, well, there are all kinds of creatures of the night. I mean, you know what I am now. Werewolves are people who turn into wolves during the full moon. We can also shift when the moon isn’t full, but we always have to at midnight on the first night. And then there’s vampires—they need human blood to survive. They drink it.” She mimicked a pair of sharp fangs with her pointer fingers. “There’s also ghouls and zombies. They eat dead humans—they each eat different parts of the body, though.”

    Yelena lifted her hand, prompting Kate to shut up within seconds.

    “You have to shift during the full moon?” Yelena asked, furrowing her brows. “That also seems impractical. And dangerous, no?”

    “Look, I didn’t come up with this stuff on my own, okay?” Kate said, shrugging again before lifting her left hand. “Don’t freak out.” She pulled back the sleeve of her cable knit sweater so she wouldn’t stretch it out too much. Then, she flexed her fingers and focused on her arm. Within milliseconds, the human limb was gone, hidden away under black fur. Her nails grew into claws, but her palms and fingers didn’t become paws. Instead, they remained humanoid —if much larger than Kate’s human hand.

    “Very interesting,” Yelena said calmly, though her hand tightened on her gun almost imperceptibly.

    “I won’t hurt you,” Kate said, shifting her hand back into its human shape before placing it carefully and deliberately slowly on the island once more. “I’ve never used my wolf powers to hurt anyone.” Her eyes dropped to her fingers, and she couldn’t stop herself from clenching them into fists.

    “Yet you call yourself a monster,” Yelena observed, visibly loosening her grip on the weapon. “A contradiction.”

    “It’s a good catch-all term,” Kate muttered, pulling the sleeve back over her arm.

    Yelena didn’t dwell on the topic.

    “Could you turn back into the wolf you were before if you wanted to? Or is that a moon-only possibility?” Oddly, Kate felt like the name Lucky drifted somewhere through the air around them, tying them together and tearing them apart. Yelena had befriended Lucky; Kate had befriended Yelena. They were not the same.

    “I could, yeah,” Kate replied, fiddling with a loose thread. “But I’d have to get naked again so I don’t explode these threads. Unless you want to kiss your pants and this sweater goodbye.” She felt the urge again—the urge to hunch in on herself and make herself smaller. She wasn’t used to this; she wasn’t used to caring so much about what someone else thought of her. No one but her mom knew who she really was; no one but her mom and a handful of other anonymous names on supernatural internet forums.

    “Okay,” Yelena said with a small nod. “Let’s not.”

    “Yeah, sure,” Kate agreed, giving Yelena a thumbs up. “I figured.” She let go of the thread, moving her eyes to Yelena’s face. “Have you and Natasha decided where you want to go yet?” The green of Yelena’s eyes gave nothing away.

    “What will happen to you?” Yelena asked, pulling the gun off the island, slipping it back in her holster, and expertly avoiding the question. Once the gun was out of sight, she placed her elbows on the island and brought her hands together, resting her chin on her interlaced fingers and studying Kate with quiet intensity. “Will they hunt you?”

    “I guess,” Kate said, pulling the sleeves of the sweater over her palms. “If I go back to New York, they might. I can try contacting my mom. If they haven’t killed her, she might be able to send me some cash. I assume they’ll be watching my bank accounts and my college and my apartment.” Kate worried her bottom lip. “And my mom’s apartment, and Bishop Security, and every fricking other place I’ve ever been.”

    “You are that important to them?” Something in Yelena’s voice struck Kate’s very core.

    Something that said: I see you; I know you; I know what this feels like.

    “Hunters exist to hunt; it’s kind of in their job description. Now that they know what I am, they won’t stop until I’m dead.” Kate thought about her parents’ penthouse and the secret wing they hid behind a sliding bookcase. Before she was turned, she and her parents planned most of their missions there and used Bishop Security resources to do it. “They have access to a lot of different information, too. Criminal databases, flight plans...”

    Again, silence descended upon them for a handful of minutes.

    “Maybe I should just get in a car and drive to Mexico,” Kate finally muttered, anger surging through her veins as she clenched her hands into fists. She couldn’t just give up her whole life, her degree, her mom, her friends. For years, she thought her intimate knowledge of hunters would protect her from being discovered, but this time, her mother’s connections might be her downfall. Her wolf blood sang with the taste for revenge; she tamped it down. Hard. Now wasn’t the time. She didn’t want to scare Yelena. “I don’t mean to be a downer.” Forcing a smile onto her lips, Kate consciously unclenched her hands and sat back in the kitchen chair. “I’ll be fine.”

    Yelena said nothing; her handsome face stayed neutral as she got to her feet and rounded the kitchen island. Without touching Kate, she headed for the front door while Kate scrambled to her feet and looked after her.

    “I will check our luggage for shoes that will fit your human paws,” Yelena said, grabbing the handle and pulling on it. “Driving is not much fun without them. Do not go anywhere.” She was gone a second later, closing the door behind herself and leaving Kate to wonder if she said something to upset her friend. Sinking back into the kitchen chair, Kate put her face in her hands. What the fuck was she supposed to do next?

    ##

    December 7th, 2016 | 03:59 P.M.

    While Yelena looked for shoes or did whatever else caused her to be gone for goddamn eons, Kate moved her sorry ass over to the couch. Once there, she leaned back and stared at the ceiling, keeping her hands folded on her stomach and her heels on the carpet at her feet. As she waited for Yelena to return, she allowed her mind to circle the drain of her sanity. She could call her mom’s cellphone—unless her mom had changed it over the last eighteen days to avoid being spied on. She could call her mom’s office—unless the hunters were tapping the phone lines at Bishop Security. She could call Greer and have her deliver a message in person—unless Greer and Franny’s phones were bugged, too. She was quickly running out of options. Sending a letter might not work, either. Her mom’s mail could be searched, and Kate wasn’t confident she could come up with a convincing cypher.

    When the front door opened, Kate’s nose first picked up on the change in smell. She’d been so preoccupied with her thoughts she hadn’t heard the truck pull up or considered how Yelena was supposed to look for shoes when Natasha’s car, with most of their luggage, was miles and miles away.

    “You weren’t exaggerating,” Natasha said, stepping inside and kicking off her shoes by the door. She carried a paper bag in her arms, her right eyebrow raised as she looked at Kate. For some reason, she looked a lot less shocked than Kate would have expected her to. But then again, after five days together, Kate didn’t feel terribly shocked to be in the presence of an Avenger, either.

    She perfectly remembered the moment Natasha first stepped into the cabin. She hadn’t recognized the woman at first—seeing someone on television and seeing them in person was a different thing entirely. All she’d known then was what Yelena had told her about the Red Room and their methods. All she’d known then was that Yelena was vulnerable in the shower and needed to be protected. All she’d known then was Yelena’s gentle smile and careful hugs and how Kate would never allow anyone to hurt her again. But later, when she finally realized who Natasha was, a shiver of excitement had passed through her. Not so much because Kate was crazy about the Avengers as a whole, but because Natasha knew Clint, the man who inspired Kate to pick up a bow instead of a machine gun.

    “Kate Bishop, is it?”

    “Uhm,” Kate said, sitting up on the couch and giving Natasha a little wave. “Hi.” She held onto her restraint by the skin of her teeth. As a wolf, she’d been unable to blurt out questions about where Clint was or what he was getting up to. Having human vocals cords fixed this dilemma. But considering her situation, the likelihood of them ever meeting was slim to none.

    Shaking her head, Natasha walked up to Kate and shoved the paper bag into her arms.

    “See if they fit, will you?” she said, dressing up the command as a question. Satisfied with her work, Natasha entered the open kitchen to pour herself a glass of water from a bottle on the counter, distracting Kate from her musings about her idol. Yelena closed the front door behind herself, prompting Natasha to roll her eyes and point at her. “Only you would bring home a werewolf as a pet.”

    “Hey, come on,” Kate said, peering into the paper bag to find a shoe box. “I’m not a pet.” Neither Yelena nor Natasha looked at her.

    “And I cannot believe you never told me about supernatural beings,” Yelena shot back with a shrug, sticking her hands into the pockets of her jeans and rolling her eyes at her sister. “You would think someone with your knowledge would spot a werewolf when she sees it.”

    “Look,” Natasha said, picking up her glass of water and waving it around as she gesticulated, making it look dangerously like she would spill its contents any second. “In my defense, you and I haven’t exactly spoken to one another in a hot minute. How was I supposed to know your new dog isn’t what it seems? You were certainly hogging all of her close-up cuddling time.”

    “Okay, ouch,” Kate grumbled, staring at the two sisters as they argued back and forth. “I’m really not a dog—”

    “Once a poser, always a poser,” Yelena said, interrupting Kate’s gentle reminder that she was in the room and could very clearly hear them. “You claim to know that werewolves are real, but have you ever actually seen one? If you have, why can you not recognize one?”

    “Actually, not all werewolves look alike—” Kate said, relieved when Natasha set down the glass.

    “Not all werewolves look alike, you little brat,” Natasha said, completely ignoring Kate, who couldn’t stop herself from pouting. “Next time you see a slightly bigger than average wolf, let me know if you think it can talk. Try not to get bit while you’re at it.”

    “Okay, wow, I am definitely bigger than slightly above average,” Kate muttered to herself, finally pulling the shoe box from the paper bag. The sneakers were black and unassuming, with what appeared to be a good sole for hiking through mountains. When she saw the manufacturer, she scowled. Jack Wolfskin. “Very funny...”

    “Anyway,” Natasha said, leaning her left hip against the kitchen island and reclaiming her glass to take a sip. “Bishop.” At the sudden acknowledgment of her existence, Kate almost dropped the right shoe she hoped to slip onto her foot.

    “Yeah?” Kate asked, looking up and glancing back and forth between Yelena’s and Natasha’s faces. Yelena’s hands were still stuffed into the pockets of her jeans, and her expression gave nothing away. Her eyes were fixed somewhere on Kate’s hair or maybe on the couch—Kate couldn’t quite tell.

    Natasha took another sip of water, her left hand settling casually on top of the island. Kate was certain that her friend’s big sister was also carrying a gun somewhere on her person. But while Yelena’s was currently in a visible holster, Natasha’s was not.

    “We have a friend who can take you to Canada,” Natasha said before finishing the water and setting the used glass in the sink. “He can set you up with some cash and a starter pack of essentials. New passport with a new name, that sort of thing. Then you can decide what you want to do and where to go.”

    Kate blinked at her.

    “Really?” she said, holding onto the right boot for dear life. “It’s that easy?”

    “Well, it is going to cost our friend a lot of money to put a rush on it, but we have a few people who owe us, so it’s not that big of an issue.” Natasha crossed her arms over her chest, her left shoulder lifting in a noncommittal shrug. “I can’t guarantee that the destination will have women who you can snuggle up to in your wolf-form, but—”

    “Shut up,” Yelena said at the exact same moment as Kate released a strangled noise from the back of her throat. Quickly, Yelena lifted her right hand to slap Natasha upside the head, but Natasha whacked it to the side before it could make contact.

    “We leave in an hour, so I advise that you find yourself a pair of socks,” Natasha said, turning to the sink and picking up the glass to wash and dry it. Kate’s face burned from the embarrassment, and she didn’t take more than a second to make up her mind. Without being asked twice, Kate disappeared into the bedroom in search of a pair of socks, right boot in hand.

    ##

    December 7th, 2016 | 05:01 P.M.

    Looking behind herself, Kate watched as the small stone cabin grew smaller and smaller until it was no longer visible above the winding road they were descending. Kate would miss this place. She wouldn’t miss being unable to shift back, but she would miss waking up next to Yelena every morning. She wouldn’t miss not being able to take showers, but she would miss playing with Yelena in the snow. She wouldn’t miss being so far away from anything that resembled good music, but she would miss that feeling of being safe and protected.

    Before they left, Yelena trudged to the electrical box to turn off all the switches—just in case one of them needed to come back to these coordinates.

    Turning back to the front, Kate leaned back in the truck’s worn-out leather seats and stared at the car ahead of them. Since Natasha arrived in her own vehicle several days ago, the three of them were making their way down the mountain separately. Natasha led the way as Yelena and Kate followed right behind her.

    “Where are we meeting your contact?” Kate asked, folding her hands in her lap as she glanced at Yelena out of the corner of her eyes. The windows were rolled up due to the icy cold wind and random bouts of snow outside. Yelena had cranked up the heat, turning the inside of the cabin into a warm summer day. Thanks to the cramped space, Kate could hardly ignore the sweet smell of Yelena’s skin and the burgeoning whispers of sweat on her friend’s body.

    “You’ll see,” Yelena said, placing her left elbow on the truck door so she could prop her chin on her palm. With her right hand, she kept the car steady. There was little chance of crashing at the slow pace they were going—unless a second werewolf jumped in front of them.

    Natasha’s truck was pretty much crawling across the snow-covered road, and Kate couldn’t blame her for being cautious.

    With a small huff, Kate stretched her legs and stared at her new shoes. She had to admit that they were practical and comfortable, even though she was ninety-nine percent sure Natasha purchased them to fuck with her. The one percent of doubt was a courtesy Kate afforded the woman due to her friendship with Clint Barton.

    “Will you totally kill me if I tell you I’m already bored?” Kate asked, putting her palms on her knees and arching her back before wriggling back and forth to stretch better. “Also, I feel like this is a great opportunity to let you know that people often think I talk too much.” “I am not surprised,” Yelena replied, glancing at Kate before visibly stifling a smile.

    “Hey!” Kate said, slumping back against her seat and mock-glaring at Yelena. “I spent eighteen days not saying a single, solitary thing to you!”

    “That is not how I remember it.” This time, Yelena couldn’t entirely stop herself from showing her affection for the wolf she’d come to know. Something in Kate’s heart ached—

    life had been so simple less than a day ago.

    With a roll of her eyes, Kate crossed her arms over her chest.

    “I think you mostly remember you monologuing,” Kate grumbled, looking out of the windshield and staring at the back of Natasha’s head. She felt slightly embarrassed about how she acted around Natasha at first. In truth, she couldn’t deny that she let some of her baser wolf-urges get the better of her from time to time.

    “If you are so very bored and want to be useful, you can read Safe Harbor to me,” Yelena said, returning her left hand to the steering wheel so she could gesture at her backpack with the right one. Most of their bags were loaded onto Natasha’s truck, but Yelena’s backpack was nestled into the foot space of Kate’s seat. “We are on page one hundred sixteen.”

    Groaning spectacularly, Kate didn’t move.

    “It’s so sappy,” she whined, feeling warmth spread through her body at the genuine affection she couldn’t curb. Yelena’s enthusiasm for a silly romance novel filled her with so much joy she hardly knew how to contain it. “I couldn’t say no before, but I can now.”

    “You did say no,” Yelena said, returning her chin to her left palm and her right hand to the steering wheel. “I chose to ignore you. Now get the book out and read it to me. I want to know how Reese and Tory’s date will finish.”

    “It’s not even a date,” Kate grumbled, bending forward to unzip the large front pocket of Yelena’s backpack. “It’s a platonic dinner because Tory isn’t ready for anything more yet…” Pulling out the book, she zipped the bag closed with one hand before leaning back. The novel’s aggressively pink and purple coloring stung her eyes, so she quickly opened it up and flipped to the right page. “Do you want me to start from the top?”

    “Sure,” Yelena replied with a soft smile, carefully maneuvering them around a bend in the road.

    “Any further looking at Reese Conlon was exactly what Tory did not want to do,” Kate read aloud, stealing glances overtop the novel every few seconds as she continued to read. After several paragraphs, she lowered the book to her lap.

    “Keep going,” Yelena said, seeming more relaxed than she had since Kate first turned back. “Do you…” Kate started, putting her right thumb on the page before closing the book over it and clutching the spine with her left hand. When Yelena glanced at her, Kate swallowed thickly and shrugged. “Do you think we’ll see each other again after I go to Canada, and you go wherever you and Natasha want to go?”

    Yelena didn’t freeze or show any visible sign of surprise at Kate’s obviously vulnerable question. But she did look at Kate for several seconds—longer than it would have been safe to take her eyes off the road under different circumstances. Then, finally, Yelena looked back at Natasha’s truck.

    “No,” she said honestly, punctuating the simple statement with a soft shake of her head. “No, I don’t think we will.”

    “Oh,” Kate croaked, clutching the spine of the book even harder. “Can I—”

    “No,” Yelena said, shaking her head again. “I’m sorry.”

    “Okay,” Kate said with a small nod, looking down at the vibrant colors of the romance novel. “I get it.” Chewing on her bottom lip, Kate turned her gaze to the side, focusing her suddenly stinging eyes on the stretch of powdery white to her right. The radio crackled in and out of reach of the station it was tuned to, serving as a terrible backdrop for Kate’s grief. “I, uh,” Kate started, consciously loosening her grip on the novel so as not to crush it. “I’ll miss you.” Yelena said nothing, and Kate resigned herself to the truth: Yelena perhaps loved and cherished the wolf, but she did not love and cherish her.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 03:26 A.M.

    They drove through the night, only stopping at roadside gas stations to go to the bathroom. Kate might have expected two fearsome former assassins to avoid any civilization by relieving themselves in the wilderness, but she wasn’t mad that they didn’t. Maybe it had something to do with the weather. As soon as they were off the mountain, the snow and icy wind turned into rain and thunderstorms.

    Kate slept for some of the car ride, twitching and jerking in her seat while Yelena kept her eyes on the road. They picked up the pace on the freeway, requiring much more focus from Yelena. Occasionally, Kate read to her, making comments about the shenanigans of the protagonists here and there. But most of the ride was made in silence. Kate couldn’t help but feel awkward after her honest admission, while Yelena remained unreadable.

    “Once we get there, you will receive a bag with all the essentials,” Yelena said, apropos of nothing, as they continued hurtling across the dark freeway. Natasha’s headlights lit up the road ahead, while Yelena’s lit up the back end of Natasha’s truck. “You will get on the plane. Once you are in Canada, you can decide what to do.”

    “Okay,” Kate confirmed, sitting up and readjusting her grip on Safe Harbor. “Are you saying we’re close?”

    “You’ll see,” Yelena repeated, sending Kate’s annoyance into a fresh stratosphere. She was tired; she was antsy from sitting in the same seat for several hours; she was facing an uncertain future that might result in her untimely death, and no one could help her. And more than anything, her heart bled every time she looked at Yelena and saw her friend stare back at her with dispassionate silence.

    “Look, I am not a fucking spy sent to kill you or rat you out to some bullshit organization I’d never even heard of before you started talking about it,” she snarled, glaring at the side of Yelena’s face. “You don’t have to be a dick. I couldn’t shift back. I couldn’t tell you who I was. If I could have, I would have!” Yelena said nothing. Her hands were curled casually around the steering wheel, and her eyes were fixed ahead. Somehow, that only pissed Kate off more. “If I wanted to hurt you, don’t you think I would have done it days ago?”

    “I don’t know what you would do, Kate Bishop,” Yelena finally replied flatly, her green eyes blinking reflexively as another car passed them by and briefly blinded her with its headlights. “I don’t know you.”

    Kate’s annoyance deflated the very exact moment that her grief resurged with a vengeance. A mere twenty-four hours ago, she and Yelena had been sleeping in the same bed. Yelena had smiled at her, had hugged her, and had called her silly. Now, Kate felt silly in an entirely different way.

    “Fine,” she said, turning back to the window. “Whatever.”

    More silence descended, disrupted only by the music from the radio that had long since stopped crackling. Kate didn’t know this song or this band, but even if she had, she wasn’t sure she would have recognized it.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 05:15 A.M.

    They left the two trucks in a stretch of forest Kate wasn’t sure anyone had visited for centuries. Somehow, a different car was parked only ten minutes away in a different stretch of the same forest, telling Kate that her assumption had been wrong. It didn’t make sense, but it convinced Kate that she knew far less about Natasha and her connections than she had thought.

    While Natasha chose to drive, Yelena sat in the passenger seat, and Kate wedged herself into the back. Out of spite, she chose to sit behind Natasha instead of Yelena, but that had the upsetting side effect of Kate being perfectly able to see Yelena’s impassive side profile. Her nose was pointy, and it always crinkled when she smiled. Though she wasn’t smiling now, Kate lingered on the memories of seeing it.

    “Once we get out of this car, let me do the talking,” Natasha said, signaling to turn off the freeway onto a smaller road that appeared to lead nowhere. Her words sparked a fresh surge of anxiety in Kate’s stomach.

    “Why? Are we dealing with mafia types or something?” Taking hold of Natasha’s seat, Kate leaned forward to establish eye contact with the woman in the rearview mirror. “Should I talk to them once you guys are gone, or should I keep my mouth shut for the whole flight?

    Because I, uh, I’m not great at that.”

    Natasha’s eyes flickered to Kate, then to Yelena as the car continued to drive through two large fields of grass. Before she could say anything, Yelena lazily turned towards her and rolled her eyes, her arms crossed over her chest.

    “Yes, she is always like this,” Yelena said, her voice neutral and calm. Natasha’s mouth moved to form words again, prompting Yelena to cut her off a second time. “Yes, also as a wolf. She barked and whined and howled and whimpered all the time.”

    “I did not whimper,” Kate grumbled, glaring at the side of Yelena’s head with new fervor. “I don’t care if you talk to whoever’s on the plane once I’m gone. I just don’t want to deal with any questions about the company I choose to keep. All of this was a bit last minute, so I probably won’t use this contact again,” Natasha said, ignoring Kate’s grumblings. A small smirk twisted around her lips as she hit Yelena’s left bicep with the back of her hand. “For some reason, Yelena didn’t mention in her letter that she had a pup with her.”

    Kate wanted to protest about her wolf-form again, but then an airplane hangar suddenly came into focus in the distance. It was obviously privately owned since no commercial hangar would ever be that small. Like the new car, this hangar seemed to be in the middle of goddamn nowhere, causing Kate to wonder just how many places like this existed all over the world. Small places, unseen and unknown until you met someone who worked there or who had a connection to it.

    There wasn’t a parking lot, but a car already sat at the side of the road when they pulled up, so Natasha parked behind it. Immediately opening her door, Kate slipped out and did a few jumping jacks to get the excess energy out of her system. The next full moon was eleven days away, yet Kate already felt the effects of its approach, especially now that the new moon was over. Turning her face to the sky, she enjoyed the first few rays of sunshine beaming down on her. For the moment, at least, the rain clouds were gone.

    “Alright, let’s go,” Natasha said, closing her and Kate’s car doors while Yelena slowly unfolded herself from the passenger seat. “You can howl at the moon some other time, Jack.” When Kate looked at her, Natasha winked, and Kate rolled her eyes.

    “You know, the joke’s on you,” Kate said, pointing at her shoes. “These are actually really comfy.”

    This time, when Natasha smiled, it seemed more genuine and much less sassy, like she was truly happy that Kate enjoyed the new shoes. The expression was gone as soon as it appeared, leaving Kate in a state of fresh confusion. She simply couldn’t make heads or tails of either sister.

    Since Kate didn’t have any belongings at the moment, neither Yelena nor Natasha chose to pop the trunk, which made Kate feel a little weird. She’d never flown on a plane without at least some kind of luggage. And yeah, okay, she wouldn’t be taking a commercial airliner or going through customs, but a part of her wished she had something to hold onto. Her eyes dropped to Yelena’s left hand, and she chided herself for the stupid image that flashed through her mind.

    They walked to the hangar, Natasha’s red hair glimmering in the early morning sunshine.

    Although they’d been traveling for hours, neither of the two other women seemed the least bit exhausted from having to drive for that long. Kate napped on and off but also had the benefit of being a werewolf. She had more energy than she knew what to do with.

    Looking around herself, she tried to spot any people walking around outside. Her nose picked up on several human scents in the area, but she couldn’t quite make out where they were coming from. Her nose was good, but not that good, especially when the full moon was still over a week and a half away. There was a small building next to the hangar—maybe a home for whoever worked this place. The big grey sliding door of the hangar was closed.

    “So, uh,” Kate said, trying to keep to Natasha’s rule that she wasn’t supposed to talk while other people were around and making the most of the time she had. “Just to make sure I don’t blow anybody’s cover or anything—how many contacts do you have in the area? Is it, like, the one person who runs the hangar, or is it them and their thirteen employees?”

    “Does this place look like it employs more than a handful of people?” Natasha asked, turning back to Kate for only a moment to flash her a raised eyebrow. “It’ll just be the pilot and the guy who owns the place. Nothing fancy. Quick and easy.”

    Opening her mouth, Kate furrowed her brows.

    “Unless those two are really bad at hygiene and also share clothing with a bunch of people who are also bad at hygiene, I’m pretty sure there’s more than just them in the area,” Kate said with a shrug, lifting her arms and waving them in the air around her. “There are, like, heaps of scents here, dude. And I’m not talking about the dog poo.”

    With her hand outstretched, Natasha froze mid-motion. She was seconds away from clasping the door handle in front of her, which would have allowed them to step inside. Instead, Natasha pulled back her fingers and turned to Kate, her expression suddenly very serious. To Kate’s right, Yelena pulled her gun from her waistband.

    “I swear to god, Bishop, if you’re fucking with me right now,” Natasha whispered, bringing her own hand to the inside of her jacket. Once it shifted, Kate could see the concealed shoulder holster inside.

    “What? Why would I be fucking with you? What are you guys—” Kate stopped, her mind going a million miles a minute as she tried to connect the dots. There were only supposed to be two people, she thought. There were only supposed to be two people, and now there are more than a dozen. Oh, shit. “Do you think it’s—”

    “Get down!” Yelena called out, bodily shoving herself against Kate, who didn’t resist being pushed to the ground. The morning air erupted with strange noises that sounded almost like gunfire but clearly weren’t.

    Something small and feathered hit Natasha’s chest and shoulder just as she ducked and jumped to roll away.

    Eyes widening, Kate watched as people swarmed the fields around them and a door further away slammed open on the hangar. The people were clad in all-black tactical gear, their faces obscured by balaclavas and green goggles. Their weapons were pointed right at them.

    “Natasha,” Yelena gasped, crawling to her sister, who had collapsed onto the gravel path and whose eyes were open and moving frantically as she realized she was temporarily paralyzed. Something like panic crossed Yelena’s features as she touched Natasha’s cheek. “No, no, no.” A dart shot through the air, missing Yelena by less than an inch. When Yelena next looked at Kate, she seemed more afraid than Kate had ever seen her. “Run! Get out of here!”

    At her words, Kate felt a surge of mindless rage flow through her bones.

    “Stay down,” Kate growled, rolling onto her front and letting go of her rational mind.

    The shift was almost instantaneous. Red-hot blood infused her veins and organs as she gave herself over to the beast. Fur sprouted all over her body, whereas the cable knit sweater and the pair of jeans burst at the seams, leaving behind only the gigantic body of Kate’s true werewolf form. Jumping upright, Kate let out a terrifying howl before jumping over Yelena and Natasha and crouching on top of them to shield their bodies from further attacks. In this form, she was taller and much more dangerous than she was during the full moon. Her limbs were more humanoid, and her broad chest was packed tight with thick, protective muscles.

    Two darts hit her in the shoulder, but she merely ripped them out and threw them back, hitting one of the approaching insurgents in the chest and knocking them down. Glancing around herself, she assessed the best course of action. Then, she jumped again, running at full speed at the mercenaries closest to them. She snarled and growled as she sliced them apart with her claws, sparing few thoughts to the lives she was taking.

    The next time she was shot, it wasn’t with darts. The bullets hit her in the side, but she didn’t howl in pain. Already, she could feel her body pushing back against the intrusion of the harmless metal. While she ran and jumped at the next group of primarily male assailants, the bullets dropped from her skin into the grass, her flesh closing before they could shoot her again. There was no silver here. They could not weaken her.

    Still, there seemed to be no end to the assault as more people streamed from a stretch of forest not too far away. Gritting her teeth, she ripped out another man’s throat with her claws before returning to where Yelena kept Natasha’s body covered with her own. Blood dripped onto the gravel pavement as Kate looked down at her companions.

    “Hold,” Kate half-gurgled with her half-human, half-canine vocal cords, crouching down beside them and indicating her broad back. “On.” She pointed again, and Yelena nodded quickly. Grabbing Natasha under her arms, Yelena clambered onto Kate’s back while more darts were fired in their direction. How interesting. They’d had no issues shooting at Kate with their guns.

    Taking care not to drop Yelena and Natasha, Kate quickly threw away another man as she ran towards their car at full speed. Three more darts embedded themselves in her leg, but she ignored them. Her werewolf blood would protect her; her werewolf blood would protect them all.

    Someone was standing guard at the car, but when Kate roared in his face, the guy dropped his weapon and ran. Maybe he’d seen the way she made quick work of his friends, or maybe he was the only person in the group who had any common sense. At any rate, Kate didn’t begrudge him his escape, and she did not follow him.

    Ripping open the back door, Kate softly laid Natasha into the backseat while Yelena unlocked the car and jumped into the front. Closing the back door, Kate turned back to the approaching assailants and made to return to the fight. She was almost in motion when Yelena rolled down her window and revved the engine.

    “You are going with us!” she yelled, her green eyes sharp and leaving no room for arguments. “Now, Lucky!”

    The tires whipped up gravel as she set it in reverse and turned before driving down the road they’d first come here from. Looking back at the approaching forces, Kate turned and followed the car, thankful for her strong and powerful legs. She barely felt the tranquilizer. They’d almost made it back to the freeway when a black SUV caught up to them. While Yelena continued to drive, Kate turned and stretched out her arms, snarling angrily at the dark-tinted glass of the vehicle. The SUV crashed right into her, pushing her back several meters as she held on, her back claws digging into the gravel. Then, with all of her strength, Kate gripped the underside of the front and threw it upwards with all her might. The car flipped, its wheels rolling ineffectually as it landed on its head. Shoving it backward and to the side to block the road, Kate turned and continued to follow Yelena and Natasha in the car. Once they were on the freeway, they would figure out what to do or where to go.

    But one thing was certain: Kate was not going to Canada today.
