    we are back in Yelena's head 😈 See the end of the chapter for more notes December 8th, 2016 | 05:45 A.M.

    Yelena’s hands were clenched around the steering wheel; Yelena’s thighs and arms were tight with tension; Yelena’s face was marred with the terror of narrowly escaping the Red Room. And Natasha? Natasha was awake but immobilized and unable to talk about what had just happened—at least for the moment.

    The freeway was deserted. The only car on the road was the one Yelena was sure was compromised. To her right, a giant black wolf ran through the empty fields of whatever state they were currently in. The wolf was covered in blood from head to toe, but it didn’t seem hurt. While this did give her some comfort, it wasn’t much. More than anything, she tried not to think about the giant trail they were leaving behind.

    Yelena kept her eyes on the rearview mirror more than would have been strictly necessary under different circumstances, but she couldn’t see a Red Room vehicle anywhere behind them. The sky was clear, too, so she assumed the Red Room was regrouping and cleaning up the mess at the hangar.

    Natasha and Yelena had left the car by the side of the road like a bunch of reckless idiots, so the chances of a tracker were more than likely. They desperately needed to get rid of this mode of transportation and find a new one. Unfortunately, for that to happen, she would need to stop somewhere to check the map and find the closest town. Natasha’s body was hard at work to rid itself of the tranquilizer explicitly designed to knock out widows, but it would take her time to recover. Their special blend of knock-off super soldier serum didn’t react well to certain chemicals, making it easy to incapacitate them if caught off-guard. Her hands tightened further on the steering wheel and her jaw clenched. This time, Dreykov hadn’t sent another widow to take them on. This time, he’d used soldiers. He wanted them alive.

    Checking the clock on the dashboard, she saw they’d been driving for nearly half an hour. Kate’s hulking wolf body hadn’t slowed down, causing Yelena to wonder just how long her strange companion could keep this up. She was going as fast as she could on this stretch of freeway without it being too dangerous, and Kate’s hulking form was keeping up with seemingly no problem whatsoever. That should have given her more pause than it did. It proved something that Yelena had realized the second her companion turned back into a human—Kate was dangerous. If she ever decided that Yelena and Natasha were a threat to her, she could do serious damage. Kate’s interactions with Dreykov’s soldiers made that abundantly clear.

    Another glance at the rearview mirror revealed nothing behind them on the road, but Yelena saw that Natasha had rolled onto her back. When Kate’s gigantic, bloody paws had placed Natasha in the backseat, she’d taken care to put her on her side in an almost crude rendition of the recovery position. There were red and brown prints on Natasha’s body from where Kate had touched her. Thanks to the pandemonium around them, Yelena barely remembered the gentle nature of those claws.

    “Feeling better?” Yelena asked, ignoring the tremor in her own voice. Natasha groaned in response, her throat clicking wetly as she did her best to swallow. “We have been on the road for thirty-three minutes. The car is probably bugged. Kate Bishop is running like the wind, and there are no visible enemies on the horizon.” Natasha groaned again, her hand twitching on her stomach in what Yelena assumed was an attempt at a thumbs up. “I need you to get the map from my backpack once you can move. And I need you to put in a bit more effort.”

    Natasha moved her hand again, this time in a gesture that Yelena chose not to interpret as a middle finger.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 06:59 A.M.

    A little over an hour after Yelena first peeled out of the ambush, Natasha regained full movement of her legs and arms. Relief swept through Yelena as she watched her sister lean between the space of the two front seats to grab Yelena’s backpack.

    “Don’t get blood on Safe Harbor,” Yelena said as she glanced at the wolf that continued to keep pace. A bitterness – feeling an awful lot like guilt – swept through her as she considered what physical toll this might have on Kate. The wolf still hadn’t slowed down at all, but Yelena couldn’t imagine that it would be able to run forever. “We need to ditch the car.” “No shit,” Natasha replied, rummaging around in Yelena’s backpack until she located the map. Shaking her head, she moved her jaw around. “Bastards,” she muttered to herself as she flexed the fingers of her right hand before wiggling them back and forth.

    “Tingles?” Yelena asked, glancing at the wolf again.

    “Every fricking time,” Natasha muttered, unfurling the map. “Alright, so…” She turned the big piece of paper a little sideways. “The hangar is down here.” She dropped it to her lap to establish eye contact with Yelena. “I trust you’ve been breaking the speed limit since Kate got us out of there.”

    “Yes,” Yelena confirmed, returning her attention to the road. “Now it has been exactly seventy-six minutes since we left the hangar, and we have been going at approximately ninety miles per hour, according to this.” She tapped the glass that covered the speedometer before returning her hand to the steering wheel. Again, she glanced at the wolf. “We should hurry.”

    Natasha followed her line of sight.

    “You know, we can let her in here,” Natasha said softly, keeping the map stationary while she looked back at Yelena. “If this car is bugged and we’re being tracked, it doesn’t matter if we stop here or twenty minutes from now. We need to switch cars one way or the other, and it’ll be less conspicuous if we don’t have a big wolf following us.”

    Something gripped Yelena’s heart and squeezed as she imagined the powerful animal body returning to its soft and unfamiliar human form. Seeing Kate grow gigantic, terrifying, and deadly was a comfort Yelena had hardly ever felt. When Kate stood over them and snarled at the soldiers, Yelena’s whole body instantly relaxed because she knew without a shadow of a doubt that she and Natasha were safe.

    “Okay,” Yelena confirmed, reaching towards the center console to press the button for the passenger side window. Keeping her eyes trained on the road, Yelena shouted. “Kate Bishop! Slow down and get in the car!” Chancing a glance to the right, Yelena saw that the big wolf was looking at her, prompting Yelena to let go of the gas and very gently engage the brakes. Pulling over carefully so the wolf would have time to move out of the way, Yelena stopped the car shortly after.

    “She will be naked,” Yelena said after pushing the button for the passenger side window a second time to roll it back up. “And covered in blood, most likely.” The wolf was bigger than the car, but Yelena recognized that its form had changed since the ambush. It looked much less humanoid than it did before.

    “So? Get her a blanket from the trunk,” Natasha said without looking away from the map.

    Rolling her eyes, Yelena got out of the car and rounded it. The panting wolf shrunk to its previous Lucky-size, then trotted over to Yelena and looked up at her with its big, ocean-blue eyes. Fresh pain surged through Yelena as she recognized the kinship in them—the kinship she missed every time she looked at Kate Bishop’s all too human face.

    “Shift back,” Yelena ordered, popping the trunk to remove a blanket from the right side. “The Red Room did not use silver on you.” Closing the lid with a quick snick, Yelena watched as Lucky turned back into Kate Bishop, who coughed into her own arm when she was back upright. This transformation seemed much less painful and aggressive than the one Yelena witnessed just a day ago. “We do not have time for you to get dressed.” She held the blanket out to Kate, who seemed unperturbed by the fact that she was naked and much more concerned with wiping crusty blood from her cheek.

    “My mouth tastes like ass,” Kate grumbled, ignoring the blanket as she spat on the ground. “Note to self: Never use teeth to bite humans.” She spat again, shivering from head to toe at the taste of killing a dozen people.

    “You will get used to it,” Yelena said, opening the trunk again, and retrieving a water bottle. “Time to rinse those teeth.” Holding out the bottle, Yelena watched as Kate accepted it gratefully and took two steps away from her to clean her mouth, face, and hands. With Kate’s back to her, Yelena allowed herself a moment to appreciate that Kate Bishop’s body showed exactly zero new wounds. Yelena had seen bullets rip into Kate’s flesh not two hours ago, but none of those marks remained. There were scars on Kate’s body, but they looked much older. As she observed the gash that turned Kate Bishop into a werewolf in the first place, Yelena spoke without giving it any conscious thought. “Thank you.”

    “Hm?” Kate asked, keeping her left hand on her right forearm mid-scrub as she looked at Yelena over her shoulder.

    “I said thank you,” Yelena repeated, closing the trunk a second time to give herself something to do.

    “Oh,” Kate replied, her tone of voice drawing Yelena’s eyes. Kate’s mouth was open as though unsure how to reply. The rest of her expression, Yelena was finding hard to decipher. She was trained in micro-expressions, but that training only got her so far.

    “Oh? Are you surprised that I am grateful to be alive?” Yelena was very conscious of the fact that they were standing out in the open at the side of the freeway. A car could pass by at any moment and wonder about the naked, blood-covered woman standing in the early morning sun. That wonder might turn to concern and then into a manhunt by the very eager local police force. And still, Yelena did not hurry them along.

    “No, I guess not,” Kate said, returning her gaze to her arm as her fingers continued to wash away some of the evidence of what happened. “But you don’t have to thank me.” Screwing the cap on tight, Kate turned back around and stepped up to Yelena to take the blanket.

    Yelena did not let it go.

    “The taste of blood in your mouth is just the beginning,” Yelena whispered, looking up into Kate’s blue eyes. Briefly, she allowed herself a moment of imagining that this was Lucky and that Lucky would not fear her. “I have killed thousands. The trail of blood that follows me, you could wrap around the entire world.” She leaned even closer until their noses were almost touching. “You only just started painting your own trail… How far will it reach if you continue to travel with us?”

    Kate’s hand tightened on the blanket, her back and shoulders straightening as she stared at Yelena.

    “I have nowhere else to go,” Kate said, her jaw jutting forward as she considered Yelena’s half-hostile, half-concerned words. “I don’t regret saving you and Natasha.” She furrowed her brows before lightly nudging Yelena’s forehead with her own. “Why do you want me to?” Kate’s forehead was wet from her recent wash, and yet her skin continued to be as warm as a radiator on a snowy winter morning. The confusion in her tone of voice shot straight to Yelena’s heart as those eyes continued to stare at her.

    “Put on the blanket and get in the car,” Yelena said, pulling herself away and taking the water bottle from Kate. Opening the trunk for a third time, Yelena threw the used water bottle into it before retrieving three fresh ones.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 07:16 A.M.

    To Yelena’s great annoyance, Kate Bishop chose to sit in the passenger seat instead of wedging herself into the back with Natasha. Yet, as Natasha and Yelena continued to speak about where to go from here, Kate stayed uncharacteristically quiet, her eyes moving along the shrubbery and dirt at the side of the freeway.

    “There’s a gas station not too far from here. We could borrow a new car there,” Natasha mumbled, her pointer finger moving along the map. “Plus, they probably have a bathroom we can use.” She glanced at the blood that remained on Kate’s hair and neck.

    “If there are any cars to be borrowed at this time of day—in the middle of nowhere,” Yelena replied, keeping her attention as far away from Kate’s unreadable expression as possible. “Plus, there are too many cameras.”

    “Like it makes a difference if they’re tracking us,” Natasha said before ceding the point at another glance at their third wheel. “Although even a regular clerk might be worried about the bloody woman in a blanket wandering inside.”

    “If there’s a creek, I can wash off,” Kate said, her mouth barely moving.

    Natasha furrowed her brows.

    “And then we have you shake off the excess moisture on this fine upholstery? I don’t think so,” Natasha said, her focus never shifting from Kate’s face. When there was no reply, Natasha made eye contact with Yelena in the rearview mirror. “I guess no gas station. Well, there’s a town about an hour away if we keep going this way and don’t slow down.”

    “How big is this town?” Yelena asked, tamping down her mounting worries about Kate’s demeanor.

    “Not very. But probably big enough to hide a slightly dented car at the side of the road without anybody asking questions about it right away.” Natasha leaned back in her seat, running a shaky hand through her hair. Ever since Kate entered the car, Natasha had stopped the flexing of her aching muscles and the adjusting of her jaw, but she couldn’t quite hide the unsteadiness of her limbs.

    “Won’t they be waiting for us?” Kate asked quietly, keeping her arms crossed over her chest and her face turned towards the window. Her bare feet poked out at the bottom of the blanket —her toes were covered in grass, dirt, and dried blood. Yelena remembered the feeling, just as she remembered her first kill and the first time she tasted someone else’s blood in her mouth.

    “It’s possible,” Yelena replied in her regular tone of voice, feeling the strangest urge to brush a wayward lock of dark hair from Kate’s cheek if only to get a smile out of her. “They could be predicting our planned exit strategy based on our movements. But as long as we are in this car, we do not have a lot of options. We can’t walk to our next destination. Very impractical.” Kate shrugged, the blanket shifting back and forth with the motion.

    “Why?” she asked, her blue eyes half-lidded as they followed the countryside whizzing past the window. “You won’t even have to walk. I can carry both of you.” She shrugged again, lifting only her left shoulder this time. “If you have a rope, feel free to tie your bags to my back, too. No big deal.”

    Pressing her lips together, Yelena looked at Natasha. Her sister raised her brows, while Yelena kept her expression carefully neutral. Finally, Natasha spoke up: “You kept pace with a car going ninety for over an hour. Aren’t you tired?” She neatly folded the map so she could return it to Yelena’s backpack.

    “Not really,” Kate said, leaning her forehead against the glass, her stare growing even more vacant. “Humans have great stamina. That’s part of what made them so deadly to prehistoric animals and what allowed them to evolve as quickly and efficiently as they have.” Kate’s words sounded rehearsed, like she was reading from a script, her voice devoid of any emotion. “From this, it is easily inferred that a werewolf—a hunter of humans—needs to be a hundred times faster, a hundred times more tenacious, and a hundred times more savage.”

    “I’m shaking in my boots as we speak,” Natasha said, zipping up Yelena’s backpack and lifting it onto the seat next to her. “We have a flesh and blood apex predator in our midst, Yelena. How exciting.” Yelena knew that smirk—years ago, when they’d been kids, Yelena had seen it often enough. “Let’s play hide and seek in town with the Red Room. How about it, Jack? If you’re a hundred times more savage than they are… should be a piece of cake.” Kate’s jaw tightened but she didn’t look away from the shrubbery. “The shoes exploded when I transformed,” she said, tugging the blanket tighter around her. “You can stop calling me by that stupid nickname.”

    “I don’t know.” Natasha leaned forward, poking her head in between Yelena’s stoic face and Kate’s obvious distress. “I think I like it too much to give it up.” Her smirk turned into a genuine smile, which never boded well for Yelena. “Besides, don’t you think Yelena Wolfskin has a beautiful ring to it? So melodic; so poetic. I can only imagine the wedding you’ll have in the woods.”

    “The darts have turned her brain to mush,” Yelena said, reaching up blindly with her hand and catching the side of Natasha’s left cheek, giving her just enough leverage to push her sister back. “It is so sad to see.” Shaking her head, Yelena looked at Kate’s glum expression, deeply perturbed by the lack of reaction to Natasha’s outlandish teasing. Unbidden, Yelena thought of when she’d originally come face to face with Lucky’s trembling teeth. After the wolf woke up for the first time, Yelena had seen so much pain in those animal eyes. With Kate’s very human suffering, she felt more unsure of herself than she did in the face of terrified aggression. “Look up the nearest creak so Kate Bishop can take a bath.”

    “I don’t need to look it up; I already know,” Natasha said, leaning back in her seat. “There’s a small road that leads to one not too far away. We can plan our next steps after we unload this piece of junk next to it.”

    “It is not very nice to refer to yourself in such ugly terms,” Yelena said, ignoring how Natasha rolled her eyes and kicked the back of her seat. Yelena’s attention was on the road ahead and the horribly silent creature to her right.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 08:51 A.M.

    Lucky’s fur was as soft as Yelena remembered, although she couldn’t quite get used to gripping it so tightly with both hands. It was foolish to wonder if they were hurting Kate— she’d been shot right in front of them with no lingering repercussions—yet Yelena couldn’t help herself. They’d been riding on the wolf’s broad back for over an hour, running in the opposite direction to the one the Red Room would likely suspect. They traveled the creek’s watery bed for a while, hoping to disguise their initial direction before they veered off and took a different trail.

    The forest all around them was tall but not densely populated with bushes, allowing for relatively easy and unperturbed travel. Behind Yelena and Natasha, most of their luggage was tied to Kate’s sides, although they’d been forced to leave behind a bag or two of pre-cooked meals. The cooler with cooked rabbit from her snares rattled every time Kate jumped over an obstacle in the forest, reminding Yelena of their shared time at the cabin.

    While they weren’t going as fast as they had been in the car, Yelena felt a whole lot better about this mode of transportation. Out here, they were as safe as they would ever be. Before they’d strapped the bags to Kate’s huge body, they’d searched them for any hidden trackers with a detector that Natasha brought with her wherever she went. Several of them now lay crumpled and stomped beneath the trunk of their former car.

    “There’s a motel not too far away,” Natasha said, reaching forward to gently scratch Lucky’s —Yelena stopped herself. Natasha scratched Kate’s ear, not Lucky’s. “How do we feel about a continental breakfast?” When Kate let out a rumble of approval, Yelena couldn’t stop her chest from feeling slightly warm at the sweet response. “Alright, then! Onwards, noble steed!”

    “I hate you so much,” Yelena muttered, poking Natasha’s cheek.

    The motel was surprisingly populated considering its location, but a quick survey of the surroundings didn’t reveal any Red Room activity—at least none that could be easily detected. Getting a room wasn’t much of a hassle either, but Yelena wished her first reaction didn’t have to be one of annoyance when she saw a double bed in the corner and a single bed a few feet away from it.

    “I call first shower,” Kate said as she walked inside and dumped three bags by the door. At the moment, she was wedged into shoes that were too small for her feet, a pair of jeans that reached above her ankles, and a green sweater that stretched across her wide shoulders and couldn’t quite hide her well-defined musculature.

    “Mother nature not enough for you?” Natasha asked, plonking her butt down on the single bed and toeing off her sneakers. A second later, she was horizontal with her hands settled behind her head. Crossing her feet at her ankles, Natasha smirked as Yelena scowled at her and closed the motel room door.

    “I don’t know if you know this, but creek water isn’t secretly mixed with soap,” Kate said, stretching her arms as she slowly made her way towards the adjoining bathroom. “If either of you needs to pee, I’d suggest doing that now. I can’t promise that I’ll be out in twenty minutes.”

    “High-maintenance puppy,” Natasha said, closing her eyes and wriggling the fingers of her left hand at Kate. “Yelena and I are big girls. We can hold it if we need to. Go on.” Her big toes moved back and forth to a tune that wasn’t anywhere but in Natasha’s own head. “We won’t get breakfast without you, so I’d appreciate it if you didn’t dawdle.”

    Kate threw up her thumb without a verbal reply before disappearing behind the bathroom door. The inbuilt lock snapped into place with a loud slap of metal on metal, prompting Yelena to leave her post by the door so she could place her backpack on the double bed in the corner. Unzipping the main compartment, Yelena checked that Safe Harbor was unharmed and unblemished.

    “You are not sleeping there,” Yelena said to her sister after sitting down on the big mattress in front of her and pulling out the book for closer inspection. “Kate Bishop will take the single, and you can share with me.”

    Natasha lolled her head to the side to lazily glare at Yelena.

    “You think that’s the best idea?” she asked, indicating towards the bathroom door with her chin. “She was damn near catatonic in the car.”

    “Don’t be so dramatic,” Yelena replied, stroking her fingers along Safe Harbor’s technicolor cover before turning it over. Next, she looked at the sides, happy to see that no harm had come upon the novel. The only blemishes were teeth marks—memories of Lucky gnawing at the book to annoy her. She hated to see that youthful joy turned into bitter resentment.

    “You need to keep an eye on her,” Natasha said quietly, sitting up and pulling her feet towards herself to sit cross-legged on the bed. Her expression was suddenly serious. “You brought her into this, whether you meant to or not. She’s your responsibility.”

    The water turned on in the bathroom, causing the pipes in the walls to groan under the strain. “We are not keeping her,” Yelena said, flipping to the page where Kate stopped reading earlier.

    “Yes, we are,” Natasha retorted, slipping off the bed. “None of my contacts are safe right now. If we aren’t careful, we’ll be caught.” She snagged the book from Yelena’s hands and pursed her lips as she looked down at the pages. “Tory leaned forward with her breasts just out of reach of Reese’s lips.” She blinked, her eyes flitting further along the paragraph. “They’re having a lot more fun than we are.”

    “We still cannot keep her with us,” Yelena said, carefully removing Safe Harbor from Natasha’s unappreciative fingers. Closing it back up, Yelena returned it to her backpack, then crossed her arms over her chest.

    “Hate to break it to you, sis,” Natasha said, placing her hands on her waist and moving her shoulders in a shrug. “That kid will die out there if we don’t help her. Those hunters she was talking about aren’t a joke. Clint and I came up against hunters a couple of times over the last few years. They’re cold-blooded. I’m surprised her mom didn’t gut her the second she found out about the whole werewolf transformation thing.”

    Without saying a word, Yelena slumped back onto the double bed. As the water continued to shoot through the bellowing pipes, she imagined Kate frantically scrubbing at blood that wasn’t there. What do you really think about all of this, Kate Bishop, she thought, her mind wandering to her conversation with Kate the day before. Turning her face towards the bathroom door, Yelena examined the knowledge that Kate was raised to be a hunter of supernatural things. Yelena wasn’t sure what training of that nature entailed, but she couldn’t imagine that it didn’t involve killing. And yet…

    “Whatever you say. I still will not be sharing a bed with her,” Yelena said, staring up at Natasha’s thoughtful face. The expression vanished, only to be replaced by a look of confused amusement.

    “It’s not like it’d be the first time,” Natasha pointed out with a shrug. “That girl slept on top of you for days, right? Must I remind you that I was there when she sat by your bedside and whined like a kicked puppy over me taking her spot?”

    “That was different,” Yelena said, vividly reliving the comfort of Lucky’s big, warm body weighing her down. She’d loved running her fingers through the thick, soft fur and feeling the huffing breaths of the wolf on her neck as it settled in. She could not let go of the certainty she’d lost, the certainty of being seen by an animal. Not for who she had been but for who she was now. “It is not the same.”

    “Yes, I know what the word different means,” Natasha said with no heat to her teasing, her amusement vanishing behind her concern. Without saying anything else on the matter, Natasha offered her hand to Yelena, who took it without question. Allowing herself to be pulled back into a sitting position, Yelena leaned her forehead against Natasha’s stomach and closed her eyes. “I think it’s time we got some breakfast into you.” Gently, Natasha ran her fingers through Yelena’s hair in a rare physical display of affection. “Some overcooked eggs… greasy bacon. Waffles doused in syrup that’s way too sweet. How does that sound, kiddo?”

    “Shut up,” Yelena grumbled, making no move to disentangle herself from Natasha’s gentle scratches along her scalp.

    ##

    December 8th, 2016 | 10:27 A.M.

    While their breakfast was exactly as terribly prepared as Natasha had predicted, Yelena found a certain level of comfort in it all the same. Until recently, she’d lived with the pre-portioned nutrient slop and cement-like energy bars of the Red Room. After that, she mostly consumed microwave meals, dried fruits, and hunted rabbits she seasoned and cooked herself. In comparison, slightly overcooked bacon and eggs were heavenly pleasures she was all too happy to experience, especially because no one could stop her from drowning it all in salt and syrup from the condiment bar.

    While Natasha eyed Yelena’s plate with some level of disgust, Kate Bishop shoved spoonful after spoonful of egg into her mouth and didn’t seem to care about anything else. A large vat of scrambled eggs that was refilled every so often sat on the buffet table, steam rising from the pot whenever someone forgot to close it back up. Currently, it was almost empty since Kate had returned to it three times already to fill her plate up to the brim. Meanwhile, Natasha had made good on her promise of waffles, though she was somewhat more restrained with the syrup.

    “I keep expecting your stomach to blow up in our faces, but it seems to be holding strong,” Natasha said, picking up her black cup of coffee before placing her elbows on either side of her plate. The room around them wasn’t bustling by any means, but they weren’t alone. Two tables over, a woman bottle-fed her baby. Somehow, Yelena kept expecting her to stand up and transform into an agent of the Red Room. “I guess running made you pretty hungry.”

    “Starving,” Kate said through a mouthful of egg, grabbing her cup of orange juice to wash them down. “I burn a lot of calories as a, uh—” She glanced at the same woman as Yelena had only seconds earlier, then shrugged.

    “As a professional dog wrangler?” Natasha whispered before sipping from her coffee and making a face. “Damn, this brew is gross.” Putting down the cup, Natasha grabbed five sugar packets from the table center. “It’s like they strained this through a pile of used socks.” “Lovely,” Yelena said, eyeing her own coffee and fervently hoping the milk and sugar she’d added to hers would save her from a similar taste explosion. “Anyway,” she continued, bringing her attention to Kate’s blue eyes. “How many calories does it take to feed you?” Kate scooped more eggs onto her fork before giving Yelena a shrug.

    “I’ve never, like, counted. I just eat whenever I’m hungry… which is pretty much all the time.” She continued shoveling food into her mouth, looking halfway apologetic. Then, through another mouthful, she said: “My mom used to say that she was glad my dad’s parents invested so much money in the right stocks and bonds, or I’d make her bankrupt within a year.”

    A glimmer of something soft and precious flickered across her face, but it was gone as soon as Yelena noticed it, replaced with the doom and gloom of the last few hours. Looking away, Kate set down her fork and swallowed what was left in her mouth. Then, she wiped the back of her hand across her face and got to her feet.

    “I’ll head back to the room,” she said without waiting for a reply. A moment later, she was gone, and Yelena couldn’t help but follow her retreating form across the room with her eyes. Those slumped shoulders did something to Yelena she didn’t care to investigate as she went back to making herself a bacon and egg sandwich with a fluffy but slightly dented breakfast roll.

    Natasha hummed thoughtfully as she tried another sip of her coffee.

    “Still gross,” she mumbled before setting it back on the table and abandoning it for good. Resting her chin on her hand, Natasha looked at her own plate and blew out a tired breath. At the sight of her, Yelena knew instantly what was coming.

    “No,” Yelena said, scowling at Natasha.

    “No?” Natasha raised her eyebrow, a mischievous smile sliding onto her lips. “Why not?”

    Temporarily abandoning her sandwich-making efforts, Yelena cleaned her hands on a napkin before crossing her arms over her chest. “Because,” she said, knowing full well that Natasha knew all of the reasons why this was a bad idea.

    “So?” Natasha said with half a shrug. “Could be fun.”

    “No,” Yelena repeated, smoothing her scowl back into a neutral expression.

    “Okay.” Natasha picked up her fork and used the side to cut off the edge of the waffle before spearing it and bringing it to her mouth. Before she snagged it with her teeth, she looked at Yelena and said: “What do you want to do, then?”

    Yelena’s lips parted right away to give a reply, but then she stopped herself. When she fled from the Red Room, she expected Natasha to arrive in a big jet with the full backing of the Avengers and the US American government behind her. But that didn’t happen. Instead, Natasha arrived in a beaten-up truck, and the Avengers were in disarray. Not only would they have to dodge the Red Room, but the American government as well, and they had to be extremely selective about where to stay and who to trust.

    Returning her gaze to her half-assembled sandwich, Yelena felt the weight of the world press down on her and longed desperately for the comfort of a big wolf’s head settling on her lap. Closing her eyes, she unfurled her crossed arms and placed them on the table before bringing her right hand to her face.

    In the darkness of her mind, she replayed the same scene over and over again. Kate Bishop falling on all fours and giving herself over to her wolf to protect them. Kate Bishop shifting into a gigantic beast and abandoning her humanity to keep them safe. Kate Bishop tearing soldiers apart before carrying them to safety. Kate Bishop revealing herself to dozens of human eyes despite her fear of discovery.

    I am you. As much as you are me, she thought, returning to that fresh winter morning when she’d looked up at the sky and thought about her sister. Now, with that safe haven so far away but with Natasha by her side, she couldn’t help but ache. I am upside down again. Leaning back in her chair, she dropped her hand and looked at Natasha, whose attention seemed solely on her waffle. How about you, Kate Bishop?

    “Fine,” Yelena said as the last of her resistance drained from her bones and bled all over the floor.

    “Yeah?” Natasha replied, never looking away from the pool of syrup on her plate.

    “Yes,” Yelena confirmed, resuming her sandwich prep. “Let’s help Kate Bishop.”

    ##

    December 9th, 2016 | 4:05 A.M.

    The car was quiet. The road ahead was empty.

    For the moment, Yelena was the only one who wasn’t asleep. Behind her, Kate Bishop’s face was mushed against the car window, her mouth open and her chin showing signs of drool.

    While Yelena enjoyed the feeling of early morning air caressing her skin through the partially open window, Natasha’s head rested on the backpack she clung to. She didn’t snore, but soft, rhythmic breathing noises occasionally eclipsed the almost silent classical music streaming from the car radio.

    Earlier, when Yelena and Natasha returned to the room after breakfast, Kate Bishop was fast- asleep on the left side of the double bed. She was still wearing her shoes, her feet hanging off the end of the bed. Yelena’s backpack was held loosely within Kate’s grasp while Kate’s face was wedged against its side, shielded from the light in the room.

    The girl didn’t wake up, not even when Yelena took off the too-tight shoes on Kate’s feet before removing her bag and slowly pulling Kate backward until she was completely on the bed. After that, Yelena offered to take first watch, while Natasha settled on the single bed and got some much-needed rest.

    For a few minutes, Yelena stayed by the front door.

    Sitting on the chair placed next to the window overlooking the motel’s parking lot, she counted cars and people from her vantage point. With her trained eye, she tried to assess the best vehicle to steal. They couldn’t take any of the newer models, or else the inbuilt GPS feature would make them horrendously easy to find. Of course, those could be disabled, but they’d have to get away far enough to do that without the people who owned it catching on and calling the cops. Better just to take an old car that didn’t have any fancy gadgets, like the truck that carried Yelena all the way up and down the mountain.

    Once her spying was complete, Yelena returned to the double bed and her backpack leaning against it. Plucking Safe Harbor out of its depths, Yelena took off her shoes before carefully sitting on the unoccupied side of the mattress. With a quick glance to make sure Kate was still asleep, Yelena returned to the first page of the novel. Provincetown’s newest deputy sheriff pulled her cruiser to a stop in the parking lot overlooking Herring Cove. Hours prior, Kate Bishop had been reading some of the later chapters to her, and even though Yelena knew that Kate didn’t much care for the story, reading on without her felt… inexplicably wrong.

    While she continued to read about Reese Conlon’s first day, Yelena couldn’t help but focus on the solid presence of the body asleep to her right. Looking over at Kate’s back, Yelena wondered why this felt so different. When it had just been her and Lucky, she had felt at ease. She had felt seen and understood by the animal across from her. Knowing the truth, she couldn’t reconcile those feelings with her fear of what another human being was capable of. Now that they were moving again, Yelena felt a little less restless. At four in the morning, with no one around but the sounds of the night and her sleeping companions, she knew she was as safe as she was ever going to be. For the moment, she had a simple goal in mind, which was to get as far away from the Red Room as possible. There was no way to know how the Red Room tracked them down this time. It could have been Natasha’s contact; it could have been an unsecure phone line; it could have been the car that another contact left for them, or it could have been that very contact. Yelena had even doubted Kate Bishop’s story right up until Kate turned into a wolf to protect them. For those few harrowing seconds, Yelena expected her fragile trust to be broken yet again.

    Lifting her eyes to the rearview mirror, Yelena let out a slow breath. What are you looking at, she thought before returning her attention to the road.

    ##

    December 9th, 2016 | 2:17 P.M.

    With a frown, Yelena brought herself to wakefulness. Her forehead felt unpleasantly cold from leaning against the passenger side window for far too long. Looking around herself, she was surprised to see that they were stationary. She must have been a lot more exhausted than she realized if she hadn’t heard the door of the car open and close.

    “Where are we?” Yelena asked, sitting up and rubbing at the skin above her right eyebrow. Natasha was nowhere to be seen, but Kate was spread out across the backseat with her left arm covering her eyes.

    “I’m not sure. Some tiny town somewhere,” Kate replied, scratching her stomach where the bottom of her sweater rode up. She didn’t remove her arm, and she didn’t look at Yelena.

    “Natasha’s buying new supplies. She’ll be back soon, I guess.”

    Looking away from Kate, Yelena scanned their surroundings instead. They were parked away from any prying eyes in an area that was densely populated enough to make a clean extraction difficult but not enough to draw much attention from onlookers. This was exactly the kind of place Yelena would have picked if it had been up to her.

    Satisfied with their security, Yelena returned her attention to Kate. “Do you need more sleep?” Kate had slept for most of the previous day. Yelena expected her to be more of a nuisance on the drive away from the motel, but Kate fell back asleep pretty much as soon as they were on the road. She even slept through Yelena and Natasha switching driving-duty so Yelena could get some rest, too. “Did you run too much?”

    “No,” Kate said, finally dropping her arm and sitting up. “Maybe. Could be the bullets.”

    “You said they did not bother you.” Yelena watched as Kate pulled her right knee towards herself so she could settle her cheek against it. To the untrained eye, Kate Bishop looked to be at the peak of health, but Yelena wasn’t so easily fooled. “Does it take conscious effort to heal the wounds before someone can see them?”

    “No, not really,” Kate replied, keeping her eyes on her socks. The too-small shoes she borrowed from Natasha lay discarded on the floor of the car. Something twitched within Yelena—a desire to tell Kate to put the shoes back on just in case they needed to flee. She pushed the surge of protectiveness down. It was stupid—Kate Bishop didn’t need shoes to run faster than the Red Room. “I don’t know why I’ve been feeling so tired.”

    “Have you ever been shot multiple times aside from that night you ran into my truck?”

    Yelena asked, her eyes catching movement just behind Kate’s shoulders — a man walking by on the other side of the road; his attention glued to his phone screen.

    “No?” Kate said, gripping her leg as she wriggled her toes. “I’ve been really good about keeping my head down.”

    “While I find this hard to believe, I suppose it is possible that the people around you were merely very stupid and unobservant,” Yelena said, returning her gaze to Kate’s face once the man was out of sight. Even though Yelena had just insulted her friends and family, Kate’s lips twitched into a small smile. “I would not worry so much about your body’s reaction to severe trauma.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said with a small nod. “Thanks.”

    Turning back to the front, Yelena crossed her arms over her chest and ignored the warmth crawling through her veins.

    ##

    December 9th, 2016 | 3:02 P.M.

    Natasha returned with four grocery bags, one of which was made oddly rectangular by what was shoved inside. After putting three bags into the trunk, Natasha climbed into the driver’s seat and pushed the fourth bag into Kate’s hands. “Try not to explode these ones,” Natasha said, buckling herself in while Kate Bishop unearthed a shoe box. “Next time, take the extra three seconds to slip them off. Preferably in the getaway car.”

    Raising her left eyebrow, Yelena looked first at Natasha and then at the logo on the box. “You think you’re so funny,” Kate grumbled, pulling her brand-new Jack Wolfskin boots from the confines of the cardboard and tissue paper.

    “Funny and generous,” Natasha replied, facing the road so she could turn the key in the ignition. “Do you know how fucking hard it was to find these babies out here?” She made a mock-derisive noise with her tongue. “Kids these days—no gratitude.”

    “Kids,” Kate grumbled, rolling her eyes even as she pulled on the new shoes and smiled at them. When Yelena cleared her throat, Kate dropped the smile as though she’d been caught showing an emotion she wasn’t supposed to. “You guys can’t be that much older than me.”

    “You’d be surprised,” Yelena drawled, annoyed with herself for missing Kate Bishop’s smile already. “Natasha is an ancient being. She is rapidly approaching fifty years of age, and her wrinkles prove this.” Reaching over, Yelena poked her sister’s temple. She pulled back before Natasha had a chance to slap her away. “I am afraid, Kate Bishop, that we will have to bury her soon. Her body simply will not—”

    “Laugh it up,” Natasha interjected, pulling up to a red light at a crossroads. “I’m not the one who will be thirty soon.”

    “In three years,” Yelena said, briefly regretting her admission with Kate in the car. She needed to be more careful on their travels and keep her guard up. Kate already knew too much about her—too much that Yelena had unwittingly divulged to her over the course of eighteen beautiful, comfortable days. “You will be dust by then.”

    “I’m looking forward to it,” Kate said, keeping the empty shoebox held loosely in between her arms. “I actually can’t wait to be thirty, flirty, and thriving.” The light turned green, but Natasha was too busy staring at Kate in the rearview mirror to notice. Someone honked behind them, and Natasha hit the gas.

    “Thirty, flirty, and thriving?” Yelena repeated, watching as Natasha rolled her eyes with a smile and Kate Bishop’s mouth dropped open in shock.

    ##

    December 10th, 2016 | 3:11 A.M.

    Another night, another motel. This time, there were two double beds.

    Yelena was keeping second watch. Next to her, a Natasha-shaped ball was fast asleep under a thick blanket, her soft breathing muffled and thus barely audible through the cotton. Kate Bishop lay sprawled across the other double bed, her broad back peeking out from beneath the blanket because she twisted and turned so much.

    On a table next to the front door of the motel room, a beam of light from outside illuminated a DVD case with a woman on it. The woman’s arms were outstretched, and her right foot was lifted up to her left shin. 13 Going on 30, she thought. What a stupid name.

    She couldn’t wait to watch it.

    Her idle musing about a film from twelve years ago was interrupted by muffled murmuring from her left. Turning her head, Yelena tried to ascertain the source of the noise. Her eyes had acclimated well to the darkness, so she had no trouble picking out the moving shape of Kate Bishop’s body. Natasha was still asleep, which didn’t surprise Yelena much. The knowledge that someone else was keeping watch was a great comfort.

    Another muted mumble rose from Kate’s bed, followed quickly by a whimper that sounded an awful lot like Lucky.

    Pulling her legs to her chest, Yelena wrapped her arms around them and pushed her forehead to her knees. Kate Bishop’s dreams were none of her business. Kate Bishop was none of her business. Natasha had gotten it in her head that they needed to help this wolf-girl. Yelena would have been fine never speaking to her again. She wouldn’t have missed her; she wouldn’t have missed—

    Another whimper, accompanied by a pitiful whine.

    “You will wake Natasha,” Yelena whispered to herself, glancing past her leg at the sister- lump to her left. There was no indication whatsoever that Natasha was aware of anything but her blanket cocoon. Still, Yelena knew she was right. If this did go on, not even Natasha’s safety in knowing that Yelena was there for her would be enough.

    Slipping off the mattress, Yelena sent one last look at the window. The blinds were drawn, but she could see a sliver of the outside world through the gap between the sides of the slats and the glass. When she was satisfied that everything was quiet and she could turn her back on it, she carefully walked to Kate’s bed. Her feet were perfectly silent as she approached, so she wasn’t surprised that Kate didn’t wake up as she moved closer.

    Even in the darkness, Yelena could see the pained expression on Kate’s face.

    Keeping her features neutral, Yelena crouched down next to the bed and placed her hands and arms on the blanket. With a soft hum, she observed the other woman’s body language. Kate’s fingers were clenched into fists, and there was an unnatural tension to her arms and back, especially for someone asleep. Kate’s mouth moved, but it only occasionally produced unintelligible gibberish-mumbling.

    Slowly—carefully—Yelena slid the fingers of her left hand closer and closer to Kate’s right fist.

    “Good girl,” she whispered, gently covering the shivering fingers. “Shh. You are safe.”

    Something jerked in Yelena’s chest when she saw a tear trickle from the corner of Kate’s eye. “I feel that this would be easier if you were awake,” Yelena said quietly to herself, briefly allowing her eyes to move around the room. Natasha was still bundled up in her blanket nest, and there was no movement visible outside of their motel room. Reassured, Yelena leaned closer. “Kate Bishop,” she whispered, softly squeezing Kate’s fist. Moving her free hand to Kate’s hair, Yelena whispered her name a second time, again to no result. “You are so stubborn, Lucky.”

    Kate twitched violently in her sleep. When Yelena moved her left hand from Kate’s fist to her wrist to apply just the right amount of pressure, Kate suddenly came awake with a start. Her mouth opened, and her eyes shone in the darkness of the bedroom. She blinked, and more tears trickled down her cheeks.

    Before she could say anything, Yelena shushed her.

    “You are safe,” Yelena repeated softly, flattening her right palm on Kate’s hair before using her pointer and middle finger to stroke the skin behind Kate’s ear gently. “It was a bad dream. You are not really there. Wherever you were just now.”

    More tears trickled from Kate’s eyes as she squeezed them shut and pushed her face against the mattress to muffle the sounds of her pain. Carefully, so as not to startle her, Yelena started moving her left thumb in tandem with the movements of her right hand, hoping that the connection to another living being would be enough to bring Kate some comfort. In a way, it was only fitting. For over two weeks, the body of a wolf had given her more security and reassurance than most anything else in her life.

    There was no way to tell the passage of time. Yelena didn’t wear a watch, and neither did Kate. The world outside never changed, and while Yelena couldn’t be sure, she wouldn’t be surprised if Natasha had woken up at this point. When Kate finally unclenched her fist and lifted her head, Yelena was grateful. Her knees were starting to hurt from the strain of leaning on them for too long. In the Red Room, she would have never been allowed to voice her discomfort, as she had been trained not to focus on her own pain.

    “Bad dream?” Yelena asked softly, removing her hands from Kate’s body to push herself off her knees. As soon as she placed her hands on the mattress and made motion to rise, Kate reached out and clasped her forearm.

    “Please don’t go,” she said quickly, desperation creeping into her voice.

    “I am not going,” Yelena replied softly, disentangling her arm from Kate’s grip without being a dick about it. Rubbing her knees with her left hand, she motioned at Kate with the other. “Scoot.” Kate didn’t have to know Yelena planned on returning to her and Natasha’s beds until this very moment.

    Kate nearly fell over in her haste to make room. Graciously choosing not to comment on it, Yelena sat down and leaned against the headboard. For a second, Kate hovered awkwardly on her left elbow, watching as Yelena got comfortable. Then, she crawled closer and hesitantly laid her head on Yelena’s lap before draping the blanket over them both and curling up against Yelena’s legs.

    Memories of Lucky flooded Yelena’s mind, tightening her throat with longing for her furry companion. Clenching her jaw, she lifted her hand and placed it on Kate’s neck. Lucky never existed. You were deceived. It is time you accepted this fact.

    Kate’s neck was as warm as the rest of her. When she huffed out a breath and snuggled further in, Yelena couldn’t help but imagine how she might be feeling right now if this was her wolf and not the woman who hid behind it. If it had been Lucky, Yelena would have stroked the back of her nose and the space between her eyes. She would have scratched her ears and made silly little noises at her.

    “M’sorry,” Kate whispered, wrapping her arms around Yelena’s leg. Her face was shrouded in darkness, leaving Yelena unable to read her expression at this angle. But when Kate Bishop’s shoulders shook with the effort of keeping her emotions contained, Yelena forced herself to relax. Besides, if she did this now, perhaps Kate would owe her something.

    In lieu of answering verbally, Yelena placed her left hand on Kate’s hair while using her right to stroke the back of Kate’s neck and brush along her shoulders. Closing her eyes, she thought back to Ohio and how Mama Melina used to calm her down after a bad dream. She remembered her gentle humming, her forehead kisses, and the way she’d use her hands to give a greater comfort than a tiny Yelena could have ever conceived of on her own. She had been so very happy in Ohio. Briefly, she wondered how their not-quite-parents were doing now. She hadn’t seen Melina for many years and Alexei for even longer.

    “You are okay,” Yelena said quietly, keeping her left hand calm and steady on Kate’s head while continuing to move the fingers of her right back and forth carefully. Slowly but surely, she felt the thick, tense muscles under her palm unclench, and Kate Bishop’s erratic breathing slowed to a much more manageable pace. “Bad dream can’t hurt you, now.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, her voice breathy and small. With reluctance, Yelena brought her fingers from Kate’s neck to the base of her head before allowing them to brush Kate’s cheek. When there was no protest from the girl in her lap, Yelena moved her thumb along the back of Kate’s ear and the skin just behind it in small, tender circles. She would have felt silly if Kate Bishop hadn’t let out the tiniest of contented sighs.

    Rolling her eyes at herself in the dark, Yelena continued to pet Kate’s ear until the girl inevitably fell asleep. And even then, she didn’t let her go.

    ##

    December 13th, 2016 | 5:25 P.M.

    The safehouse they eventually came upon was an old farm on a stretch of land that nobody seemed to own but that someone whom Natasha had never met was keeping tidy and organized. They’d arrived an hour earlier in a truck they liberated from the side of the road somewhere. It had seen better days, but Yelena couldn’t help the affection she felt for it. Three days ago, Yelena had fallen asleep while keeping watch. She couldn’t remember that ever happening before, and she attributed everything about it to Kate Bishop and her warm body anchoring her to the darkness. When Natasha shook her awake in the morning, there was no anger on Natasha’s face, but Yelena blamed herself, regardless.

    “This place is nice,” Kate said, moving her left palm across the kitchen table. Her boots were by the front door, discarded next to the shoe rack.

    “I haven’t been here in years.” Natasha placed three plastic grocery bags on the counter, the crinkling briefly filling the whole room. As it petered out, Yelena moved her attention back to Kate, whose eyes were fixed on the arched doorway connecting the kitchen to the dining area and living room. “She remodeled some of this in the intervening years.”

    “She?” Yelena asked, allowing herself a moment of distraction for the first time in three days. “Someone knows about your safehouse?” Carrying over her own grocery bags, Yelena kept her eyes firmly on her sister. “Are you not a wanted fugitive? Should we be in a known location?”

    “She’s not going to send anyone,” Natasha said without looking up from the pile of pre-

    packaged food she was busy rifling through. “Even if she knew we were here.” Her voice never wavered, but there was something odd about her face. It was an expression Yelena had never seen on her sister, although that didn’t mean very much. Before being recaptured after her first escape, Yelena had only spent a few days with a grown-up Natasha.

    “Girlfriend?” Kate asked with innocence and genuine curiosity writ large across her features. “Yes, Natasha,” Yelena said, wondering if perhaps Kate Bishop had seen something in her sister’s eyes that she could not. From what Yelena could gather, Kate had lived a standard US-American life aside from the hunter and supernatural business. Perhaps her knowledge in this regard would be useful in deciphering this new facet of Natasha’s body language. “Is it a girlfriend?”

    “I love the fact that you’re bonding,” Natasha said, snagging a protein bar from the pile of food and unwrapping it slowly. Turning around, she leaned her waist against the kitchen counter and bit into the bar. With a full mouth, she gestured between them. “Wouldn’t be opening up a detective agency just yet if I were you.”

    Furrowing her brows, Kate shrugged.

    “So… not a girlfriend, I guess?” she asked, clearly unperturbed by Natasha’s deflection and uninvested in the result of this conversation.

    “No,” Yelena interjected, placing her grocery bags on the kitchen table next to Kate’s left arm. “Do not be fooled by Natasha’s petty deceptions, Kate Bishop.” She may not know what her sister’s face looked like when she was in love, but she knew Natasha’s deflection-face. “Is she an Avenger? Is it very scandalous? Must you keep it a secret for the sake of her reputation?”

    “Funny,” Natasha said, pointing at Yelena with the protein bar again. “If you weren’t an assassin, you’d be a comedian.”

    “I am, in fact, hilarious,” Yelena confirmed, sidling up behind Kate’s right side before placing her left hand on the tall girl’s shoulder. As always, Kate’s body felt densely muscled, but Yelena ignored the instant rush of safety her solid presence brought her. “And you are a very bad liar. She is not an Avenger. But she is someone who works with the Avengers, yes?”

    Pulling her lips into a smirk, Yelena shook Kate’s shoulder back and forth. “Is it a workplace conflict? Are you not allowed to fraternize? How sad.”

    Natasha rolled her eyes; Yelena burst into laughter; Kate smiled tentatively.

    Letting go of Kate’s shoulder, Yelena crossed the distance between herself and her sister. With little fanfare, she pulled Natasha into a hug and squeezed her with as much gusto as she could manage, nearly lifting Natasha off the floor.

    “I am so happy for you,” she whispered, uncaring that Kate’s powerful ears might be able to hear. For just a few minutes, she allowed herself a normality she could hardly ever indulge in. “You are such a bad loser, and yet you have found a woman who likes you. I am both happy and proud.”

    “Kate,” Natasha said, patting Yelena’s back with fake enthusiasm while protein bar crumbs landed on the kitchen tiles beneath their feet. “Your wolf-bride sucks.”

    “You guys are so weird,” Kate muttered seconds before Yelena felt a pair of strong arms wrap around her and Natasha. “Thanks for taking me with you.” Next, Kate’s cheek settled on Yelena’s head. “Sorry if this is awkward.”

    “It is,” Yelena confirmed, feeling the instinctive urge to wriggle away and confront Kate about her Lucky-sized lie. But then, Natasha slipped her right arm from around Yelena to around Kate and pulled her closer, and Yelena didn’t say anything about it.

    “We love awkwardness,” Natasha said, audibly clapping Kate’s back. “Couldn’t you tell?”

    Their big hug felt a little silly after roughly five more seconds, yet Yelena didn’t let them go. Instead, she closed her eyes and slowly unfurled her left arm from around Natasha’s body. With some hesitation, she placed her hand on Kate’s side before moving it along the girl’s back. Kate squeezed them even more tightly in response.

    ##

    December 14th, 2016 | 9:01 A.M.

    By the time Kate Bishop descended the stairs at nine in the morning the next day, Yelena and Natasha were already done with their breakfast. In truth, they were done with their whole morning routine, which included working out and play-fighting to keep their senses sharp. The day before, Yelena inspected the whole house for any possible listening devices or motion sensors, but she found nothing of interest. Funnily enough, the basement had a much more modern finish and was surprisingly clean. It also held several training mats and other workout equipment. Yelena had yet to discover any hidden compartments with weapons, but she was starting to suspect that she just hadn’t looked hard enough. Whoever the woman was that Natasha had mentioned off-handedly, she certainly wasn’t boring.

    The morning had brought one truth into sharp focus for Yelena: She never wanted to be separated from her sister again.

    “What happened to your face?” Kate asked through a mouthful of cornflakes five minutes after sitting down with a bowl and her food. She was dressed in an oversized, black sweater that Natasha bought for her a few days ago, and Kate’s poor ankles were finally covered by a pair of grey sweatpants in her size.

    “Yelena doesn’t know the meaning of the words light sparring,” Natasha replied, reaching up to touch the band-aid slapped across her right eyebrow while Yelena continued to sip her coffee. “If she ever asks you, don’t feel obligated. Just say no.”

    “Okay,” Kate said, giving a thumbs up before returning to her cereal. If Yelena hadn’t been told about Kate Bishop’s life as a former hunter, she likely would have assumed that the woman had never so much as stepped foot in a ring before. Let alone to spar. “How long do you plan on staying here?”

    “As long as it takes,” Yelena replied, putting her coffee mug back on the kitchen table.

    Kate furrowed her brows. “As long as what takes?” she asked, her spoon of cereal hovering in the air and dripping milk back into the bowl. She looked from Yelena to Natasha and back again, apprehension dawning on her face. “What are you planning on doing?”

    “Well,” Natasha started with half a shrug, leaning back in her kitchen chair, lifting her right leg, and propping her foot up on the seat next to her. Yelena almost rolled her eyes at her sister’s theatricalities. “It occurred to us that we all have some sort of bounty on our heads. You have your hunter problem, Yelena’s being chased by the Red Room, and the United States government wants to put me in a cell for not signing the Sokovia Accords.”

    “And?” Kate asked, dropping the spoon back in the cereal and crossing her arms over her chest. “Not much we can do about any of that.”

    “See, that’s where we disagree,” Natasha continued, mimicking Kate’s body language and crossing her own arms. “Tell her what we think, Yelena.” She never took her eyes off Kate; her Black-Widow-Avenger-poser act was in full effect, which was precisely why Yelena had to ruin it.

    “We will kill the hunters who want to kill you,” she said with a shrug.

    “What?” Kate asked, her voice barely audible above the scraping of wooden legs on tiles as Natasha sat back upright and scowled at Yelena.

    “What my sister meant to say is that it also occurred to us that the easiest problem to solve out of all of them is the one that concerns you,” Natasha said, putting her forearms on the kitchen table in a much less pretentious fashion. “Hunters are dangerous, but they aren’t the government or a top-secret organization that creates super-soldier assassins. Yelena and I may have to live like this for the foreseeable future, but you don’t.”

    When Kate uncrossed her arms and carefully placed her fists on the table, something told Yelena that this had been the wrong thing to say.

    “Hunters aren’t easy to kill,” Kate growled, her upper body shaking with the effort to keep herself calm and collected. A few days ago, Yelena might have reacted to the clear display of suppressed aggression by removing her gun or even just putting her hand on it. Now, after everything that happened, she merely ignored her instincts and continued to sip her coffee. “They have connections in all the wrong places, and they never hesitate.”

    Your mother did, Yelena thought, studying Kate’s face and the terror behind her blue eyes. It would be an even more wrong thing to say, so Yelena merely cupped both of her hands around her warm mug and tilted her head to the side. Kate’s whole body was overwrought with tension, her muscles brimming with poorly concealed fear. Yelena had no idea if Kate’s emotions affected her transformation. If it did, it was only a matter of time until Kate freaked out and shifted. Then again, that might not be such a bad thing. Yelena had far more experience with calming down a howling Lucky than she did a distraught Kate.

    “It is still better than the government or the Red Room,” Yelena said softly, leaning on her forearms and catching Kate’s frantic gaze. “You saved us from being captured, Kate Bishop. Natasha and I have decided that we will return the favor.”

    Kate’s eyes remained steadfast on Yelena’s face—then, when they suddenly grew misty, she swiped her hands across them, severing their connection. At the sight of Kate’s obvious distress, a sharp stab of pain lanced through Yelena’s heart.

    “Sorry,” Kate muttered, rubbing furiously at her face with her left hand while her right fist stayed rooted to the table. What she didn’t see were the looks exchanged between Natasha and Yelena. When Natasha raised her eyebrows, Yelena scowled. With a roll of her eyes, she abandoned her coffee mug on the table and got to her feet.

    “Get up,” Yelena said, passing by Kate and grabbing her right bicep. When she squeezed, she felt the muscle underneath and wondered not for the first time just how deadly Kate Bishop really could be if she felt so inclined. She’d seen some of Kate’s strength against the Red Room, but she had an inkling that there was more hiding just beneath the surface.

    “Wha—?” Kate said, looking up at Yelena with confused eyes.

    “Get up right now. We are going for a walk,” Yelena said, pulling at Kate’s bicep and feeling her resistance. For several seconds, Kate’s arm didn’t move for so much as an inch. Then, Kate gave in and allowed herself to be pulled up and out of her chair. Interesting.

    “Your favorite pastime, I’m sure,” Natasha said, her face far too stoic for such a bad joke. It was a testament to how upset Kate truly was that she didn’t comment on Natasha’s dog-

    reference while Yelena pulled her along by the bicep.

    ##

    December 14th, 2016 | 9:19 A.M.

    Yelena didn’t know anything about werewolf physiology or immunology beyond what Kate had told her a week ago; she had no idea if Kate could get a cold or contract pneumonia.

    When Yelena first came upon her on that snowy, winding road, Kate had been shot to shit, and she’d spent a good chunk of time on Yelena’s truck in the freezing cold. Back then, she’d pulled through just fine. Still, when they stopped by the shoe rack and jackets, Yelena made sure to stop them so they could both get bundled up for the rough, mid-December weather.

    As soon as they were outside, Yelena stuffed her hands into her coat pockets and descended the porch steps. The cold wind caressed her cheeks and the sliver of neck unprotected by her scarf. She didn’t shiver, but she acknowledged the drop in temperature and slowly inhaled the freezing air to calm her body. She had survived Russian winters much worse than this. Lifting her eyes to the sky, she watched as the water particles of her exhales drifted up into the air. “Are you sure we should be walking around outside?” Kate asked, sidling up to Yelena’s right elbow to nudge it briefly with her own. “What if there are cameras anywhere? What if there’s a satellite trained on us right now?”

    Closing her eyes, Yelena felt the cold seep into her bones and fill up her insides. If she wanted to, she could slow her heart rate down to match the sluggish atmosphere all around them. If she wanted to, she could have Kate Bishop down on the ground in seconds, begging for mercy. Or could she? The moment in the kitchen gave her pause. Had she been unable to move Kate because she had been fully aware of her surroundings, or was she always that hard to move?

    “Natasha has reassured me that this is a safe house in the true meaning of the words. This is not like the cabin,” Yelena said, opening her eyes and looking at Kate Bishop, whose eyes were trained on the ground and whose shoulders were hunched in on themselves. Her exhales were visible, too. Almost as visible as her continued distress. “You have stopped speaking.” “What?” Kate asked, keeping her eyes down and her arms crossed. “No, I haven’t.”

    Grabbing Kate by the bicep again—by the left one this time—Yelena started walking slowly away from the house, Kate following along obediently. A row of trees was visible in the distance, and their arrangement made it evident that they would bear some sort of fruit or nut in the warmer months.

    “It is not hard to guess why you no longer speak,” Yelena said, letting go of Kate’s bicep only when she was sure that Kate would keep walking on her own. Slowly, they traversed the patch of grass in front of the farmhouse, drawing closer to the barren fields and the small orchard.

    “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Kate muttered, finally unfurling her arms from across her chest to mimic Yelena’s hands-in-pockets-pose.

    Exhaling slowly, Yelena imagined herself a dragon as her breath gently spiraled into the atmosphere, visible for anyone who might take the time to notice. She stopped, and it took Kate several seconds to realize Yelena wasn’t by her side anymore. When she turned to look at Yelena, Kate blinked slowly, her blue eyes full of such terrible pain that it struck Yelena to her very core.

    “The Red Room would have taken us back,” Yelena said, refusing to close the distance between them. “They would have tried to break our minds and put us back to work as assassins. And if that didn’t work, they would have killed us. Or maybe they would have done much worse. Perhaps they would have sold us off. Natasha is an Avenger. She would fetch a high price on the open market.”

    Kate’s hands visibly clenched in her pockets, and her neck turned red from the tension that suddenly seemed to overtake her whole body.

    “We would be back in Russia by now,” Yelena continued, ignoring the little voice in the back of her mind that told her not to provoke the angry, confused, and deeply upset supernatural girl across from her. “Tranquilized and strapped down to transport cots. Dreykov would not wait for us, but he would send his scientists. I think, perhaps, they would have sold Natasha right away. No point trying to reintegrate her after so many years—”

    “Stop,” Kate said, ripping her hands from her pockets and finally taking the three long steps that separated them. “Why are you—stop! That’s not going to happen!”

    “Not right now,” Yelena said with a shrug, fighting tooth and nail against her instinct to defend herself against the predator who just advanced on her. To give herself the illusion of control, she put her right hand on Kate’s sternum and met her gaze head-on. “Because you ripped those men apart, Natasha and I are not being dissected right now. Our minds are as intact as they will ever be because you protected us. You tore them limb from limb, and they deserved it. They deserved it, Kate Bishop. Because they would have done worse to us.”

    Kate was so close to her; it was easy to spot the self-doubt underneath the explosive fury. When Kate didn’t reply, Yelena leaned even closer.

    “I don’t regret saving you and Natasha,” Yelena repeated quietly, wishing the layers of clothing didn’t prevent her from feeling Kate’s heartbeat to better gauge her mood and reactions. “That is what you said. Is that no longer true?”

    Kate shook her head.

    “Of course, it’s still true. But that doesn’t—that doesn’t make me—” Pulling her fists from her jacket, Kate looked down at her hands. “I don’t know how to—” Curling them briefly back into fists, Kate soon clasped Yelena’s forearm instead. Even through the jacket, Yelena could feel the warmth of Kate’s touch. “How do you do it? How do you—you said you’d killed thousands. How do you—live with it?”

    This was too much. All of this was too much. Kate Bishop’s eyes were trained on Yelena too intently like she held all the answers in the universe. Like she was the expert on shutting away her guilt and forgiving herself for all of her sins. She wanted to pull away; she wanted to tell Kate to respect her personal space; she wanted to rip her hand from Kate’s grip; she wanted to pull Kate into her arms and hold her until she stopped looking so sad.

    “I do not have the answers you seek,” Yelena said, looking from Kate’s desperate eyes to the desperate way she clung to Yelena’s arm. “I want to say that you will forget their screams or that you will no longer feel their flesh as it gives way beneath your hands. I want to say that you will stop dreaming about their faces or forget the smell of their blood.” She took a step back and took her hand with her, Kate’s fingers sliding along her arm until they dropped away. “I have been free for as long as you have known me. I cannot tell you when it will stop. I can only tell you that I would not take back your actions, even if I could.”

    “Do you think,” Kate started, reaching out and tentatively taking Yelena’s hand with her own. “Does it make me a bad person that I would make the same decision? My parents always said that they killed werewolves and vampires to protect our family. Do you—am I—”

    “I don’t know if you are a good person,” Yelena said, squeezing Kate’s fingers. “But I know you are not a bad one.” It was an admission as much as it was the truth. A second later, Yelena knew it was the right thing to say because Kate closed the distance and pulled Yelena into her body, her arms wrapping themselves around Yelena’s shoulders in the softest embrace Yelena had yet experienced.

    “Thank you,” Kate whispered, her big hands flattening themselves on Yelena’s back and burning her through her jacket.

    “You’re welcome,” Yelena replied, loosely placing her hands on Kate’s waist and looking up at the sky.
