    Kate, Yelena, and Natasha were all crammed into the study.

    Kate hadn’t done a great deal of exploring since their arrival the day before. She’d been too preoccupied with the various thoughts in her head, so she didn’t realize there was a study down the hall until Natasha told her about it. After she and Yelena came back in from the cold, Kate finished her soggy cornflakes before asking Natasha and Yelena how they planned on taking down the hunters who wanted her dead.

    “Are you sure this is a good idea?” Kate asked, pulling her socked feet onto the luscious easy chair she was sitting on. The study was furnished lovingly with an expensive red-and-gold carpet, comfortable furniture, and a sprawling mahogany desk. Behind the desk, a large painting took up most of the wall, showing a cloudy sky above an unruly sea.

    “Trust me,” Natasha said, booting up the PC in front of her with the click of a button. “If anyone knows how to keep unwanted eyes off her online activities, it’s my friend.” Yelena leaned against the wall next to the painting, her arms crossed over her chest. She looked unconcerned, but Kate wondered how much of that was for show and how much of it was real. What she remembered most vividly about the last week were Yelena’s small moments of kindness amidst all of Kate’s uncertainty.

    “That does not sound suspicious at all,” Yelena drawled, keeping her eyes on the desktop while Natasha started typing. “Should I be concerned that you know this woman’s five security passwords and that your thumb works on the scanner? Did you get married without telling me?”

    “I told you,” Natasha said, grabbing the mouse and moving it across the desk. “This is a great place to hide out at.” She pulled her red hair into a tiny ponytail at the back of her head, showing off her sharp cheekbones. “My friend has great taste in surveillance and subterfuge.” “Maybe one day you’ll even invite her to dinner,” Yelena said, rolling her eyes.

    “Anyway,” Natasha said with a smirk, shifting her attention from the desktop to Kate. “Who are these hunters we’re going after? You got names? Any other way to track them down?

    Yelena said something about them seeing your face mid-shift? Sounds like a pretty hairy situation.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, pulling her left knee to her chest and wrapping her arms around it. “I couldn’t stop myself from turning, so I fell from a tree…” The memories of her encounter with the Red Room had eclipsed nearly everything else in the last few days. Her first nights at the cabin were filled with nightmares about the hunters. Nightmares about being caught; nightmares about being dissected; nightmares about luring the hunters to Yelena. But those dreams were nothing now. They were nothing in comparison to the new dreams. Dreams where Kate was the killer. Dreams where Kate lost control and ripped apart the people dearest to her. “But I know them. They wanted to work with my mom and our company.”

    “Any idea how they found out about you?” Yelena asked while Natasha’s fingers moved across the keyboard.

    “Ah,” Natasha interjected before Kate could reply. A terrifying grin crawled across her lips. “Bishop Security. Hello, old friend.” Her tone of voice sent shivers down Kate’s back. “You didn’t mention that your mother is Eleanor Bishop.”

    “You know her?” Kate asked, slowly placing both feet on the ground and curling her hands into fists.

    Pushing herself off the wall, Yelena crossed the room. “Relax, Kate Bishop,” she whispered after slumping into the second easy chair. Sometime after lunch, she’d pulled her hair into an intricate braid. She could have looked like an intimidating insurgent had it not been for the ripped jeans and the bright green tweed jacket.

    “I know of her,” Natasha clarified, leaning back in the desk chair and regarding Kate with a thoughtful expression. “When you spend much of your life digging through security systems and the best way to dismantle them, you come across a lot of information that you were never really looking for. Some of it can be useful.” Pursing her lips, she looked out of the window and hummed to herself. “Before SHIELD collapsed, they sometimes farmed smaller projects out to Bishop Security, but I never really paid much attention to them. Shame.”

    “We are furious,” Yelena drawled, crossing her legs and folding her hands on her right knee. “How could you not have foreseen these very specific circumstances?”

    “How much of Bishop Security is compromised?” Natasha asked, returning her attention to the computer screen. She typed something else, her fingers flying across the keys in perfect, ten-finger form. “Is it just your family, or?”

    “I wish it were,” Kate said, relaxing her hands before pulling her feet back onto the easy chair. This time, she wrapped her arms around both shins. Pressing her forehead to her knees, she closed her eyes and listened to the wind howling past the house. “The whole place is full of them.” Squeezing herself, Kate ignored the dread that crawled along her skin. “But they’re not the ones who shot me. They didn’t know I was a wolf. They thought I was one of them.” Lifting her head, she looked straight at Natasha. “The people who found me are new, and they’re even more influential than my mom.”

    “If it makes you feel any better,” Natasha said, turning the screen around. “Your mother seems to be doing just fine. She held a press conference yesterday about a merger she’s pursuing.” The screen showed a newspaper article with a picture of Kate’s mom standing at a podium. And right next to her… “Seems like Bishop Security will be Bishop & Kelly Inc. in just a few weeks.”

    Staring at the screen, Kate didn’t realize Yelena was on her feet until the other woman came to a halt in front of the desk. Grabbing the desktop, Yelena turned it away from Kate and towards herself, her eyes fixated on the face of Ms. Kelly.

    Kate’s ears buzzed as the truth started sinking in. Her mother was alive. Her mother was in good health. Her mother was in the middle of the biggest business decision of her career. And all of it—all of it was happening without Kate.

    “This is one of the people who shot you, yes?” Yelena asked, her mouth pulled into a neutral line. Her words pulled Kate from her spiraling emotions of confusion, anger, revulsion, and horrible relief. “Charlotte Kelly?”

    Natasha whistled when Kate nodded her head.

    “Gotta say, Jack,” Natasha said as she pulled the monitor back towards herself before hitting a few more keys. A printer came to life behind her, startling Kate into straightening her back. “You really know how to pick ‘em.”

    “It’s not my fault,” Kate grumbled, desperately trying to rationalize her mother’s choices. She had no idea what she would say if either Natasha or Yelena asked her what she thought of the merger or its implications. After you were nearly killed, your mother carried on as though it was nothing. They probably told her about it, yet she’s working with them anyway. How’s it feel, Jack? How’s it feel? How’s it feel?

    “She is someone important?” Yelena asked, placing her hands into the pockets of her tweed jacket. Her shoulders looked relaxed, and her stance was unthreatening, but Kate was pretty sure there was anger hiding behind her gaze.

    “I’d call her the product of nepotism, personally.” Natasha reached behind her to pick up the article she’d just printed. She handed it over to Yelena, whose eyes immediately returned to the picture. “Her daddy used to be a big deal in conservative politics. He almost became president at one point.” Natasha rolled her eyes. “Americans.”

    “Conservative politics?” Yelena didn’t look away from the picture. Kate wondered if she was committing Kelly’s face to memory. At the same time, Kate thanked the moon and her children that her parents had never taught her about Senator Kelly’s life and career. Memories of venerating this man could well have killed her after being turned. Realizing that her entire life was constructed around a lie nearly had.

    “He hated mutants,” Kate muttered, recalling her conversation with her mother. Things were different then. “My parents loved him. I bet they supported his stupid campaign for the presidency.” She looked at her feet. The full moon was only five days away, and its pull grew stronger with every passing second. Very soon, she would have to make a choice. “I saw Kelly and the others in my mom’s office that day. I have no idea how they found out where I was going or how they knew about me being a wolf. I’d never seen them before.”

    “Where were you when they attacked?” Yelena asked, placing the article on the desk before leaning against its edge.

    “I picked a random stretch of forest in Maine. I even drove for four fricking hours to get there.” Like many times over the past month, Kate thought about her mom’s SUV. Now that she knew for certain that her mother was alive, she felt less unsure about its fate. Perhaps the towing company had contacted her mom, or maybe it would have been the person who owned the lot where the car inevitably ended up. But the duffle… the duffle was still up on that fir tree, waiting for her return. She imagined the messages that were piling up in her absence. Messages from Greer, Franny, and the rest of the archery team, increasingly worried about her whereabouts. E-mails from professors, sitting unanswered in her inbox.

    “Then they either followed you, or they put a tracker on your car,” Yelena concluded, rounding the desk to get a better view of what Natasha was doing. “I think it is unlikely that you would randomly run into a group of hunters you met the same day. By accident, no less.” “Actually,” Natasha interrupted, aggressively hitting enter, “they absolutely followed you. One hundred percent.” When Kate furrowed her brows, Natasha continued. “Think about it.

    If they were on your ass mid-shift, they couldn’t have been very far behind. It makes a lot more sense if they were right behind you the whole time.”

    “I guess,” Kate muttered, getting off the easy chair to wander over to the windows.

    “I am not surprised,” Yelena announced, abandoning her post behind Natasha in favor of taking up residence at Kate’s right shoulder. At her words, Kate nearly flinched. I am not surprised you allowed them to follow you, Yelena’s voice seemed to say. You should know better, Kate Bishop. She could see the reflection of Yelena’s face in the glass, but she couldn’t tell if there was judgment in it, or something else. The next time she spoke, Kate almost didn’t hear her. “Considering all that you have told me… I suspect they were surveilling both you and your mother.”

    Clenching her jaw, Kate stared at the forest in the distance. Her mother had been right. She should have stayed in that cage. She should have locked herself away. She should have paid more attention. She should have. She should have. She should have. She didn’t.

    ##

    December 16th, 2016 | 10:49 A.M.

    The basement smelled surprisingly clean. It also didn’t look much like a basement.

    For the last two days, Yelena and Natasha had spent most of their time on research and planning. Kate had mostly let them do their work without butting in. She’d never gotten to the stage of her hunter training where she planned any of the missions herself, so she didn’t feel like she had much to contribute. Besides, she remained unconvinced that attacking a legacy hunter and her mother’s new company venture was a good idea. At least they had the safehouse going for them. Since neither the Red Room nor the US government had come looking for them, Kate assumed that Natasha was right about her friend.

    As Kate made her way down the stairs, she allowed her fingers to trail along the wall, noting the rough texture of the plaster. Whereas the farmhouse itself was very cozy and rustic in its décor, the basement reminded Kate more of a lab or an office building. White walls and ceilings framed the uniform grey floor down here, giving off a nearly sterile vibe.

    Once she reached the bottom, she found herself in a hallway with four doors, none of which were labeled. “Not creepy or suspicious at all,” Kate said to herself, stopping in front of the first door to the left and tentatively reaching for the handle.

    With Yelena and Natasha occupied, Kate was left with a lot more alone time on her hands than she was used to. She spent much of it on the porch, sitting in the cold and watching the clouds go by. With no one around to distract her from her lingering feelings of guilt and self- doubt, taking her mind off everything that happened was—difficult.

    Peeking into the room, she was surprised to find a fully furnished gym inside, complete with a set of dumbbells, a pull-up bar, and a dedicated area for sparring. When Yelena and Natasha had told her about working out in the mornings, Kate had sort of assumed that they’d been doing squats, push-ups, or lunges. As a werewolf, Kate didn’t need to work out. Her wolf blood kept her stronger than any regular human could ever hope to be, and her high metabolism burned off excess fat or sugar. Still, her fingers itched to grab the pull-up bar. The full moon was only three days away. Already, her body crawled with excess energy, held back only by her fear of what might happen if she allowed herself to show it. The last time she did, she ended up in front of Yelena’s truck.

    Returning to her mission, Kate closed the gym door before opening the one immediately to its right. The walls of the storage space she found beyond were hidden behind several high shelves. Cardboard boxes, tin cans, water jugs, and big gasoline drums lined the wood. There was no dust on any of the items.

    “Not serial killer-y at all, Natasha’s friend,” Kate said, eyeing the drums.

    Leaving the storage room behind, she turned to the other side of the hallway. The third door opened to reveal two washing machines and two dryers lined up against the room’s left wall. A long, thin table was pushed right up against the opposite one. Along the ceiling, Kate spotted several rubber cords strung from one end to the other. The cords were adorned with clothes pins, revealing their purpose.

    Walking inside, Kate stopped underneath the window at the far side of the room. Metal bars were visible just beyond the glass, ensuring no one could enter through it if it were smashed. Even without them, it would have been virtually impossible for an adult to squeeze through the small opening. A shudder went through her, nearly forcing her to back away from the visual and the accompanying memories.

    “Stop it,” she told herself, looking down at her shaking fingers. “Stop.” She curled them into fists, willing them to calm down. When she returned to the door, she was glad to find a lock and the corresponding key. Falling to her knees, she checked the space between the wood of the door and the ground before pushing herself back to her feet and leaving the room behind.

    The door opposite the gym opened to reveal yet more storage space and a backup generator, only cementing Kate’s decision. After one last check of the laundry room, she returned to the first floor.

    She spotted Yelena on the three-seater when she passed by the living room. Natasha was nowhere to be seen. The television was off, but a jaunty melody permeated the room, originating from a portable radio on the coffee table beside Yelena. The last time the two of them were alone together, Yelena’s nose had been close enough to touch. The last time the two of them were alone together, Yelena had held her close. The last time the two of them were alone together, Yelena told her that she wasn’t a bad person.

    Yelena had been kind to her. Maybe kinder than Kate deserved.

    “You still haven’t finished that thing?” Kate asked, nodding at the book in Yelena’s hand as she did her best to suppress her sudden nervousness. What was she supposed to say to Yelena at this point? Thanks for trying to help me out, but I think it might just be for the best if I got caught. Thanks for doing your best to stop the hunters, but I think my mom might actually be better off this way. Thanks for working so hard to save my life, but maybe you shouldn’t. “I am savoring it,” Yelena said, closing Safe Harbor and resting it cover-up on her stomach. Today, her hair wasn’t braided. She’d exchanged her tweed jacket for a comfortable yellow jumper and her ripped jeans for a pair of black leggings. Three earrings gleamed at her left ear. “Who knows when I will get my hands on the sequel.”

    “What page are you on now? Did they get married already, or is Tory still afraid of the best thing that’s ever happened to her?” Kate tried her hand at a smile. Lingering behind the two- seater, she resisted the urge to sit down.

    “I restarted the book to keep myself in suspense,” Yelena revealed, righting herself on the sofa so she could sit cross-legged. “Personally, I think Tory has every right to be suspicious.” She nodded towards the couch. “Sit down, Kate Bishop.” She held out the book. “Read to me. Make yourself useful.”

    The side of Kate’s mouth twitched as she did as she was told, grabbing Safe Harbor more roughly than she meant to. Slumping down on the two-seater, she flipped to the last page she remembered reading from.

    “I’m useful,” she whispered, staring at the words in front of her. For a brief moment, the only sound in the living room was the sweet melody from the radio. When Yelena said nothing, Kate closed the book and looked away. “I’m sorry.”

    Yelena hummed. “I did not mean it as an insult, but the joke was not optimally done,” she said, placing her hands on her ankles. Kate shrugged, hating herself for her response. She wanted to stop feeling this way. She wanted to just be normal again, or at least as normal as she and Yelena had been before she turned back.

    “It’s fine,” Kate said, reopening the novel. “From the top?”

    “If you like,” Yelena said, her eyes never wavering from the side of Kate’s face.

    “Okay,” Kate said, feeling an embarrassed flush crawl up her neck. “Well, here goes nothing.” She cleared her throat, ignoring the way Yelena kept looking at her. “’You’re shaking,’ Tory whispered, hoping that Reese couldn’t feel the trembling in her own body.” “I have been meaning to thank you,” Yelena said, interrupting Kate after the first paragraph and sending her heart into another tailspin.

    “What?” Kate asked, laser-focusing on the word that introduced the next paragraph: Nervous. “For keeping watch on the porch,” Yelena explained, letting go of her ankles and stretching out her legs. Then, she resumed her previous position on the couch, reclining along its length with her head on the armrest. With a lazy motion, she turned down the volume on the radio, presumably so she could hear Kate better over the music. “I assume you do not feel the cold as humans do?”

    “No,” Kate confirmed, the words on the page beginning to swim together. “Wolf blood. Runs hot.”

    Yelena hummed a second time, drawing Kate’s eyes to her.

    “I imagine this could be useful.” Yelena’s voice was quiet, floating into the air and wrapping around Kate’s head. Kate had gleaned little more about what Yelena truly thought and felt about most things in life—but she remembered the soft caresses of her hair in a dark motel room in the middle of nowhere. With Yelena by her side, Kate had slept without nightmares for the first time since they left the cabin. “Russian winter is much worse than this. Snow and ice cover everything. The missions were so annoying! And the suits were shit most of the time.”

    “That sounds dangerous.” In her mind, Yelena moved across a snowy field, protected from the elements only by a flimsy pair of tactical pants and a sweater. She stopped herself before she could imagine those lips and fingers turning blue or black. “Are you sure it’s a good idea that you and Natasha are going after—”

    “It is a very good idea,” Yelena interrupted, turning her face towards the ceiling. “Now, keep reading.” The conversation was over, and the soft relaxation about Yelena was gone. There would be no further discussion about the Red Room, the hunters, or about Yelena’s misplaced gratitude. Kicking herself mentally, Kate returned to the novel, her mouth pulling into a frown.

    ##

    December 18th, 2016 | 4:00 P.M.

    The frozen grass in front of the farmhouse didn’t move as the wind raced across its blades. The darkness of winter was already moving in, cutting off the last of the daylight hours early. Kate didn’t mind—she had nothing better to do than watch and observe.

    Ignoring the persistent urge to run around, Kate wrapped her arms harder around her legs and continued to stare at the horizon. The full moon was tomorrow, and she had finally made her choice. Inside, Natasha and Yelena were cooped up in the study again, staring at the computer screen and speaking to each other in low, urgent voices as they devised a good plan.

    Kate would have liked to cook them meals to make herself useful, but she was afraid to burn down the whole house. Honey, please. You could burn water. With a sigh, Kate settled her nose against her knees and closed her eyes. Her mother’s voice was a frequent visitor out here. The immense relief of finding out she was alive constantly warred with the painful truth that Eleanor Bishop was working with the people who wanted Kate dead. The truth was difficult to parse. Was her mother a monster, or was she only working with the monsters to preserve herself? And was there a difference?

    Before Kate could spiral any further, the door to the farmhouse opened.

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said, looking very adorable and tiny in a big green jumper and a pair of oversized jeans. In lieu of saying anything else, Yelena merely indicated that Kate should follow her with a jerk of her chin before disappearing back inside. Quickly, Kate unfurled her body from the big chair by the door. When Kate walked in, Natasha was leaning against the kitchen counter, whereas Yelena cradled a mug of steaming hot tea between her fingers.

    Natasha wore an oversized jumper, too. It was Kate’s.

    “Any news?” Kate asked, standing under the arched door frame that separated the kitchen from the living room. “Did something happen?”

    “Sit down,” Yelena said, gesturing at the chair at the head of the table.

    Fear slipped into Kate’s stomach, making her wish she’d never come inside. Perhaps, if she’d stayed on that chair, whatever bad news Yelena had to tell her would have never come to pass. Perhaps, if she’d stayed on that chair, she could have frozen time itself.

    “What’s going on?” Kate asked, not quite able to hide the excess energy that ravaged her body. Forcing her hands into her lap, she squeezed the tip of her left middle finger with her right hand before moving on to her left ring finger and then her pinky. “Is it my mom? Did they—is she hurt?”

    “What? No,” Natasha said, furrowing her brows and placing her palms on the edge of the counter. “No, Jesus. Your mom’s fine.” She rolled her eyes and kicked ineffectually in Yelena’s direction. “You were supposed to tell her we have a plan, not scare her half to death.”

    Yelena did not look guilty, but she didn’t look particularly pleased with herself, either. “Kate Bishop,” she said, drawing Kate’s full attention. “We have a plan.” With a small nod, Yelena looked at Natasha, who sighed. “There. I told Kate Bishop we have a plan. Are you happy?” While Natasha shook her head and said “No! I am not happy!”, Yelena took a sip of tea.

    Getting to her feet, Kate paced back and forth next to the kitchen table, ignoring Yelena’s questioning looks and Natasha’s half-concerned, half-exasperated eyes. Balling her hands into fists, Kate tried to calm her racing heart. Mom’s safe, she told herself. Mom’s safe. And even if she weren’t safe, it wouldn’t be your fault. It wouldn’t be your fault because they would have found out one way or another. It’s not your fault. And it won’t happen again, not to Yelena and Natasha.

    A hand settled on her right bicep.

    “Kate Bishop?” Yelena’s voice was low, her expression unreadable. Those green eyes never wavered, even when Kate clasped Yelena’s wrist with the full intention of pushing her away. While Yelena’s pulse was initially steady, it ticked up a beat when Kate’s warm hand met Yelena’s colder one. Her natural scent was muddled by printer ink, black tea, and the faintest hint of gun oil.

    “Tomorrow’s the next full moon,” Natasha said, pushing away from the kitchen counter. “Are you doing okay?” She turned her head to the side in the exact same manner as Yelena often did, a gesture so familiar that it helped calm some of Kate’s nerves. “You seem…”

    “Paranoid,” Yelena finished, squeezing Kate’s bicep.

    “I would have said jumpy, but I guess that works, too,” Natasha said with a good-natured eye roll.

    “I’m not paranoid, and I’m not jumpy,” Kate said, feeling the pout build on her mouth before she could stop it. Finally removing Yelena’s hand from her arm—gently, always gently—she took a step away and shook out her shoulders and legs. “The moon just makes me feel more alive than I think I’m supposed to be. It’s part of preparing the body for the transformation. Probably. I think.” She balled her hands into fists and squeezed them a few times as she rolled her wrists back and forth. Yelena looked at Natasha; Natasha looked at Yelena; then, they both looked at Kate. “Stop looking at me like that!”

    “Like what?” Yelena asked, her hands hanging loosely at her sides.

    “Like I’m some idiot you need to hang out with because I killed a bunch of guys for you,” Kate said, loosening her fists as she glanced from one former black widow to the other. “Like I don’t know what I’m doing. Like—I don’t know.” She stalked to the living room before pacing back. Her heart was pounding like it had been set loose in her chest. “You don’t have to go up against those hunters for me. You don’t.”

    Yelena nodded, her hands disappearing into the pockets of her jeans.

    “Yes, we know that,” Yelena said with a shrug like it was the simplest thing in the world. “You have told us many times. You did not ask for our help. You do not expect anything in return for what you have done.” Her green eyes swept Kate’s jittery form from head to toe. “We are not helping you because we feel obligated. We are helping you because we want to.” “No one makes us do anything, Jack. Not anymore,” Natasha said, putting her left hand on the kitchen table. “And there’s a reason why we’ve been keeping you out of our planning for the most part, but it’s not because you’re an idiot.” Head turning sharply, Yelena opened her mouth. Before she could say anything, Natasha continued. “It’s because we know what our last encounter with the Red Room did to you. You weren’t trained to handle large-scale assaults with multiple casualties. We were.”

    “I can learn to adapt,” Kate said, wrapping her arms around her stomach.

    “You should not have to,” Yelena interrupted, crossing the distance between them. Her hands stayed soft and undemanding as she curled them around Kate’s left elbow. With a gentle tug, she pulled Kate further into the kitchen. “It is not a sign of weakness to be upset at the death of others. I wish I remembered what it is like. Don’t throw it away.” When she indicated that Kate should sit down, Kate did not resist.

    “Do you want some tea?” Natasha asked, putting her hand on Kate’s shoulder. The expression on her face was new, but it didn’t look bad. Instead, there was something comforting about it. “Maybe it’ll help with the moon fever.”

    Kate snorted. “My mom calls it that, too. It’s such a stupid name for it.”

    With half a shrug, Natasha walked to the counter and removed a mug from a cupboard in front of her.

    While Natasha prepared the tea, Yelena sat down next to Kate. She removed her hands, but Kate continued to feel the lingering phantom traces of her touch. She wasn’t so foolish as to imagine herself in love with Yelena—and even if she did, there wasn’t anything she could do about it.

    “I want to help,” Kate said, returning to squeezing the tips of her fingers to ward off the excess energy. “I appreciate that you want to protect me, but I’ll be okay.” She thought of the Red Room soldiers; she thought of the blood; she thought of the screams; she thought of Yelena’s anguished cry as Natasha fell to the ground. “I can’t let you do this on your own.” Swallowing thickly, she reached out to Yelena’s fingers on the table and clasped them softly. “Maybe you guys don’t feel the same way I do, but… I, uh… I think of you as my friends.”

    Kate was twenty-three years old, yet she felt increasingly embarrassed at her admission the longer Yelena and Natasha looked at her. “Stop looking at me again, please! It’s not that weird!”

    Chuckling, Natasha placed the mug of tea in front of Kate.

    “It’s not weird,” she agreed, patting Kate’s head with the palm of her hand. “It’s cute.” “That’s worse!” Kate exclaimed, jumping to her feet because she couldn’t contain herself. “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said as she got up from her chair. “I have only had one friend. You can be the second.” At her words, Kate’s heart nearly exploded with affection and a terrible desire to grab Yelena by the face and kiss her forehead.

    “Aw. I’m honored,” Natasha said, putting her left forearm on Kate’s shoulder.

    “I do not mean you.” Yelena rolled her eyes. “You are my sister. I do not have to like you for that to be the case.”

    “Ouch,” Natasha retorted. “Right for the jugular.” With her right hand, she patted Kate’s sternum, sending tiny signals of comfort to Kate’s hindbrain. “Guess I’m not furry enough to be your friend. It’s alright. I’m sure Kate will fill the position adequately.”

    Pursing her lips, Yelena shook her head and crossed her arms.

    “So?” Kate asked, feeling slightly foolish about the fact that they were all standing around in the kitchen together because she couldn’t sit still. “Will you let me help on the mission?” “They will have silver,” Yelena hedged, showing her concern despite what Kate assumed were her best efforts. When Kate opened her mouth to protest, Yelena poked her between the eyebrows, shutting her up immediately. “We will discuss this when we discuss the plan.”

    “Okay,” Kate said, rubbing her forehead. “Fine.”

    “Good. Now that that’s settled,” Natasha said, shaking Kate’s shoulder back and forth. “Let’s talk about how we’ll get the bastards.”

    ##

    December 19th, 2016 | 11:25 P.M.

    They had a plan.

    They had a plan, and the first steps were put in motion. More would have to wait until tomorrow since Kate was about to disappear into the throes of the full moon. She’d spent much of the day in the basement gym, working off the excess energy as best as she could.

    “Are you certain this is a good idea?” Yelena stood next to the open door of the laundry room, her arms crossed over her chest and her eyes scanning the walls and furniture inside. Today, she wore a different pair of oversized jeans a shade darker than the ones she wore the day before. The yellow sweater she wore over it was thick and woolen and had a green trim around the sleeves. Natasha was somewhere upstairs. “Would it not be better to let you out the backdoor and do your business there?”

    “Ha-ha,” Kate said as the moon’s magic did its work. She felt it in her very fingertips, kicking her senses into high gear. Due to her greatly enhanced sense of smell, she was sure she could smell Yelena’s uncertainty underneath her comforting scent. With her face to the window, Kate longed to see the moon between the clouds. From down here, all she could see were the rays of moonlight on the lawn in front of the building. “It’s better this way. We can’t risk being spotted.”

    “I think the chances of that are very slim,” Yelena said in a low tone. Kate could barely hear her. Her eyes were fixed on the glittering light passing through the clouds, dancing on the frozen grass. Before she knew what was happening, she’d placed her fingers on the glass of the small window and let out a deep, anguished whine. “I think we should—”

    “No,” Kate said, snapping herself out of the trance and turning away from the window. Her body shivered all over, but she tried to hide it from Yelena, who looked halfway between concerned and mutinous. “I’ll be okay down here. It’s safer this way. Just lock me in here until morning, and don’t let me out. No matter what you hear.”

    “This does not fill me with confidence, Kate Bishop,” Yelena insisted, staying rooted to her spot by the door while Kate’s mind continued deteriorating. Midnight was so close, and the moon’s pull so strong; she could hardly make sense of right and wrong in this situation. All she knew was that she couldn’t screw it up again. She couldn’t put her new friends in danger. The hunters would be looking for her. The hunters could be anywhere.

    “Please, Yelena,” Kate said, crossing the space between them. She didn’t dare touch her friend for fear of squeezing her too hard, but she looked at her with all the puppy-dog energy she could muster in her current state. “Please do this for me.”

    “I will do this, but only because you have asked me to. If you change your mind, I will let you out.” Yelena nodded to herself as though her last statement settled it for her. Shaking her head, Kate lifted her hands, then dropped them.

    “No!” she said, digging her nails into her own arms instead of Yelena’s. “You cannot let me out. My wolf won’t be happy that we’re locked up, but it’s not thinking rationally. I am.” The moon called to her—go outside, it said. Run with me, it said. Be free beneath my glorious rays—

    “You are shaking,” Yelena said, cupping Kate’s cheeks. Suddenly, Kate realized she was bent over at the waist, her hands on her knees and her eyes closed to ward off the call. When she looked up, Yelena’s eyes seemed more worried than Kate had ever seen. “This is not right, is it? Locking you up in here? It is not right.”

    “No,” Kate whispered, swallowing thickly around the sudden lump of emotion in her throat. “But it’s what I want.” With a slight frown, Yelena brushed her right thumb across Kate’s cheek, her cold fingers burning the overheated skin. At the touch, a pitiful whine fell from Kate’s lips. “Please…”

    “Okay,” Yelena said, moving her left hand from Kate’s cheek to her hair. “Okay. We will lock you away.” At the mention of the word we, Kate wondered what Yelena would tell her sister after she’d closed the door and left the basement.

    “Thank you,” Kate said, leaning forward to touch her forehead against Yelena’s chest. It was an instinctual movement borne of the pain of trapping herself and the simple desire for comfort and connection. Still, almost as soon as she felt the soft fabric of Yelena’s yellow sweater on her skin, she wanted to recoil. Before she could move, Yelena’s arms snaked around her torso and held her close. One hand brushed the sweaty back of Kate’s neck while another stroked her shoulders and spine.

    “You will not die from this, will you?” Yelena asked softly, settling her chin on Kate’s hair and squeezing her closer with her arms. Yelena’s body was warm and strong and smelled so good that Kate almost forgot to answer. As a wolf, she did not have the ability to purr, but if she did, Kate knew she would be revving up a storm right now. For just a few seconds, even the call of the moon quieted down, allowing her a moment of peace.

    “I’ll be fine. My wolf won’t be happy, but it won’t do anything to hurt us,” Kate said, putting as much confidence into her words as possible. “I promise.” Rubbing her cheek against Yelena’s chest, Kate let out a sigh of pure relief. “You should go. It’s almost time; I can feel it.”

    With a hum, Yelena moved her hands from Kate’s neck and back to her cheeks, then gently pushed her away from her chest. Keeping Kate loosely in her grip, she tilted her head to the side and studied her face from top to bottom.

    “Tell Lucky I said hi,” she whispered, then immediately smoothed her expression back into perfect neutrality. Dropping her hands, she turned and walked to the door, grabbing the key in the lock on the way out. When she closed the door, she looked at Kate through the rapidly closing space between it and the jamb. Kate waved, and Yelena waved back. Then, the door was shut and locked, and Kate was left by herself.

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 12:31 A.M.

    The window was right there. Why couldn’t she climb through? With a growl, she jumped at it, her thick body pushing against the obvious exit. As she bounced off the wall, the growl grew louder, and the snarls more vicious. The moon called for her; her rays slipped around her bones and flesh and ripped at her to chase the night. Again, she threw herself at the window, her mouth opening to bare her teeth at the offending glass that wouldn’t let her pass. When she bounced off the wall this time, she did so with much more force, crashing against a solid object in a heap of limbs and fur. Wood splintered underneath her, crunching as she pushed herself upright. She didn’t feel any pain from the impact, her mind too preoccupied with the moon and how she might be able to get to it.

    Slowly lumbering to the window, she sat down in front of it and stared at the grass beyond. She howled.

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 01:45 A.M.

    She felt stranger and stranger with every passing second. There were pieces of wood all over the floor, and one of the washing machines was on its side. Her howl reverberated around the room, sounding odd to her own ears. When she looked down at her paws, she saw they were not what she had expected; a moment of clarity sent her spiraling back to where she was—the basement, the laundry room, the full moon.

    Pushing herself further upright, she glanced from wall to wall, seeing the deep, red gouges in the concrete. Whose blood was that?

    With a growl, she bent over at the waist—why was she upright? Why wasn’t she on all fours? Was she human again? Another glance at her paws revealed them to be thin and humanoid but still unmistakably covered in fur. She was not the wolf she was supposed to be.

    She placed her paw-hands on the window and focused on the frozen grass outside. It glittered and shimmered in the moonlight, taunting her with its unmoving blades. It was out there with the moon—why wasn’t it swaying to her music? All she wanted was to move. All she needed was to run.

    Blinded by a sudden fury she didn’t recognize, she slammed her claws against the glass over and over again. She repeated the motion. Over and over again. She repeated the motion. Over and over again. She repeated the motion. Again. Again. Again. But nothing happened. The window didn’t break, and she couldn’t leave.

    The dangerous growl in her throat turned into a pitiful whine as she watched her paws leave streaks of red in their wake. Whose blood was that?

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 03:14 A.M.

    The door was there; the door needed to stay closed.

    The door could be pulled off at the hinges; the door needed to stay closed.

    The door was the way to the forest; the door needed to stay closed.

    The door shut her off from the moon; the door needed to stay closed.

    The door opened.

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 03:15 A.M.

    She lunged for the moving door with a snarl before catching herself mid-jump. Digging her nails into the concrete floor, she stopped and forced herself to the ground, pressing her paws over her muzzle and howling desperately. No, no, no, no! Can’t go out! Can’t be caught!

    Can’t put us in danger!

    The door made a clicking sound as it was closed and relocked, but her mind instantly focused on something else. There was a new scent in the room, sticking out prominently amongst the smell of detergent, water, and blood.

    Opening her eyes, she came face-to-face with a woman. Pupils dilating, the wolf lowered her paws from her muzzle and placed them on the ground.

    “Kate Bishop?” the woman asked, standing stock-still in front of the wall, watching slowly as the wolf got up on her hind legs.

    Chest heaving, the wolf shook its head. The scent was too much, and the room was too small. She needed to get out of here and run with the moon, and the woman—the woman was between her and that door.

    “Kate Bishop,” the woman repeated, her words sounding very far away. The wolf’s arms and legs trembled as it stood up to its full height, its ears brushing the ceiling. Spit dripped from her teeth as she bared them in a snarl. “Lucky—”

    The wolf jumped, crossing the distance between them in a matter of seconds. Within an instant, the woman’s back was against the door and the wolf’s paws flat against it, metal groaning under the strain of the claws. Pulling back her right arm, the wolf barely noticed the pair of hands grabbing its chest.

    Just as the claws were about to strike, the woman cupped the wolf’s cheek.

    “No,” the woman whispered, keeping the other hand tangled in the fur of the wolf’s chest. “You will not hurt me. I have given you no reason to.” The fingers on the wolf’s cheeks moved to her ear, slowly scratching the skin behind it. “I’m so sorry you are hurting. You don’t have to be alone now.”

    Paws falling away, the wolf took a step back. Then, the wolf took another. Body shaking, she brought her paws to her muzzle once more, quickly shuffling into the corner and covering herself up as best as she could.

    “Shh,” the woman said, following close behind and kneeling on the splinters. “I am not mad.”

    A whimper fell from the wolf’s mouth as it looked at the woman—the woman—Yelena.

    Yelena was here. Her hands were gentle as she drew her fingers through the fur of the wolf’s ears. Kate’s ears. Kate was down in the basement—Kate had nearly tried to—looking down at her paw-hand-claws, Kate trembled from head to toe.

    Moon sickness, she thought, thinking of her mother’s disapproving eyes. Moon fever.

    Monster, monster, monster. Unable to speak, Kate could merely whine and whimper, barely registering Yelena’s hands slowly cupping her big wolf-head. Softly, Yelena guided Kate to lie on her side, her head coming to rest on Yelena’s lap.

    “That’s it, Lucky,” Yelena whispered, running her fingers along Kate’s head and neck. “Good girl.” Just before her eyes closed, Kate saw that none of the four washing machines or dryers looked quite right. She didn’t remember destroying them or knocking them over. She barely remembered anything from before Yelena opened the door. “Shh. Try to sleep if you can.”

    Shaking like a leaf, Kate felt her body shrink to its regular full moon size. Her paws shifted, and her limbs receded, leaving behind the body of a larger-than-average wolf instead of a fully grown werewolf.

    With a hum, Yelena hugged Kate’s head to her stomach.

    “There you are,” she said, scratching the skin of Kate’s chin and throat. “I have missed you, my friend.”

    If Kate had been able to cry as a wolf, she would have. As it were, she had to make do with looking up at Yelena’s face and licking her cheek. But then again… judging by the smile on Yelena’s face, it was better this way.

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 05:30 A.M.

    Kate did not sleep that night. Instead, she shifted in and out of consciousness, twitching and growling in her half-coherent state. Whenever she shivered especially violently, arms would encircle her head and shoulders, and a voice would accompany the embrace.

    You are safe. No one will hurt us here. You can rest now and run tomorrow.

    Was it the moon who spoke so sweetly to her? Was it the moon who cradled her close and kissed her muzzle? Was it the moon who hummed and sang to her? It must be, she thought. It must be her. My moon. My beautiful moon.

    When she finally got back to herself, she noticed the splinters of wood all over the floor first. They poked and prodded at her naked body, never penetrating the skin but irritating her overwrought senses. What were they doing here? And why did the forest smell like wet laundry?

    A memory dripped into her ear at the sound of another’s gentle breathing so close to her. It spread across her brain and dug its teeth into her neurons, awakening that awful feeling of dread Kate became increasingly more familiar with as time went on.

    “Yelena,” Kate breathed, shivering on the laundry room floor. She was naked, and the air was freezing cold, yet Kate barely felt it. “Yelena.” She remembered her friend stepping into the room with her; she remembered—“Yelena!”

    “I am right here,” a voice said, drawing Kate’s attention. Slowly opening her eyes, Kate turned her head on the soft surface of what she’d thought was the forest. A halo of bright hair fell around a calm and gentle face. There you are, Kate thought, reaching up with a trembling hand to cup the warm cheek of her moon.

    “Did I hurt her?” Kate asked, half-blinded by the lingering effects of the shift. “Did I hurt Yelena?”

    Her moon was quiet, her expression impossible to read. Then, she cupped Kate’s hand on her cheek and softly shook her head.

    “No,” her moon whispered, holding Kate’s hand tenderly to her skin.

    Tears gathered in Kate’s ears, slipping out as she turned towards the source of the warmth under her cheek. Dropping her hand, she curled it around the moon’s strong waist and sobbed in unrestrained relief. Fingers gently moved across her scalp, scratching the skin behind her ear.

    “Sleep,” her moon said, holding her close. “You have suffered enough.”

    Kate did not argue with her.

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 09:55 A.M.

    There was a blanket wrapped around her and a pillow under her head.

    “What…” she muttered, pulling her arms free and rubbing her eyes with her left palm as she let out a mega-sized yawn. Sitting up, she felt the crunch of wood underneath her and grimaced at the sensation. “Definitely not one of my better full moons.” Stretching her arms over her head, she enjoyed the pop-pop popping along her back before looking around herself. When she saw movement out of the corner of her eyes, she flinched. “Oh—jeez!

    Yelena! You scared me half to death. What the heck are you doing down here?”

    Yelena was sitting against the wall next to the door of the laundry room. She wore different clothes than the day before, but Kate was too preoccupied with calming her racing heart to take special notice of which colors she was mixing today.

    “Waiting for you to wake up,” Yelena said simply, her expression as neutral as it often was. Her knees were pulled to her chest, and her arms wrapped loosely around them. Despite her neutral expression, something about her seemed supremely pissed off. “I would have taken you upstairs after you turned back, but I thought it unwise to ruin more of the house.” She gestured at the room, and Kate finally focused her mind enough to take in her surroundings. The washing machines and dryers were completely torn apart and pushed over in different directions. The table that had once been pushed against the opposite wall was completely shattered and strewn across the floor. The window was damaged in several places as tiny spider-webbed cracks crawled along the surface. There was blood on it; there was blood on the walls.

    “Whose…” Kate was almost afraid to ask. Looking down at her hands and arms, she was shocked to see them covered in blood, too. “Yelena.” Shaking like a leaf, Kate stared at her friend in unabashed horror. “Yelena, whose blood is this?”

    Never flinching, Yelena leaned her head against the wall behind her.

    “It’s yours,” she said, poorly designed anger flitting across her face before she could conceal it. Her fingers clenched on her shins, and the corners of her mouth unmistakably turned down as she stared at Kate. “You promised you would not hurt yourself.”

    Continuing to stare at the bloody gashes in the concrete walls, Kate wondered just what the hell had happened. Shaking her head, she tried to remember the night, but everything seemed covered by a fog of her wolf’s fury. “It’s never been like this,” Kate muttered, putting her hand against her head and squeezing her eyes shut. “When I first turned, my mom had me locked up every full moon, and it was never—I promise, Yelena, it was never this bad.”

    “How long has it been?” Yelena asked, her voice sounding very far away as Kate continued to push and prod at her own head. Her wolf-heart beat erratically in her chest, refusing to give up its secrets. It hurt to feel so separated from herself. The wolf was a part of her—one of the parts she liked the best. It made her strong when she was weak and gave her solace when she felt alone.

    “What do you mean?” Kate asked, slowly opening her eyes.

    “How long has it been,” Yelena repeated, letting go of her legs, “since you were locked up like this.” The words were soft when they left Yelena’s lips. When they reached Kate, they slammed into her chest like bricks from a crumbling building striking unprotected earth.

    After a moment of stunned silence on Kate’s part, Yelena allowed her legs to straighten and her back to bow, her hands landing on her knees and her blonde hair falling about her face. “I only ask because… I could not imagine it. Going back to the restrictions… the rules… the cage. Not now. Not after everything. Not after tasting true freedom,” Yelena said, leaning back against the concrete and allowing her eyes to sweep the room. More anger briefly passed over her face. This time, Kate didn’t think she was the recipient. “You said you were used to the cage.” Pursing her lips, Yelena shook her head. “Kate Bishop, I believe you underestimated your own desire to be free.” Suddenly, her green eyes were locked on Kate, and what Kate saw in them was kinship. “Do not lock yourself away again.”

    “I wanted to protect you,” Kate admitted, staring at Yelena while the destruction of the laundry room took up her periphery. “The last time I was out there, I got caught and started this whole mess. I don’t want that to happen again. I don’t want to risk it.” Looking away, Kate caught sight of the blood on her arms, barely remembering the sight of her own hand- like paws.

    “It is not easy.” Yelena pushed herself to her feet, slowly closing the distance between them. When she slumped down next to Kate, she sat with her legs crossed and her hands held loosely in her lap. “Living your life with a death sentence over your head if you slip up is stressful.” She leaned over to nudge Kate’s shoulder with her own. “But you could have asked us for help. We could have spent the night out there with you. We could have kept each other safe.”

    “I have already asked so much of you,” Kate muttered, her eyes fixated on the dried splotches of blood on her forearms.

    “You have asked for nothing,” Yelena retorted, reaching over to take Kate’s hand. At the gentle touch, butterflies erupted in Kate’s stomach, and a flash of memory hit her like a freight train. The image in her mind was of a woman leaning over her, brightly shining hair falling around her face as she spoke sweetly to her. Soft warmth spread through Kate’s chest and limbs, making her feel almost as strong as the pull of the full moon. “Besides,” Yelena continued, prompting Kate to look at her, “you protect us; we protect you. Simple math, yes?”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, throat tight and heart beating double-time. Leaning forward, she nudged Yelena’s shoulder with her head. When there was no resistance, Kate closed her eyes and let out a long breath. A beat later, Yelena’s fingers settled at the base of her neck. “Thanks.” “Stop being ridiculous, Kate Bishop.”

    “Okay.”

    ##

    December 20th, 2016 | 10:22 A.M.

    They sat together for several minutes. While Kate continued working on her memory from the previous night, Yelena merely seemed to stare into the distance. Neither of them spoke, which was a first for Kate. She had a million thoughts and ideas running through her head at any given time, yet she felt no desire to voice any of them.

    When they finally parted, Kate grabbed the blanket and bundled herself up – no use getting any fresh clothes dirty when they wouldn’t have the means of cleaning them. With a guilty look, Kate eyed the washing machines before making her way upstairs.

    Kate didn’t dawdle in the shower, mainly because she was hungry enough to eat two entire boars and also because Yelena promised her bacon and eggs for breakfast. Maybe, if she got lucky, Natasha would make pancakes for dessert. All the sugar from their expensive maple syrup never failed to give her a major energy boost.

    When Kate descended the stairs in a fresh purple sweatsuit, she saw Natasha in the living room. At the moment, Natasha was walking back and forth in front of the windows with a phone held to her ear. Slowing down, Kate watched as a smile flitted across Natasha’s lips, a smile that Kate had only caught glimpses of here and there.

    “Hey,” Natasha said, stopping in front of the window and looking out over the lawn. A moment of silence elapsed, in which the person Natasha called answered the greeting. The grin on Natasha’s face grew wider and warmer. “I know, I know. You don’t have to yell at me this early in the morning.” The genuine fondness in her voice almost startled Kate, who had mainly dealt with Natasha’s teasing. “Look, I’m just calling to report damage to your farmhouse—I wouldn’t call that an excuse, necessarily.”

    Natasha’s fingers brushed the windowsill in front of her, and the slight movement was enough to kick Kate back into gear. Further descending the stairs, she tried not to look like she listened in. When Natasha spotted her, she lifted her chin in a greeting, not quite able to hide her smile. Then, she turned back to the window. “Come on. We both know you won’t.”

    Kate was almost under the arch of the doorway when she heard Natasha laugh. “Because you like me too much.”

    Yelena was at the stove, moving her spatula through a large helping of eggs.

    “Feel better?” Yelena asked, pulling the pan from the stove to shovel the eggs into a large bowl already half-full of the first round of scrambling. When Kate nodded, Yelena nodded as well. “These are for you.” Setting the bowl on the table, Yelena handed Kate a spoon and a fork and gestured at her to sit down. “Natasha and I already had breakfast.”

    “She’s on the phone with someone,” Kate said, glancing over her shoulder at Natasha’s back. When her stomach growled at her too loudly to ignore, she sat down and dug into the eggs. Yelena had already placed several sauces on the table, so Kate grabbed the nearest bottle of green salsa. With a full mouth, she continued. “Is that safe?”

    Yelena wiped the pan with a paper towel before walking to the refrigerator and pulling out several packages of bacon. “Should be,” she said, opening the first plastic cover and turning the gas stove back on. “Natasha is not stupid enough to use a phone that could be compromised. But she refuses to elaborate.”

    With a hum around another mouthful of delicious egg, Kate tried to ignore the soft laughter from the living room. It was quiet enough that Yelena wasn’t able to hear, but Kate’s ears were superior. Even though the waning moon sapped some of Kate’s strength, her hearing still worked too well to be considered human.

    “No, don’t worry,” Natasha said, her voice so affectionate it made Kate wish her hearing was worse. “I’m not in any trouble. Not any more than I already was. Once this is all over, I’ll tell you about it. I promise.”

    Yelena flipped the first round of bacon in the pan. “Eggs good?” she asked, picking up the crunchy meat with a pair of tongs and putting it on a paper towel. Her question pulled Kate back to the kitchen and out of the living room, for which she was grateful. Listening to Natasha flirt was weird.

    “Really good,” Kate said, cheeks bulging with egg and salsa. “Thanks.”

    “Okay,” Yelena replied, transferring the bacon from the paper towel to another plate while the second batch sizzled in the pan. When she turned to put the plate down next to Kate, Yelena’s right hand settled on Kate’s upper back. It was a casual gesture, yet it sent shivers through Kate’s body. “How much bacon?”

    “Uh,” Kate replied, looking up at Yelena with a no doubt stupid expression.

    “I will make all of it,” Yelena said, stroking her palm across Kate’s shoulders before turning back to the stove. A fuse fizzled in Kate’s brain, leading to a powder keg she really didn’t need blowing up amidst all of this mess.

    “Thanks,” Kate mumbled again, forcing her mind to focus solely on her food.
