    now, all that's left for me to say is: MERRY CHRISTMAS 🎄🎅 cw: blood and violence See the end of the chapter for more notes December 24th, 2016 | 11:25 A.M.

    Yelena only ever celebrated two Christmases. Back in Ohio, there was a tree and a small mountain of presents for her and Natasha. She only vaguely remembered the jaunty Christmas music pouring from the radio as they unwrapped the boxes of toys and articles of clothing. One year, there was a little red bear Yelena loved with all her heart that she’d henceforth taken with her everywhere she went. After everything, it probably ended up in a landfill somewhere, never to be seen again—like they’d planned to do with her.

    “Hold it steady,” Natasha said, pushing pine needles out of her face as she attempted to fasten the mount. “What’s the point of werewolf strength if you can’t even stop a Christmas tree from shaking back and forth?”

    “I am holding it steady! You keep knocking into it!” Kate defended herself, glaring down at the top of Natasha’s head as she clutched the tree trunk with both hands. There were pine needles in her face, too.

    For her part, Yelena lounged on the two-seater with a cup of hot chocolate cradled in between her hands. “You are both doing a terrible job,” she said before sipping the sugary sweet drink and closing her eyes. “Why are you like this?”

    “In my defense,” Kate started immediately, looking at Yelena through the dense foliage around her face. “My mom always hired someone to set up the trees after my dad died.” With a helpless half-shrug, Kate readjusted her grip on the trunk. “I was too young to help before. And then we only really decorated the one in the living room. My mom left the others in the foyer to the professionals, too.”

    “Finally,” Natasha grumbled as something audibly clicked into place. Pushing herself to her feet, Natasha surveyed their accomplishments while Kate seemed reluctant to let go just yet. “I think we did a great job, personally.”

    Yelena lazily gazed up at her sister.

    “It is crooked,” she said, taking another sip of hot chocolate.

    “You’re crooked,” Natasha replied, stopping next to Yelena to shake out her sweater. Pine needles flew in all directions, but Yelena merely covered her cup. “Now get off your ass. I bought some cheap stuff in town to decorate this thing with.”

    Finally, Kate slowly let go of the tree, ready to grab it just in case it toppled over. Looking between Kate and her sister, Yelena forced herself not to smile. Maybe she didn’t have her bear or a life of happy Christmases, but right now, she had a lovely alternative.

    ##

    December 24th, 2016 | 11:39 P.M.

    Putting the mission into motion was postponed until after the new year, putting Yelena in a bit of a mental slump. Working on the mission with Natasha had given her a clear goal and kept her mind busy. They still spent some time planning now—with Kate included as per her adamant request—but not as much as before.

    With so much meaningless downtime on her hands, Yelena found herself missing the routine of the cabin. Getting up in the morning to let Lucky out for her morning wee while Yelena herself went to the bathroom. Walking around the cabin and checking her snares for fresh meat. Preparing and cooking that meat after feeding Lucky. Washing up after herself and keeping the cabin clean. Taking another walk around the area—always with Lucky towards the end of their stay, after the wolf’s leg healed sufficiently to walk longer distances. Coming back and reading Safe Harbor with Lucky, either in bed or on the sofa. Then, getting to bed and sleeping with the considerable presence of a warm and fuzzy animal next to or on top of her.

    The farmhouse was much more comfortable than the cabin in many ways. It was warmer, it was bigger, and it was better furnished. But she couldn’t say that it felt safer. With the Red Room catching up to them at the hangar, the illusion of safety Yelena had allowed herself to become wrapped up in had been snatched away.

    Now, sitting underneath the lit-up Christmas tree, Yelena wondered how much more time she could borrow. In a way, she understood Kate’s fear of discovery all too well. The pain and terror in her eyes before Yelena locked her away was as real as the resulting chaos that sprung up around her.

    “Oh,” someone said quietly, shaking Yelena out of her thoughts before they could drift to the hulking body of the mindless werewolf that she had discovered after hours of listening to Kate Bishop’s excruciating cries. “I didn’t know you were still up.”

    Pushing down her immediate fight response, Yelena looked up at Kate Bishop’s sheepish expression.

    “I should say that to you. You went to bed an hour ago, no?” Yelena asked, returning her gaze to the Christmas tree. Yelena’s knees were pulled to her chest, and her arms were curled around them. After Natasha went to sleep, Yelena had grabbed a blanket to wrap herself in. “What do you have behind your back?”

    In a Christmas bauble, Yelena saw Kate’s mouth open and close.

    “Nothing,” Kate said, her voice sounding mildly squeaky.

    “You are not a good liar,” Yelena stated, grabbing the edge of the blanket with her left hand and pulling it tighter around herself. There was a chill in the air that Kate Bishop could not feel—but then, Yelena wasn’t sure if she was imagining it. “Did I ruin a surprise?”

    “Ruin is a strong word,” Kate said, finally revealing her hands and the presents they held. “But I think it would have been sweet to catch your expression first thing in the morning after seeing these. I was going to get up early and everything.”

    Yelena watched in the reflection of the same bauble as Kate joined her in front of the tree, kneeling and placing the presents underneath it. She only looked away when Kate plonked down beside her, the force from her back against the couch jostling Yelena.

    “I’m sorry,” Yelena said, surprised that she meant it.

    “Eh,” Kate said with a shrug, putting her hands on her thighs and leaning forward to stretch her back and work off some extra energy. Some part of Kate was always moving, and Yelena was starting to appreciate this. Kate’s many little quirks gave Yelena something to focus on when she talked to her friend—always something to catch her attention. She and Natasha were different—they were trained to be still, to be silent, to kill without mercy and without being noticed. “Natasha knows what she’s getting, anyway. She probably snooped.”

    “You have sent her out to purchase these?” Yelena asked, nodding towards the presents.

    “To pick them up from town, actually. She showed me how to online-shop on the supercomputer and where to send the stuff so we don’t get caught,” Kate explained with a slight nod to herself, leaning back against the couch while her naked toes moved back and forth. She’d already dressed for bed in a comfortable-looking black sweatsuit, her hair pulled into a ponytail. “I, uh,” Kate continued, lifting her hand to scratch at her cheek. “I still don’t feel super comfortable, you know, leaving the house. Going places. Places where there might be cameras that I can’t see or that I’ll miss.”

    With a hum, Yelena placed her chin on her knees and once more returned her gaze to the Christmas tree. She wasn’t sure why she spoke when she next opened her mouth, but she couldn’t quite stop herself.

    “This is the first Christmas I’ve spent with Natasha since we were kids.”

    Kate’s toes stopped moving, and her hand dropped back to her lap.

    “Oh,” she said, her blue eyes focusing on the side of Yelena’s face. After a beat of silence, Kate looked at her knees. “This is the first Christmas I’ve spent without my mom.” Her chin moved, clearly visible in the golden surface of the bauble.

    With an inaudible sigh, Yelena turned her head towards Kate and placed her cheek on her knees instead.

    “If you want to talk about it, I will not go. I can listen,” Yelena said, surprising herself again by how much she meant her clumsy offering of support. There had been something terrible and beautiful about the massive werewolf in the basement—something that she could not quite shake. Something behind those eyes that she had recognized.

    “Thanks,” Kate mumbled, rubbing her side where Yelena knew the bite wound from the other werewolf to be. “I—I don’t think I’m ready. To talk about it.” Mimicking Yelena’s posture, Kate pulled her knees to her chest and wrapped her arms around them.

    “Okay,” Yelena said softly, leaning slowly to the side until their shoulders touched.

    “The tree turned out great,” Kate whispered, sliding her right hand up her leg and left arm until her fingers were almost touching Yelena’s shoulder. As expected, she was warm, whereas Yelena was cold. “You have a great eye for decorating and symmetry.”

    The left side of Yelena’s mouth lifted as she turned her head upwards to gaze at the star all the way at the top of the tree. Yelena stood on a chair from the kitchen to reach the top while Kate held onto the back and Yelena’s waist like Yelena wasn’t a skilled assassin with perfect balance. It had its perks, she thought. Being cared for.

    “Ah,” Yelena breathed, her eyes catching the reflection of the clock over the doorway that led to the stairs. Turning to Kate, Yelena found her friend was already looking at her. Leaning closer, Yelena nudged Kate’s forehead with her own before pulling back. “Merry Christmas, Kate Bishop.”

    “Merry Christmas, Yelena,” Kate replied softly, unfurling her arms from around her legs.

    Blinking quickly, Kate brought her right hand to Yelena’s blanket-covered shoulder. “Hug?” she asked, sounding hopeful and much younger than she was.

    Instead of replying verbally, Yelena opened her blanket and lifted her arms in a clear gesture of assent. A second later, Kate leaned into her personal space, her chin on Yelena’s right shoulder and her big arms wrapped around Yelena’s waist. With the blanket wrapped around them both, Yelena instantly felt warmer than she had all day.

    ##

    December 25th, 2016 | 07:45 A.M.

    The concept of sleeping in was known to Yelena—she’d just never been particularly good at it. She’d been trained from a very young age to function on very few hours of sleep. Kate Bishop, however, seemed to be an expert who was content to stay unconscious for hours on end. So, when Yelena descended the stairs only to find Kate Bishop already sitting on the couch, the only reason she wasn’t surprised was the memories of the previous night.

    “Good morning,” Yelena said, stopping behind Kate, who looked up at her with the big, glowing eyes of someone pleasantly surprised by something.

    “Hey!” Kate replied before returning her gaze to the space she was staring at before. “Did you do this? Or was it Natasha?”

    Lazily, Yelena swept her gaze across the mountain of presents under the tree. The craftsmanship of the packaging was the only way to tell the new presents apart from the ones Kate put there the night before. While Kate’s presents looked a bit haphazardly put together, the new presents had perfect edges and beautiful seams.

    “These are not mine,” Yelena revealed, moving her eyes to the radio on the coffee table.

    “What is that?”

    “Huh?” Kate asked, tearing her gaze away from the presents before following Yelena’s line of sight. “Oh, that. Uh, if I remember correctly, the radio guy said something about singing saxophones?” She waited for a few seconds, listening to the music. “Yep. This one’s Last Christmas.”

    “Last Christmas?” Yelena asked, passing by Kate to turn up the volume of the radio.

    “Yeah! It’s from the eighties! La-ast Christmas, I gave you my heart. But the very next day, you gave it away-ay,” Kate sang, clashing horribly with the melody of the singing saxophones. The way she swayed her body and head gave Yelena a surprising amount of joy.

    “That’s, like, the most popular Christmas song ever made.”

    “In your opinion,” a third voice said from the doorway. Yelena had heard the footsteps a few seconds ago, but Kate had evidently been paying attention to Yelena and the radio too much to notice the approach. Whipping her head around, Kate pouted as she looked at Natasha.

    “Personally, I think there are some other strong contestants.”

    Leaving the two to their debate, Yelena hummed along to the singing saxophones as she crossed the threshold to the kitchen.

    ##

    December 25th, 2016 | 08:33 A.M.

    “It’s snowing,” Kate said with her face nearly pressed against the window. Her innocent excitement would have been enough to melt even the hardest of hearts, but Yelena ignored the feelings in favor of sipping her hot chocolate. Natasha was sprawled across the three- seater with a blanket on her lap, her left foot moving in time with the music. By now, the singing saxophones had been replaced with actual Christmas music—or so Yelena had been told. “Whenever it snowed in New York, my dad and I would go out and build a snowman in Central Park.”

    “Do you want to build a snowman?” Yelena asked, eyeing the presents under the tree. If Kate Bishop wanted to prioritize her childhood memories, then Yelena would put aside her curiosity about what lay beneath the wrapping paper and tape.

    “Come on, let's go and play,” Kate said in a high-pitched sing-song voice that would have startled Yelena had it not been for her training.

    Natasha’s laughter pulled Yelena’s attention away from her odd new friend.

    “Oh my gosh, your face,” Natasha hollered, leaning back her head and letting out a loud and obnoxious snort of glee. “Merry fucking Christmas, Elsa.” There was a blanket draped over her lap that nearly slid off as Natasha continued to laugh.

    “You are unwell,” Yelena said, returning her hot chocolate to the coffee table and getting off the two-seater. Picking up a pillow, Yelena held it tightly with both hands, her expression cool and collected. “I will put you out of your misery.”

    “Okay,” Kate said, rounding the Christmas tree and nearly pulling the plug on the lights in the process. The Santa hat on her head jingled as she pushed herself in between Yelena and Natasha, her hands settling over Yelena’s on the pillow. “How about we open our presents before we murder each other?”

    “You are lucky Kate Bishop cares for you,” Yelena said, rolling her eyes and pulling away from Kate’s very warm hands. With an unimpressed look at her sister, Yelena tossed the pillow back on the couch to her left.

    “My hero in Jack Wolfskin boots,” Natasha drawled, sitting up on the three-seater.

    “Anyway.” She poked Kate’s backside with her left foot. “Kate, be a good boy and pick up the three identical boxes under the singing reindeer decoration.”

    Making a derisive noise with her tongue but feeling inexplicably warm and happy, Yelena sat back on her couch and crossed her legs on the cushions. “Undank ist der Welt Lohn, Kate Bishop. Better get used to it, hm?”

    “Un-what?” Kate mumbled, crouching down in front of the tree. “All of this over a stupid movie reference…”

    ##

    December 25th, 2016 | 08:51 A.M.

    There was an ugly reindeer on Yelena’s chest that sang every time she squeezed its nose. She hated and loved it in equal measure, especially because Natasha was in the same ugly sweater. Only the decoration on her sister’s chest was a snowman with an uncomfortably long carrot nose that flashed obnoxiously. And then there was the ridiculous wolf woven into Kate’s oversized jumper that howled festively whenever Natasha accidentally bumped into her.

    Since Natasha occupied the three-seater all by herself, Yelena had graciously allowed Kate to sit with her. They’d switched off the radio in favor of the television, which showed an animated movie Yelena had not seen before. A red-headed girl and a red-headed boy in fancy party clothes were singing to each other about doors, only occasionally drawing Yelena’s attention away from the present in Kate’s hands.

    “Oh,” Kate said as she tore away the final piece of wrapping paper. “I wonder what this is…” “It is a pair of shoes,” Yelena offered helpfully, pointing at the words on the box.

    “I was being sarcastic,” Kate mumbled out of the corner of her mouth as she pulled off the top to reveal a new pair of boots. “I know exactly what these are…” She picked up the left shoe and examined the thick and richly textured bottom. With a shake of her head, she showed Yelena the logo while looking over at Natasha. “Thanks.”

    “I thought you’d like them,” Natasha drawled, holding a pillow and a card loosely against her stomach. Kate had written her the card to go along with the first present. The small, rectangular piece of cardboard in Natasha’s hands had once been intended to congratulate someone on buying a house, but Kate had glued a blank piece of printer paper over the Congrats on Your First Home!-slogan. Instead, she’d written Sorry About Wrecking the Laundry Room on it in terrible handwriting before coloring in the washing machine that was already on the card. “They didn’t have any purple ones.”

    “Black works fine,” Kate said, putting the boot back in the box and closing the lid. When she set the box on the floor, Yelena caught the quick flash of a smile on her friend’s lips. The visual filled Yelena’s chest with warmth, especially when Kate accidentally set off the wolf howling, and the smile on her mouth turned into a pout.

    “Yelena,” Natasha said, gesturing at one of the more misshapen presents on the floor. “You go next.”

    “If I must.” Getting to her feet, Yelena crouched down and grabbed the first one with her name. The present was not terribly heavy nor particularly large. When she sat back on the couch, she felt something shift inside the present, telling her that the shape did not reflect the contents. As she pulled at one of the glued-down red-and-green flaps, she thought back to her two Christmases in Ohio. She remembered being very excited about what lay beneath the colorful wrapping paper. Although over twenty years had passed and she’d suffered more than her younger self could have possibly imagined, she still felt her heart flutter as she unearthed a brown cardboard box.

    Slowly, carefully, Yelena placed the wrapping paper on the floor. In her periphery, she saw Kate squeeze her crossed ankles, her friend’s shoulders closing around her ears as she watched Yelena’s every move.

    Pulling open the box, Yelena felt her brows furrow as she spotted the book inside.

    “Beyond the Breakwater,” Yelena said, reading the title while her eyes took in the pink and blue cover art and the author’s name. “One summer in Provincetown, three women learn the true meaning of love and friendship.”

    “You, uh,” Kate said with half a shrug, gesturing at the present. Her voice went up an octave, sounding increasingly unlike herself as she continued to babble. “You said you didn’t want to finish Safe Harbor until you could be sure you’d get your hands on the sequel, so...” Kate tapped on the cover. “There you go. Now you can find out what happens to Reese and Tory.” “But maybe don’t read the synopsis until you’re done with the first one,” Natasha interjected, the emotions on her face pulling at something in Yelena’s brain that she couldn’t quite put her finger on. “There’s a pretty big spoiler on the back.”

    “Yeah,” Kate quickly said with a nod, her shoulders still sitting just under her ears. “Sorry, maybe I should have taped it up or something. Or put a warning on it.” Her blue eyes stared at the lighthouse on the cover as she half-shrugged again. With startling clarity, Yelena realized that Kate was waiting for her reaction—Kate cared about what she thought of this present.

    With slow, deliberate movements, Yelena returned Beyond the Breakwater to the box it had arrived in, her fingers trailing across the cover for another brief second. Then, she set the present on the couch cushion to her left.

    “Thank you,” Yelena said, turning to Kate and placing her hand on the girl’s forearm.

    Perhaps, if Natasha had not been there to witness this moment, Yelena might have pulled her closer. Kate’s embraces were always gentle and never overbearing, not even when she was upset. More than anything, they were warm. “This is a good present. I am looking forward to reading this with you.”

    Kate’s body stilled, her blue eyes staring at Yelena.

    “Oh,” Kate breathed, her cheeks and neck growing red with blood. “Sure, yeah. Awesome.”

    A part of Yelena could hardly believe that this soft girl had been a ceiling-sized werewolf with glowing eyes and razor-sharp teeth not even a week ago. She couldn’t help but compare the two as they looked at each other now. She couldn’t help but think of the raw, undiluted rage she’d encountered in the basement. Kate Bishop had not been present down there, not really. In those eyes, Yelena had seen something without a name. Something that did not recognize itself. Something that recognized the suffering of the cage—and the woman that entered it.

    You saw me, Yelena thought as Kate covered her hand on her forearm and squeezed it. You saw me, and you trusted me when you did not trust yourself. Why would you do that?

    On the television, a blonde woman fled across a river, never to be seen again.

    ##

    December 26th, 2016 | 01:03 A.M.

    The living room was cloaked in darkness, with only the flickering light from the screen illuminating the far side of it. Natasha was asleep on the three-seater, her arms wrapped around a pillow and her body covered in a thin blanket Kate Bishop draped across her several hours ago. Kate herself was curled up in the easy chair, her long legs hanging over the left armrest. Her feet occasionally twitched in her sleep, always accompanied by a few incoherent whispers.

    Yelena, for her part, stared intently at the television.

    Perhaps it wasn’t necessary to keep watch at the farmhouse, considering the many different security systems installed all over the place, but Yelena liked the thought of being useful. With Natasha asleep to her left and Kate asleep to her right, just keeping them safe and allowing them the rest they deserved was enough.

    Humming along to the song, Yelena leaned forward to stare at the transformation taking place.

    “Hm,” someone mumbled to her right, lips smacking together as the person rolled over.

    “Good song.” Kate’s eyelids fluttered while her arms moved underneath the blanket Yelena placed on her torso just a few hours ago. Kate had fallen asleep just after Natasha, her half- open mouth drooling on the upholstery of the easy chair.

    “Shh,” Yelena said, reaching over to touch Kate’s left ankle. “Go back to sleep.”

    “Hm,” Kate replied, leaning up on her left elbow before wiping at her chin with her right hand. “Cold,” she grumbled, wriggling her ankle in Yelena’s loose grip until she let go. “Why are you up?” A small yawn escaped her, only half-covered by tired fingers. Kate’s eyes were mostly closed, invoking the image of a half-asleep puppy dog. “Are you okay?”

    “Yes,” Yelena confirmed, patting Kate’s shin. “Go back to sleep, Kate Bishop.”

    “Oh,” Kate said, her eyes fixed on the television. “You’re watching Frozen again.” On the screen, a castle made of thick, blue ice rose into the air, accompanied by intense singing. The feeling of a toe poking her arm drew Yelena’s attention back to Kate’s face. “Can I watch it with you?”

    There was something fragile about Kate’s hopeful question. A simple no would have been enough to crush it, but… Scooting to the left, Yelena patted the space Kate Bishop had occupied during the morning hours. It didn’t take long for Kate to make her home there and even less for her head to land on Yelena’s lap.

    Staring down at the side of Kate’s face, Yelena cataloged the sensation of Kate rubbing her cheek against her thigh to get comfortable. Memories from Kate’s nightmares came to Yelena; memories of Kate seeking comfort in much the same way.

    “Are you okay?” Yelena asked softly, placing her right hand gingerly on Kate’s shoulder.

    Like before, she thought of Mama Melina and her gentle touches. Whenever Yelena had been upset, Melina had cupped her cheeks and wiped away her tears. She’d kissed Yelena’s forehead and hugged her softly to her chest. It was not real—but Kate Bishop; Kate Bishop was real. Mimicking the only mother she’d ever known, Yelena stroked Kate’s hair and squeezed her shoulder.

    “Yeah,” Kate breathed, her eyes fixed on the movie. “Just thinking about my mom.” With an understanding hum, Yelena brushed her thumb along Kate’s temple. “Maybe she thinks I’m dead. I think… I think if you hadn’t found me, I probably would be.” Kate’s right hand curled into a fist against Yelena’s leg. “Maybe she had a funeral with an empty casket, and maybe she told my friends. Maybe my college shipped all of my stuff back to her.” Suddenly turning onto her back, Kate looked up at Yelena. “Should we just let it go? If she thinks I’m dead, I— she might be better off. At least I can’t disappoint her anymore.”

    Keeping her left hand in Kate’s hair, Yelena moved her right to Kate’s neck. The pulse that beat under her palm was elevated, and the way Kate stared at her made Yelena wonder. At some point, Kate had clearly decided that Yelena’s opinion could be trusted. And if the wolf’s reaction was anything to go by, it wasn’t just her opinion. Without warning, Yelena felt her own heart speed up in turn, startling her. Why was her body displaying this reaction?

    “You said they will hunt you,” Yelena said softly, forcing herself to keep calm instead of freaking out. Kate’s warm body seemed to worsen Yelena’s elevated heartbeat. When Kate reached up to clasp Yelena’s forearm, Yelena briefly panicked that her friend would be able to feel how out of sorts she truly was, even through the layer of sweater. But Kate’s expression revealed nothing.

    “If they’re even looking for me. They may think I’m dead, too,” Kate said, moving her hand from Yelena’s forearm to the crook of her elbow.

    “No,” Yelena said, feeling the strangest urge to solve her emotional reaction with violence. A part of her wanted to wrap her hand around Kate’s throat and choke her until she could no longer confuse her. But a larger part—one she was most scared of—wanted to cup Kate’s cheek and brush her chin and see what Kate would do about it. Besides, even if she did try to choke Kate, the likelihood of the murder being successful would be slim to none. And she rather enjoyed hearing Kate ramble—it filled the silence and kept Yelena tethered.

    “No?” Kate asked, squeezing Yelena’s arm.

    “No,” Yelena repeated, picking up the remote with her left hand to pause the movie. “Let’s go outside.”

    “Uhm,” Kate started, screwing up her face. “Do we have to?”

    “Yes.” Yelena glanced at the window and the darkness that lay beyond. It would help—and so would the temperature. If her heart sped up out there, it would be to keep her warm, not because Kate Bishop’s blue eyes looked at her with such unwarranted and unearned trust.

    “Get up.”

    ##

    December 26th, 2016 | 01:27 A.M.

    When Yelena opened the front door after disabling the alarm, she first noticed the snowflakes falling from the sky. As she walked out onto the porch, she watched how they glittered and reflected the gleam from the automatic light above the steps. Kate grumbled as she shut the door behind them, her feet jammed into her new boots and her head covered in a black, woolen beanie. Yelena was wrapped in a thick jacket, a long scarf, and a pair of mittens Kate handed her by the front door. They were easy to slip off in times of emergency.

    When Yelena descended the stairs, she closed her eyes and turned her head to the sky like she had done so many times before. The wind was cold and punishing against her skin, but she enjoyed it all the more because of the pain.

    “It is nice out here,” Yelena commented off-handedly, listening to her boots crunching in the snow as she stepped further away from the house. The night was quiet, as no cars ever followed the path to the farmhouse. As long as her eyes were closed, she could pretend she was back at the cabin, seconds away from feeling Lucky’s warm breath on her hand.

    “It was nice on the couch, too,” Kate grumbled, her crunching steps coming closer and closer until Kate was by Yelena’s side. “You know, speaking of, there were actually a lot of jokes a couple of years ago when Frozen first came out about Elsa joining the Avengers—”

    “You should shift,” Yelena said, opening her eyes before blinking away the melted snowflakes on her eyelids. When she turned to Kate, she spotted the half-open mouth and the clearly confused expression on her friend’s face. “I think it would be good for you.”

    “No,” Kate said, voice shaky. Her eyes shifted to the darkness around them, only illuminated by the porchlight.

    Tilting her head to the side, Yelena studied her friend’s ashen face.

    “Why not?” Taking a step back, Yelena stuck her mitten-covered hands into the pockets of her jacket, keeping her face neutral and her inflection calm. When Kate’s face crumpled further, Yelena ignored her instinctive desire to make it better. Her hands twitched in her pockets, invisible to the only onlooker that mattered.

    “What if—” Kate started, snowflakes sticking to her beanie and shoulders. Her body stood perfectly still, seemingly unable to fidget at the prospect of being discovered. When Yelena shrugged, Kate’s mouth opened, but no words came out.

    “You said we should let it go.” Yelena took another step back. “You said they probably think you’re dead.” Another step. “You said your mother will be better off if you never return.” Even as the distance between them grew, Yelena’s heart did not slow down. “So, Kate Bishop. If all of that is true… you should not be afraid to shift.”

    The porchlight switched itself off.

    Blue wolf eyes shone in the darkness.

    “Why aren’t you afraid?” Kate asked, her voice quiet but not shaky. The wind picked up, shooting along their faces and into the spaces between Yelena’s scarf and neck. “We could all be killed if we decide to go through with our plan. Why do you keep pushing?”

    “There are worse things than death,” Yelena replied, her eyes slowly adjusting to the lack of light. “And there are more important things than my fear.” Bracing herself for her body’s unfavorable reaction to Kate Bishop, Yelena took the same steps forward that she’d taken back before. Once she was close enough, she leaned up to whisper. “There is nothing I will not do for my freedom. There is nothing I will not do for Natasha’s.” Removing her left hand from her mitten and pocket, Yelena reached up to cradle Kate’s warm neck. The skin burned her freezing fingers. “There is nothing I will not do for yours.” Pulling back, Yelena stared at the gleaming eyes. “How about you?”

    A stronger gust of wind swept across the open space in front of the farmhouse, jostling Yelena enough for Kate to grab her waist instinctively to keep her steady. The sudden movement caused Yelena to look off into the darkness for just a moment. When she turned her face back up, she misjudged the distance between their faces. Their noses brushed against one another, sending shockwaves of nervous energy through Yelena’s body.

    Kate’s hands and arms held her close—close enough to feel every inhale and exhale.

    “I am not insecure,” Kate whispered, lifting her right hand. Gently, she clasped the fingers Yelena had yet to remove from her neck. “I am—I don’t doubt myself. Not like this. I’m an award-winning archer; I’m a fencing champion—I’m a frigging black belt.” Holding their hands loosely against her skin, Kate leaned her forehead against Yelena’s. “The last time I felt this way…” Her fingers trembled, but Yelena didn’t find it terribly hard to guess. “When I was turned, I thought my life was over, but it wasn’t. I kept going, and it was good. Being a wolf… it’s amazing. But maybe I’ve just been living on borrowed time.”

    “Maybe you are,” Yelena said, finding it hard to speak while trying to catalog all of her body’s reactions to this enhanced proximity. It wasn’t like they’d never hugged or touched before. But this—this had the potential to be worse. “Does it make a difference? Even if it is only borrowed, do you not deserve to live a life?”

    “I don’t know,” Kate replied, softly squeezing Yelena’s fingers. It seemed like an absent- minded gesture—like Kate had forgotten she was even holding Yelena’s hand. The wind whipped past them again, bringing the snowflakes into disarray. Kate stepped to the side in response, blocking Yelena from the cold flashes that swung like blades through the air. “I wish I didn’t feel this way.”

    “And yet you do.” Yelena pulled her left hand free of Kate’s grip, missing the warmth as soon as Kate’s skin left hers. “I wish I had answers for you, Kate Bishop. You look to me for guidance, but I cannot offer you any.” Slowly, she cupped Kate’s cheek, her heart beating double-time. “I can stalk my prey, but I cannot give you comfort. I can kill the people who are after you, but I cannot tell you what your mother will think upon your return. What I can offer you, Kate Bishop, is blood.”

    Fingers trembling, Yelena wished Mama Melina had prepared her better.

    Kate let go of Yelena’s waist before gripping Yelena’s shoulders with both hands. “I would do anything for your freedom, too,” she said, voice hard and serious, blue wolf eyes shining brighter in the darkness. In this moment, Yelena saw it again—that nameless thing behind Kate’s eyes. That raw power of something terrible and beautiful. This time, Yelena wasn’t sure if it recognized itself, but—it recognized her. Again, it recognized her.

    “Your freedom is tied to mine,” Yelena said, pulling her right hand from her pocket and mitten before clasping the front of Kate’s jacket. The wind whipped snow around their legs and feet. “For now, if you cannot fight for yourself, you can fight for me.” She thought of the wolf standing above her and staring down the Red Room; she thought of the snarling and the growling and the bloody teeth; she thought of giant paws gently placing Natasha into the back of a car; she thought of the wolf wanting to stay behind so they could get away; she thought of the haunted look in Kate Bishop’s eyes after she killed those soldiers.

    Now cupping both of Kate’s cheeks, Yelena leaned up to press her mouth against the back of Kate’s nose.

    “Do not die,” Yelena murmured, her breath rushing across Kate’s face. “Kill them first.”

    “Okay,” Kate said, her fingers digging into the fabric of Yelena’s thick jacket. “As long as you promise to do the same.”

    “For hurting you, I will show no mercy,” Yelena said, afraid of her own actions as she pressed another kiss to Kate’s skin. There was a fire in her brain—a fire that consumed her mind and threatened the clarity of her actions. In the basement, when the wolf rose up to its full height, Yelena had not been afraid. When the wolf lunged at her, Yelena had not been afraid. When the wolf moved to strike her, Yelena had not been afraid.

    For the first time in years, Yelena shivered.

    Loosening her grip, Kate clasped Yelena’s left wrist like she had done before. Her touch was gentle, sending goosebumps racing along Yelena’s arm. “We should go back inside before you catch pneumonia,” Kate said, stepping back and taking Yelena’s hands in her own. “I know you’re, like, from Russia and have lived through worse storms in worse clothing, but— I don’t know, I feel like we shouldn’t purposefully try to top that.” The porchlight flickered back on when she took a few steps back while tugging Yelena along.

    The sudden change in brightness caught Yelena off guard, forcing her to squint and briefly close her eyes. This did not help her strange mood. Her plan of keeping her distance from Kate had backfired—somehow, Yelena felt more confused than before leaving the couch.

    Whatever this weird reaction was, she wanted it to go away. Her relationship with Kate was already complicated enough.

    ##

    January 3rd, 2017 | 07:05 A.M.

    The air around her was perfectly still, undisturbed by her presence. Through the scope of her sniper rifle, the empty field in view of her hiding place appeared just as calm. The sun had yet to rise fully, coloring their surroundings in murky, early-morning fog.

    Her earpiece crackled.

    “Scanners are picking up several heat signatures coming from the North,” Natasha said, her voice even and unperturbed. “Too many to be a coincidence, especially at this time of day.” Yelena knew where she was, even though she could not see her. “It’s showtime.”

    Repositioning her hands on her rifle, Yelena closed her left eye and focused on the far side of the field.

    “Hello,” she whispered to herself, forcing herself to wait.

    “Seventeen.” Natasha’s voice was accompanied by the sound of a gun being unloaded and reloaded, a nervous habit of hers. Yelena had cataloged this little quirk during the brief period of time they’d traveled together before her initial recapture.

    “Try leaving some for me,” Yelena replied, which garnered a chuckle from her sister.

    “Can we be serious, please?” a second voice grumbled, no doubt sulking in the place she’d been told to hide. “They’re not playing around if they brought seventeen fricking people.” Kate’s voice trailed off as she mumbled something under her breath.

    “Aw, Kate Bishop,” Yelena drawled, watching as the enemy group stopped at the edge of the trees. Through her scope, she could see their lips moving. There were two women at the front, flanked by a group of two men. Behind them, several more hunters clutched their weapons. “These people were scared enough to bring more than a dozen idiots. You should be flattered.”

    “I count three hunting rifles, five machine guns, two crossbows, and seven semi-automatics,” Natasha interjected before Kate could respond. Yelena imagined her sister with a pair of binoculars, and the stupid mental image pleased her. “Yelena’s right, Jack. These dumbasses are shaking in their boots. If only they knew how silly and sweet you really are.”

    “Are you sure you guys can handle this?” Kate asked, disregarding Natasha’s words entirely. “I do not appreciate your lack of faith.” Yelena waited for the group of hunters to make a mistake. The two women leading the charge spoke to one another in quick, harsh tones, but their lips were turned too far away for Yelena to make out their words. “Natasha and I will be done with this before lunchtime.”

    When Kate huffed into her microphone, Yelena felt the strangest urge to reassure her. She pushed the urge down—now was not the time. Over the last several days, she’d only sparingly indulged in her new fascination with Kate Bishop. By the time they’d returned to the couch after their heartfelt exchange in the snow, Natasha had disappeared upstairs.

    They’d finished the movie while keeping a safe distance, as Yelena opted to curl up on the empty three-seater.

    Between Christmas and New Year’s Eve, Yelena had kept a mental checklist. Her experiments were simple—she engaged in various activities with Kate and judged her own reaction based on the physical symptoms of her current ailment. Out of all the tests she performed, the sparring had been the worst, followed closely by the reading session in Kate’s bedroom.

    Even now, Yelena detected a faint sense of apprehension when she thought of Kate facing this threat. Those weapons contained enough silver to kill a hundred werewolves—maybe a thousand. And the last time Kate came into contact with them, she ended up in front of Yelena’s truck, shot half to death.

    “They’re talking about strategy,” Natasha said, pulling Yelena back from her musings about Kate’s many scars. “We should start.” With a wordless grunt of agreement, Yelena moved her left thumb from the side of the rifle to the attachment on its side.

    “Three,” Yelena said, placing her thumb on a button. “Two.”

    “Be careful,” Kate interjected, causing Yelena to pause for just a moment. “Please?”

    “Don’t worry,” Yelena replied, giving herself the time to hesitate now instead of later, when it would matter. “You should remember that they have silver weapons. Do not come out unless it is absolutely necessary.” Kate’s confirmation was neither as quick nor as enthusiastic as Yelena would have preferred, but it would have to do. “Okay. Counting down again. Three, two…”

    “One,” Natasha said just as Yelena pressed the button affixed to her rifle.

    Somewhere to Yelena’s right, a howl shot through the morning air, sounding exhausted and horrifically hurt. The sound burrowed into Yelena’s bones, even though she’d been the one to help Kate record it days ago. Returning her hand to her rifle, Yelena observed the hunters through the scope. The obvious excitement on their faces disgusted her.

    Mumbling an insult in Russian, Yelena watched as the hunters looked at the trees around them.

    “That’s right,” Yelena whispered, switching off the safety on her rifle. “Wouldn’t want to run through the open air and give away your location.” The two women gesticulated at each other while they quietly discussed the best course of action. Yelena knew who they were—she’d studied many pictures of them since she and Natasha started planning this operation. As she allowed her gaze to wander from face to face, she recognized more people in the crowd; most were Kelly and Trask’s employees and hunting buddies.

    “Any moment now,” Natasha whispered, sounding both deadly serious and weirdly amused.

    Yelena could relate. The last time she killed someone, she was under the influence of the Red Room—this time, she wasn’t killing for Dreykov. This time, it would be for Kate. This time, the kill would ensure her friend’s safety.

    “In position,” Yelena confirmed, training her rifle on a chest. “Waiting for the signal.” “They’re splitting up,” Natasha said seconds before Trask turned to the left and Kelly turned to the right. Both women waved their hands at their stooges, and roughly half of the group switched to each side. Following the chest with her rifle, Yelena’s pointer finger tightened on the trigger in preparation.

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said on her next exhale, seconds before Natasha gave her the signal. “What? Are you okay?” Kate asked just as Yelena pulled the trigger and the first hunter went down.

    “This is for you,” Yelena replied as she reloaded her weapon with the deadly accuracy and efficiency of an assassin.

    Shock—fear—anger—the hunters either stared at their fallen comrade or ran for cover. While Yelena took down the second one with another perfect shot to the chest, Charlotte Kelly fell to her knees. The tuft of a dart stuck out of her neck; her body was instantly unable to move, thanks to the tranquilizer.

    “Three down,” Yelena said as she reloaded and shot again. “Four down.”

    “Six down,” Natasha replied as Francesca Trask and the man beside her dropped like flies. “One of them is fleeing,” Yelena replied without an ounce of concern or empathy as she reloaded her weapon and shot again. In the distance, she was vaguely aware of the runner being taken down and landing on the forest floor in a heap of weapons and tactical gear.

    “Nine little piggies,” Yelena softly said to herself as she reloaded and shot and reloaded and shot. “Seven little piggies.”

    “I take the rest on the left; you take the rest on the right?” Natasha asked, sounding perfectly calm and not even a little winded, even though Yelena knew she was jumping from branch to branch to keep up with the hunters.

    “My pleasure,” Yelena replied, feeling nothing as she set her sights on the back of a frantically running woman who looked barely older than her. “Wrong place, wrong time,”

    Yelena said to the woman, even though she could not hear her.

    By the time the rest of the group was down, the sun was only barely higher in the sky, beaming down on the corpses and the blood that pooled beneath them. The field and the surrounding forest were quiet as Yelena got up from her crouch and stretched her limbs, her high-powered sniper rifle sitting innocently in the sunshine.

    “Is it over?” Kate asked, her voice barely penetrating the killing fog that had taken over Yelena’s brain as soon as she started shooting. “Did you do it?” The words slipped inside Yelena without her permission, stopping her briefly from grabbing her rifle. Earlier, she had been sure. Earlier, she had known who was in control. Now that it was over, she couldn’t help but ask: Was it her who shot those hunters? Even as she finally put her hands on the weapon and began disassembling it with practiced ease, her mind kept spinning around the same problem. Who guided her hand? Dreykov? Melina? She had felt in control before they started shooting, but—fingers trembling, Yelena shut the case.

    “All clear, Jack,” Natasha replied, clearly visible in the distance as she dropped from a tree. Surrounded by bodies, she looked exactly like Yelena remembered her. The Red Room had sent them on missions together only a handful of times when they had both been so much younger, yet the carnage never changed. Upside down? She wondered. Or right side up?

    Bringing a hand to her head, Yelena shook herself. These memories—these worries—why now?

    “Do not come out yet,” Yelena said, taking the case and climbing out of her bird hide. The camouflage made it nearly impossible to distinguish it from the trees around it, giving her the perfect vantage point. “Wait for our signal.”

    “Make up your minds,” Kate grumbled, sounding suspiciously like she was petulantly fidgeting in the backseat of the car where she was stashed. But Yelena had made up her mind on this before she’d descended the steps of the bird hide. Does it make me a bad person, she thought, thinking back to Kate’s distraught face and hunched shoulders as she’d uttered those words. Yelena’s brisk walk carried her over to Natasha within a matter of minutes. Up close, the death and destruction loomed even larger. You are not the bad person, Kate Bishop.

    “She’s looking a bit rough, but she’ll be fine,” Natasha said after rolling Charlotte Kelly onto her back. The woman’s face was covered in dirt. Since the snow had melted over New Year’s Eve, the water had left behind a layer of mud at the edge of the forest.

    While Natasha turned the woman back onto her stomach so she could zip-tie her, Yelena set down her case and went to the first person they needed to dispose of. Kneeling down, she ignored the stench of voided bowels and spilled blood as she checked for a pulse on the man’s neck just to be sure.

    Maybe, if she pulled them into a pile, Kate Bishop would not freak out so much. Maybe, if Yelena was careful to turn away their faces, Kate Bishop would not dream of them like she had dreamed of the Red Room.

    ##

    January 3rd, 2017 | 07:35 A.M.

    Yelena’s hands and forearms were covered in blood. Her face itched from sweat, but she did not reach up to scratch. She still felt nothing as she dragged another body onto the pile of corpses stacked up in front of her. She felt nothing but apprehension at Kate’s reaction. “You know,” Natasha said to her, having clearly shut off her comms. Following her lead, Yelena wiped her hand on her suit before switching off the comms as well. “This would be much easier if the big girl were over here. I know you think it’s not a good idea, but we should get out of this place as soon as we can. Werewolf super strength would come in handy to make that happen. If you don’t want her handling the excess cargo, I get it, but she could at least dig the hole.”

    Turning away from the bodies, Yelena looked toward their hidden car, hands hanging loosely at her sides. When she said nothing, Natasha wrapped her arm around Yelena’s shoulder, her mouth finding Yelena’s temple in a gentle kiss.

    “She used to be a hunter, kiddo,” Natasha continued, leaning their heads together. “I’m pretty sure she’s seen her fair share of dead bodies.” Closing her eyes, Yelena let out a slow breath. “Maybe this won’t be like last time. I mean, at least she didn’t kill these ones.”

    “Yes.” Keeping her eyes closed and her other senses focused on her sister, Yelena wondered how different her life would have been if she’d had Natasha with her through all of it. The warmth of Natasha’s half-embrace seeped into Yelena’s soul, warming her from the inside out. It was easier to be sure of who she was with Natasha right next to her. “Maybe you are right.”

    “How gracious of you to admit that,” Natasha teased, pressing another kiss against Yelena’s temple. This one was bigger and showier, making a loud smacking noise and causing Yelena to laugh and shake her off.

    “Shut up,” Yelena groused, nearly reaching up to rub at the spot where Natasha’s lips had been before remembering the blood and thinking better of it. She’d just dropped her hand back down when she saw movement from the corner of her eyes and heard sounds that hadn’t been there just a second ago. Turning in the direction of the forest, Yelena had half a second to reach for the handgun at her waist before a bullet hit her square in the chest.

    Pain spiderwebbed from the wound, crawling along her skin.

    “Aim for the head, dumbass!” a voice called out seconds before a second gunshot slammed into Natasha’s shoulder.

    Rage suffused Yelena’s brain at her sister’s pained gasp. Lifting her gun, she aimed it at the four men before her, ignoring the searing pain in her side in favor of aiming properly with both hands. She saw the rifle in the guy’s hand discharge, but before she could move, a giant black body of fur and claws appeared in front of her.

    “No!” Yelena shouted an instant before the silver bullet struck the wolf’s side. There was no yowl or pained yip like Yelena had expected. When she looked up into the eyes of the wolf, she saw the creature from the basement—dangerous, feral, and willing to do anything to protect her.

    Reaching out to grab the wolf and make it go away, Yelena missed the fur by inches as the wolf turned and ran at the hunters.

    “Shit,” Natasha grunted, running after the wolf just as it took down its first victim. Yelena wasn’t far behind. Another gun went off, but the bullet hit the trunk of a tree. Giant teeth tore into a man’s throat as Yelena executed three perfect, rapid-fire shots to the chest of a man who had aimed his rifle at Kate’s head.

    The fourth guy tried to turn and run, but Natasha was on him within seconds, slicing his throat and pushing him to the ground.

    Panting and sweating, Yelena pushed her gun back into its holster, her eyes trained on the blood that dripped to the forest floor from the wolf’s side. Horror swept through her at the realization that the wound wasn’t healing.

    “We need to get it out,” Yelena said to herself, crossing the distance between herself and the wolf. When the wolf turned her head, her blue eyes were frantic, and her teeth covered in blood and flesh. The hunter beneath her body was unrecognizable.

    Without thinking, Yelena cupped the wolf’s big head.

    “You were supposed to stay in the car!” Yelena suddenly exploded, unable to restrain the mounting terror that swept her body. “What the fuck are you doing out here?!” The wolf growled and bared its teeth, but Yelena bared her teeth right back. “This is not what we discussed!” Yelena’s body trembled as the pain of her own wound and the realization that Kate and Natasha were hurt truly sunk in. “You were not meant to be out here,” Yelena whispered as she pulled the big head against her chest in a hug, her cheek smushed against the space between the big ears. “This is not funny, Kate.”

    The wolf nudged her back, finally emitting that pitiful whine she had expected before. Then, as the adrenaline of the moment quickly wore off, the wolf collapsed, nearly taking Yelena with it as it lost consciousness.

    ##

    January 3rd, 2017 | 05:59 P.M.

    There was an ache in Yelena’s very bones that she was afraid would never go away. For the first time in her life, she felt genuinely ancient and tired beyond her years. Before, when she’d returned from missions, she had never been allowed to simply feel the pains of her body. She’d always been studied by the Red Room scientists, who would forever try to figure out how to perfect the super soldier serum they’d attempted to reverse-engineer so many decades ago. She would have never been allowed to take a shower or change into a comfortable pair of sweatpants after a grueling fight or an injury. She would have never been allowed to lie down, rest her head on a pillow, and simply look at the slowly rising and falling chest of the woman on the bed next to her. At the Red Room, revealing her exhaustion would have been punishable by reconditioning.

    Feeling warm and comfortable after her shower, Yelena turned her gaze away from Kate Bishop’s sleeping face. Staring at the ceiling, she placed her left hand on the right side of her ribs. She winced when she poked the impact site of the bullet, conscious that no one could see her weakness. Her bulletproof vest had taken most of the damage, but the bruising couldn’t be helped. At least she’d gotten away without broken or cracked ribs this time. The pain was a momentary inconvenience—soon enough, her enhanced physiology would take care of the purple blotching that accompanied it.

    Flattening her left palm on her side, Yelena turned her head back to Kate, whose breathing was slow and steady. With a grunt, Yelena pushed herself into a sitting position, crossing her legs in the process. Her left knee rested against Kate’s thigh, and where Yelena would have expected uncontrollable warmth, she was instead met with regular human temperature.

    “Stupid,” Yelena muttered, noting the pallor covering Kate’s face and neck. Shaking her head, Yelena pulled up the bottom of Kate’s flannel and looked at the gauze pad and bandages underneath. Black veins poked out beneath some of the edges, worrying Yelena more than she wanted to admit—even to herself. “This is not what we discussed,” she repeated to herself, feeling the desperate urge to follow the black veins with her fingers until they went away. “Stupid.”

    Gently lowering the flannel, Yelena took Kate’s left hand in both of hers and turned it over to look at the palm. The removal of the bullet had been painful for Kate; that much was obvious when the unconscious wolf dug its claws into the earth and howled into the morning sunshine. Yelena had meant to dig the projectile out herself, but her hands had shaken too badly, and her eyes had blurred with blinding tears. If Natasha had not been there, Yelena thought she would still be out in the woods.

    “This is not normal,” Yelena said, following one of the lines of Kate’s palm with her pointer finger. “I am not supposed to act this way.” Furrowing her brows, Yelena turned Kate’s hand over and poked at the knuckles. “Why is this happening to me?” But Kate’s sleeping face gave her no answer to her question.

    In the forest, Kate’s body did not turn back until the bullet was out.

    For several harrowing minutes, Yelena had kept the sluggishly bleeding wound on the wolf’s flank shut with Natasha’s jacket. When Kate’s body shrunk until she was human again, the relief nearly overtook Yelena’s senses, blinding her to the truth: This time was somehow different.

    Shaking her head, Yelena placed Kate’s hand back on the bed. She did not understand the difference between these wounds and the ones Kate sustained just before they met. Lucky had been okay the next morning. Yes, she had been unable to shift back because of how long the silver had been in her system, but she had been awake. She was not awake now.

    “One bullet,” Yelena whispered, dropping Kate’s hand gently back on the bed. “One bullet.” Scooting off the mattress, Yelena strode to the window of the guestroom they were occupying. Rain and wind beat against it, coloring Yelena’s view of the outside world. They were not at the farmhouse—their excess cargo had made that impractical and even dangerous. This safe house was smaller, but it was just as remote.

    “Hey,” a voice said from the doorway, accompanied by a light wrapping of knuckles on the doorframe. In the reflection of the window, Yelena watched her sister enter and close the guestroom door behind her. Leaning against it, Natasha crossed her feet at the ankles and her arms over her chest. “Any update on Sleeping Beauty?”

    “Not yet,” Yelena replied, consciously relaxing her face, her shoulders, and her hands.

    Natasha did not need to see her concern—Natasha did not need to see her uncertainty—

    Natasha did not need to carry this burden, too. “I have detected no signs of healing, but also no signs of her condition worsening.”

    “I’m not surprised,” Natasha said, her eyes visibly moving from Yelena to the bed. Shaking her head, Natasha pushed herself away from the door. “We just passed the new moon,” she explained quietly, sitting at the edge of the bed to lift up Kate’s flannel. Finally, Yelena turned her back to the window, her gaze following Natasha’s fingers on Kate’s stomach. “Her wolf powers will be especially mid right now.”

    “How do you know so much about this?” Yelena asked, sitting gracefully on a chair next to the window. There was no sign from Kate that she realized Natasha was inspecting the black veins on her stomach—in a way, Yelena envied their uncomplicated relationship. “The Red Room taught me none of this! Did they train you to handle supernatural threats? I was ripped off.”

    With a soft snort, Natasha lifted the edge of the gauze to peer underneath. “The wife of a friend is a member of the supernatural community,” she said, making a face at the bullet wound in Kate’s side. Shaking her head, Natasha folded the edge back down before dropping Kate’s flannel. “Couldn’t have picked a worse time to get shot, Jack.” Pursing her lips, Natasha put the back of her hand against Kate’s forehead. “Dumbass.”

    Forcing herself to stay relaxed and appear undisturbed, Yelena got to her feet.

    “Are they awake?” she asked, passing by the bed to open the guestroom door. The short hallway beyond was dark from a lack of natural light.

    “They were when I left them,” Natasha confirmed, brushing her thumb across Kate’s hair before following Yelena’s lead and leaving the bedroom. When Yelena glanced at Kate instinctively, Natasha threw her thumb over her shoulder in the vague direction of the sleeping girl. “Puppy will be fine. She just needs to work the silver out of her system.” “Idiot,” Yelena said flatly, thinking back to the moment the wolf was hit. She remembered the terror amidst the pain of her own wound and the worry for Natasha’s. A moment longer, and Yelena would have shot the bastard before he could hurt any more of them. “Next time, I will not bring her.”

    “Sure,” Natasha said, drawing the door closed behind them. “I believe that.”

    “What?” Yelena asked, following after her sister as they both descended the stairs to the first floor.

    “Nothing.” Natasha looked back at her; her expression was oddly challenging to read. She was grinning, but it wasn’t with the usual sarcastic amusement that Yelena was used to. “I think it’s adorable how much you care about her. That’s all.”

    “She is clumsy and makes a mess,” Yelena insisted, perturbed by the feeling of her heart speeding up like it did over Christmas—like it had so many times since. She followed Natasha to a bedroom, where they both changed into new tactical suits. “I care that she does not blow our cover and get us captured by the Red Room.”

    “Okay,” Natasha said with a shrug, zipping up her black jacket.

    “What?” Yelena repeated, squashing the desire to grab her sister’s wrist and force her to explain why her emotions were not her own once more. There was no mind control now—

    there couldn’t be. Yet, at the same time, Yelena could hardly control the reactions her body was displaying. “Why are you saying it like that?”

    “I’m not saying anything.” Natasha left the bedroom, passing by the small kitchen and living room, the wooden floors groaning as she walked across them. Yelena was hot on her heels, unsure what her sister was trying to get at. It still did not feel like she was being teased, which baffled and confused her even more. “Before we go in there—”

    “I know how to conduct an interrogation,” Yelena said, stopping behind Natasha before readjusting her fingerless gloves. The door to the basement was closed and evidently locked —the additional deadbolt was a giveaway. “Don’t worry, I will not break anything.” She balled her left hand into a fist. “Not yet.”

    With a slight nod, Natasha unlocked the door and pulled it open, gesturing for Yelena to go down ahead of her. Already, Yelena could hear the soft tones of a conversation from beyond the darkness. Unfortunately, the ceiling lights above the stairs came to life as soon as she made her way down. Abruptly, the sound of quiet voices cut off, leaving Yelena to ponder the lives of the women waiting for them. She knew their upbringing had driven them to act this way—at least in that regard, Yelena could relate, even if that didn’t change the anger burning in her heart.

    The basement was as small as the rest of the building. Unlike at the farmhouse, this basement did not have multiple branching rooms. Instead, there was only one, divided into two distinct parts by a set of titanium bars.

    “Charlotte Kelly,” Yelena announced, idly strolling up to the bars with her hands behind her back and her eyes on a woman bound to a chair. “How nice of you to be here on such short notice.” They hadn’t bothered to scrape the forest floor dirt off their captive’s face. Yelena imagined it itching and Kelly being unable to scratch. The thought made her smile on the inside. “To what do we owe the pleasure of your visit? What do you think?”

    Natasha descended the stairs behind her. Unlike Yelena, she didn’t walk up to the bars, choosing to lean against a table in the corner instead.

    Kelly did not respond verbally, but her body trembled with rage.

    “It is very rude not to say hello,” Yelena whispered as she grabbed the bars and tilted her head to the side. Suddenly, Yelena saw Kelly rear up as best as she could, her mouth curving until she could spit at Yelena. Unfortunately, she was too far away for it to land where it was intended, and Kelly’s movement was too restricted to make it work even if she had been closer. “Is this fun for you?” Yelena asked, allowing her eyes to move across Kelly’s body from head to toe. “Behaving like an animal?”

    “You’re the animals!” the woman beside Kelly bellowed, a vein nearly popping at her temple. “Gunning down good, hard-working people! You should be ashamed of yourselves!”

    Droplets of moisture shot from Francesca Trask’s mouth, coating only her chin and the concrete floor.

    “Truly, I am on my knees to beg for your forgiveness,” Yelena drawled, eyebrow raised, blinking slowly.

    “And you!” Trask shouted, her eyes moving from Yelena to Natasha, who didn’t move a muscle after being spoken to. “I know who you are! Black Widow.” Trask let out a noise of disgust. “They should have never let you into this country. They should have gunned you down in Russia like a dog in the street! You and all of those other freaks who pretend to protect us!”

    “Hm, I know,” Yelena said, drumming her fingers on the bars, her voice shifting up and down. “Look at us immigrants, taking your jobs. You want to be the only ones killing good, hard-working people, I’m sure.”

    The vein at Trask’s temple visibly throbbed.

    “We hunt monsters, you idiots,” she said through gritted teeth, her hands twitching on the arms of the chair she was tied to. Her sleeves barely managed to conceal her swollen biceps —ready to enact violence at the slightest chance. Ridiculous. “We keep our communities safe with what we do.”

    “Don’t bother,” Kelly said, leaning back her head. “Don’t waste your breath on them.”

    “That’s funny,” Yelena said, pulling a knife from her tactical belt to play with it while continuing to lean against the bars. “I think that is good advice, Charlotte Kelly.” With a nod, Yelena moved her thumb along the sharp side of the blade. “Maybe you should have said this to your friends before they drove to a forest to butcher a girl. Maybe then your friends would not have wasted their breaths, hm? What do you think?”

    Kelly’s tongue snapped into a noise of derision.

    “We both know that thing isn’t a girl,” she said, lifting her nose into the air. “If you’re deluded about what it is that you’re protecting, let me enlighten you.” Mouth open, Kelly took a deep and foreboding breath.

    As soon as she started to speak, Yelena laughed. Grabbing her knife with her right hand so she wouldn’t cut herself, she bent over at the waist, taking a step back to make room in front of the bars. Her laughter was loud and uproarious, and if Kate Bishop had not been shot with silver and had not thus been fully unconscious, Yelena might have been worried about waking her.

    When she righted herself, a tear having trickled down her face from her amusement, Kelly and Trask were both looking at her. Their mouths were open, their brows furrowed, and their noses scrunched in undisguised hatred and disgust.

    “Charlotte Kelly, you are so funny,” Yelena said, wiping at the tear with her left hand. “You should do stand-up in Vegas, truly.” With another chuckle, Yelena waved the knife in Kelly’s direction. “Hilarious. Wow.” Shaking her shoulders, Yelena pretended to shiver for dramatic effect. “I mean,” leaning back against the bars, Yelena stared the woman down, “that must work on a lot of people, yes? Telling them there is something they do not know. Stoking their fears.” Schooling her expression back into perfect neutrality, Yelena twirled the knife back and forth. “You are a parasite upon this earth, Charlotte Kelly. Do not forget this for a second while we speak to you.”

    With an angry laugh, the woman shook her head.

    “What do you even want from us?” Trask asked, looking from her companion to Yelena.

    “They want to torture us, France,” Kelly supplied, her eyes never leaving Yelena’s. If she was afraid, she was good at hiding it—for now. “They want to feed us to their monster pal. Or eat us themselves.” Kelly’s eyes narrowed as she looked Yelena up and down. “What sort of freak are you?”

    Yelena smiled.

    “Who’s to say?” she replied, relishing the familiarity of this interrogation. Down here, there was no room for her to feel insecure or unsure of herself. Down here, she was in control. “But you are wrong, Charlotte Kelly. You are wrong about why we captured you.” Righting herself, Yelena returned her knife to its holster. “Besides, if we fed you to Kate Bishop, I think she would get terrible heartburn.” Yelena stuck out her tongue in a yuck expression, rubbing her sternum. “All that self-righteousness in your blood…”

    “What you call self-righteousness, I call justice.” Trask’s facial expression was not terribly difficult to decipher. “Kate Bishop died the night she was turned. That thing that you’re hiding away is wearing her body like a suit. Kate’s mother didn’t even know the truth.” This time, it was Trask who shook her head, her brows furrowed and her mouth open. “When Eleanor realized her child had been murdered by the creature that took her place… the sorrow on her face…” She shook her head a second time. “But maybe you are too broken to understand.”

    “Finding out the truth must have been devastating for her,” Natasha said, raising her voice for the first time. “Was this before or after you told her? That you were the ones who shot her child twice in the back and left her for dead.”

    “We did what we had to do,” Trask said, shifting her gaze to Natasha. “Eleanor understood.” “Do you have children, Francine?” Yelena asked, wondering how someone could be so willing to kill while being so naïve to the many truths of the world. Upside down or right side up? If nothing else, Yelena knew where these two fell on the cosmic scales.

    “Francesca,” Trask growled lowly, unable to hide the twitching of her fingers and the tightening of her calf muscles. “What does it matter?”

    “Well, France,” Yelena explained, crouching down to bring herself to Trask’s eye level.

    “Some people love their children. Some people love their children more than they love what they’ve believed their entire lives. Some people love their children more than the lies they’ve used to justify their atrocities.” With a shrug, Yelena righted herself. “But maybe you are too broken to understand.” Slow realization broke across their faces like the rising current of a river, bringing their carefully constructed façades into disarray. “And I will tell you why you are here.”

    Throwing her knife into the air, Yelena watched the two women follow its flips for a few seconds before she caught it expertly and slipped it back into its holster. Slowly, she moved three steps to the right, allowing her hand to trail along the titanium bars until she reached the numpad on the locked door of the cage. Instead of typing in a code, she merely placed her thumb on the scanner and waited for the door to unlock.

    She heard Natasha’s unmistakable footsteps behind her, but she didn’t turn. She already knew what would happen next.
