    January 9th, 2017 | 03:20 A.M.

    The empty road stretched out for miles in the darkness, illuminated only by the headlights of their car. At least this one had not been stolen off the side of the road. This vehicle was clean, sturdy, and modern and did not reek of stale coffee or unwashed clothes. The GPS tracker and most of the original electronics had been ripped out and replaced with custom tech that Natasha had either built herself or received from trustworthy contacts.

    Yelena was driving; Natasha was asleep; Kate Bishop was pretending.

    The road, the cabin. The road, the farmhouse. The road, the hole in the wall. And now, they were on the road again. Only this time, their next destination was New York City. Yelena had never been there in person, but she remembered glimpses of it from the movies of her childhood. She also knew that it was a dangerous place for her sister—as well as for herself. Cameras were nearly everywhere, and all the people they were running from had access to them. Even if the US government didn’t want to admit it, their surveillance state was heavily compromised.

    In the backseat, Kate Bishop sat with her forehead resting against the window. Unlike Natasha, Kate didn’t have a blanket draped over her. Instead, she’d placed her jacket across her lap to cover her legs. With her eyes closed and her face relaxed, she could have been mistaken as asleep by any number of less observant people, but Yelena knew better.

    When a car passed them by, the headlights briefly caused Kate’s eyes and face to twitch, giving Yelena the perfect opportunity to call her friend on her poor deception skills. There was only one problem: Yelena did not want to.

    The radio gurgled in and out of stations, intermittently filling the car with static. As with so many other things, the sound made Yelena long for the simplicity of cabin life. The only radio station up there was terrible, but the atmosphere was so beautiful, so uncomplicated. Lucky the wolf had kept her warm at night and during the day—in body and mind. Kate Bishop, the human, kept her in a constant state of confusion.

    “How much longer?” a soft voice murmured from the backseat, drawing Yelena’s eyes briefly off the road and to the woman behind her. Kate’s blue eyes seemed brighter than usual, proving Yelena’s theory that her friend had not slept at all since they started driving.

    “A few hours,” Yelena replied as she dragged her eyes back to the road, keeping her voice just as quiet. “Plenty of time for you to dream of snowy mountains and rabbits.” The words were out of her mouth before she could stop them. Jaw stiffening, she barreled on quickly. “You should rest now. Before it is too late for you to do so.”

    “Can’t,” Kate said, sitting up and leaning closer to Yelena’s seat. When her hands clasped the headrest, Yelena wanted to tell her to let go immediately, but something stopped her. Perhaps it was the strange expression on Kate’s face; perhaps it was the memory of Kate’s hands on her waist, holding her close; maybe it was her own weakness. “I keep thinking about…” The half-finished sentence hung in the air, settling swiftly on Yelena’s shoulders to distract her. “Stop,” Yelena said, readjusting her grip on the steering wheel. Natasha’s breathing was even, and her eyes were closed, her body language giving no indication that she wasn’t asleep. And still, there was always a chance.

    “Stop what?” Kate muttered, wrapping her arms around the seat before putting her chin on it. “Stop thinking,” Yelena said as she did her best to keep her expression neutral. Kate’s forearms rested lightly against Yelena’s upper back, only distracting her further. There was no intentionality behind the touch, yet she felt more comforted than she had in days. She hated this connection they shared—it made her weak.

    Huffing out a breath, Kate rolled her eyes. Then, she closed them and reshuffled her legs to get more comfortable—a bad sign.

    “Can’t,” Kate repeated quietly, her face settling into a mix of melancholy and something else. When she next opened her eyes, the blue seemed duller than before. “If I stop thinking about you, I’ll start thinking about them.” A snarky reply lived on the tip of Yelena’s tongue, ready to see the light of day and ruin the safety that Kate Bishop was desperately searching for. Biting back her own instincts, she forced herself to stay quiet. “It’s like their blood is in my mouth. All the time.”

    Yelena slit Charlotte Kelly’s throat first. Even now, her hand felt the phantom weight of the scalpel. Squeezing the steering wheel, Yelena willed herself to forget the sight of warm, red blood welling up from her incision. Francesca Trask followed next. Another clean incision, another well of blood. Neither of them ever saw it coming.

    “You should not have watched,” Yelena whispered, allowing the memory of the two kills to wash over her. Killing someone with her hands was always more personal than killing them with sniper rifles. She didn’t feel guilty for killing Kelly and Trask—they were the reason Kate was in this mess. They were the reason Yelena worried so much about her friend—a friend she now wanted to kiss. A most undesirable development. The only regret she had was the look in Kate’s eyes as the heart monitors started flatlining. “You should have stayed upstairs.”

    “No,” Kate replied softly, her blue eyes focused intently on Yelena. “I had to see it.” No one else was on the road, so Yelena indulged in a few extended seconds of eye contact. “You did it to protect me. The least I can do is share the burden.” Unfurling her right arm from around the seat, Kate placed her fingers on Yelena’s shoulder. Never breaking eye contact, she slid her hand across Yelena’s upper chest. Not being shrugged off seemed to have given her more courage. She did it slowly and with only the barest touch of her palm, as if wanted to make it clear that it would be easy to tell her no. Kate’s hand did not stray into uncouth territories, though Yelena wondered how she might have reacted to something weird and off-putting like that. Instead, Kate’s fingers came to a stop on Yelena’s other shoulder, engaging her in a half- embrace. The position was probably not all that comfortable for Kate, considering she was half-hanging over the seat, but Yelena didn’t call her on it.

    “Share the burden,” Yelena echoed, staring blankly at the cone of light ahead. Tonight, this car was her sanctuary. “That you have not stumbled and fallen from the weight is a miracle.” Tonight, she needed no sustenance. Kate’s touch and Natasha’s soft breathing—they were enough. “With each passing day, the load you wish to carry will grow heavier. Until, inevitably, Kate Bishop, you will be crushed under your own hubris. And the magnitude of what we have done.”

    “Then let me be crushed,” Kate murmured, leaning forward between the seats to nuzzle Yelena’s cheek. When she leaned back, her mouth lingered by Yelena’s ear, her words barely audible even to her. “Let me stumble; let me fall. But let it be my choice.”

    Exhaling slowly, Yelena allowed her right hand to abandon the steering wheel. When she touched Kate’s arm across her chest, she was not startled by her warmth. The strength that slumbered underneath the surface was nothing to fear. The wolf interwoven between human skin and bones would not hurt her, and neither would Kate.

    “Why must you be so adamant to ignore my warnings?” Yelena asked, immediately startled at her own voice. When had she decided to show such vulnerability? Let me stumble; let me fall. But let it be my choice. The choice was hers, wasn’t it? The choice was hers and hers alone. Kate Bishop would not take this choice from her. Even when she squeezed Yelena again, the touch was not a threat.

    “Because you deserve it,” Kate said, stroking Yelena’s shoulder with her thumb.

    “After everything—” Yelena started, her nails digging into Kate’s sleeve.

    “Yes,” Kate interrupted her, eyes staring intently at Yelena’s face in the rearview mirror. “After everything.” Taking a deep breath, Kate readjusted her legs a second time, the jacket sliding off her knees. “I wasn’t turned because a wolf decided to attack me. I was turned because I decided to attack a wolf. If the wolf hadn’t bitten me that night... I want to say I wouldn’t have ended up like Kelly and Trask. I want to say I wouldn’t have followed in my parents’ footsteps, but I can’t know that for sure.”

    “You were young,” Yelena said, voice soft and loaded with all too much understanding.

    “I was seventeen,” Kate replied, her thumb moving back and forth on Yelena’s arm. When Yelena next looked at her, Kate’s eyes had also shifted to the road. “How old were you when the Red Room took you?”

    This time, Kate didn’t apologize for asking.

    “Six.” Yelena blindly moved her fingers along Kate’s forearm, smoothing down the fabric, giving no thought to the consequences of revealing more of herself. “When it happened, Natasha tried to protect me. She told them: She’s only six. You can’t take her.” The memory spilled from her tongue like rotten milk, expectedly sour. The road blurred in front of her briefly as the faces of men from her past hovered before her, Kate Bishop’s warmth her only anchor to the present. “She held me so tight, I thought they would never have the strength to separate us.” Alexei’s words to Natasha floated back to her through time and space. Back then, Yelena didn’t understand. You were even younger. But she understood now. “She’d just turned twelve.”

    “I’m sorry,” Kate said, leaning their heads back together.

    “The past is the past. But thank you for saying that.” Letting out a deep, calming breath, Yelena released Kate’s arm. Reaching further up, she clasped the back of Kate’s neck. “I cannot change what I was or what I have done.” Kate’s nose nuzzled her hair, sending Yelena’s heart into a flutter. “I cannot change the death and destruction I have brought upon this world.” Her fingers slipped higher, finding the baby hair at the top of Kate’s neck. It was soft—soft like the rest of Kate. “We cannot change what we were. We can only choose who we are.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, closing her eyes and taking a deep breath. A second later, her stare returned while her fingers started drumming on the seat and Yelena’s shoulder. “So…” she began, which never boded well for Yelena. “If you’re saying I can totally be whoever I want…” Her eyes flitted to Natasha, who still gave every sign of being unconscious, before quickly flitting back. “Can I be someone who gets to kiss you?”

    “Don’t push it, Kate Bishop,” Yelena replied, removing her fingers from Kate’s hair so she could gently but decisively slap her palm against Kate’s face instead. The thwack of the unexpected impact caused a nearly imperceptible flinch in Natasha’s body, a movement so subtle that only a fellow widow would have noticed. “Now you have done it! You have woken the beast from its slumber.”

    “I didn’t do anything,” Kate grumbled quietly before slinking back into her seat. Her pout was almost as adorable as the way she rubbed her face.

    “Children,” Natasha groused as she lazily rolled her head towards Yelena, opening her eyes only wide enough to glare at her. “I know you’re both too ugly for beauty sleep to matter, but some of us over here are fighting for our lives to stay perfect on this mission.”

    “Wow.” Yelena rolled her eyes. “Your delusions grow worse every day.”

    “My mom always said I inherited the handsome Bishop features,” Kate muttered simultaneously, crossing her arms over her chest. Her pout grew with renewed vigor, almost making Yelena wish for a life where she didn’t have to be afraid of taking and owning photographs.

    With a hum, Natasha leaned to the side until she could look at Kate around the edge of her seat.

    “Cute,” she said, her smile not quite managing to be sarcastic.

    “I’m not cute!” Kate shot back, her biceps muscles flexing under her sweater sleeves as she leaned towards Natasha.

    “It would be such a shame if we ran headfirst into a pole right now and you broke your neck from the impact,” Yelena muttered to her sister, putting her hand on Natasha’s shoulder and pushing her back so she couldn’t look at Kate’s grumpy face anymore.

    “Don’t threaten me with a good time,” Natasha replied instantly before readjusting her blanket. “Now shut up and keep driving. As for you, Jack—” Again, she leaned around the seat, ignoring Yelena’s eye roll and derisive snap of the tongue. “Stop barking in the back, or I’ll be forced to put you in the trunk.”

    “I’ll be forced to put you in the trunk,” Kate repeated in a mocking tone of voice. “Your dog jokes are so original.”

    “I know,” Natasha said, finally sitting back upright and snuggling further against her seat. “I’m hilarious. You can clap and cheer for me later.” With a self-satisfied smile, she closed her eyes and let out a long, slow breath. “Nighty-night, kids.”

    “Nighty-night,” Yelena muttered derisively, rolling her eyes as she leaned her left elbow against the car door to rest her cheek against her first. Keeping her right hand on the steering wheel, she glanced first at Kate’s pout and then at her sister’s self-satisfied expression. “Next time we stop to pee, I will leave you both by the side of the road.”

    “Unfair!” Kate protested while Natasha merely snorted.

    ##

    January 10th, 2017 | 10:57 P.M.

    Yelena didn’t know much about the birthplace of the Avengers. What little she did know, she’d learned from mission briefings and Red Room video footage. Before that, the movies of her childhood had been her only point of reference. In reality, the city was different from her preconceptions, yet somehow exactly the same. The streets of New York were almost never empty. Thousands of civilian cars struggled to pull ahead while taxi drivers weaved around them like seasoned racecar drivers. The honking never ceased, audible even in this abandoned bunker underneath it all.

    The streets full of people made it easy to hide, but it also made it more dangerous. Anyone could recognize them here—anyone could attack them here. Yelena didn’t know much about the birthplace of the Avengers, but she knew it wasn’t safe.

    “Go fish,” Natasha said with a smirk, leaning back in her seat before taking a sip of her beer. She’d pulled her hair into a bun, but several strands hung around her face. The baby-blue sweater she wore was adorned with an Avengers logo on the front, but the sweater wasn’t hers. If Yelena wasn’t mistaken, Natasha had pilfered it from Kate Bishop’s duffle bag.

    “Are you lying to me?” Yelena asked, looking from Natasha’s face to the cards in her hands. “About having any twos? Why would I lie about that?” Natasha took another sip before pulling her left foot onto her chair and wrapping her arm around it. The bottle dangled from her fingers, appearing dangerously close to falling and spilling its contents all over the concrete floor of the bunker. Every now and then, subway cars rattled past their hide-out, shaking their eardrums but somehow not their walls.

    “Because,” Yelena said, taking a card from the draw pile, “you are a liar and a cheat.”

    “Fair point,” Natasha replied, seconds before a voice spoke in Yelena’s left ear.

    “Are you really playing Go Fish! right now?” Kate asked, sounding exasperated and just a little freaked out. Yelena couldn’t blame her. She remembered being terrified during her first solo mission. But then again… she’d been eight years old. “You’re supposed to be paying attention to my surroundings.”

    “We are,” Yelena replied, her eyes moving to the giant wall of monitors to her right. The one in the middle displayed the interior of a bus and its inhabitants, while another showed the illuminated evening streets through a window. “The man in front of you is a doctor. He is tired from his shift. The woman to his left is a musician returning from practice. She is holding her violin case very close to her chest because it cost her north of four hundred dollars. The woman to her left—”

    “I get it,” Kate grumbled just as the camera angle shifted upwards with her movements. Over the comms, Yelena heard the announcement of Park Avenue reverberate through the bus seconds before Kate got off.

    “I feel I should point out that we offered to come with you,” Natasha said, gesturing at the screens with her bottle even though Kate could not see her. “You’re the one who wanted to do this on your own, tough girl.”

    “No two for me,” Yelena finally said as she slipped the card onto her hand. “Your turn, cheater.”

    “You’re unbelievable,” Kate grumbled as she hurried down Park Avenue, the footage shaking with every step. No one seemed to be following her, which gave Yelena a great deal more comfort than it should have. The chances of Kate being discovered were slim to none. They all needed to get a grip on this mission.

    “Time to give me your threes,” Natasha said, holding out her empty hand. On the screen, a street sign flashed with the lettering 41st Street. Immediately after, a building that Yelena recognized from their reconnaissance pushed itself into frame.

    “Good evening,” Kate said politely as she passed by the doorman, who returned her greeting in kind. Recognition passed across his face, but he didn’t say anything else. When Kate left him behind, Yelena watched on the second monitor as the man turned back to the front without so much as twitching. Several seconds later, Kate let out a shaky breath. “I can’t believe that worked.”

    “Your lack of faith is duly noted,” Natasha said, wriggling the fingers of her outstretched hand. “And here I thought dogs were supposed to be man’s best friend.” Tearing her eyes from the elevator on screen, Yelena looked down at the three threes in her hand. With a hum, she shook her head.

    “Go fish.”

    “Ignoring that,” Kate muttered as she pressed a number on the panel in front of her before removing a keyring from her pocket. As soon as she’d turned the key in the lock next to the panel, the elevator closed its doors and started to ascend. “This was a good idea,” Kate forcefully whispered as she stashed the keys back in her pocket. “This was a good idea. This was a good idea.” The cameras shook, causing Yelena to surmise that her friend was moving up and down on the balls of her feet. Warmth flooded Yelena’s heart. If Kate Bishop were here, she might have considered hugging her.

    “Well,” Natasha said, drawing a card from the draw pile. “Now it’s getting to be insulting, Jack.”

    “How do I change this drama channel? I am bored of you both,” Yelena interjected as the elevator made its way further and further up the skyscraper. As she reshuffled her cards, she tried to catch the reflection of her sister’s cards in her dangling beer bottle. If only Natasha would hold it still for a second longer.

    The elevator stopped.

    “Okay,” Kate said, and the camera shook one last time. “I can do this. I can do this. It’s not a big deal. It’s actually fine. Why wouldn’t it be fine?” In the second camera, Yelena watched in the pristine copper-colored wall as Kate’s reflection touched the left side of her stomach. Underneath, hidden by several layers of clothing, forty-two scars were laid out in the shape of a bite. Several days ago, right after Natasha was done cleaning the gunshot wound on Kate’s other side, Yelena had taken the time to count them, her fingers moving from scar to scar. “Take a deep breath,” Yelena said softly, wishing she had disobeyed Kate’s instructions to let her do this alone. If she dropped down from the elevator ceiling now to hold Kate’s hand, she knew she would not be shrugged off. Just as Kate followed her gentle suggestion, the doors opened, and a richly furnished foyer came into view. “It is time to say hello.”

    The camera moved further inside, but before the elevator doors had a chance to close, Yelena watched Kate’s hand slide off her side.

    “Hello?” Kate called out, raising her voice before taking another step forward.

    “No three for me,” Natasha said quietly, placing the card on her hand. “Your turn.”

    But Yelena couldn’t tear her eyes off the monitors as a woman appeared on screen.

    “Charlotte, what a pleasant surprise,” the woman said, her brows furrowing for only a second as the skin tightened around her eyes. She was tall—taller than she appeared in photographs. Her brown hair shimmered in the light of a dozen or so lamps scattered across the walls, cupboards, and ceiling. Long, swishy sleeves completed her dark green pantsuit, while heavy, golden jewelry adorned her fingers, neck, and ears. “Jerry must have forgotten to buzzer up, or I would have poured you a drink.”

    Kate said nothing.

    The doors to the elevator finally closed.

    “I’m glad you decided to stop by,” the woman said, closing the distance between them. Liar, Yelena wanted to say, staring intently as the woman made to lean in to air-kiss each cheek. When Kate pulled back before she could, the woman took a step back in kind. “Are you alright, darling? I’ve been concerned about your lack of communication. Did something happen on the mission?”

    The camera moved another step back. Then, Yelena saw an arm move through the frame. She didn’t need to be there to know what Kate was doing now that the cameras installed in the building hallways and elevator could no longer see her.

    The earpiece was just about strong enough to pick up the mechanical click and swish of a ripple effect as the Widow's Veil on Kate’s face switched itself off. A second later, the same hand moved back through the frame, loosely holding the limp nano mask.

    The beat of silence following the revelation lasted for a split second.

    “Kate…?” the woman whispered as all control, composure, and careful diplomacy left her face. Slowly, carefully, she crossed the two steps that Kate had taken back, fingers visibly trembling as she lifted them to what Yelena assumed were Kate’s cheeks. “My baby.”

    “Hey, Mom,” Kate replied, voice thick with tears. “I’m home.”

    A sob traveled between time and space, sending shivers and shockwaves through Yelena’s nervous system. Had Melina ever been that worried over her? Had Alexei? She remembered Melina stroking her hair and telling her bedtime stories; she remembered riding on Alexei’s shoulders as he squeezed her ankles and laughed; she remembered Melina kissing her injuries after silly childhood accidents; she remembered Alexei hugging her after her first day of kindergarten; and she remembered their voices on the day of their separation. My girls, she thought. My girls.

    There was no convenient mirror or reflective surface to watch the reunion, yet Yelena averted her gaze all the same. This moment did not belong to her. This moment didn’t need to be witnessed by anybody else. Without saying a word, Yelena lifted her pointer finger. When she looked at Natasha to make sure she understood, she saw her sister’s eyes were still glued to the screens. Was it wistfulness that kept her sister’s attention? Or was it something else? Folding her cards, Yelena placed them face-down on the table before getting to her feet.

    Making no sound, she tried to pass by her sister’s chair when Natasha’s hand caught her wrist. On the screen, Eleanor Bishop had not moved from her position. Reaching up to her ear, Yelena was on the verge of switching off her side of the comms when Eleanor finally spoke.

    “Charlotte told me you were dead,” Eleanor said, voice trembling as she pulled back without removing her hands. Her gaze moved hungrily across Kate’s face, drinking in her daughter while her blue eyes glittered with unshed tears. Blue eyes. Such a familiar color. “She said —” Instinctively, Eleanor’s right hand went to her mouth as she stumbled over the words.

    Clearing her throat, she quickly wiped away the first tear that dared to fall. “I’m sorry, darling.” She cleared her throat again, then straightened her back and shoulders. “I can’t believe you’re really here. I thought…”

    “They shot me,” Kate explained, her voice sounding just as rough around the edges. Curling her hands into fists, Yelena closed her eyes and focused on Kate’s breathing. On her words. This, she thought. This is why. This is why they’re dead. This is why I killed them. “That’s why they thought I was dead, but… I got away before they could finish the job. I got help.” “Oh,” Eleanor gasped, returning her right hand to Kate’s face as a fresh surge of pained emotions passed nakedly across her face. “Honey.” More tears fell, but Eleanor didn’t wipe them away this time. She seemed too busy staring at her only child. Would she ask herself, Yelena wondered. Would she ask herself if she was a bad person? Would she agonize the way Kate agonized over this question? “This is all my fault.”

    “Yes,” Kate breathed, hands trembling as they clasped Eleanor’s forearms. The nano mask had long since disappeared into a pocket. Before Eleanor could pull away or say anything else, Kate visibly squeezed her mother’s arms. Yelena knew this comfort-giving behavior from Kate all too well, yet seeing it displayed with someone else her friend cared about caused Yelena’s heart do throb in a weird and irregular way. Quickly, she pushed the thought and feeling as far away from herself as possible. This proved nothing. “But I forgive you, Mom. I forgive you.”

    The words pierced Yelena’s chest in a way she didn’t expect. They’d all talked about what they needed from Eleanor Bishop to get them where they wanted to go, but they hadn’t discussed the emotional aspect. Uncharacteristically, Kate had kept most of her thoughts on her mother and their reunion to herself. Now, watching this moment, Yelena couldn’t help but understand.

    “My baby,” Eleanor repeated, tears falling from her eyes like rain fell from the sky. “I’m…” A sob broke free of her lungs, and her hands moved to her own face to cover her shame. A second later, the camera went black as Kate dragged her mother into an embrace.

    Almost in unison, Yelena and Natasha switched off their microphones. Then, finally, with Eleanor Bishop’s sobs and Kate Bishop’s words of reassurance and forgiveness in her ear, Yelena left the table to grab herself a drink.

    ##

    January 13th, 2017 | 05:01 A.M.

    Pulling into the parking lot of the private airplane hangar in front of them should have sparked bad memories or possibly even anxiety in Yelena’s stomach. Instead, when she unfurled herself from the passenger seat of Natasha’s supercar, she was relieved.

    “It’s interesting,” Natasha said as she got out and handed the keys to a valet who awaited their arrival and quickly bowed to them. After rounding the car, Natasha leaned her forearm on Yelena’s shoulder and watched as the valet drove further into the hangar space. “You don’t notice how nice Jack’s rambling is until she’s gone. Like white noise, almost.”

    Yelena looked at the hangar and what hopefully awaited them inside. A dozen or so people went about their jobs, moving back and forth without a care in the world and without sparing them a second glance. She shook her head and started walking. Most of the time, discretion was the way to go. But occasionally, it had its perk to be disgustingly rich.

    “I cannot wait to hear you complain about that white noise once we’re in the air, and you will be stuck with it for ten hours,” Yelena muttered out of the corner of her mouth, intermittently nodding and smiling at the people they passed. Despite her relief, she couldn’t stop looking for Red Room insurgents hidden amongst the crowd.

    “That’s on you, kiddo. You’re her in-flight entertainment,” Natasha said seconds before they rounded the hangar and could see into its huge, open front door. With a low whistle, Natasha shook her head. “Remember how I told you SHIELD would occasionally farm out smaller jobs to Bishop Security?” When Yelena grunted in recognition, Natasha let out a derisive chuckle. “They should’ve known better than to give that company access to all the good toys.”

    “Their mistake is now our benefit,” Yelena said, looking up at the imposing nose of the black jet in front of them. “I thought you would come pick me up in one of these after I freed myself. Not in a bad truck with even worse tires.”

    “I’m sorry my insurrection was inconvenient for you,” Natasha said with a smile and a roll of her eyes. Sticking her hands into the pockets of her suit jacket, she started crossing the distance between them and the jet, Yelena following close behind. Even from here, Yelena could see their luggage being carried inside while a pair of women stood by and watched.

    Eleanor Bishop seemed even taller in person, although Yelena surmised that this had much to do with her fierce expression and imposing fashion choices. The woman looked good, and under different circumstances, Yelena might have taken the time to admire her sense of style. Knowing about Eleanor’s history, however, somewhat dampened Yelena’s enthusiasm.

    “Good morning, America,” Natasha said as she closed in on Eleanor and Kate, removing her hands from her pockets in favor of spreading her arms. “How are my two favorite millionaires.” Behind her, Yelena shook her head, watching the people around them for any sign of mistrust. There was nothing. Even as the valet left the jet, he only bowed and smiled again before quickly hurrying off to give them more privacy.

    “Francine,” Eleanor said as a muscle jumped at her jaw. “So good to see you.”

    “Charlotte, Eleanor,” Natasha replied genially, moving closer to Kate’s mother to kiss her cheekbone and give her an enthusiastic air hug. If Yelena had been standing any further away from them, she would have missed her sister’s next words entirely. “Does Director Fury know you kept one of his Quinjets?” When Natasha pulled back, a brief spell of sourness crossed Eleanor’s features before it was gone. Yelena had to hand it to the woman—her poker face was almost as good as theirs.

    “I’ve provided everything you asked for,” Eleanor said to Natasha in a calm and sweet tone of voice, but Yelena’s eyes were no longer on her. Instead, they were focused on Charlotte Kelly’s impassive expression. Brown eyes stared back at her. Brown instead of blue. A fierce and terrible jaw instead of a strong and gentle one. Yet Kate Bishop could not deny the physicality of her body, and the trench coat she wore could barely hide the presence of her wolf. Yelena had no idea how it had taken this long for Kate to be discovered. Perhaps it had been the sheer will of her mother’s love that kept her employees at bay.

    “That’s not what I asked,” Natasha replied, leaning forward while raising her eyebrows. Her smile was like a shark’s, all teeth with the promise of future carnage. “But I suppose we can discuss this at another time.” Turning to Kate, Natasha closed the distance between them before giving her an exaggerated air hug and cheek kiss as well.

    Without giving any indication that she was troubled, Eleanor turned her eyes to Yelena. For some reason, Yelena had never given much thought to meeting Kate’s mother. From what seemed like a hundred years ago, Natasha’s words returned to Yelena’s mind. I’m surprised her mom didn’t gut her the second she found out. In the morning sunshine that fell through the open hangar door, Eleanor Bishop looked nothing like the broken woman on the bunker monitors. I’m sorry, she’d said. I’m sorry. But did that really matter in the grand scheme of things?

    “Eleanor,” Yelena said, injecting just a hint of steel into the name. When she crossed the distance, she saw the reaction Kate’s mother had to her advance. She was a hunter through and through; that much Yelena was certain of. And yet, she’d made an exception for her child —an exception Melina and Alexei would have never made.

    “Erica,” Eleanor replied, reaching out to shake Yelena’s hand.

    Holding on tight, Yelena leaned up on her toes. “I shot Erica in the head,” she whispered, thickening her access while hiding her mouth by turning it towards Eleanor’s cheek. “She came to kill your daughter. I put her down like an animal. No one will ever find her.” Pulling back, Yelena kept their faces close together. “You would do well to remember what happens to hunters who cross me.”

    “Very charming,” Eleanor replied, squeezing Yelena’s hand back equally as tightly. Her blue eyes flashed dangerously, but Yelena didn’t feel threatened. What was the point? Eleanor Bishop could do nothing to her. Not here. Still, she didn’t expect the corners of Eleanor’s eyes to soften. Nor did she expect the sad smile that marred Eleanor’s handsome features. “I didn’t protect her.”

    “No,” Yelena agreed, loosening her hold on Eleanor’s hand only for Eleanor to keep squeezing.

    “Will you?” Eleanor asked, her voice almost too quiet to understand.

    “Yes,” Yelena confirmed without hesitation, increasing the strength of her handshake until Eleanor was finally forced to let go. Staying very close to Kate’s mother, Yelena lifted her chin. “Check your pocket once you are back at work. You will find a thumb drive.” Out of the corner of her eye, she saw Kate and Natasha move towards the back of the jet. “Follow the instructions.” Eleanor’s mouth pinched into the early stages of mutiny. “Do it for her. Be a good mother. Make sure it is safe for her to return. She deserves to feel safe.”

    “Who are you?” Eleanor asked, clearly unable to contain herself.

    “I am the person who will kill you if you hurt Kate again,” Yelena said before smoothing her mouth into a friendly smile. “Anyway, so good to see you, Eleanor. Thank you for supporting us. We’ll be back in no time.” There was something endlessly satisfying about using her fake American voice. The voice she’d had as a child but lost after being moved back to Russia to serve the Red Room. “Have a great day. Remember to check your inbox.”

    Leaving Eleanor behind, Yelena rounded the butt of the Quinjet before walking onboard. The jet was on a smaller scale than some of the others Yelena had seen during her time. Yet she already knew it was better equipped than anything else they could otherwise get their hands on. The interior was sleek, modern, and polished. Their luggage had been stored neatly away and while there were no beds, the seats were clearly reclinable. Considering that Yelena had spent much of the last few weeks sleeping in a car, this was a luxury.

    As soon as Yelena was on board, Natasha initiated the closing of the hatch. “Get your butt in your seat,” she said, hands already on the controls in preparation for take-off. Kate sat up front, though she wasn’t buckled in.

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said as soon as the hatch snapped shut. Almost startled, Kate looked at her, although the expression seemed all wrong on Charlotte Kelly. There was something nearly perverse about how they wore their victims’ faces. Every time Yelena looked at Kate and Natasha, she felt the handle of the scalpel in her hand, saw the blood well up from the cuts. And yet, when Kate closed the distance between them to greet her, Yelena didn’t hesitate. She faced no resistance as she dragged Kate down to her level before wrapping her arms around her friend’s shoulders and embracing her. “You have been gone for two days.”

    “Yeah,” Kate said, wrapping her arms around Yelena in turn. “Missed you, too.”

    “Do not assume,” Yelena said, squeezing shut her eyes as she replaced the memories of murder with this moment of reassurance.

    “Shut up,” Natasha called from the front, driving the jet out of the hangar with practiced ease. “We both missed you, Jack! Next time we’re stuck in a New York bunker, you’re stuck there with us. Making fun of Yelena is like punching water.” Under different circumstances, Yelena might have rolled her eyes. For now, she made do with dragging her nails down Kate’s neck and enjoying how she trembled.

    ##

    January 13th, 2017 | 05:36 A.M.

    They were in the air. The hangar was visible, but the people moving back and forth around it had disappeared, becoming too small for the eye to see. Natasha was still working the controls, checking to see if any modifications had been made to the machine since Bishop Security took it over. As Yelena leaned back in her seat and idly tapped the side of her Widow’s Veil, she wondered if Bishop Security’s new name would remain the same. For the moment, no one knew that Charlotte Kelly of Bishop & Kelly, Inc. was dead and gone, buried in a landfill under mountains of trash where no one would ever find her. But there would no doubt come a time when friends and family would start to worry about Kelly’s new mission and what might have happened to her there.

    After the nano mask slipped off her face, Yelena felt herself relax for the first time since they left the hole in the wall they’d last called a safe house. The bunker they used for the last four days was more than secure, but there’d also been the undeniable truth that New York City wasn’t a safe place for them to be.

    “These things smell weird,” Kate said as she took off her own nano mask, lips pursing as she glared down at the distorted face of Charlotte Kelly before the Widow’s Veil returned to its uniform, standardized shape.

    “I don’t smell anything,” Yelena proclaimed before unbuckling her seatbelt, grabbing the Widow’s veil from Kate’s hands, and getting to her feet. The Quinjet was no longer climbing higher, and the warning light on the dashboard that signaled no one should be moving around had gone out several minutes ago. “Maybe you forgot to brush your teeth this morning.”

    “I did not,” Kate grumbled as she surreptitiously lifted her palm to her face to check her breath. With a soft but hidden smile, Yelena took Natasha’s Veil before moving towards their luggage to store the three nano masks in their cases. Kate blew a strand of hair out of her face before unbuckling her seatbelt and getting to her feet. “I can’t believe we’re really flying to Russia in a jet.”

    “In a jet your mother stole from the US government,” Natasha said casually, seemingly satisfied that the Quinjet she operated had not been changed all that much. Hitting a button on the dashboard, she took her hands off the controls. “I’d almost have to applaud her if I didn’t know what she probably used this for.”

    “I didn’t know she had this,” Kate said, her fingers moving restlessly at her sides. Pursing her lips, Yelena allowed her eyes to trace her friend’s body from head to toe. The full moon grew closer with each passing second, its power visible in every nervous movement of Kate’s hands and feet. It pulled at her human body, making her jumpy and anxious. Perhaps they should have waited. Perhaps they should have allowed Kate to transform and run somewhere in the States.

    “Maybe I’ll use this to bust Clint and the others out of prison once we’re done with the Red Room,” Natasha mused before getting to her feet and stretching her arms over her head.

    Francesca Trask’s clothing style looked horrible on her—a mismatch of pantsuit and tactical gear. Somewhere along this journey, Yelena had grown used to seeing her sister in oversized, comfy sweaters. “There is no way I am returning this baby to your mom.”

    “If it even survives the trip,” Yelena muttered under her breath, ignoring Natasha’s eye roll. “Clint?” Kate asked, her right hand squeezing the fingers of her left from thumb to pinky, one after the other, a nervous habit Yelena had observed upon her before. The trench coat did little to hide the powerful predator underneath; it was especially noticeable now that Kate was excited about something. “Can I meet him, do you think? I mean—can I help you bust him out of prison? He’s my favorite—"

    “If you tell me about your love for Clint one more time,” Natasha said, pushing past Kate to join Yelena at the luggage hatch. When Kate pouted and crossed her arms over her chest, Natasha couldn’t entirely hide her smile. Yelena would have never admitted it, but she loved how her sister cherished Kate’s presence. Their friendship felt incredibly simple. For one, Natasha probably didn’t think about Kate Bishop’s lips every time she looked at her. “You should only have one favorite Avenger.”

    “Yes,” Yelena concurred, patting Natasha’s back. “And it should be one of the big ones.”

    Natasha whipped up her head, her mouth opening and her brows furrowing. “What are you talking about?” she asked, taking her hands off the piece of luggage that housed their snacks, even though they’d had a proper breakfast only recently. “Who are the big ones?”

    “I’ll be the biggest Avenger once I join up,” Kate said, slapping her right fist into her left palm, her body vibrating with excitement rather than moon sickness. Hunching forward a little, she flexed her biceps and let out a little growl.

    “It is not about size,” Yelena said, patting Natasha on the shoulder as she gave Kate a shake of the head. “It is about power.”

    “Right for the jugular every time,” Natasha replied, resuming her hunt for snacks. “I don’t know why I put up with either of you. You know, just because I’m not six feet tall doesn’t mean I’m not every bit the super soldier that Rogers is.”

    “I didn’t say anything,” Kate grumbled, moving her shoulders up and down to keep her body moving. “Clint’s always been my hero because he didn’t have any powers.” Shrugging off the trench coat, Kate tossed it onto one of the seats. “He inspired me. I saw him jump off a building with no parachute while shooting arrows at aliens. It was so cool.” A spell of wistful memory slipped across her face. “I decided to pick up archery because of him. Mom said I should choose something more practical…” Her mouth clicked shut as her hands grabbed the back of the seat.

    “If you knew how much of a boring-ass dad Clint really is, you wouldn’t—” Natasha started, stopping only when Yelena placed her hand on her shoulder. Furrowing her brows, Natasha looked up from the bag of beef jerky she was holding. When she turned around, the confusion cleared from her face.

    “Archery is very impractical,” Yelena said softly, keeping her hand on her sister to stop herself from crossing the distance between herself and Kate. “You have a very limited range of motion, and if you want to bring extra ammo, it has to be a big bag.”

    The padding of the headrest groaned under Kate’s strength.

    “You know that’s not…” Kate whispered, staring down at her hands. Like she only just realized she was damaging the jet, she released the padding before crossing her arms. “She wanted me to use machine guns and handguns because they’re more efficient.” Seconds later, the tension in Kate’s body slackened, and her hands dropped to her sides. “I’m sorry, you guys. I don’t mean to be a downer. I haven’t seen you in two days, and the first thing I do is whine about my fricking mom.” Rubbing her face, Kate shook her shoulders again while Natasha nudged Yelena’s arm and gestured at Kate with her head.

    Pursing her lips, Yelena nudged Natasha back, using enough strength to topple over any regular person. Natasha shook her head and finally tore open her snacks. Breathing out slowly through her nose, Yelena marched around her stubborn and annoying sister until she stood beside Kate. “Will you rub your face until it peels off? I don’t know if the Widow’s Veil can help with that,” Yelena said, her words as dry as Natasha’s favorite brand of jerky. When Kate dropped her hands to look at her, Yelena was glad to see that they weren’t red. Still, she wrapped her arms around Kate’s torso and smushed her cheek against Kate’s shoulder. “You can always talk about your mom if you want to, Kate Bishop.”

    “Thanks,” Kate said, carefully placing her hands on Yelena’s upper back. For the first time since they boarded the plane, Kate’s body slowed down. Her twitching stopped, her muscles relaxed, and her arms tightened around Yelena in a calm and gentle hug.

    Closing her eyes, Yelena dug her nails into Kate’s back and allowed herself to enjoy the connection.

    ##

    January 13th, 2017 | 10:12 A.M.

    Cradling an almost empty bag of beef jerky to her chest, Natasha napped on a reclined seat in the last row. One of her feet hung over an armrest while a blanket haphazardly clung to her torso. It probably had less to do with gathering warmth in this nicely climatized Quinjet and more to do with feeling just a little bit more secure—even subconsciously—with an added layer of protection.

    In contrast, Kate and Yelena sat up front in the first row while the Quinjet continued steering them to their destination on autopilot. They were above the cloud line, revealing the perfect sky all around them. Yelena might have found the sunlight uncomfortable if it hadn’t been for the special windshield glass. Instead, it warmed her cold hands and danced across Kate Bishop’s shiny black hair. Kate held a device in her hands Yelena had not seen before, although it evoked memories of her childhood. She remembered seeing toys with similar features; brightly colored joysticks and buttons swam across her brain. The decades in between when she’d last played games like this had sharpened their designs and improved the underlying technology. A desire itched beneath Yelena’s skin to take it apart and see how it worked.

    “And then you just run around and fulfill any quests the NPCs give you. Aside from the main quest, obviously, which I usually forget to do until I’m, like, super over-leveled and everything’s too easy to do,” Kate said, tilting the screen a little more so Yelena could see it better. They were sitting close together, shoulder to shoulder, staring at a small screen that struggled to stand out against the sunlight. “It’s really fun. I usually pick the archery route, obviously. Which also means I sneak around a lot.”

    “Then this game must be very unrealistic,” Yelena said, moving one of the joysticks to see the camera spin. The world on the screen looked much more detailed than the pixelated offerings Yelena had seen during her three years in Ohio. By the time she’d been old enough to play them herself, her time there had been almost over. She remembered watching Natasha yell at the television while Yelena sat on Alexei’s lap with her back against his big chest. She remembered his laughter rumbling against her much smaller body. You are terrible at this game, Natasha!

    “I can be sneaky,” Kate protested, glaring at Yelena with a pout on her lips.

    “No,” Yelena said, putting her hand on Kate’s right forearm to pat it, dragging herself back to her friend and away from her memories. “You are a big wolf. Big wolves are not sneaky.”

    Kate’s sleeves were rolled up to her elbows, leaving the skin of her arm free for Yelena to touch as much or as little as she wanted. Moving her eyes from the toy to Kate’s skin, Yelena drew her pointer and middle finger along the muscles and tendons. “How is the fever?”

    “Better with you around,” Kate said earnestly, her hands curled steadily around her game. “I really did miss you.” Releasing the left side of the console, Kate lifted her hand to her chest, rubbing at the skin and bones above her heart. Leaning forward, she nudged Yelena’s temple with her own. “I’ve been meaning to thank you and Natasha.”

    “For what?” Yelena asked, keeping her eyes on the small screen instead of acknowledging the comfort of Kate’s closeness. Since Kate’s character had been idle for too long, the camera began to spin, rotating around and around. Back and forth, over and over again, circling Kate Bishop.

    “For letting me have that time with my mom,” Kate replied, voice barely audible above the hum of the engines and the wind rushing along the jet. “For trusting me to do this on my own, and for having my back when I needed it.” A soft, careful exhale caressed the side of Yelena’s voice, bringing with it the smell of beef jerky. “My mom cried a lot.”

    “You deserve to be trusted,” Yelena said, her eyes moving from the spinning screen back to Kate’s warm skin. As a black widow assassin, Yelena had spent much of her life with fingerless gloves on her hands. She hadn’t worn them at all since leaving, aside from their mission to the forest to take down Charlotte Kelly and her crew. In contrast, Kate’s skin was naturally tan from being out in the sun, possibly from practicing archery outdoors. Since nothing seemed to blemish that tough, werewolf exterior, Yelena spent precious seconds considering whether Kate’s skin retained the way it looked from before she’d been turned. If the steel or tungsten of armor-piercing bullets couldn’t leave a mark, why should the sun be able to?

    “Yelena,” Kate whispered as the muscles in her forearms tightened, alerting Yelena to a shift in Kate’s demeanor. Turning her head to the left, Kate brushed their noses together before tossing her gaming device onto the seat to her left. A second later, her hands were on Yelena’s cheeks, and her mouth was a hair’s breadth away.

    Heartbeat going into overdrive, Yelena curled her hands into fists on her lap and squeezed shut her eyes. With every shaky breath brushing along her lips, more of her resolve melted away, even though she knew this was a terrible idea. Kissing Kate Bishop in the basement had been a stupid, illogical decision. Yelena was not an illogical person.

    “You’re trembling,” Yelena pointed out, wondering why she didn’t feel threatened or upset about Kate holding her face. Perhaps it was the way Kate’s whole body seemed to vibrate with nervous, unthreatening energy. Maybe it was Yelena’s own waning willpower to stop herself from touching Kate’s strong shoulders or biceps. Maybe it was the roaring in her ears that had nothing to do with the engines or the wind. “Is it the fever? Did we miscalculate the timeline?”

    “No, it’s not just the moon. It’s—I don’t know,” Kate started, softly rubbing Yelena’s nose with her own in a gesture that made Yelena want to hug Kate and never let her go. “I don’t feel the way I did last time. The moon is close; I know it’s growing stronger. I hear its call, but… I feel you, too.” Kate’s mouth produced a shaky exhale full of fear and confusion. “It’s like there are two moons calling my wolf.”

    Two moons, Yelena thought, suddenly certain that whatever this was… it would not end well. She wanted to make a joke out of it—wanted to tease Kate for comparing her face to the surface of a massive interstellar object. But she knew this situation wasn’t funny, and Kate wouldn’t laugh. So, Yelena did the next best thing: She closed the distance between them. Again, she thought, burning lava searing through her veins and arteries. Again, she thought, as her heartbeat jumped out of control. Again, she thought, hearing the crackling of static where a thought should have been. Again, she thought, lifting her hands to dig her nails into overwrought skin. Again, she thought, as the pressure of hands kept her steady when she should have been shaking.

    Seven days had passed since their kiss in the basement. Seven days of forcing herself not to think about Kate Bishop’s soft lips. Seven days of nightmares interspersed with beautiful memories of Kate’s hands holding her close. Seven days of Kate looking at her like a puppy dog whose master was mad at them. Seven days of Yelena avoiding Kate wherever possible.

    Seven days—two of which Kate spent alone with her mother in a New York City penthouse.

    Two of which Yelena and Natasha sat in a bunker between subway cars, playing card games and wondering how Kate was doing. Two days where Yelena lit up only when Kate Bishop gave them a brief status update.

    Sunlight warmed her fingers and body; Kate Bishop warmed her heart.

    Digging her nails into the tense muscles of Kate’s shoulders, Yelena allowed her friend to set the pace of their kiss. Despite the clear urgency of Kate’s demeanor, her lips did not move frantically. Instead, there seemed to be purpose behind each brush of her mouth, behind each tilt of her face and chin. Yelena’s life had only recently been blessed with an overabundance of love and affection. Her first true choice was to abandon the Red Room and flee into the mountains. Up there, when a wolf ran into her truck, she made her second choice.

    Why am I always upside down, she thought, as she slowly pulled away from the kiss. When Kate dropped her hands and stared at her with half-lidded eyes a hundred times bluer than the ocean below them, Yelena wondered why she wasn’t more afraid. Seven days ago, the fear of discovery nearly drove her mad. Now, with Kate shivering and lost in the fever of her rapidly approaching transformation, Yelena felt nothing but the desperate need to protect her friend. Lifting her right hand, Yelena drew her thumb across Kate’s bottom lip. As soon as her palm touched the overheated skin of Kate’s cheek, Kate’s body went still, and her eyes closed like her body needed the soothing calm of Yelena’s presence more than she needed to rip out of her skin. So many possibilities, she thought, thinking of the Red Room and the scientists that had once poked and prodded her. Did Kate have any control over what she was doing? The thought left a sour taste in Yelena’s mouth. Was the wolf controlling Kate’s actions? Did it sense Yelena’s strength and seek to harness it? And what of Kate Bishop’s desire to kiss her? Was that an illusion borne of the moon sickness?

    Leaning back in her seat, Yelena removed her fingers from Kate entirely. Keeping her face neutral, she watched as Kate blinked at her for a few seconds before sitting back. Almost immediately, Kate returned to squeezing her fingers and rotating her feet around, clear signs that whatever connection they shared produced a chemical or perhaps a supernatural reaction. “What do you see?” Yelena asked, listening for any sign that Natasha was no longer asleep. When she heard only soft, almost inaudible breathing, she relaxed. This time, her paranoia had nothing to do with the fear of discovery. Kate Bishop was not in her right mind; that much was obvious. She deserved every bit of privacy Yelena could give her. “I mean when you look at me. What do you see? Is it different now than how it was before? Is there a voice in your head telling you to take my hand and keep me close?”

    Kate’s brows furrowed almost imperceptibly as if she wasn’t sure what Yelena was trying to suggest. “I guess… technically, there’s always a voice in my head,” Kate said hesitantly, lifting her hand to poke at her temple. “I do hear my thoughts as normal words spoken to myself. I know that it’s not the same for everybody, which is also really interesting, but—” “That is not what I mean,” Yelena said, lifting her hand to cut off Kate’s rambling. Pushing down her affection, Yelena sat up and critically studied Kate’s eyes, mouth, and ears. “Is there a voice in your head that is not your own?” Leaning forward in her seat, Yelena lifted up Kate’s wrists and squeezed them. “Like now. Is there a voice now—one that is not your own—telling you to let me touch you?”

    “Uh,” Kate replied, nearly going cross-eyed as Yelena moved closer and closer. Blinking rapidly, she opened her mouth to respond, but no words escaped from between her lips. Then, quickly, she booped Yelena’s nose with her own. “You’re really pretty when you’re scary.

    That’s what I’m thinking right now. I don’t think that’s anybody else’s opinion but mine?” Narrowing her eyes, Yelena smelled Kate’s breath on the off chance she might catch any hint of a parasite or unnatural chemical. But Kate’s breath didn’t smell weird—no weirder than her own or Natasha’s after a handful of beef jerky.

    “And, uh,” Kate whispered, her eyes darting away from Yelena’s. “Look, I’m not saying that I thought you were cute and had any ulterior motives when we stayed together at the cabin. I’m not a creep who pretends to be a wolf to hit on girls later; that’d be—that’d be kind of weird, actually, now that I think about it. And probably counterproductive. But anyway, I wasn’t doing that in the first place, so.” This time, Yelena let her ramble, using the time to catalog Kate’s responses and compare them to how Kate behaved around her when they first started their journey. “Okay, so I’m not saying that, but I did notice that you were really attractive and also very kind and very lonely, and maybe that did, a little bit, lead to me, sort of…” Her neck started turning red from an overabundance of blood, the color creeping up and up until it reached Kate Bishop’s face. “I don’t want to say that I—” She stopped herself, her throat bobbing as she swallowed whatever saliva accumulated in her mouth during her rapid-fire talking.

    At this very pregnant pause, Yelena took stock of her own emotions. She was concerned for her friend. Check. She was worried for the future. Check. She was angry at the idea of mind control. Check. She was annoyed with herself because she hadn’t thought to ask Natasha whether this reaction might be expected from a werewolf. Check. Because asking her about it would have meant talking about it, and talking about it would have meant talking about it. Check, check, and double-check.

    “Your behavior is quite strange,” Yelena remarked, slowly removing her hands from Kate’s wrists to grab the front of her shirt instead. “What has changed?” she asked, her eyes once again moving across Kate’s apprehensive expression. “Who is controlling you?”

    “No one,” Kate muttered, closing the distance between them to kiss Yelena again. When Yelena wouldn’t allow it, Kate chose to nuzzle her nose instead, letting out a shaky breath that seemed to have nothing to do with the moon sickness at all. “I think that’s the problem.”

    ##

    January 13th, 2017 | 06:47 P.M.

    Yelena did not spend the rest of the flight staring at Kate Bishop’s face. It was tempting because there were so many facets to explore, but she decided against it out of simple practicality. What she ended up doing instead was to watch Kate play her video game while keeping a hand on her friend’s back the whole time.

    It was odd. She wasn’t used to having a positive impact on the human beings around her. But it was good, too, to feel and see another person’s reaction to her presence and her touch. The last time she’d put her hand on someone for such a prolonged span of time, they’d ended up dead. She hoped that wouldn’t happen to Kate Bishop.

    She did not allow another kiss between them. Her mistrust of Kate’s autonomy was too great. Now, hours later, Yelena walked behind her sister while the sky turned darker and darker above them. With the last rays of sunshine melting away, so did the warmth in the air, sapping Yelena’s will to keep trudging along. Not that she would mention it to the others; she knew how to control herself. Unlike Kate, who’d started whining about being hungry pretty much the moment they stepped off the Quinjet. Their expensive and top-secret mode of transportation did not follow them into the city. With its invisibility shields in full effect, the jet stood in a forest clearing far away from any prying eyes.

    “Can’t we get take-out?” Kate asked, looking up at a sparkling neon sign that advertised Taiwanese food to passersby. “I’ll be quick.” The whine that escaped her throat sounded almost exactly like Lucky’s. “Extra spicy chili noodles would really hit the spot right now.” “Not yet,” Natasha said, turning around to grab Kate’s arm and drag her along. The pout that crossed the face borrowed by Kate’s nano mask looked much less adorable than Kate’s real one, but Yelena chose to look in a different direction regardless. There was always reconnaissance to be done. Relaxation got you killed. In truth, Yelena had been chastising herself far too little for her recent hubris. Less than two months ago, she’d been the greatest child assassin in several generations of widows. Even now, she traveled with another powerful widow and a young but dangerous werewolf. That still didn’t mean she couldn’t be tricked.

    Predictably, Kate whined about several more restaurants and take-out spots, her hand brushing Yelena’s as they traversed the streets of Budapest. She never tried to take Yelena’s hand, possibly because she was concerned about Yelena’s preference to keep things between them and away from Natasha. But it was obvious that she wanted to.

    When they reached a courtyard between several five-story apartment buildings, Yelena allowed herself a moment of kindness. While Natasha looked around, Yelena slipped her left hand against Kate’s right palm. The warmth was expected but not unwelcome, especially when Kate slipped her fingers in between Yelena’s and squeezed them. Before Natasha could turn back, Yelena let go.

    “Looks the same,” Natasha whispered, her unfamiliar face giving nothing away. “But keep your eyes peeled. This location is known to several of my contacts. If any one of them has been compromised, we could be walking into a trap.”

    “There’s no way anyone’s expecting us,” Kate mumbled with a pinched face, laying both hands on her stomach in a pathetic attempt to look even hungrier than she was. The spiel might have worked, too, if Kate hadn’t eaten most of their rations right in front of Yelena just before they touched down in Budapest.

    “Could be an old trap, Jack,” Natasha pointed out, poking the space between Kate’s furrowed brows. “My enemies are legion.” She smiled, but there was something awfully sincere about her words. Natasha had made many powerful people very, very angry—especially recently.

    “Anyway,” she continued, stuffing her hands into the pockets of her thick, black coat. “I have a lot of good memories here. It’s nice to be back.”

    Kate grumbled something under her breath that Yelena didn’t quite catch. When Natasha disappeared into the building across from them, Yelena nudged Kate’s elbow with her own.

    “This is where it all started for her,” Yelena whispered, sliding her arm through Kate’s. “This is where Barton offered her a way out. A way to leave the Red Room.”

    Blinking into the dim light of the courtyard, Kate pursed her lips. “Oh,” she said before nodding slowly, her fake green eyes staring intently into Yelena’s. When Yelena nodded as well, Kate looked up at the stars and the growing moon above them. “Maybe this place makes her feel safe,” she whispered, her pupils widening even though the full moon was still three days away.

    “Certainly more than Russia ever would,” Yelena said before tugging Kate along like Natasha had done earlier. “Come on. The sooner we get inside and secure the perimeter, the sooner you will have your dinner.”

    “Yeah…” Kate whispered, craning her head backward so her eyes could stay on the moon.

    She didn’t look away until Yelena properly took her hand. When Kate’s eyes focused on Yelena, pupils as wide as they had been during the last full moon, Yelena stopped just inside the hallway of the building. “Have I ever told you,” Kate started, lifting her free hand to Yelena’s cheek. “You’re a hundred times more beautiful than the moon.”

    Swallowing thickly, Yelena cupped Kate’s hand for only a second.

    “Say that to her in three days,” Yelena replied, slipping Kate’s fingers from her face before removing her own hands entirely from her friend’s body. “I have a feeling Lucky will not agree by the time the transformation hits.” Mimicking Natasha, Yelena slipped her fists into the pockets of her coat before ascending the staircase one slow and deliberate step at a time.

    ##

    January 13th, 2017 | 07:31 P.M.

    There was no immediate explosion when they entered the apartment. The tall doors were hooked up to the hinges that supported them but not to a kill switch, a pressure sensor, or any other kind of alarm. There were also no traps anywhere inside the foyer. Two small baskets full of mail sat to the left of the entrance; one was placed on a small cupboard, and another right in front of it. There could always be explosives or acids in the envelopes, of course, but they’d have to be opened to cause a mess.

    The apartment was on the fourth floor, so Kate settled on the staircase that led up to the fifth while Natasha and Yelena slowly crept inside. Kate’s stomach growled loudly at their backs, briefly causing Yelena to wonder if werewolves could control that sort of thing and use it to their advantage. She supposed it might work on gaining sympathies with especially gullible people.

    Forcing herself not to acknowledge Kate’s obvious (though overly dramatized) distress, Yelena held her handgun close to her body as she rounded a corner. It would be all too easy to knock it from her outstretched hands if she wasn’t careful.

    “Bedroom’s clear,” Natasha whispered, her words reaching Yelena through their private comms channel. They switched up the frequency every day with the help of a random number generator. Kate still hadn’t quite gotten the hang of it, so Yelena tuned her comms for her every morning.

    “Bathroom’s clear,” Yelena replied as she opened and closed the lid of the toilet. For an apartment that hardly saw use, the place was surprisingly clean. Maybe Natasha’s contact, who kept an eye on the place for her, also paid for a cleaner. “No dynamite in the bathtub.” “Couldn’t find any cameras, either,” Natasha said, entering the hallway at the same time as Yelena. Holstering her handgun, Natasha gestured for Yelena to move away from the windows before flipping the light switch. They waited for several seconds. When nothing happened, Natasha moved towards the curtain and twitched it. Again, they waited for several seconds. Again, nothing happened. “Alright,” Natasha finally said before tapping her earpiece to shut it off. “Looks like we can cross Marcel, Mason, Stefano, Lucinda, and Ophelia off our list of compromised contacts.”

    “You seem surprised,” Yelena said, waving at Kate to come inside.

    “Eh,” Natasha said with a shrug after Kate closed and locked the front door. “Ophelia’s been upset with me for a few years, so you can’t fault a girl for thinking she might try something.” Slipping off her jacket, Natasha allowed her eyes to move about the place she’d once called home. “I should know better. Remote detonation or motion sensors aren’t her style. She’d make it personal.”

    “Ex-girlfriend?” Kate asked, drumming her fingers on her belly while refusing to take off her own jacket. Yelena rolled her eyes as she watched Kate lift up and down on the balls of her Jack Wolfskin boots. Demonstrably, Yelena kicked off her shoes and placed them on the rack by the door.

    “I stole from her, actually,” Natasha said, readjusting her handgun in its shoulder harness. “Was it her heart?” Kate asked, making Yelena snort.

    “Funny,” Natasha replied, wagging her finger at Kate. After taking off her own shoes, she disappeared into the kitchen area, her fingers moving up to remove the nano mask. “Let’s see if Mason’s been keeping up his side of the deal.”

    “You know,” Kate called after her, staying rooted to her spot by the door and keeping her nano mask fixed firmly in place. “You saying funny to everything I ask doesn’t actually refute any of the points I’m making!”

    “Cute,” Natasha said loud enough to be heard over the opening and closing of cupboards.

    “That doesn’t, either!” Kate said with a pout, sticking her hands into the pockets of her pants. Tapping the tips of her boots against the hardwood floor, Kate leaned into Yelena’s personal space with a frown. “I know why she’s like this, but why is she like this?”

    “I can hear you,” Natasha said, leaning around the open doorway of the kitchen. She winked. “Either of you in the mood for canned beans, canned bread, and canned tomato soup? We even have hard cheeses to make weird and off-putting grilled cheese sandwiches.”

    Kate’s next pout was even worse than the last.

    ##

    January 14th, 2017 | 06:15 A.M.

    There was one master bedroom and one guestroom in the Budapest apartment, but even the bed in the master wasn’t very big. When Yelena fell asleep the night before, she curled up into a ball on the left side of the bed, with Natasha half hanging off the other side. Minutes before Yelena laid down, Kate Bishop had slunk off to the guest bedroom to sleep by herself —Yelena was sure of that. And yet, when she woke up, her hands were tangled in black fur, and the head of a warm, snoozing animal rested on her neck.

    Blinking into the early morning sunlight that the curtains couldn’t quite keep out, Yelena saw that Natasha was already awake. She held Yelena’s beloved copy of Safe Harbor in her hands while her eyes leisurely passed across the pages.

    “When did this happen?” Yelena whispered, feeling comforted against her will by the warm presence of Lucky—Kate. Sleeping through the night with only minimal nightmares no longer surprised her. Some of the best sleep she’d gotten in recent memory involved a big wolf keeping her company. Across from her, Natasha gazed at her with all the lazy tranquility of an early morning. The full moon was now two days away; there was nothing for them to do but wait. Although apparently, even though the moon wasn’t ready, Kate Bishop had grown sick of waiting.

    “Not sure,” Natasha murmured, flipping to the next page. “But her wolf butt nearly knocked me out of bed at one point. You haven’t trained her very well.” She smiled, although Yelena figured it had little to do with their groggy conversation. Unfortunately, the book was angled too far away for Yelena to check what turn of phrase had garnered her sister’s affection. “Although I suppose I can’t be too upset. I forgot how fricking cold this apartment is. It’s too old. I should have Mason renovate it after we leave. Put in better insulation.”

    Grunting in acknowledgment of Natasha’s future plans, Yelena moved her hands from Kate’s flank to her head. Slowly, she started stroking and scratching the wolf behind its ears and along its neck, hoping to wake her friend without startling her.

    “Tory is really going through it,” Natasha said before softly clearing her throat and reading aloud from the book. “Now, all I can think about is Reese. I can’t look at her without wanting to touch her. I don’t even recognize myself—it’s like I’m another person. A stranger.” She looked right at Yelena, her green eyes unwavering and full of unspoken words.

    Thankfully, the big wolf in Yelena’s arms lifted its head before she was forced to figure out a good response. A powerful jaw split open to reveal massive teeth and a pink tongue. “You smell very bad,” Yelena said, putting her hands on Kate’s face and closing her mouth.

    Seconds later, the wolf surged forward to lick at her face, tail wiggling back and forth in excitement. “Stop it, Kate Bishop. You are a menace.”

    “And way ahead of schedule,” Natasha said, closing Safe Harbor and tossing it onto the nightstand. Sitting up further, she stretched her hands and arms along her blanket-covered legs, showing off her perfect ballerina posture and flexibility. Natasha’s soft and well-worn tank top left her arms bare to the early morning chill, briefly causing goosebumps to race across her skin.

    When the Kate-wolf realized Yelena wouldn’t allow a doggy kiss with lots of tongue to the face, it rolled onto its back and showed off its belly. Shaking her head, Yelena sat up, slipping her hand through the thick fur. “You know,” Yelena said casually, grabbing Kate’s left paw and shaking it up and down. “You should be glad you can turn into such a cute wolf.

    Otherwise, I might have been compelled to hurt you for sneaking in here.”

    Kate’s tongue lolled out of her mouth while her four legs windmilled through the air.

    ##

    January 14th, 2017 | 07:01 A.M.

    Nobody sniped Yelena through a window while she took a shower, which was a good sign. If their arrival the night before hadn’t sparked a reaction by now, it was unlikely that anything else would follow. Still, perhaps the apartment should have made her feel uneasy. Natasha had mentioned quite a few people’s names—people who might eventually be bought off and give up this location. Yet it was nice to be back in a place that hadn’t seen as much bloodshed and sorrow as the hole in the wall they’d briefly frequented before.

    When she entered the kitchen with a towel around her neck while wearing her fluffiest yellow sweatpants and a mauve cable knit sweater, she saw that Kate had reverted back to her human form. Her sister had changed into a pair of straight black jeans and an oversized navy sweater, her shoulder-length hair sticking out of a quickly assembled ponytail. Since the eggs seemed fresher than anything else in the apartment, Yelena surmised that her sister had made a quick run to the store while Yelena had been busy scrubbing off the last vestiges of Charlotte Kelly and Francine Trask.

    When Yelena only shrugged, Natasha shook her head and returned to making breakfast. Only now that Yelena was closer could she see the empty cartons of what had once been four dozen eggs.

    “Shoot me now,” Kate grumbled without uncovering her face, her elbows on the table and her feet twitching against her chair.

    “Finish your eggs,” Yelena said gently, closing the distance between them until she stood right next to her friend. Hoping not to startle her, she softly placed her left hand on Kate’s shoulder, feeling the tension that radiated through her whole body. Leaning closer, Yelena whispered in Kate’s ear. “You know I’m always happy to see Lucky.”

    Dropping her hands, Kate stared up at her with those big, sky-blue eyes. A second later, Kate’s arms were around Yelena’s waist, and her cheek wedged against Yelena’s stomach.

    Feeling at once relieved and slightly exasperated, Yelena put her right hand on Kate’s head. “Group hug,” Kate said, loud enough to catch Natasha’s attention. Turning around, Natasha blinked at the slightly awkward embrace behind her. The corners of her mouth twitched dangerously, but for some reason, she chose not to voice her thoughts. She didn’t need her quoting Safe Harbor again, especially where Kate could hear. “Natasha!” Kate said again, sounding a little more vulnerable this time.

    “Guess we’ll starve,” Natasha muttered, pushing the mostly cooked eggs onto one of the cold stovetops before turning off the heat on the one she’d just used. Green eyes flashing with mirth, Natasha stopped behind Kate. “The both of you are worse than Thor.”

    “Just give her a hug,” Yelena said, ignoring the safety and warmth of Kate’s embrace and how good she felt about the family unit they and Natasha had created for themselves. Was this what love was supposed to be like? In truth, she’d never been very good at love. As a child, she’d loved her parents, and she’d loved her sister. As a widow, they’d tried to force her to love Dreykov, but all she’d ever loved were the widows who fought and died beside her. But neither circumstance had been her choice. Her old family had been a lie; she and the widows could not act under their own power. Neither her parents nor the widows could ever love her without the strings that bound them to Dreykov. Natasha—the eternal exception to the rule—had cut those strings before they were reunited. Kate Bishop—the woman animal who protected them more fiercely than any human should—was untouched by Dreykov’s influence. And Yelena herself—the child assassin—the most dangerous widow ever created —still struggled with the strings that sought to entangle her even now.

    “Little Miss Bossy Pants,” Natasha whispered as she slung her arms around Yelena and Kate. “You are a terrible person,” Yelena replied, moving her left arm around the small of Natasha’s back while gently scratching her nails along Kate’s scalp and running her fingers through her friend’s dark hair.

    “Okay,” Kate said, rubbing her cheek against Yelena’s sweater while tightening her arms around Yelena’s waist. “Now we just need to stay like this for the next two days.” Her feet were still, and the tension about her back and shoulders was gone. Maybe it was time—

    maybe Yelena should tell her sister about how odd Kate had been acting around her.

    Maybe.

    January 14th, 2017 | 07:26 A.M.

    Yelena decided it was best not to talk to Natasha about the touching thing until after breakfast. It seemed unwise to bring it up while Kate was around, and Kate was too busy plowing through more eggs to leave the kitchen and give them some privacy. Especially because she had no idea Yelena was concerned about her. While Natasha prepared bacon and eggs, Yelena sipped her coffee and sifted through the first box of mail in front of her. The letters contained within were unanimously addressed to Natasha’s previous fake identity.

    While some appeared to be years-old bills, the addresses on quite a few were handwritten and thus far more interesting. None of them had return addresses written on the back.

Leaning closer, she closed her eyes and concentrated on listening for any sign of ticking. Slicing open the side of the package didn’t guarantee that nothing would happen, as it still gave her a one out of four chance, but she liked those odds a lot better than the alternatives.
