On the back, their ages were inscribed in neat handwriting. Yelena and Natasha, 6 and 11. Just a few weeks before their return to Russia. Mere days before Natasha’s twelfth birthday. “I don’t remember when they took it from me.” Abruptly, Yelena got to her feet and dropped the pictures face-down on the table. Bringing her hands to her face, she squeezed her eyes shut. Remember, she thought. Why can’t I remember? Her head was empty and full at the same time. If they found a personal item on her, she would have been severely punished. They would have made sure she remembered the pain, so why didn’t she?

    “Hey,” Natasha said, keeping her hands on Yelena’s shoulders. “We’ll figure this out.”

    “They took it,” Yelena said, dropping her hands from her face, her body trembling with rage. “They took the picture, and they took the memory of taking it.” Turning to Natasha, Yelena stared at her sister’s face lest she was taken from her, too. Behind her, Yelena was vaguely aware of Kate getting to her feet and tentatively closing the distance between them. “Why would they do that?”

    “I don’t know,” Natasha said, packing as much reassurance as possible into her words. “But we’ll figure it out.” Reaching out, Natasha squeezed Yelena’s arms. There was so much pain writ large across her sister’s face. Memories. Horrible, beautiful memories. “Someone sent that to me for a reason.”

    “And I don’t think it’s a bad one,” Kate said tentatively from behind Yelena, sounding unsure about whether her intrusion on their moment would be welcomed or rejected. When Yelena turned to her, Kate reached out to touch her side, her palm and fingers leaving tiny fires of comfort in their wake.

    “Explain,” Yelena said shortly, trying her best to curb her fear and anger.

    “It didn’t explode,” Kate said with a gentle shrug as she picked up the package. Reaching inside, Kate pulled out a stack of glowing, red vials. A substance that hovered between liquid and powder shimmered inside, seemingly unperturbed when Kate turned them upside down.

    “Whatever this is, I don’t think it’s an attack.” Setting the vials carefully on the table, Kate picked up the picture strip. When she looked down at their much younger faces, her expression softened. She shook her head. “From what you’ve told me, the Red Room only cares about two things: Money and power. Why would anyone who works there send you this?” Turning the picture strip around, Kate held it up. “Who loves you enough to remind you of this?”

    Yelena’s first instinct was to deny the question altogether. Who loved them at the Red Room? No one. But that wasn’t true. Yelena loved the widows who were trapped there—she was sure the same held true for most of them. But they didn’t have the power to send them a message. Dreykov and his men held no affection for them—although most probably didn’t know anything about the mission in Ohio. Pausing, Yelena stared at the tiny reproduction of Natasha’s poorly dyed hair and toothy grin.

    “Who remembers Ohio?” Yelena whispered, reaching out to take the picture strip.

    “Petrov, Orlov, Gobulev, Kuzmin, Dreykov,” Natasha said without having to think about it. Yelena was sure her sister remembered every official of the Red Room who had ever crossed her. She’d probably done even more research since getting out. “Alexei.”

    “Petrov is dead. I slit his throat on Dreykov’s orders,” Yelena said, imagining herself stepping right into the picture strip so she could hold the two girls who stared back at her. Protect them from everything that was to come. “Orlov is in prison. Gobulev fled to Kiev. Kuzmin and Dreykov would not send this.”

    “Alexei, then?” Natasha asked, picking up the vials Kate had set down.

    “No,” Yelena said, forcing out the memories of Alexei hoisting her on his shoulders and walking her around a zoo while Melina held Natasha’s hand and told them interesting and slightly disturbing facts about the animals. “He is in prison, also. Seventh Circle.”

    Natasha snorted, air passing derisively through her nose as she shook her head.

    “Anyone else?” Kate asked, looking back and forth between them.

    With a shrug, Natasha turned the vials back and forth to assess them from all angles. “Could be any number of people. Soldiers, medics, pilots, or other cronies of Dreykov’s we never saw or interacted with. My Red Room files are out there, too.”

    “They do not mention Ohio,” Yelena muttered, tracing her six-year-old face with her eyes. She remembered being that little girl; she remembered looking in her parents’ bathroom mirror and seeing her green eyes. She remembered looking into her mother’s eyes and seeing how warm and brown they were. With a shaky exhale, Yelena turned over the strip of paper to stare at the words. Yelena and Natasha, 6 and 11. “Melina.”

    “What?” Natasha asked, even though Yelena knew she’d understood the name just fine.

    “Melina knows,” Yelena said, the picture strip shaking in her grip. “Melina could have taken this from me. She could have taken my memory with a chemical agent.” It sounded unbelievable, even to her own ears. Melina was one of the original black widows. She was powerful; she was beautiful; she was expendable. Or so Yelena thought when Melina stopped coming to the Red Room.

    “Why would she?” Natasha asked, plucking the pictures from Yelena’s fingers, only the faintest hint of bitterness clinging to her words. “She was always in Dreykov’s pocket. She’s like us.”

    “We are not in his pocket,” Yelena pointed out, turning around to stare at her sister.

    Natasha rolled her eyes. “I mean, she’s a widow. She doesn’t have the reach or the pull with Dreykov or anyone else to pull this off. Whatever this is.” For a second, she also seemed to become transfixed with their young faces. “Besides, she was Dreykov’s favorite.”

    “So were you,” Yelena said softly, taking the vials from Natasha’s other hand. “So was I.” “That’s different,” Natasha said, slamming the photo strip face down on the table before rounding it and retaking her place in front of the stove. When she pulled the bacon back towards herself, her movements were imprecise and jerky. “We escaped when given the opportunity. Melina never cared about anything but the mission.”

    “What did she say to you before they took her away?” Yelena asked, setting the vials down on the table because she didn’t trust herself not to break them by accident. Whatever substance was in there was important. She could feel it. Absentmindedly, she rubbed the scar on her right thigh where she’d dug out her Red Room tracker after she escaped. Why did she remember seeing red right before it happened?

    “What do you mean?” Natasha asked without turning around.

    “Melina,” Yelena said, frowning at her sister’s stiff back. “The day we were taken back to the Red Room. She was bleeding out on the stretcher; she was in a lot of pain, but she grabbed your hand and said something to you. What was it?” Yelena remembered seeing the exchange, but she couldn’t for the life of her remember what had been said. The fear had been too great. Her mother was hurt; there were men she didn’t know with weapons and angry expressions; her father was acting strange, and their surroundings were unfamiliar to her. She remembered wanting to go back home to Ohio. “What did she say?”

    Natasha didn’t answer.

    “Never let them take your heart,” Kate suddenly muttered, holding up the square piece of cardboard Yelena had put aside earlier. Natasha turned back towards them, her expression serious. When Kate noticed her grim stare, she shrugged, her fingers clenching and unclenching around the cardboard. “I’m sorry. That’s what it says on here.”

    “I didn’t see anything,” Yelena said, never taking her eyes off her sister’s strange reaction. “You wouldn’t,” Kate replied, flipping the cardboard back and forth between her nervous fingers. “I guess whoever sent this used invisible ink, but, uh, I can see it.” She tapped her left temple. “Perks of being a werewolf. No UV light needed with these bad boys around.”

    “Never let them take your heart,” Natasha repeated while her shoulders sagged, her expression changing from dangerous to delicate. “Never let them take your heart.” The bacon popped and sizzled behind her, but she didn’t seem to notice. “That’s what she said. Right before she sent me back to them.” Putting the cardboard back on the table, Kate joined Natasha in front of the stove. Gently nudging her aside, she removed the bacon from the heat and shoved it onto a plate beside the eggs. “If she sent that to us, I don’t think it’s good news.”

    Never let them take your heart, Yelena thought, thinking back to Melina’s bloody hand on her sister’s cheek. The words meant something. She just wasn’t sure what. The vials were important, too. They were part of something bigger—Melina would not have sent them otherwise.

    “If she wanted to hurt us, she would have sent us a bomb,” Yelena said, sitting back down and unclipping one of the vials. As she turned it around, she watched the red particles inside swirl back and forth. “And if she wanted to threaten you, she would not have done it like this. Dreykov would have set a trap for us, or he would have sent agents. We would not be sitting here. We would not be having breakfast.”

    Kate set the bowl of eggs and the bacon on the kitchen table before handing Yelena a plate. A moment later, she put her hands on Natasha’s arm and tugged on her until she sat down opposite Yelena. Opening the fridge, Kate plucked out a bottle of hot sauce. Once cutlery had been placed in their hands, Kate sat down and looked back and forth between them. “So,” she said, putting her elbows on the table and her chin on her hands, her fingers drumming back and forth against her cheeks. “Melina, huh?”

    “Yes,” Yelena replied while spearing a piece of bacon.

    “Yeah, yeah.” Kate nodded awkwardly, still supporting her head. She blinked quickly, her blue eyes moving restlessly from Yelena to Natasha. “So,” Kate said again, tapping her feet under the table. “Who, uh—who is she, exactly?”

    Yelena chewed her bacon; Natasha stared at her eggs.

    ##

    January 16th, 2017 | 11:31 P.M.

    Perhaps it should have made them suspicious of how easy it was to find Melina’s location, but Yelena wasn’t all that surprised. Melina had taken a risk by mailing them the vials—if she didn’t want them to find her, she wouldn’t have sent them. Still, it surprised her that the woman had decided to stay in Russia instead of moving to safer borders. The Red Room’s reach was far greater within their homeland than elsewhere. But then, maybe this was all a trap—maybe Melina was on orders from Dreykov to collect them after all.

    For the moment, however, it didn’t matter what would happen next. Tonight, the full moon was upon them. Tonight, Kate Bishop would return to her wolf form in a safe environment.

    Tonight, there would be no laundry room and no cages.

    “I think we’ve gone far enough,” Kate said, lifting her head into the air and taking a deep breath, probably to sniff out any nearby humans. The nervous anticipation surging through her was so strong that Yelena could almost taste it in the air. “Are you sure you guys want to stick around? I’ll be happy just running around for a few hours and then curling up under the jet once I get tired.” She tugged off her hoodie and handed it to Natasha. “I’ll probably end up chasing squirrels and hogs.”

    “Don’t worry about us, Kate Bishop,” Yelena said, allowing her eyes to trace the scars on Kate’s arms that stretched with her handsome muscles. They were less than half an hour away from Kate’s complete transformation. Even though she would never admit it, she looked forward to seeing a version of Lucky that wasn’t restrained by injury or fear.

    Stuffing Kate’s hoodie into her duffle, Natasha bumped her shoulder against Yelena’s and smiled. “This will be fun,” she said before slinging her right arm around Yelena’s waist and smirking at Kate. “You’re like the dog I never had.” Rolling her eyes, Kate toed off her Jack Wolfskin boots and socks before picking them up and handing them to Natasha as well. “I see you’re learning. Good boy, Jack.”

    “Maybe I’ll go feral,” Kate muttered to herself as she wiggled her toes in the damp forest ground and crossed her arms over her chest. “See who’s a good boy then.” Yelena wanted to smile but she forced her face to stay neutral and vaguely disinterested. It was getting harder every day not to respond to Kate’s stupid charm. Maybe after this transformation, she would finally talk to Natasha about werewolf behavior and Kate’s reaction to her presence. Maybe. If Melina didn’t kill them.

    As though led by someone invisible, Kate stumbled forward a few steps in her sweatpants and tank top until she stopped in a clearing that allowed the moonlight to shine onto the forest floor. When the pale rays landed on her face, a shudder went through Kate, and her arms dropped to her sides.

    “Have you seen this before?” Yelena whispered as they slowly followed Kate to the clearing. “Only once,” Natasha replied, briefly and softly knocking their heads together. “A few years ago.”

    “What time is it?” Yelena asked, transfixed by Kate’s face in the moonlight and her glowing blue eyes. Next to her, Natasha checked her watch. It was an analogue model whose best feature was the glow-in-the-dark-arms. Which poor, unsuspecting pedestrian she’d stolen it from, Yelena wasn’t sure.

    “Eleven forty-nine,” Natasha said, squeezing Yelena’s waist before letting her go and only slightly raising her voice so Kate would hear her. “Time to take off the rest, kiddo. You’re ten minutes out from the big bang.”

    Turning her head, Kate almost didn’t seem to recognize them. Her glowing, blue eyes stared vaguely in Natasha’s direction, but she didn’t show signs of recognition. Like she’d merely reacted to the sound and not the meaning. Before them, the night was vast, and Kate’s mind had disappeared into it—the thrall of the moon working its magic too well.

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said, taking a step forward. Almost lazily, Kate’s eyes swayed towards her. As soon as they locked eyes, Kate’s mouth opened, but nothing escaped. “Natasha is right. You need to disrobe, or you’ll be going home without pants.” When nothing happened, Yelena figured that Kate finally had her answer on whose grip on her was stronger. But then, Kate tugged at the back of her tank top to strip it off before slipping down her sweatpants and underwear.

    “I could make a rude comment about your power over women, but I’ll be good,” Natasha muttered out of the corner of her mouth. “I guess I’ll just pick that stuff up after she’s turned. Seems like a bit much to ask her to hand it over.”

    Kate’s eyes were so blue that Yelena hardly heard her sister’s words. A part of her feared she would not move from this spot for hours if she kept staring into those eyes. Maybe she would stand right here, transfixed, for the rest of the night. Then, Kate turned her face back to the sky. The howl that burst from her chest sounded more animal than human. It startled Yelena, though not enough to make her draw a weapon. The sound was familiar—as was the pain contained inside.

    Leaving Natasha where she stood, Yelena followed her instincts. Closing the distance between herself and her friend—her wolf—she lifted her hands. Fire seared through her hands as she touched Kate’s right bicep to draw her attention. Up close, she realized Kate’s jaw was clenched, and her muscles were tense all over.

    “Shh,” Yelena whispered, lifting her right hand to Kate’s left cheek before laying her lips against the right one. With her nose still softly nestled against the burning skin of her friend, Yelena spoke the words without meaning to do so. “I’m here. You are safe. Shift now. Run. Seek your freedom. You’ve earned it.”

    When she took a step back, Yelena knew it was over. A roaring snarl slipped from Kate’s mouth as her body contorted and sank to its knees, bones creaking and shifting under the skin. Thick, black fur sprouted all over her body as her claws emerged from her human nails, and her face split in two, revealing the animal underneath. Humming softly, Yelena kneeled next to Kate while the transformation took its course. She didn’t reach out just yet. She had a feeling that if she tried, her fingers would be burned beyond recognition by the intensity. By the time it was done, the wolf’s chest heaved from the exertion, eyes closed while its wet nose blew loose leaves across the forest floor with every shaky exhale. With care, Yelena finally laid her hand upon Lucky’s head and softly stroked the fur between her eyes. The fur was soft to the touch and pleasantly warm in this cold Budapest weather. Yelena did not stop herself from smiling this time.

    Kate’s eyes fluttered a moment later, and her trembling legs pushed her upwards.

    Getting back to her own feet, Yelena took a step back to give their werewolf friend time to adjust if she needed it. This version of Kate looked exactly like Lucky the first time Yelena had seen her. She didn’t have any markings of being human—no elongated limbs, no upright posture, no hand-like paws with clawed fingers and opposable thumbs. When this wolf shook its coat and leaned back to stretch in a typical downward-facing dog position, it reminded her so acutely of her time at the cabin that it made her ache. She had loved Lucky—she could admit that now. She had loved Lucky in a pure and uncomplicated way with a passion for companionship that only the presence of an animal could inspire. Kate Bishop had thrown a wrench into those feelings. Her terrible humanness had destroyed that pure, easy love. Now, Yelena wasn’t sure what she felt for the girl who wanted to kiss her. Whatever it was, it sure as hell wasn’t uncomplicated.

    Rising back to full height after her big stretch, Kate looked up at the moon and howled again. The sound was powerful. Guttural. It should have provoked fear in them—a predator had emerged from beyond the shadows. A predator with the support of the moon-mother. But Yelena merely admired the unstoppable presence of her friend.

    “Never gets old,” Natasha said to herself without even a hint of sarcasm. Yelena didn’t reply. A nervous energy had taken hold of her body. When she looked to the sky, the sight of the moon nearly startled her. She suddenly felt the urge to run and hunt. Was this what it was like for Kate? Did she somehow infect Yelena with her lycanthropy? Or was this empathy? She wasn’t sure. She didn’t have a lot of experience with either. “Oh. Damn.”

    Natasha’s voice drew Yelena’s attention off the moon and back to the wolf in front of them. There was no fear in the air between them, so she wasn’t sure at first what was going on. Then, she looked into the wolf’s eyes and found herself startled all over again.

She didn’t flinch or move away when Melina picked up the plate between her forearms, but Kate saw the momentary hesitation in her posture before Natasha regained her full composure. “Seeing as you’re his favorite. He must call you.”

    Setting back the plate, Melina pointed at Natasha with the cake lifter. “Natasha, don’t slouch.”

    “Damn,” Kate whispered without thinking about it, causing the one thing she didn’t want to happen, which was for Melina to look at her. Blinking owlishly up at her, Kate did something even more foolish: She opened her mouth. “No, I just mean—I mean no disrespect. I’m just, uh, surprised, I guess.” Melina’s dark eyes looked empty of all emotions—somehow even emptier than Yelena’s and Natasha’s when they went into their widow modes. “That you, uh —”

    “Kate Bishop,” Melina said, causing the hairs at the back of Kate’s neck to stand up straight, prompting Kate to follow suit. “Do you want one piece or two?” Reaching over, she picked up Kate’s plate. Only now did Kate realize that the cake lifter was made of silver, too. Great. “You know Kate Bishop?” Yelena asked, her fingers unclenched and unmoving on the rifle.

    “I think you would like two pieces. With plenty of cream,” Melina concluded when Kate didn’t answer, ignoring Yelena’s question in favor of loading up Kate’s plate. Once it was back in front of Kate, Melina looked at Kate with far too intense eyes. “Fascinating. You reached wolf maturity only recently.”

    “What?” Kate asked, unable to look away from the silver cake lifter hovering all too close to her.

    Passing behind Yelena, Melina scooped up a piece of pie for her before sitting down on the chair to Yelena’s left and picking up her own slice. From the back of the chair at the head of the table, Melina pulled a satchel that Kate had paid no mind to previously. The satchel incorporated a screen with a map, confirming their suspicions that Melina had a top-of-the- line surveillance system. With a few swipes of her fingers, she could probably pull up a live feed, if Kate were to guess.

    “Did you bring it back with you?” Melina asked, tapping on the screen while no one at the table so much as touched their pie. Yelena and Natasha were both staring at her, clearly still on edge but unsure how to proceed. When they didn’t speak, Melina looked back and forth between them. “I am talking about the vials.”

    “Why did you send them?” Yelena repeated her earlier question, allowing just the vaguest hint of annoyance to creep into her tone. The aroma of the pie wafted around them, combining with the rest of the smells that permeated the house. Underneath it all, Kate could sense Yelena—and her concern. “And why this?” At Yelena’s words, Natasha reached into one of the pockets of her white vest, pulling out the photo strip from their childhood. Putting it on the table, she slid it towards Melina. “You took this from me after I was recaptured. Why?”

    With her eyes focused on the strip, Melina reached out to touch the bottommost image.

    Natasha and Yelena’s grins were almost too big for the frame, Natasha’s spindly arms wrapped so tightly around her sister’s even smaller shoulders that indents of her fingers were visible on Yelena’s skin. Words pushed themselves into Kate’s mind—spoken in Yelena’s halting voice. When it happened, Natasha tried to protect me. She told them: She’s only six. You can’t take her. She held me so tight I thought they would never have the strength to separate us. Unbidden, the urge to take Yelena’s hand on the rifle took root in Kate’s brain. Her wolf howled in her chest. Protect her, it seemed to say. Help her. She forced the feelings down. This was not the time. Yelena would not want their sort-of-maybe relationship visible to Melina.

    “Do you know why Dreykov sent us to Ohio?” Melina asked, stroking her pointer finger across the little faces on the aged, yellowed photo paper. Kate wasn’t sure if she was imagining the spark of emotion on her face—maybe she wanted Melina to feel something.

    Maybe it would make Yelena and Natasha feel better. “Do you know what he wanted us to steal?”

    “Some weapon of mass destruction, no doubt,” Natasha replied, curling her hands into fists on the table. “It’s not like we were briefed about the details before you shipped us out. Stop evading our questions.”

    “We infiltrated the North Institute in Ohio after Dreykov heard about it from one of his spies. It was a front for SHIELD scientists—actually, it was Hydra scientists at that time,” Melina spoke the words like she hadn’t heard Natasha say anything at all. This time, Kate was sure there was a hint of wistfulness and nostalgia laying itself thickly across Melina’s face. “In conjunction with the Winter Soldier project, they had dissected and deconstructed the human brain to create the first and only cellular blueprint of the basal ganglia.” Picking up the photo strip, Melina turned it over to read the words on the back. Yelena and Natasha, 6 and 11. “Funny how things have a habit of looping back around, hm?” Melina’s eyes lifted to study Natasha’s face. When there was no reaction, Melina shrugged. “I thought it was quite funny when you defected from the Red Room to join SHIELD. Maybe it was meant to happen.

    Maybe you only joined them because of your time in Ohio. That is what I thought at the time.”

    “Stop deflecting,” Natasha said, the edge in her voice making Kate uncomfortable. No one should have the power to unsettle her that much. No one. “I don’t care what you think about me leaving the Red Room or joining up with SHIELD. We’re not here to make nice with you and eat pie.” Natasha shoved her plate further away from herself, leaning forward to glare at Melina. “You will tell us why you sent us those vials, and then you’ll tell us where Dreykov is so we can kill him.”

    “If you make a face like that, you will get more wrinkles,” Melina chided, swiping on her satchel screen without looking away from Natasha. A second later, Kate heard the front door unlatch and open. Turning her head, she furrowed her brows. Why did it suddenly—“Come in,” Melina said in a slightly raised tone of voice, seconds before a pig rounded the corner and waddled over to her, its feet making clacking noises on the bits of floor that weren’t covered in carpet.

    “Did that pig just open the door?” Kate asked, leaning sideways in her seat to check if a person was hiding behind the corner, even though she could clearly smell that there wasn’t. She had half a mind to get up and close the door if only to keep the pigsty smell from creeping inside. Then again, with a pig in the dining room, that point might be moot already. “Yes,” Melina replied, reaching into the satchel to grab a slice of cucumber. When she fed it to the pig, she praised it and patted its head. The name she called it was familiar to Kate, although she didn’t have the same reaction as Yelena and Natasha, who stared at their adoptive mother with mild interest and confusion.

    “You named a pig after Alexei?” Yelena asked, staring at the animal’s happily munching face. Before Melina could answer, Yelena pursed her lips and made a thoughtful noise. “I do see the resemblance, I suppose.”

    “I prefer this good boy,” Melina said, swiping into a different screen on her satchel without looking down before continuing to speak in a strong and slow voice. “Stop breathing.” She swiped her fingers from the right to the left, and an orange alert flashed under her hand. Immediately, the pig’s throat rattled and gasped as the airflow was cut off. In horror, Kate stared as the pig obeyed its cruel orders. “Where was I… ah yes, Hydra was working on the first and only cellular blueprint of the basal ganglia. Which is the hub for cognition, voluntary motor movement, procedural learning.” The pig keeled over, throat still rattling as it tried to breathe but physically couldn’t. Bile rose at the back of Kate’s throat. “We didn’t steal weaponry or technology. We stole the key to unlocking free will.” Melina leaned back in her seat, her eyes moving from Yelena to Natasha to Kate. “Dreykov has chemically subjugated agents planted all over the world—”

    “Stop this,” Kate growled, getting to her feet and balling her hands into fists. “You’re killing it!”

    “Hm? Oh, don’t be so dramatic, Kate Bishop,” Melina said, swiping her fingers back to the right while the pig gasped and got back to its feet. “Alexei could have survived another eleven seconds without oxygen.” Reaching into the satchel once more, she pulled out another piece of cucumber. “Good boy, Alexei. Good boy.” She patted the pig’s head.

    Still on her feet, Kate couldn’t help but balk at the woman’s utter disregard for another sentient being as her pig breathed heavily next to her. Yelena’s knuckles turned white from her grip on the rifle as she stared out the window. A fresh surge of anger rose in Kate’s chest when she looked back at the screen of the satchel and wondered who had been on the other end of Yelena’s strings.

    “You know who they test this stuff on, don’t you?” Kate asked, voice low and gravelly in an effort to stop herself from transforming and ripping this whole farm to shreds. There were over a dozen pigs in that small building next to the house. Over a dozen victims of Melina’s cruelty. Cruelty that directly translated to—

    “That’s not my department,” Melina said, letting go of the screen to reach out for the photo strip once again. Kate wanted to call her a liar, but her teeth were clenched so hard she feared they would turn razor-sharp and deadly if she opened her mouth. Power rippled across her hands and arms, tufts of fur sprouting under her jacket as her wolf wrestled for control. When Melina looked up at her, something about her expression changed, but Kate was too distracted and furious to pinpoint the emotion. Turning her head to the side, Melina mimicked one of Yelena’s favorite ways of looking at Kate. This did not help. Then, Melina turned to Yelena. “Give me one of the vials.”

    Hand white-knuckling the rifle, Yelena didn’t look at her adoptive mother. Watching Alexei, the pig nearly choking to death on his own disobedient lungs, had only worsened her clear emotional turmoil. Fresh waves of anger surged through Kate, her back almost cracking at the weight of her desire to transform.

    “It was me,” Yelena said, looking at her lap as she pushed herself back against her chair, the corners of her mouth turned towards the floor. “Those chemically subjugated agents planted all over the world… that was me.” When she looked at Melina, Yelena’s eyes were red-

    rimmed. “After Natasha left, Dreykov closed ranks. He made sure no one else could escape.” At the opposite side of the table, Natasha’s jaw tightened, and her hands balled into fists. “You came to see me after I left the first time. You took the picture. You took my memory. Why?”

    Melina didn’t look at any of them the next time she spoke. Her fingers lingered on the pictures.

    “Dealing with the subjects is not my department,” Melina repeated, her face returning to its stoic, dead façade. “I am a researcher, not a doctor. My science is exact. The world functions better when it is controlled. Dealing with the subjects… is not exact.” She turned the picture strip over once more, her eyes finally moving from Natasha to Kate to Yelena in a circle. “But I am Dreykov’s architect. I used what the Hydra scientists were working on to prove some of my own hypotheses.” Slowly, she lifted her hand to Yelena’s head, her fingers trailing along Yelena’s temple and behind her ear. “They recaptured you, but you were not the same when they brought you to me. Dreykov called me in to examine. To recondition.”

    Melina’s hand trembled under Yelena’s chin. When she pulled back, Kate got the feeling it had nothing to do with Yelena.

    Finally, Kate sat back down, taking care to move as little as possible so as not to disturb the moment. Sensing Yelena’s desire to listen to her adoptive mother’s story, Kate’s wolf stopped howling, and the tufts of fur receded back into her skin. Still, Kate kept her hands curled into fists under the table, lest she forget she wasn’t supposed to touch Yelena in the presence of Melina.

    “I found this,” Melina said, indicating the photo strip. “When I examined your cell. Dreykov asked me if your talents could be salvaged. If not, then I was free to dissect your brain and see what had gone wrong with the serum.” Alexei, the pig, grunted before laying down on the floor without needing to be told, as if it was used to its mistress forgetting about it for long stretches of time. “I took samples, of course. Did scans. It was fascinating.” For the first time all night, Melina seemed invigorated. “Somehow, your body rebelled against the control. A specific cocktail of enzymes and hormones made you susceptible to free will.” She turned back to Yelena, stretching out her hand. “Now, please. Give me one of the vials.”

    “But,” Yelena said, clearly still unsatisfied with Melina’s answers.

    “Just give her one,” Natasha said, looking conflicted herself. Pulling her arms back towards herself, she crossed them over her chest, her red hair standing out starkly against the white of her uniform. In the bright yellow light from the ceiling lamp, the shadows on her face made her sharp cheekbones more pronounced. Kate was a predator deep down, but Natasha actually looked like one.

    Without further argument, Yelena reached for the strap across her chest. Slinging the container to the front, she unlocked it with her thumb before opening it up. The red powder- liquid gleamed menacingly in the same light that made Natasha look fiercer than a mountain lion.

    “Thank you,” Melina said as she accepted the vial. “I ask that you please stay calm.” With those ominous words, she turned in her chair until she faced Alexei, uncapping the vial and pouring some of its contents into her left hand. “Get up.” Her voice turned into that same slow and loud tone from before. Unsurprisingly, the pig unfurled itself and got to its feet, looking up with those big, trusting eyes. Kate’s nose twitched as the smell of the powder liquid reached her. She would have expected a sharp, angry poke at her nostrils, but instead, the scent triggered memories of forests and freshwater springs. “Smell this, Alexei.” As soon as Melina lowered her hand, the pig’s snout snuffled forward and buried itself. At the force of the movement, the powder liquid became partially airborne, laying itself across Alexei’s face and floating around its small, portly body. Almost immediately, Alexei reared backward, shaking its head and sneezing, its eyes blinking furiously. Red particles gleamed all across its face.

    It was over as soon as it started. One moment, Alexei was choking and twitching. The next, it was back to normal, looking around the dining room like it couldn’t remember how it got there. Furrowing her brows, Kate watched Melina reach into her satchel to pull out another slice of cucumber.

    “Good boy, Alexei,” she said, feeding the pig before recapping the vial. Laying the satchel back on the table, she gestured at the screen, and Alexei’s motor functions visible on it. “Now,” she started, straightening her back before briefly looking at Yelena. “Stop breathing.” She slid her finger along the screen in a repeat of her earlier display of power, causing Kate to tense in anticipation of the sound of Alexei’s choking. Instead, Alexei just looked at Melina and blinked, clearly unsure what to do. An error message popped up on the screen, but Melina ignored it. “It was impossible,” she said, abandoning her satchel in favor of cupping Yelena’s cheek. “You were impossible.” With her left hand, she picked up the vial. Now, with both, she took Yelena’s fingers off the rifle before pressing the half-empty vial into them. “I took your pictures so Dreykov would not find them, and I took your memory of our conversation in the lab because it would have been dangerous for you to remember.” She squeezed Yelena’s fingers and the vial within. “I brought you back under Dreykov’s control because I knew it would not last.”

    “This…” Yelena stared at the vial.

    “Yes,” Melina confirmed, letting go of Yelena with a nod. In Kate’s head, the gears turned rapidly. “You introduced a new element into our research. Before then, I had not conceived of creating an antidote.” Her words hung in the air for several long seconds before she got to her feet, grabbing a leash from a cupboard to her left and attaching it to the pig’s collar. “I will be right back. Come on, Alexei. We’ll take you back home. Back home where it’s safe.”

    Without another word, she picked up her satchel and slung it across her chest, leaving the room with Alexei right behind her. Listening to her footsteps, Kate and the sisters sat in tense silence until the front door opened and closed with an audible click.

    “Did that really just happen?” Kate asked, her eyes glued to the half-empty vial in Yelena’s hand. “You’re…” Reaching out, Kate placed her hand on Yelena’s shoulder. Whether she wanted to reassure herself or Yelena, Kate couldn’t tell. All she knew was that she felt better the instant her palm made contact. “If what Melina said is true—”

    “We can save all of them,” Natasha said, her voice quiet and reserved, like she couldn’t believe her own words. Getting to her feet, she turned towards the window behind her, placing her right hand on her stomach. Kate thought it was a testament to her training that she didn’t break down. “If that’s real, we can stop Dreykov.”

    “Without hurting the others,” Yelena muttered, tilting the vial sideways, seemingly just to watch the red particles swirl back and forth behind the glass. “We can take them all away from him. Find every last one and free them.”

    “I’m in,” Kate said, squeezing Yelena’s shoulder. When Yelena looked at her, there was something in her gaze that Kate couldn’t fully decipher. Maybe it was appreciation; maybe it was exasperation. Maybe it was a mixture of both. “Let’s burn the dude’s place to the ground and get all your friends out of there!”

    “Your enthusiasm and optimism never fail to surprise me after everything you’ve seen since joining up with us,” Natasha said, moving her hand from her stomach to her hair. Since it had grown quite a bit over the course of their journey, Yelena had felt compelled to take a pair of scissors to it the day before and return Natasha’s hair to a much more manageable length. “You should know better by now, Jack.”

    The front door opened and closed, causing them all to fall silent as Melina returned to the dining room. She was by herself, her satchel sitting in the exact same position as before. She stopped between Yelena and Kate, her eyes moving across the table and towards Natasha’s turned back.

    “Sit down,” she said with a shake of her head. “We are not finished with our after-dinner snack, Natasha.” Placing her hands on the table, Kate tried her best not to look at the woman’s haunting eyes. Something that Melina said wouldn’t leave Kate’s mind. I took your memory of our conversation in the lab because it would have been dangerous for you to remember. She wondered what had been discussed between them that could have been so dangerous. Kate remembered Yelena’s first days at the cabin. She’d been cautious, but she’d also been so incredibly free. She’d talked a lot at the wolf, unaware that her words would not be carried away by the wind and snow, never to be seen again. Thinking about it now, Kate could almost feel Yelena’s gentle hands on her head and the warmth of her embrace as they drifted off together.

    A sharp stab of pain lanced through Kate’s neck and forced a gasp from her mouth. She’d been so lost in thought she hadn’t seen the movement behind her. A silver needle pierced her skin, and a warm rush of burning liquid entered her bloodstream, prompting Kate to go into full survival mode. Pushing away from the table, she rolled off the chair and backed herself into the corner of the room, uncaring of the cupboard she slammed into. Several cups clattered to the floor, shattering at her feet as she raised her hands and growled at the rest of the room, her teeth growing long and sharp. Her eyes moved frantically back and forth, trying to ascertain the source of the attack. Blinking quickly, she brought a hand to her head to stem the splitting headache that came on with a vengeance.

    The muzzle of the rifle was in the air, pointed straight at Melina. To Kate’s right, a pair of handguns had been drawn, Natasha’s face flush with anger as her fingers twitched on the triggers. Bringing her second hand to her head, Kate let out a mixture of a roar and a scream as the burning liquid seared through her whole body.

    “What did you do?!” Yelena demanded at the same time as Natasha bellowed: “What are you playing at?!”

    In the corner, Kate tried not to topple over as her vision blurred and all the muscles in her body clenched in response to whatever had been injected into her bloodstream. Her wolf howled in her chest, confused and unsure how to fight off this unforeseen attack. Her wolf blood was supposed to burn off anything that wasn’t laced with—

    Melina’s face was void of all emotion as she showed them the syringe she’d used before placing it on the table. When she turned to Kate, her brown eyes ran up and down her trembling body as though to gauge the severity of her reaction.

    “Kate Bishop matured only recently,” Melina said as though it explained everything. When she stepped closer to Kate, Yelena and Natasha moved in further, their weapons raised threateningly. Melina gestured at Kate. “A recently matured werewolf can be dangerous to have around.”

    “Kate Bishop is not a danger to us!” Yelena hissed, her jaw too tight for comfort.

    “Well, not anymore,” Melina said, clearly unperturbed by the weapons aimed at her. She was beside Kate within less than three full steps, who would have probably sliced her to bits if her hands had been cooperating.

    “Get away from her,” Yelena growled as she turned over her rifle so she could use the butt as a weapon instead of shooting in Kate’s direction. Just as she lifted the firearm to punch Melina in the temple, Melina cupped Kate’s cheeks and peered into her eyes, obscuring Kate’s direct visual of Yelena.

    “It will be over soon,” Melina said gently, rubbing Kate’s temples as she looked back and forth between Kate’s eyes. “If you want to protect her, this is the best way.” Within a second, Kate realized why she recognized the expression on Melina’s face—it was the same way she looked at Alexei, the pig. “Just a few more minutes, hm?”

    The butt of the rifle came into view. Before it could hit Melina, Kate instinctively reached out to grab it, using what strength she had left to stop Yelena’s attack on her adoptive mother. Yelena’s beautiful green eyes were so expressive when she didn’t control herself. Right now, all Kate saw was confusion and anguish.

    “Kate,” Yelena whispered, brows furrowed as she pulled away the rifle.

    “You will protect her like you did at the hangar,” Melina said, patting Kate’s cheek with her left hand. “You tore those men apart to protect my daughters, and you will do so again.” Her voice was strong and clear, and Kate found herself nodding. Was this mind control? Had Melina drugged her with the compound that Yelena had resisted? Kate couldn’t imagine that to be the case. Her wolf blood would have protected her. What was it, then? And why did the burning fire in her veins slowly return to the comfortable warmth that she was used to?

    “Good girl.” Melina smiled, although the corners didn’t touch her eyes. “There, see? All better.”

    Slowly dropping her hands, Kate rose back to her full height, her left hand touching the back of her neck. She didn’t feel any blood or other proof that she’d been injected at all, and her body didn’t show any sign of the fire in her veins, either. She didn’t feel different in the slightest. What little remained of her agony were the shards of broken porcelain all over the floor.

    “What did you do?” Kate asked quietly, looking at her right hand and turning it over. She barely had to concentrate—within a second, her hand shifted into a furry paw with claws and back again. Nothing had changed.

    “Made you stronger,” Melina said, patting Kate’s left shoulder. Pulling her satchel to the front, Melina tapped on the screen before holding the bottom of the scanner up to Kate’s neck. Immediately, Yelena surged forward to push herself between Melina and Kate.

    “Enough!” Yelena growled, pushing the satchel aside. “Enough, or I break your toy.”

    “Scans are looking good,” Melina said to herself, studying the screen as she rounded the table before sitting back down. “No more boiling blood.” She nodded to herself and swiped back and forth. “You picked a strong one, Yelena.” Melina nodded again, her eyes never leaving her readings.

    Trembling, Yelena turned to Kate, her face marred with anger as she slid the rifle strap over her chest so both her hands were unoccupied. A moment later, they touched Kate’s neck and face, a thumb running across the injection site, searching for the very same evidence as Kate and finding nothing. Lips pursed, Yelena lifted her gaze to Kate’s, her hands causing little bursts of warmth to bloom wherever they made contact.

    “How do you feel?” Yelena asked softly, running her palms along Kate’s shoulders and biceps.

    “Good,” Kate started with a shrug, touching Yelena’s cheek without thinking about it.

    “Normal.” Turning her head, she found herself properly glaring at Melina for the first time all night. “Don’t do that again. That really fricking hurt.”

    Melina hummed. “Don’t need to do it again,” she said without looking up. “One is enough.” “Your mom is a piece of work,” Kate said to Yelena as Natasha stepped over to check on her, too. It hadn’t escaped Kate’s notice that Melina knew about the hangar. Stroking Yelena’s jaw one last time, Kate dropped her hand before taking a step closer to the table. “You developed an antidote, but you never left the Red Room. Why? Why did you send it to Natasha? You could have put a stop to everything yourself.”

    Finally lifting her gaze from the satchel screen, Melina leaned back in her seat. “No,” she said with a shake of her head, her eyes giving nothing away. When she didn’t say anything else, frustration bubbled up in Kate’s chest, but before she could express her feelings, Natasha shook her head and stepped in front of both Kate and Yelena.

    “You’re a coward,” she growled, her back tight with tension. “You sent Yelena back into the Red Room instead of freeing her. And after everything you’ve done, you don’t even have the decency to spread the antidote to the girls you helped brainwash. Oh, and while we’re talking about hurting people who don’t deserve it, don’t you ever lay a hand on Kate again.” Melina stared at her emotional outburst, her perfect façade cracking just enough for Kate to catch it. “We are not your daughters. Don’t tell Kate to protect us when you won’t do it yourself.” Yelena looked away, the corners of her still eyes red-rimmed from trying not to cry. Chest nearly bursting with the desire to help both sisters, Kate took Yelena’s hand and clasped Natasha’s wrist, hoping that the simple touches would be enough.

    “I tried,” Melina said, keeping her hands flat on the table. “I tried to protect you.”

    “Bullshit,” Natasha hissed, never pulling away from Kate’s touch.

    “I sent you the vials because I knew if any one of us would be strong enough, it would be you,” Melina continued as though she hadn’t heard Natasha’s insult at all. “Out of all the mice in Dreykov’s cage, you escaped… you did what I didn’t think was possible.” Getting to her feet, Melina picked up the picture strip and held it to her chest. “You kept your heart. After everything that happened to you. Both of you.” She took a step closer. “How did you do it?”

    “Pain only makes us stronger,” Yelena said, voice hoarse. When she looked at Melina, a tear slid down her cheek. “Isn’t that what you said?” She gripped Kate’s hand tight enough to break any ordinary person’s bones. Kate could handle the spikes and tingles of approaching numbness. For Yelena, she could handle anything. “We survived.”

    With a nod, Melina smiled tremulously before reaching for the table to steady herself. No one spoke for a minute or two while Kate did her best not to ruin the moment by making any sudden moves or saying anything stupid.

    When Melina finally spoke again, her voice was almost back to normal. Almost.

    “You cannot find the Red Room,” she said, closing the distance between them only far enough to take Yelena’s wrist and place the photo strip in her hand like she’d placed the vial of antidote in it earlier. “It does not stay in a single location for very long. If you want to find it and take down Dreykov, it is best if he comes to you.”

    “And how do we make that happen?” Natasha asked, finally unclenched her fists. When Kate made to pull away her hand, Natasha grabbed and held it lightly between them like she didn’t want to lose the connection and the strength it gave her.

    “It is already in motion,” Melina said, moving back to pick up her satchel and swipe around on it. When she turned it over, Kate could see several dots moving across a map, closing in on the farm slowly so as not to draw attention. “If I had severed my connection to the Red Room, he would have suspected. I did not want him to come here. To ask me what I was doing. I did not want to tell him about you.”

    “Goddamn it, Melina,” Natasha swore as Yelena quickly wiped at her eyes and pulled her rifle back to the front, letting go of Kate’s tingling hand while Kate tried not to freak out about Red Room goons coming to capture them. Again, she blocked out the memories of their last encounter. All of that blood… all of those men dead on the ground. Her claws, chest, and maw covered in the stuff. Red, red, so much red.

    “I’m sorry.” Melina reached out to touch Yelena’s face like she’d done before only for Yelena to brush her away. “You both grew up to be so very beautiful. I wanted to talk to you before they arrived. I wanted you to see that I wasn’t lying.”

    “That makes it better,” Yelena muttered sarcastically, her eyes fixed on the satchel screen. “They will take us to the Red Room. You came here to find it—now you will,” Melina said with another nod, bringing her hands to the front of her shirt. When she started unbuttoning it, Kate briefly felt immensely uncomfortable before quickly realizing that Melina wore a tactical suit underneath it. “Don’t worry. Mama will go with you.”

    Blinking quickly, Kate rubbed her temples. “This is why you get yelled at all the time,” she muttered at Melina before lifting her right foot to the back of her left so she could slip off her Jack Wolfskin boots with little to no hassle. Although, now that she thought about it, she probably wouldn’t return to this house, so the gesture was pretty useless.

    “Keep those on,” Melina said, pointing at Kate’s feet before slipping off her black shirt entirely and neatly laying it across the back of a chair. “They might not take you if you transform. Or they might take you in pieces.”

    “Cheerful thought,” Natasha muttered, reaching for her handguns before evidently thinking better of it. Turning to Kate, she forced herself to smile—probably for Kate and Yelena’s sake. “Don’t worry, Jack. I’ll get you a new pair once all of this is over. Promise.”

    With a nod, Kate slipped back into her left shoe.

    Time to face the music.

    ##

    January 20th, 2017 | 11:23 P.M.

    Kate wasn’t aware of tranquilizers that could take her down. Hunters shot werewolves to kill them, not to knock them out so they could be questioned later. She was thus very surprised— and annoyed—when she was shot in the chest with several small darts and suddenly felt her world tilt on its axis. Yelena and Natasha had warned her that the Red Room knew who and what she was, but Kate hadn’t expected them to come so very well-prepared. When she landed on her back, the floorboards cracked under her weight, but that was about as much as she noticed before she blacked out.

    When she came to, she was no longer in Melina’s dining room. Squinting against the bright light of the lamps above her, she tried to make sense of her surroundings, instantly aware that she wasn’t alone. There were several unknown human scents in the vicinity, although a strong undercurrent of antiseptic and other chemicals permeated the space, making it more difficult to parse. Among the unfamiliarity, one particular scent stood out among the rest. Blinking a little faster, Kate turned her head to the side to survey her surroundings with more than just her nose, at once happy and distraught to see Yelena strapped to a cot much like her own.

    She wanted to call out to see if Yelena was awake, but she didn’t want to draw the attention of the lab staff around her. They probably already knew that Kate cared for the two women she traveled with—if they filmed the carnage at the hangar, they would be stupid not to suspect a bond—but she didn’t need to give them any information they didn’t already have. “Subject KB-1 is awake,” a voice said to her right, sounding bored and unaffected. A clipboard came into view when Kate turned her head. Behind the clipboard, a white man in an even whiter lab coat looked down at her with a neutral expression. Since he wore a pair of green-glowing goggles, she couldn’t see his eyes, but she didn’t think she needed to.

    “KB dash 1?” she asked, focusing her attention on Yelena as best as she could. When there was no response from the man in the lab coat, Kate rolled her eyes and shook her head while she listened to Yelena’s heartbeat. Too slow to be awake. Dang it. “I’m just saying, dude. That’s not a very creative nickname. Why not KB dash Ultra or, uh, 8 dash 00 dash 8 dash 5.” No response again, although Kate could see a muscle twitch at the side of the guy’s mouth. “You’re not wearing a nametag, by the way. I feel like that’s a bit of a faux pas if you’re going to be having guests over. What’s your name?” She wasn’t surprised at his silence this time. “You know what? I’ll just call you Bob. It certainly would have been helpful on this mission. Just then, Dave said something to Bob that she didn’t understand. Wake up, it’s Mama. Yelena’s eyebrows twitched, but Bob and Dave were both looking at Kate and the syringe. “You know what that reminds me of, actually? Liquid Kryptonite from Smallville. Did you guys get Smallville here in Russia? I loved it, although I had to watch late-night reruns because my parents didn’t approve. Tom Welling really was such a great Superman, don’t you think?”

    The next lab they entered was empty of people, but it had an access grate to the ventilation system that they forced themselves into. Dreykov has overwritten my protocols.

    When they dropped down from the next vent grate, Yelena took down four Red Room guards with nothing but her arms and legs while Kate contemplated shifting. She could hear more shouts from the distance, but the smell of gunpowder and blood covered up any human scents in the vicinity. Turning her head around, Kate allowed herself to be pulled onward by Yelena, who walked across the dead bodies of the guards—broken necks made for impossibly uncomfortable sights.

They were trapped.

    “How nice of you to join us tonight, Yelena,” a voice echoed throughout the room from speakers in all the corners. Kate didn’t recognize the voice, but Yelena obviously did. Her body tensed as the man continued to speak. “And you brought your friend, the werewolf.

    Natasha and I will enjoy seeing you two fight to the death. Won’t we, Natasha?” A picture appeared on the mirrors, showing a man in a nice suit and a bleeding Natasha.

    “We need to get out of here,” Yelena said, squeezing Kate’s wrist. “Break down the door.” “It’s too late, little one,” Abrasha Dreykov said with an ugly smile as a door to the far side opened to reveal several heavily armored Red Room soldiers with rifles. They crept inside slowly. Before either Yelena or Kate could react, one of them raised an odd-looking gun and fired, hitting Kate in the chest with another dart. This one, however, did not knock her out. “What the—” Kate tried to reach for the dart, her fingers not quite cooperating as a surge of panic went through her. What was going on? Why did her heart suddenly feel like it would jump out of her chest? She finally managed to grab the dart, but her hand didn’t look right. It didn’t look human. Why was she… Zippers and seams popped as Kate grew in size, fur sprouting against her will. Her hands and feet grew into thin, clawed paws, whereas her face elongated and sprouted horrible teeth.

    “Marvelous creatures, aren’t they?” Dreykov said to Natasha, crossing his arms over his chest and twisting his mouth into an ugly smile as Kate continued to transform until her ears nearly touched the ceiling. She felt bigger than she ever had, her muscles swollen with rage and a horrible power she wasn’t sure she could control. “With just a few drops, they become so much more. It’s too bad they’re messy… Now, Kate Bishop.” He raised his voice, drawing Kate’s attention. Chest heaving, Kate looked towards the projection on the mirrors, ears twitching and drool dripping from her half-open mouth. “Why don’t you rip Yelena apart limb from limb, hm?” Fire burned in Kate’s veins from the forced shift, and she quickly shook her head to rid herself of the confusion that came with it. Dreykov spoke again, his voice a mixture of amusement and impatience. “No need to fight against your new programming. You cannot resist the serum.”

    The wolf howled in Kate’s chest, struggling for complete control of their body and mind. Let me do this, it whispered, bloodlust evident in its every word. Let me do this. Let me do this. Let me do this. She needed to resist, didn’t she? She needed to resist. To protect Yelena, she needed to be strong. We are strong, the wolf whispered. Trembling all over, Kate squeezed her eyes shut and continued to struggle against herself. It was futile. She was not in control here. Showing her massive teeth, Kate dropped her shoulders back and turned her head towards the ceiling. Her howl was so loud and powerful that it forced the Red Room soldiers and Yelena to cover their ears. Instinctively, they crouched and protected their soft, vulnerable bellies as Kate gave herself over. Good, her wolf whispered. Let me do this. For us.

    Kate had no concept of time. One moment, she was staring at the mirror screens; the next, her claws were buried in a throat. She heard gurgling and felt a pair of hands on her forearm, desperately clinging to life. Rifles went off behind her, but all she could see was the blood. So much of it. She felt the spine against her palm, briefly giving her pause. What was she doing? Who was she killing here? Why was she doing this?

    Don’t look, her wolf said. Don’t remember.

    But Kate couldn’t look away. She couldn’t look away as she tossed the soldier into a wall and turned on the others. Blood dripped from her claws and fur, reflecting the fluorescent light from the ceiling. Somewhere in the distance, a man’s voice bellowed for her to stop and attack Yelena, but Kate didn’t listen. Some of the rifles were pointed at her, but some—

    Our moon, Kate and the wolf thought as one. Protect our moon.

    The second howl was as loud and fearsome as the first, forcing the Red Room soldiers into a crouch once more. Whatever was in that serum made Kate ten times more dangerous, ten times more furious, and ten times more deadly. And yet, its effects were a surprise to the people around her. Didn’t they know what they’d created? Hadn’t they studied its consequences? Dreykov spoke like he held the world in his hands, yet he couldn’t hold her back from tearing down his underlings. She ripped through bodies like they were made of sandpaper, unheeding of the toll this might take on her later. The blood rage was too strong to resist, and most of her thoughts were relegated to illegible growls, snarls, and howls. The more soldiers she killed, the hungrier she became. More, more, more, she thought. Hurt them. Break them. Stop them.

    The live feed projected onto the mirrors turned off, returning them to their reflective state. When the final soldier fell, Kate found herself staring at nothing but herself. Chest heaving from the exertion, she saw a monster standing in a circle of mauled flesh and unrecognizable evil. Blood. Viscera. Carnage. The desire to kill was still there, prominent and overwhelming, but there was no one left to kill. There was no one left. No one… no one but Yelena.

    If Yelena spoke, Kate couldn’t hear it. She needed to shift back—she needed to become herself again—she needed to—she howled in pain, her handlike paws clenching as her whole body trembled with the power of the serum. Slumping back down, Kate growled at herself in the mirror before staggering closer, her knees suddenly wobbly. With her paws pressed against the mirror, Kate stared at her own eyes. They glowed, which wasn’t the unusual part, but they—they were different. One of them—one of them was—one of them—

    “Kate,” Yelena whispered, her voice suddenly very close. “Let’s get out of here.” Bloody pawprints slipped along the mirror as Kate continued to stare. A blue eye, a green eye. A blue eye, a green eye. A blue eye, a green eye, two green eyes. She stared at Yelena’s face, seeing the very same shade. The next noise out of Kate’s mouth was a confused and desperate whine. Instead of laughing at her, Yelena nodded, a strange expression settling around her mouth. “I know,” she said, cupping Kate’s bloody cheeks, fingers tangling in the fur. “But we need to focus. We’re here for a reason.” She applied more pressure, the tips of her fingers digging into Kate’s skin. “Natasha needs our help.”

    Flattening her ears, Kate blinked down at the woman they loved. Our moon, her wolf growled in response, tearing Kate away from her guilt. Focus, her wolf echoed Yelena, making Kate pull away from Yelena’s hands and shake her head back and forth. The soldiers were dead. The soldiers wanted to kill Yelena. Now, they were gone. Now, they could not hurt her. You tore them limb from limb, and they deserved it. Because they would have done worse to us. The words came back to her with a vengeance, crowding out the screams and the sound of flesh tearing from bones. Leaning forward, Kate nuzzled Yelena’s cheek before licking across it. I don’t know if you are a good person, Yelena had told her that cold, wintery morning. I don’t know if I’m a good person either, Kate thought, cupping Yelena’s other cheek with her giant paw, ignoring the blood. Yelena didn’t seem to care as she looked up at the giant wolf that stood before her. The giant wolf that just killed a dozen Red Room soldiers without hesitation for her.

    Forcing her vocal cords to submit, Kate nudged Yelena’s forehead with her nose. “Love…”

    Kate managed to growl, her voice low and strangled, almost entirely inhuman. I don’t know if you are a good person, Yelena had told her that cold, wintery morning. But I know you are not a bad one. Kate had to believe her. She had to believe her. If she didn’t, she might just lose herself.

    Blinking slowly, Yelena wiped at the cheek where Kate’s wolf tongue had been.

    “I love you, too,” she said, her voice quiet and earnest and all wrong for where they were standing.

    Their moment was interrupted by another spell of buzzing in Yelena’s ear. Melina’s voice sounded a little less calm than before, though Kate thought she wouldn’t have been able to tell if she hadn’t spent the last few months traveling with two former black widow assassins. I had to blow up the Northern turbines. We are going into a controlled crash.

    Kate blinked, her bloody paw still cradling Yelena’s cheek.

    “Yes,” Yelena said, removing Kate’s paw from her before tugging her along by the wrist.

    “This sounds bad, also.” Kate let herself be dragged, putting up no resistance as Yelena ordered her to smash the door through which they’d entered the weird dance studio. “Come on.” And Kate followed her—because Kate would always follow her.

    ##

    January 21st, 2017 | 12:59 A.M.

    Yelena was blasted off Dreykov’s helicopter, flying into the air like a ragdoll. Kate was on the other side of the hangar with Natasha, trying to free more widows that were pouring from the Red Room’s inner workings. The moment Kate saw Yelena fall, she tore away from Natasha’s side. “Kate!” Natasha called after her, but she was already running, her body shifting from its more humanoid shape into the wolf that had once played with Yelena in the snow.

    Dreykov was dead—Yelena made sure of that. Melina and Natasha were alive, helping the widows who weren’t dead onto helicopters and planes. By the time Kate and Yelena joined Natasha in Dreykov’s office, the bastard was gone, having fled out of a side entrance while Natasha fought for her life. But none of it would matter if Kate couldn’t save Yelena.

    Kate couldn’t fly, but she was heavy. Kate couldn’t fly, but she was strong. Kate couldn’t fly, but she loved Yelena with every fiber of her being. When she jumped off the Red Room, she spotted Yelena straight away. Bits of shrapnel and parts of the Red Room fell alongside her, narrowly missing her by mere inches at times. Jumping from debris to debris, Kate slowly made her way down, ignoring bits of concrete and metal slamming into her. She couldn’t be damaged. She couldn’t be hurt. She couldn’t be stopped.

    The air whipped around her, pushing against her free-fall. Yelena was close. If only Kate could—she roared as her paw closed around nothing, a falling steel beam hitting her square in the back and knocking her away from Yelena. Turning around, Kate shoved the steel beam away from herself, her tail whipping in all directions as she checked to see where Yelena had ended up. She was further up than Kate now, drifting aimlessly.

    Our moon, the wolf howled in Kate’s mind and chest. We need to get closer. Closer. Closer. The waning moon hung high above them, bathing the collapsing Red Room in its cold, blue light. Help us, Kate thought, staring at Yelena and the moon as the ground grew closer and closer. Help us save her. Please. Kate’s desperate howl ricocheted along the pieces of metal and other debris. Help us. Help me. Help her.

    Reaching out her arm, Kate willed herself to close the gap, to stop them both from falling. As the moon began to glow uncomfortably bright, Kate saw the distance between them growing shorter and shorter, until her paw curled around Yelena’s arm and dragged her close. Holding Yelena to her chest, Kate allowed another howl to rip from her throat. Thank you, she thought. Thank you. Her wolf howled in unison as Kate made the arduous climb back up through the debris. Using her claws and strength, she jumped from beam to beam, plating to plating, until she saw what she had hoped for.

    “Kate!” Natasha yelled, waving at them from a helicopter not too far away.

    Jumping again and again, Kate finally lunged herself at the helicopter, grabbing onto its side with her ginormous claws, Yelena held securely in her right arm. Quickly, the helicopter pulled away from the debris, and Kate watched as the Red Room continued its slow but unrelenting crash. As the helicopter drew further away from the inevitably impact site, Kate heard a buzzing in Yelena’s ear. Thank you, Melina said, sounding truly emotional. Thank you for protecting her.

    Snuffling her face against Yelena’s hair, Kate closed her eyes and felt the strength of the moon seep into her bones and skin. She’d not been the only thing out there in the air. Her power alone had not brought her to Yelena. Allowing a soft whine to pass her massive teeth, Kate squeezed Yelena to her and thanked the moon for her kindness. You brought her to me, Kate thought. That night on the mountain. Didn’t you? You brought her to me. The moon was silent, its call a mere caress along Kate’s shoulders. This time, Kate’s howl sounded strange even to her own ears, a powerful and ancient sound that would have sent her parents into a killing frenzy out of fear. Instead, the moon glowed with pride, warming her body and mind, and lending them enough strength to wake Yelena.

    “What?” Yelena asked, her face squished against Kate’s fur. “Did we win?” Kate nuzzled her head and licked her cheek as best she could, forcing a chuckle from Yelena’s tired mouth. When she looked around, she saw the Red Room in the distance, and felt the moon inside her chest. Kate wasn’t sure how she knew, but she didn’t question the connection. Not just yet. “Oh,” Yelena murmured, her hands sliding around Kate’s hulking body, sliding her fingers through the fur. “He’s dead. We’re…”

    “Free,” Kate growled, her claws flexing against Yelena’s leather tactical suit. “Free.”

    Yelena looked at the helicopter, where Natasha stood in the open doorway, her eyes fixed on the Red Room as well. Natasha’s face was covered in scratches and blood, but to Kate and Yelena, she’d never looked better.

    “Hey,” Yelena called, barely audible over the sound of the blades above them. Like she’d been waiting for Yelena to wake up all along, Natasha’s gaze left the crumbling remains of her former home. When they made eye contact, Natasha smiled. “You look like shit!”

    “You look worse!” Natasha yelled back, slapping her hand against the side of the chopper. “And you’re in love with a mutt!” She laughed, and a tear trickled down her cheek. She didn’t bother wiping it away, nor did Yelena bother with her own tears when she gave her sister the finger. “Fuck!” Natasha yelled as loud as she could, her smile brighter than the moon. “I love you guys!”

    “Loser!” Yelena shouted, pressing her cheek to Kate’s chest and digging her nails into Kate’s broad back. “We are the worst,” Yelena whispered, her eyes overflowing and her chest bursting with all the relief in the world. When she left, she’d been tired of the relentless silence of the Red Room. Hundreds of girls conditioned to be quiet, where the only sound that ever reached her ears were the footsteps of the doctors and military personnel who guarded and studied them. She was out of it now, not for the moment, not for the next few months. She was out of it now. For good.

    “I am you. As much as you are me,” Yelena whispered to herself, looking up at Kate, who stared back at her—one eye blue, one eye green. “I am upside down again.” Expelling her breath in a slow and steady exhale, Yelena closed her eyes and smiled. “How about you?”

    “It was a clear Provincetown morning in August, with a bright blue sky punctuated by scattered wisps of clouds overhead,” Yelena said, leaning back against the headboard. Right beside her, the body of a mostly naked woman turned away from her. “The waves on the bay broke gently against the sands at Herring Cove. Reese sipped her coffee, waiting.”

    “I get it,” the woman grumbled, pulling a pillow over her head. “They lived happily ever after.”

    With a smile, Yelena closed Safe Harbor and carefully set it on the nightstand. A pile of books graced the far edge, displaying such titles as Beyond the Breakwater, Distant Shores, Silent Thunder, and Storms of Change. Her copy of Safe Harbor had seen better days, but Yelena held no desire to replace it.

    “Is someone very tired from their night in the woods?” Yelena teased, slipping down the bed until her head was on a second pillow. She was already dressed, her stomach was full, and her first three cups of coffee were gone. Kate, on the other hand, had not moved from her spot on the bed since Yelena brought her home eight hours ago. “You are a poor excuse for an enhanced werewolf.”

    “Shut up,” Kate muttered, pulling the pillow even tighter against her head.

    With a sneaky grin, Yelena slid closer, wrapping her arm around Kate’s waist and snuggling against her warm, naked back. Basking in the rays of sunshine falling on them through a gap in the curtain, Yelena allowed herself a moment of perfect tranquility.

    “Are you not hungry?” Yelena asked, smoothing her palm across Kate’s stomach and side.

    “There’s two dozen eggs just waiting to be scrambled.” The apartment was completely silent, save for the noise of constant traffic going past the windows. In this little world, there was nothing that could hurt them. Sometimes, in quiet moments, the apartment reminded Yelena of their cabin. Only she didn’t have to set snares here, and the radio offered more than one channel.

    “No,” Kate asserted petulantly, even though Yelena could clearly hear her stomach rumble at the mention of food. “Just sleep.” Smile widening, Yelena shuffled further onto Kate’s back, slipping her leg across Kate’s butt and thighs. “Last night was crazy.”

    “Mhm,” Yelena agreed, having no trouble picturing the wolf that emerged under the moon’s radiant light. Large and imposing—larger and more imposing than before—with teeth that would have sent any ordinary person running for the hills. The Red Room dart had left permanent changes on Kate’s wolf. They’d asked Melina about it, but Yelena’s adoptive mother had done little more than shrug. It made Kate Bishop stronger, she’d said like the matter didn’t need to be discussed any further. Interestingly, Melina had been tight-lipped about most things mind control and werewolf research. She also refused to address the injection she gave Kate. “I think you should eat something. A bigger wolf needs a bigger meal.”

    “You’re just bored,” Kate groused, emerging from her pillow to glare at Yelena over her shoulder. Wayward hair stuck to her cheek and forehead, and a shiny patch of drool clung to the corner of her mouth. Yelena forced her expression back into neutral territory.

    “Maybe you are simply boring,” she replied before pushing herself into a sitting position. Today, she wore her neon yellow crop top with the wolf’s head on the chest, although she wasn’t sure if that was why Kate stared at her or because Yelena had also foregone a bra after waking up. “Get up, Kate Bishop, or I will not cook for you today.”

    “You’re so rude,” Kate complained while turning onto her back, her naked chest glowing beautifully in the early afternoon sunshine. “You were never this rude when we were on the run.” She pouted, severely testing Yelena’s ability to keep her face from twitching into a smile.

    “Your memory is very selective,” Yelena said, sliding off the bed and into her fluffy slippers. She wore a pair of Kate’s purple sweatpants, legs rolled up at the bottom. “Besides, Kate Bishop, I endeavor to be as rude and unaccommodating as possible.”

    With a huff, Kate slumped back against the mattress.

    January 21st, 2017 | 3:45 A.M.

    The helicopter landed far enough away from the crash site. When the Red Room made impact with the ground, it sent a shockwave across the surrounding countryside. Craggy rock and pieces of metal exploded in all directions, hitting trees and digging into the Earth, leaving the final wounds in a long and arduous war. Yelena was glad to be done fighting.

    The chopper blades stopped moving, making it safe for Natasha, Melina, and four other widows to disembark. The wolf’s claws elicited squeaks and groans from the metal as it readjusted its grip without letting go. With her hands buried in the fur and her cheek pressed against the big chest of her wolf, Yelena felt warm and protected, as if nothing could truly hurt her again. Now, with the Red Room gone, it might even be true.

    Tapping Kate’s shoulder, Yelena leaned up to kiss the big, wet nose. “Let me down.” When Kate nuzzled her temple and whined in response, Yelena couldn’t stop herself from smiling. She was certain her body should be exhausted. She barely remembered exploding Dreykov and his men, but she remembered losing consciousness. She remembered the air. She remembered being in pain. Now, all she seemed to feel was Kate’s strength and the unmistakable influence of something else… something ancient. “Don’t worry,” she told Kate, hugging her tighter. “I’m okay.”

    Kate nudged the side of her face for another few seconds before finally slipping off the helicopter and putting Yelena back on her feet. It was still strange to see Kate like this. Her previous transformations had left her much shorter and less bulky, which wasn’t to say that Kate’s wolf had never been scary. Reaching up, Yelena rubbed the spot on Kate’s massive chest that the Red Room dart had entered. There was nothing but fur and solid muscle there now.

    A hand settled on her shoulder before an arm slipped around them seconds later.

    “We did it,” Natasha said, her eyes on the burning hull of the Red Room. “He’s dead.”

    Turning into her sister’s side, Yelena slipped her hand around Natasha’s waist and leaned their temples against one another. “Yes,” Yelena confirmed, watching smoke and ash drift into the air. “Now we just need to fix your government problems.”

    Natasha laughed as she turned her head, pressing a kiss to Yelena’s head and tugging her into a real hug. “We have all the time in the world to deal with them,” Natasha murmured, squeezing Yelena to her chest before letting out a long and definitive sigh. “He’s dead.” Big, warm paws and furry arms slipped around both Yelena and Natasha, holding them together as Kate’s wolf settled its big head on both of theirs and let out a pleased rumble. Disentangling one of her hands, Natasha reached up to pat Kate’s big shoulder. “Jesus, Jack, let a girl breathe.”

    Kate only hugged them both closer.

    ##

    April 12th, 2017 | 2:51 P.M.

    Leaning over a mountain of scrambled eggs, Kate kept her chin on the palm of her propped- up right hand, her eyes firmly closed as Yelena walked around the kitchen to find everything they needed for a very late breakfast and lunch.

    “Try this,” Yelena said, setting a bottle of green hot sauce on the table before nudging Kate’s shoulder, ignoring her very gentle snoring. When Kate didn’t budge, Yelena rolled her eyes and slipped her hand around Kate’s bicep and onto her chest, leaning over Kate’s right shoulder. Clearing her throat, Yelena raised her voice and spoke directly into Kate’s ear. “Your mother is here.”

    Jumping up in her chair, Kate nearly swiped the plate of eggs right off the table as she startled awake. Her blue werewolf eyes glowed in alarm as she frantically looked around, hoping to spot the danger. When Yelena couldn’t hold back her laughter, Kate’s shoulders relaxed, and her pout materialized in full force.

    “Not funny!” she said, glaring at Yelena. “You can’t invoke Mom when I’m barely human.”

    “I can and I will,” Yelena replied, shoving the bottle of green hot sauce in Kate’s face. “Now try this. It will help wake you up.” After ensuring that Kate wouldn’t drop the condiment, Yelena rounded the table to pick up a cup of coffee from their fancy new machine—her fourth.

    “Nothing will wake me up after last night,” Kate groaned, uncapping the sauce and pouring a dollop onto her pointer finger. “I don’t know what the Red Room puts in its super-soldier serums, but it—” She spluttered as the burn of the hot sauce finally registered on her tongue. “Damn, dude. This one’s—” She stuck out her tongue, her werewolf senses clearly unwilling to suppress the pain of the spice. “Are you trying to kill me?”
