Settling her elbows on the wood, she closed her eyes and sipped her coffee, enjoying the sounds of Kate trying to rid herself of the fire. “Now you can stop whining about being so tired.” She hid her smile behind her cup, feeling a surge of warm protectiveness wash over her.

    Setting aside the hot sauce, Kate leaned over to rest her head on Yelena’s shoulder.

    “You’re a menace,” Kate muttered, rubbing her ear and cheek on the yellow crop top.

    “Maybe I’ll stay home. There. Deal with that disappointment.” Kate was warm and soft and terribly strong all at once, yet Yelena allowed herself the pleasure of wrapping her right arm around Kate’s shoulders.

    “You will not stay home,” Yelena asserted, drawing circles and squares on Kate’s arm.

    Blowing a disbelieving breath through her teeth, Kate slipped her left hand around Yelena’s waist while keeping herself steady on the table with her right. “My wolf’s love for you doesn’t work like that. Just because you say a thing in a very sexy voice doesn’t mean I’ll listen.”

    Humming thoughtfully, Yelena set aside her coffee. Turning towards Kate, she hugged her silly wolf girl.

    “I cannot say I share this experience,” Yelena said, rubbing her cheek against Kate’s head and enjoying the normality of this moment. The Red Room was no more. Three months had come and gone. Thrice, the full moon had taken Kate away and given Yelena the wolf. They could sit at this kitchen table for hours and never once worry about being followed or captured. “You always listen when I tell you what to do.”

    “I cannot say I share this experience,” Kate mocked her, squeezing Yelena’s waist and letting out a pleased growl—possibly against her will. She smelled overtly of Yelena’s favorite shampoo, but underneath it all, Yelena detected an undercurrent of rich, wet earth. “When do you want to leave? I’m still crazy tired. I’ll fall asleep on the drive over.”

    “Aw,” Yelena cooed, gently raking her nails along the back of Kate’s neck. “You continue to be a very bad werewolf, Kate Bishop. You are not scary or tough at all. But don’t worry. I will call Natasha. Her horrible voice can keep me company.” Sometimes, Yelena still wondered when this touching thing became quite so easy for her. Kate was heavy, yet Yelena felt no desire to tug herself away. Most upsettingly, Yelena somehow always wanted more.

    ##

    January 31st, 2017 | 11:30 A.M.

    The flight back to the US felt almost surreal, especially because of who the other passengers were. Ten days of traveling all over Russia would have been enough to give Kate a culture shock, but spending that time around several recently freed black widows didn’t make it any easier for her. Yelena knew Kate had expected something else from the girls, but since they didn’t know Kate as anything other than Yelena and Natasha’s tall werewolf friend, their companionship was uncomplicated in ways Kate’s relationship with Yelena or Natasha never was.

    Melina refused to accompany them on the Quinjet for a variety of reasons. She had her farm and her pigs and a plan to break Alexei out of prison. Yelena didn’t know how to feel about that, so she shoved the thought down, just like she’d shoved down Melina’s many revelations. It was impossible, Melina’s voice rang in her ears. You were impossible. She leaned back in her seat; Kate was belted in beside her. I brought you back under Dreykov’s control because I knew it would not last. She wished Melina had told her sooner. She wished Melina had not taken her memories of their first conversation.

    “Alright,” Natasha said, getting up from her seat after allowing the autopilot to take over.     “Wow, rude,” Kate muttered, crossing her arms over her chest and glaring at Natasha while several of the other ex-widows smiled at their antics. Ten days of traveling together without the fear of reprisal from the Red Room had given the girls what ten days of freedom had given Yelena all those weeks and months ago. “Embarrass me in front of our cool new friends.”

    Yelena kept her expression detached, but she couldn’t help herself from nudging her foot against Kate’s. The dart’s contents changed Kate’s wolf, but they did not change her. She looked the same as before—tall, strong, muscled, and very goofy because of her stupid facial expressions. Still, Yelena worried about the next full moon. Judging by the anguished growls and whines when Kate turned back after her forced transformation, her full moons would not be as easy as before.

    With a huff, Kate sunk lower in her seat while her foot nudged Yelena’s right back. The small connection was enough to dump a fresh bucket of butterflies into Yelena’s stomach. What would life be like now? Now that the Red Room was gone, there was no reason for Yelena to stay in hiding, and with the thumb drive from Eleanor Bishop… there would be no reason for Kate to remain in hiding, either. Which probably meant Kate would return to university to finish her degree, and Yelena…

    “I think I should get a fake passport,” Kate mumbled, crossing her arms over her chest and glaring at Natasha with all the passion of an angry five-year-old. “What if someone’s looking for me? I could get pulled off-stage by the TSA.”

    Lightly gripping the armrests to keep herself from reaching out, Yelena lazily turned her head towards Kate and lifted an eyebrow. “Where do you think we are landing this jet?” she asked, momentarily distracted from her concerned musings about her future. If Kate resumed her studies, there was no reason for her to sever ties with them. If anything, the thought made Yelena angry with herself for even considering it. Kate was many things, but she wasn’t disloyal. She was too loyal. “Do you think we will go through customs?”

    “Uh,” Kate replied, biceps flexing briefly as she shifted.

    “I will get you a fake passport when we are back,” Yelena promised, unable to stop the corner of her mouth from twitching upwards. “We can make up a new backstory for you together.”

    She steadfastly ignored Kate’s eyes, staring at her like she was more than a former assassin with an affection for lonely wolves. “Start thinking about names, Kate Bishop.”

    Brows furrowing, Kate nodded and sat up straighter, leaning closer to Yelena. If any of the other ex-widows noticed their closeness, they didn’t give any indication. Yelena was grateful for it. Now that the Red Room was gone, there were other things for her to worry about—

    better things, most certainly. But terrifying things all the same.

    ##

    April 12th, 2017 | 4:59 P.M.

    “Are you sure we should go today? It’ll be dark soon,” Kate said, putting her palms on the sill before sticking her head out of the open window. Closing her eyes, she shivered as she drew in the late afternoon air. Whatever she smelled, it didn’t seem like the horrid New York City pollution dampened it, which Yelena envied her for. New York reeked of many things—

    mainly weed, old urine, and exhaust—but Kate was rarely bothered.

    “The bags are packed,” Yelena countered as she wiped down the counter after their prolonged late breakfast and lunch. Her fourth cup of coffee had finally kicked in about five minutes ago, giving her a boost she enjoyed without the crash of a Red Room-sponsored enhancement drug. “Are you afraid, Kate Bishop?”

    “No,” Kate replied, leaning forward until she looked on the verge of dropping out of their second-story apartment onto the cold concrete below. Yelena didn’t need to worry—even if Kate did fall, she would be fine. She wasn’t a cat, but werewolves always seemed to land on their feet, too. “I’m just—”

    “I know you are tired,” Yelena said, throwing her rag in the sink. “You can sleep in the car.” “Yeah, no, I know,” Kate leaned back and turned, rubbing her chest. “I was going to say that I’m itching for a run. I think there was something in that hot sauce.” Crossing the distance between them, Kate put her palms on Yelena’s waist and lifted her easily. Without needing to be prompted, Yelena wrapped her arms around Kate’s neck and her thighs around her stomach. “The moon’s waning.” Kate’s mouth found the underside of Yelena’s jaw. “But I can still feel it.” Her hands tightened on Yelena’s waist without turning painful. “Just like last time.”

    “You can run once we’re out of the city,” Yelena offered, ignoring the surge of energy searing through her body at the display of Kate’s strength. Even months later, Kate’s raw physicality wasn’t something Yelena was used to. In a way, she hoped the rush of surprise and desire would always stay the same. “I wanted to be on the road an hour ago.”

    “I know,” Kate growled, readjusting the grip of her left hand before sliding her right higher up on Yelena’s back, the tips of her fingers running across Yelena’s skin until they reached the bottom of her crop top. At the same time, Kate’s mouth sucked a bruise into the underside of Yelena’s jaw, like she’d heard Yelena’s protests but didn’t process them.

    “Kate Bishop,” Yelena said sharply, moving her right hand to Kate’s hair. She barely needed to tug for Kate to abandon her neck and look up at her, one of her glowing blue eyes slowly turning green. Kate’s wolf was still a mystery to her in a lot of ways, but she now knew the truth behind this oddity. Still, it wasn’t enough to make her cave. “Car.”

    With a pout, Kate set her back on the ground.

    “Fine,” she mumbled, irises slightly enlarged as her eyes followed Yelena to the living room. “But later?”

    “Yes.” Putting her hand on the door jamb, Yelena turned back to look at her tall, handsome, stupid girlfriend. “If you are a good girl on the way there.” These days, keeping the cool and aloof façade around Kate Bishop got harder and harder, but Yelena still pulled out the assassin face when she needed to or when she wanted to tease her. When Kate pouted harder and stomped after her, Yelena turned back towards the bedroom and allowed herself a smile. “Come on, come on.”

    “Yeah, yeah,” Kate replied, passing Yelena after stopping only for a second to kiss the top of her head.

    ##

    January 31st, 2017 | 9:21 P.M.

    Natasha stepped off the Quinjet, her hands swinging back and forth in a lazy, comfortable pattern. Watching her sister leave the protective hull of their jet made her tense up instinctively, even though the Red Room was no longer a threat, and the US government was too incompetent to detect their return. When no one gunned her down, Yelena relaxed. Kate stood close to Yelena, eyes fixed just as intently on the back of Natasha’s head.

    “Let’s go,” Yelena said softly, grabbing Kate’s wrist to put her at ease before they both descended the ramp. They’d seen the farmhouse on their Quinjet’s screens, but it didn’t seem nearly as small up close as it did from several hundred feet up. Kate’s wrist was warm, and her pulse was a steady presence that Yelena appreciated, especially when she saw a woman approach her sister.

    “Man,” Kate muttered, looking from Yelena to the woman and back again. Leaning down, she whispered in Yelena’s ear. “I’m one step closer to meeting him.” Unfortunately, her excitement somewhat overwrote her previous whisper-training, prompting the woman to look at them. Suddenly, Kate’s pulse spiked, and Yelena found herself fighting very hard to keep her disaffected expression.

    “I’m afraid Clint’s not home,” Laura Barton said, meeting Natasha in a hug.

    “Sorry,” Kate squeaked, reaching for a bow that wasn’t there as though to show that she belonged. “I know he’s, uh, indisposed. Sorry. I’m sure you’re just as cool ‘cause you’re married to him.” Pulling her hands free, Kate fired a pair of finger guns at Laura. Next to her, Natasha rolled her eyes.

    “Ignore Jack,” Natasha said, looking at the door of the farmhouse. “I want to see my godson.”

    “He’s asleep,” Laura replied, leaving Natasha’s side to shake hands with everybody else.

    Laugh lines crinkled at the corners of her eyes when she saw Yelena. “Natasha has told us so much about you. It’s lovely to finally meet you.” Unsure how best to reply, Yelena shook the woman’s hand and nodded.

    “It is good to meet you, too,” she finally settled on, unused to speaking to anybody but her sister and Kate. The other widows didn’t count; they weren’t any better at talking to strangers than she was, at least not without putting on a fake personality. Swallowing the reflex to hide behind a façade, Yelena forced herself to speak. “Thank you for taking care of Natasha for so many years.”

    “She’s family,” Laura replied easily, taking Yelena’s hand with her right before covering the back of it with her left. Without needing to be told, Yelena knew—there was something different about Laura. “Welcome to our home.” Her palms were very warm. Next to Yelena, Kate stared at Laura, nose twitching as she opened her mouth to say something. Laura shut her up with a small but definitive glance. “Later,” she promised quietly. “First, let’s get some dinner into all of you.”

    ##

    April 12th, 2017 | 8:40 P.M.

    The freeway was mostly deserted. Here and there, a car going the other way passed them by, but even those were few and far between. Yelena didn’t mind. She enjoyed focusing on the passing scenery instead of driving behind a row of other vehicles. Even after it turned dark, she didn’t miss the visual stimulus. Somewhere further down the mountain, the radio began to crackle more and more, so she switched it off. The car had a CD player in the center console, but she didn’t need to fill the silence, not with music.

    “It was a clear Provincetown morning in August, a bright blue sky punctuated by scattered wisps of clouds. The waves on the bay broke gently against the sands at Herring Cove. Reese sat in her patrol car sipping her coffee, waiting.” Kate’s voice didn’t rise too much above the sound of the engine, but Yelena understood her perfectly.

    “I read that bit this morning,” she interjected, smoothing the fingers of her left hand across the metal of the truck door in a circle pattern while she kept her right hand on the steering wheel. She could have picked a different car at the dealership, but the truck had spoken to her the moment she laid eyes on it. There’d been no reason to move fast with the purchase, but Yelena enjoyed making quick decisions after having to think so much about her every move. “You’ve subjected me to this book a million times,” Kate replied, her blue eyes glowing in the dark. The overhead light wasn’t on, yet Kate had no trouble reading. “I think you can handle hearing the same paragraph twice a day.” With Kate Bishop in the car, the relentless silence of the Red Room was very far away. She’d never gone back to the place where she cut out her tracker and started her journey, about three hundred miles away from where they were right now. Maybe she ought to go back one day—see the place from a different perspective. If she took Kate there, she was bound to make better memories.

    “Stop dawdling and keep reading,” Yelena said, confident that Kate wouldn’t see her smile. When they passed around a curve in the road, the headlights illuminated a pair of trees Yelena remembered, returning her mind to the first time she’d been here. Last year, she’d been nothing more than the most lethal and prolific child assassin the Red Room ever created. Now, she was more. She was different, and no more new girls were being bought. No more new girls were being kidnapped. No more new girls were being conditioned. And Yelena, talented and dangerous, would never be property again. “I want to know how it ends.”

    “You know how it ends. You’ve read three of its seven sequels,” Kate complained under her breath, bringing her finger to the page to mark the place where she left off. Clearing her throat, she continued. “She smiled as a flicker of color far off to her left caught her eye. Her heart stirred as she followed the course of the red kayak, cutting swiftly through the early morning sea.”

    There had been no red kayak on the road in November. But there had been redness all the same. As Kate’s words washed over her, Yelena thought of the radio suddenly cutting off the music, replacing it with horrifically loud static. She’d been frustrated and scared to be alone with herself. Outside, a snowstorm had been brewing. There’d been stretches here and there of gentle snowflakes falling from the sky, which hadn’t been much of a problem while it was still daytime and fairly bright outside. But after nighttime, with only her headlights and the full moon above to show her the way, she’d been greatly unhappy with the added distraction of snow. She thought about taking her eyes off the road only long enough to make sure she was moving the dial in the right direction. She thought of the moment right before impact, of realizing that something large and furry was crossing the road up ahead. Red blood on fur— far more blood than the collision with her car could have caused. Even in its slumped-over form, its shoulders nearly came up to Yelena’s stomach, and its limbs were sprawled out underneath it.

    “Tory’s rhythm was so steady the craft seemed barely to touch the water as she paddled toward the lighthouse at Race Point. When she could no longer imagine she saw her, Reese drove away, filled with peace,” Kate continued, unaware of Yelena’s spiraling memories. She only looked up when Yelena pulled off the road and brought the truck to a stop. “Yelena?” Kate asked, lowering the book to her lap before looking around carefully. “What’s wrong?” “This,” Yelena said as she pulled back the hand brake and put the gear shift into the neutral position. Leaning back her head, Yelena closed her eyes. “This is…” With a long breath, she sat up straight before unbuckling her seatbelt and getting out of the car, Kate scrambling to follow her. Taking a step towards the road, Yelena tilted back her head and gazed at the stars above. Tonight, the air was cold but not unpleasant.

    “Hey,” Kate said, sliding her hand under Yelena’s jacket to touch the small of her back.

    “I’m not upside down anymore,” Yelena said, leaning against Kate’s chest. “Kate Bishop.”

    Looking left and right, Yelena stepped onto the road. Turning around, she smiled at her girlfriend. “This is where you found me.”

    The waning moon hung high above them, painting Kate’s face in ghostly shadows.

    “She guided me,” Kate asserted confidently, Safe Harbor held loosely in her left hand. “She brought me to you.” To anyone else, the statement might have been over the top, but Yelena knew Kate wasn’t lying to be romantic. Yelena wasn’t sure she believed it, but she had no reason to question it, either. After everything, she knew better.

    “Let’s go home,” Yelena said, stepping back into Kate’s personal space.

    “Yeah,” Kate confirmed, wrapping her up in a hug. “Let’s go home.”

    ##

    February 9th, 2017 | 10:00 A.M.

    The Barton farm was lovely, if a little crowded, with so many former widows needing a place to stay. A few were gone after one or two days, but more than a dozen remained. They spent every waking moment together, enjoying their newfound freedom. Laura Barton and her children didn’t seem to mind. Laura showed anybody who wanted to learn how to make her favorite peach cobbler, although Kate was banned from the kitchen after her first attempt, which resulted in the fire alarm going off twice. Preparing easy and efficient meals was a prerequisite training module at the Red Room, but Yelena hadn’t spent a great deal of time learning the finer details of baking.

    “Good,” Laura said, watching Yelena knead the dough with her hands. “You’re a natural.”

    “Thank you,” Yelena said, genuinely pleased to be complimented on her newly acquired skill. “I’ve made bread before, but not like this.” She rolled the dough between her palms to create a uniform ball while a lightly flowered steel bowl and a warm tea towel sat beside her. “I like it.”

    “If you like, I can show you how to make your own sourdough starter,” Laura offered, sipping a cup of coffee while little Nathaniel sat on her lap and watched Yelena tuck and roll the dough. Behind her, Yelena could see Kate and several widows through the window, watching Lila Barton shoot an arrow at a target. When the arrow hit one of the circles close to the center, Kate and the other widows cheered for the girl, who looked very pleased and a little bashful.

    “That sounds nice,” Yelena replied, looking away from Kate when she spotted movement from the corner of her eyes. The two widows in the kitchen with them got up from their chairs and said something about taking a walk, leaving Yelena alone with Laura Barton and her son. Focusing back on the dough, Yelena gently placed it in the steel bowl before placing the warm towel over it like Laura had shown her the day before. “Letting this ferment will take longer than Natasha’s mission.”

    “She has a good and simple plan,” Yelena confirmed, rounding the kitchen island to wash her hands in the sink. Looking back outside, Yelena watched Kate nock an arrow. She wore a black muscle tee, dark jeans, and a new pair of Jack Wolfskin boots, although Yelena was much more interested in the visual of Kate’s back muscles growing taut with tension. “We will be out of your hair soon.”

    “It’s nice to have guests,” Laura said, getting off her chair to join Yelena by the window.     “Sure,” Yelena replied, accepting Nathaniel with both hands before hoisting him up on her waist.     “I know,” Laura said, leaning against the counter and following Yelena’s gaze outside. Kate’s arrow sat at the perfect center of the target. “Natasha mentioned something about hunters at Bishop Security.” A spell of annoyance flit across the woman’s handsome face, marring what was usually a much friendlier complexion. “That can’t be easy for Kate.”

    Nate put his head on Yelena’s shoulder, his fingers winding around a few strands of hair, never tugging hard enough to hurt. With a hum, Yelena rubbed the boy’s back and wondered if Natasha had told Laura Barton about Kate being a werewolf or if Kate had told the woman herself. She didn’t think the other widows would have—they knew better than to share such information with a stranger, even a kind one.

    “She’s strong,” Yelena settled on, the corner of her mouth twitching upwards as she watched Kate show a widow how to hold a bow properly. Lila stood next to them, soaking up every word like a sponge. “But she deserves better.”

    Laura nodded slowly, squeezing the fingers of her left hand with her right.

    “Many of us do,” she said, voice quiet as she turned to Yelena to brush her son’s hair back from his young face. At her words, Yelena tore her eyes away from Kate, her arms and hands tightening instinctively on Nate’s small body. Laura’s gaze didn’t leave her son. “Tell Natasha not to destroy most of New York again.”

    “She will do anything for the people she cares about,” Yelena said, seeing Laura Barton in a whole new light. “We will not rest until New York is safe for Kate Bishop. If that means tearing down the infested parts of the city, then so be it.”

    It was barely ten fifteen in the morning, and the full moon was three days away. Yet when Yelena spoke about protecting Kate Bishop, her heart surged with an ancient power, like every word was in a language only those touched by the moon would understand. Evidently, Laura Barton was no exception because she only smiled.

    ##

    April 12th, 2017 | 11:02 P.M.

    This time, when Yelena got back in the truck, her hands weren’t covered in someone else’s blood. This time, when she pulled back onto the road and continued driving, she felt like she could slow down. This time, when she turned the dial on the truck’s air conditioning, she didn’t have to worry about a dying wolf freezing to death in the cold November air.

    Completing the rest of the journey took another two hours and change, and Yelena didn’t stop to check the map they’d brought along. She knew the coordinates of where she wanted to get to, and she remembered every exit she needed to take. While they drove, Kate continued to read, although they took a lot of breaks throughout to talk about nothing in particular.

    The waning moon lit up the one-story stone cabin she drove up to, sparking too many emotions to put into words. Cutting the engine without even a hint of worry about having been followed, Yelena got out of the car and went to the back of the truck to retrieve their bags. Kate exited the truck after stuffing Safe Harbor into the backpack at her feet, her boots flattening a spot of grass as she stopped to stare at the cabin.

    “Can you believe we’re really back here?” Kate asked reverently as she slung the backpack across her shoulder and looked back at Yelena. “I fell in love with you the moment I saw you.” Her blue eyes shimmered. Blue and blue. Blue and green. Yelena smiled.

    “Very dramatic,” she said, tugging their big suitcase from the truck bed before pulling her own backpack over her shoulder. “I fell in love with you the minute I saw you, too. Although I must admit, it was not quite the same.” Kate joined her by the truck bed to pick up the suitcase. With a dopey grin, she kissed Yelena’s temple.

    “I’m a sexy wolf,” she said, her voice jovial as she left Yelena’s side. “No shame in it.” “Funny,” Yelena muttered, chest warm with affection as she readjusted the tarp across the truck bed. The key to the cabin was hidden inside a hollow stone several feet away, embedded in the thick roots of a tree. After unlocking the door with a satisfyingly loud click, she left Kate to carry their things further inside so she could turn on the electricity at the control panel at the side of the cabin.

    When Yelena returned, Kate was nowhere to be seen. Closing the front door behind herself, Yelena hit the switch by the front door and squinted at the sudden burst of light. A second later, Kate returned from the hallway empty-handed, her backpack still slung over her shoulder.

    “Backdoor’s locked; bathroom and bedroom are empty,” Kate said with a smile, putting her bag on the kitchen table. “And I’m fricking starving.” Turning towards the counter, she spotted the little radio and switched it on. Terrible music filled the kitchen and open living area. “It’s so weird how much I’ve missed this.”

    Humming softly, Yelena placed down her own bag by the door before locking it behind herself. Shedding her jacket, she hung it over a chair and joined Kate by the kitchen counter. Slinging her arms around her girlfriend’s sturdy waist, Yelena looked at the fireplace, the couch, and the bookshelf. Aside from Natasha, no one knew they were here, and no one knew this cabin existed.

    Closing her eyes, she basked in the feeling of Kate’s hands settling on her back and neck. As she breathed in the familiar cabin air, she went through everything that had taken place in the last few months. Escaping from her handlers, ridding herself of everything that tied her to the Red Room, hijacking a truck and driving it away, finding a bleeding wolf and nursing it back to health, reuniting with Natasha, meeting and falling in love with Kate Bishop, taking down the people who hurt her, discovering Melina and the Red Room before putting it to ruin, and finally destroying the remaining hunters at Bishop & Kelly Inc. She was tired from the trip here but no longer worried. She was as safe as she would ever be, and she would never be made powerless again.

    She squeezed Kate’s waist and kissed her shoulder. No one was controlling her now. No one was looking at her now. No one was expecting anything of her now.

    February 12th, 2017 | 11:45 P.M.

    Natasha sat in a rocking chair on the porch, a bag of beef jerky held loosely in her lap. One other widow—Sonya—sat next to her, sipping on and off from a glass of red wine. The other widows were nowhere to be seen, but most of the curtains on the second floor were closed, so Yelena didn’t have to guess.

    “Waiting,” Natasha said, gesturing at the phone beside her book. “What are you doing?”

    “Water break.” Moving over to the fridge, Yelena leisurely retrieved two bottles before uncapping the first and taking a sip. Leaning against the counter, she tilted her head to the side and hummed. “Those glasses make you look like a nerd.”

    “You’re the expert,” Natasha said easily, putting down another number.

    “Ha-ha,” Yelena mocked her, taking another sip from her bottle. “She thinks she’s funny.” Down in the basement, Kate lay sprawled on her back after a tough workout. The halfway point between full moons made her more susceptible to Yelena’s attacks, though not by much. Still, it was fun to train with her. She made for a worthy adversary and never complained about Yelena practicing her assassin tricks on her.

    “Mhm,” Natasha agreed, leaning back in her chair to roll her shoulders.

    “What are you waiting for?” Yelena asked, eyeing the phone, willing it to make a noise so she could uncover one of her sister’s many secrets. Even now, they hardly knew anything about each other. In her optimistic moments, Yelena told herself they had all the time in the world to change it.

    “A call.” Natasha scribbled another two numbers on the magazine.

    “Speaking with you is like watching paint dry.” Grabbing the second bottle, Yelena rounded the table to catch Natasha’s gaze. She wouldn’t go so far as to say that Natasha’s monosyllabic answers worried her, but they did catch her interest.

    “I love how you’re turning the fact that you’re nosy into my shortcoming,” Natasha said, putting down her pencil so she could cross her arms over her chest. The smirk on her lips might have infuriated others, but it only put Yelena at ease. “You’re worse than Steve.”

    Yelena lifted one eyebrow.

    “A very funny insult,” she said, setting her bottles on the table before pulling back a chair and taking a seat. The phone sat between them, screen dark and unresponsive. “He’s in hiding too, yes?” When Natasha tilted her head, Yelena frowned. “He’s not coming here, is he?”

    “No, don’t worry,” Natasha said, reaching for the second bottle. When she unscrewed the cap, Yelena marveled at how normal she looked. She could have been anybody’s older sister. She could have been the teacher with the two kids Yelena dreamt up at the Red Room. “He’s got his own stuff to deal with. I don’t think he’s even in the country at the moment.”

    “Good.” Yelena pulled over the magazine, studying her sister’s shoddy penmanship. “Your other man-friend was bad enough.”

    “You said you liked Clint.” Natasha rolled her eyes when Yelena added two more numbers.

    “I said no such thing. You are lying again, making up stories in your head. It’s sad. You are sad.” The farmhouse was quiet and comfortable, although Yelena had yet to get used to sharing a bed with someone who wasn’t Natasha. The Barton farm gave her plenty of time to practice, yet she sometimes still rolled over and found herself woken up by the presence of Kate’s body. She experienced some other inconvenient emotions, too. It was all too easy to push Kate onto her back and kiss her while they were in bed together—all too easy to slide her hands along Kate’s chest and sides to feel closer to her. The sparring in the basement caused a physical reaction, too. The water wasn’t just a measure to hydrate; it also allowed her to escape her own desires for just a few minutes.

    “I put out some feelers about the task force that’s looking for me,” Natasha finally revealed, running a hand along the top of her head. Her hair definitely needed to be cut again, so for now, she’d pulled it back in a ponytail. “I assume busting Clint and the others out of prison poked the hornets’ nest. As much as I love a good kill first, ask questions later policy from the government, I also love knowing if it applies to me.”

    “Hm,” Yelena hummed in agreement, staring down at the mostly empty sudoku.

    “I thought about flying back to Budapest.” Pulling her right foot onto her chair, Natasha placed her chin on her knee, her left hand curled around the neck of her bottle. “Or to Russia. Maybe I’ll help Melina with her Alexei project.”

    “Natasha Romanov, liberator of mediocre men.” Yelena mimicked Natasha’s posture, putting her own chin on her knee and keeping her eyes on the table. She wondered if his hair and face would look the same. Would he still be clean-shaven? Would his smile be as wide as before? She wasn’t sure she wanted to know the answer. “I don’t want to go back. Not yet.” There was a list of widows on a thumb drive not too far from where they sat. Widows that needed to be rescued by someone. Widows waiting for orders that would never come. Alexei had helped Dreykov put them there—his right-hand man.

    “Well, you don’t have to go,” Natasha said quietly, voice warm and gentle. Years had gone by since her escape. Maybe the distance had given her a new perspective. Or maybe it just helped her compartmentalize better. “But I’ll be pissed if you and Jack get married without me.”

    With a noise that bordered on a snort, Yelena slapped her palm on the table to emphasize her mirth. The phone jumped a little from the impact mere seconds before it started ringing. The screen lit up, flashing the word Anonymous Caller at the ceiling. For a second, Yelena considered scooping it up and learning who was on the other side, but her hesitation proved to be her undoing. Before she could make a choice, the phone was in Natasha’s hand.

    “Hey,” she said, smugly grinning at Yelena from across the kitchen table. The murmuring of another person’s voice buzzed at her ear, prompting Natasha to roll her eyes and grin even harder, though her mirth was quickly replaced with genuine affection. “If you’re so keen on following protocol, why don’t you tell me your code word first?” Her voice settled in a lower register, causing Yelena to fake gag with great exaggeration. Natasha barely glanced at her. Just as Natasha laughed, Yelena heard footsteps on the stairs to the basement. Setting down her foot, she rose from her chair, a half-empty water bottle held loosely in one hand. Kate’s head appeared in the doorway; a matching towel was wrapped around her neck. There was little to no sweat on her, making Yelena wonder if she’d donned the towel out of a sense of solidarity.

    “It’s sweet how you think using the wrong passcode would make an imposter reveal herself. Remember, she’d have to be more talented than me,” Natasha continued while Yelena shook her head and met Kate halfway across the living room.

    “Did I miss anything interesting?” Kate asked, leaning sideways to peek into the kitchen over Yelena’s shoulder. “Who’s she talking to?” Furrowing her brows, Kate squinted first at Natasha, then at Yelena, her next words much quieter. “What’s got her smiling that much?

    Can’t be good, can it? Is she planning something else? I know I said I was down with taking down the government, if we have to, but I didn’t think it’d be so soon. We only just got back here, and we’ve got the stuff with my mom, and—”

    Utterly and horribly charmed by the incoherent babble, Yelena covered Kate’s mouth with her hand, failing to hide her own smile.

    “Don’t worry, Kate Bishop,” she said, patting Kate’s chest with her other hand. “Natasha has merely lost her mind over a woman. It happens.” Natasha was too far away to hear their conversation, but even if they’d been standing right in front of her, Yelena didn’t think her lovesick sister would have noticed. With this new teasing material mentally stowed away, Yelena dropped her hand from Kate’s mouth. “Let’s go. I want you to read to me.”

    With a pout, Kate allowed herself to be dragged along by the hand. “Safe Harbor?” she lamented, following Yelena like a puppy who was being punished. With a smirk that Kate couldn’t see, Yelena ascended the stairs to the second floor of the farmhouse.

    Maybe later, she and Natasha would talk some more about splitting up. Maybe later, after spending more time with Kate and her soothing voice, Yelena would feel ready for it. The thought of Natasha somewhere else—somewhere Yelena couldn’t easily get to her—was not a pleasant one. Maybe later, she would tell Kate. Maybe later.

    ##

    April 13th, 2017 | 09:38 A.M.

    Exhaling deeply, Yelena expelled the last of her thoughts through her mouth, wishing she could watch the water particles escape into the air. Wintery air always made her feel like a dragon, emitting sulfurous vapors in preparation for spewing fire. The thought never failed to make her smile, but the morning sunshine wasn’t bad, either. She especially enjoyed the wind gliding across her bare arms, her torso protected by an olive-green vest with a vast number of pockets.

    “In the mood to set up a snare or two?” she said to Kate, never taking her eyes off the sprawling landscape before her. When she didn’t get an answer, she looked to her left, seeing nothing but the tree line and the truck. Kate was nowhere to be found, and a ball of worry settled in the pit of her stomach. “Kate?” Stepping closer to the cabin, she turned her head in all directions but still couldn’t make out any movement among the gently swaying leaves.

    “Kate?!”

    “What?” Kate asked, poking her head out of the front door, her hand wrapped around a bag of banana chips. “Sorry, I just went back in for a quick snack. Are you okay? Did something happen?” Throwing the bag behind herself, she jogged down the front steps of the porch, her eyes instantly concerned. “Yelena?” Heartbeat settling, Yelena slapped her palms against Kate’s chest. “Hey, hey,” Kate said, rubbing her palms up and down Yelena’s arms. A piece of banana chip clung to her cheek. “I’m sorry?”

    “No,” Yelena said, grabbing the front of Kate’s jean jacket. “I’m sorry.” Letting out a shaky breath, she pulled Kate close enough to kiss her chin. “I’m sorry. I was stupid.”

    “No, hey,” Kate replied, smiling tentatively. “I should have said something.” She lifted her hand, wiping at her cheek and missing the banana chip entirely. With a smile of her own, Yelena pulled off the chip. “Thanks.”

    “Go grab our bag,” Yelena said, taking a step back after letting go of Kate entirely. Quickly, Kate closed the distance between them before leaning down to kiss Yelena’s cheekbone.

    Then, she did as she was told, jogging back up the porch steps to make her way inside.

    Quietly, Yelena spoke a prayer Melina had taught her when she was a little girl, one she’d sometimes recited in her head during reconditioning or while recovering from a particularly grievous wound. Glancing behind herself, Yelena thought of Natasha. She was with Melina—

    maybe even with Alexei if their plan was successful. There’d been no communication between them for a couple of days, but Yelena wasn’t worried. She wasn’t.

    “All ready to go,” Kate said, closing and locking the door to their cabin. The back of her jacket was embroidered with a wolf’s snarling head. “Can’t wait to see all the blooming flowers. You think there’ll be any squirrels or rabbits?”

    “Yes,” Yelena said, a flash of warmth shooting up her arm when Kate grabbed her hand and started walking. While Kate continued to chatter on and on about the flowers and animals they might see, Yelena found herself pondering once again how much had changed in the short time she’d been free. In the Red Room, no widow had ever been left unattended or on her own for very long while stationed at home base. At all times, she had shared a room with another widow, but never for more than a day or two. It did little to stop Yelena from caring about the others, but she always recognized the attempt at separation for what it was. Living with another person—living with Kate—was so unlike everything else she experienced; she didn’t know how to put a name to her own feelings.

    Yet, despite her lasting paranoia and trauma, she found herself settling into the rhythms of her new life with terrifying ease. There was no need to roll into a blanket cocoon every night because she knew Kate would be there the second someone might attempt to sneak in. Every day, she woke to the sound of gentle snoring, and every morning, she made a big breakfast for her sleepy werewolf while watching the sunrise. After feeding Kate, Yelena then went about the business of discovering what she liked to do for fun. She learned to bake a variety of breads (all the while speaking to Laura Barton on the phone). She learned how to play the new and improved video games of the twenty-first century (all the while listening to Kate prattle on about her favorites). She learned how to shoot a bow and arrow (all the while listening to Sonya laugh about her form).

    Later in the day, Yelena and Kate usually went out for walks around Central Park. Partly because Central Park was the only spot of green in a city of concrete and brass, and partly because the thought of getting to walk somewhere under her own power filled her with so much joy and purpose that she couldn’t bear to sit still inside the apartment for hours on end. In some ways, this newfound life settled into just as much of a routine as her previous one. But one thing that struck her every time she made love to Kate, cooked a big breakfast, or walked around the city was how different it felt. How different it felt to make decisions and have them mean something. No matter what happened with the government, she knew she would never go back to how it was before. The Red Room was gone, but there were men like Dreykov all over the world, men she knew she’d stop if given the chance.

    “Oh, this spot is nice,” Kate said, pulling Yelena to a boulder and a fallen-over tree. Plonking her butt down on the trunk, Kate shucked off her backpack and set it on the ground. Looking up at Yelena, she smiled, her cheeks flushed with pleasure. While the Red Room had made Yelena long for something other than silence, it had also made her want to be alone and away from other people. Away from prying, judging eyes. It had taken a while for her to understand that Kate was different. To Yelena, who had not known the loving touch of another person since she was six years old, the connection she and Kate had felt like a terrifying thing—a fragile offering of the universe, of the moon and her children. Kate, who seemed just as much of an aberration to the natural order of things as Yelena herself, felt as real and tangible to her as few other things ever had.

    Sitting down next to Kate, she leaned her back against the boulder and stared out at the lush, green landscape all around her. Thriving life could be found all over the place. Small patches of hardy moss grew on the side of trees or on especially big root structures. Birds flew about the thick canopy, and other critters suited to the surroundings could be heard and perceived in other ways at nearly every time of day. If she listened hard enough, she could even make out the soft splashing of a nearby creek, finally unencumbered by snow or ice.

    “I am you. As much as you are me,” Yelena whispered to herself, looking up at the sky.

    “Look, Natasha. Look.” She closed her eyes. “I am not upside down anymore.” Expelling her breath in a slow and steady exhale, Yelena opened her senses to the woods. “How about you?” Leaning her head to the side, she gazed at Kate, who gazed back at her. “How about you?”

    Eyes and hands as warm as the cabin fireplace, Kate cupped Yelena’s cheeks and pulled her closer. Pressing their foreheads together, Kate brushed Yelena’s nose with her own, her breath sweet and soft as it brushed Yelena’s lips.

    “I don’t think I’ll ever be upside down again,” Kate murmured, like she knew what Yelena was talking about, and the games Yelena used to play with her sister. Heat seared beneath Yelena’s skin as she closed the distance to capture Kate’s lips in a kiss. A spring breeze gently blew across her arms, doing its best to cool her down. Kate drew herself back only far enough to kiss the back of Yelena’s nose. “If I call you my moon and my stars, will you laugh at me?”

    “Maybe later,” Yelena said, stroking Kate’s neck.

    With a grin too wide for her face, Kate leaned back in, her kiss failing horribly because she couldn’t stop smiling. This time, when Yelena leaned back against the boulder, she didn’t feel anything but relief. The Red Room could not hurt her anymore. The Red Room could not silence her anymore. The Red Room could not take her family anymore.

    “Will you read to me?” Yelena asked, squeezing Kate’s hand.

    “Sure,” Kate said, unzipping the backpack to pull out the rumpled old copy of Safe Harbor. “Final page?”

    “Yeah,” Yelena confirmed, watching a couple of birds chase each other across a branch.

    Kate cleared her throat, the snarling wolf’s head glittering in the sun as her Jack Wolfskin boots crunched on the ground while she readjusted her feet. “Happy to be back at work?”

    Kate read, her voice smooth and rich and beautiful. “Just happy, Reese said softly, smiling tenderly.” Her handsome profile looked even better in the light. When she briefly faltered in her reading, Yelena stepped in, knowing the words by heart.

    “See you at home, Dr. King,” she said, reaching out to brush her palm across Kate’s shoulder —the one that now-dead hunters had put a bullet in once upon a time. Kate rolled her eyes, but she couldn’t help smiling.

    “You can count on it, Sheriff,” Kate finished, closing the book and holding it loosely in both hands. Turning her head, she grinned at Yelena. Blue and blue, then blue and green. Yelena didn’t fight the urge to lean in and kiss her wolf. When she pulled back, Kate nudged her temple with her forehead, her voice low and soft. “The End,” she whispered, while Yelena hugged her waist and let out a breath she’d held for the past six months.

    “The End,” Yelena repeated, her shoulders relaxing. “Foolish wolf. You owe me three rabbits.”
